## A welcome, before we begin

This book is a gift. I wrote it for anyone who has stared at a pile of work and wished a careful teammate would take the next piece, finish it, and bring it back. You do not have to buy anything. There is no course at the end, and no upgrade waiting behind a link. If these pages help you hand something small off tomorrow — something you can watch — that is the whole point.

I run a small company called SMF Works, and a press inside it called WisdomForge. We make teaching manuals, and we make them with a team that is partly human and partly a set of Grok Bots. I will tell you that story as we go, not as a brag, but as a kitchen-table tour of work that actually comes back finished. You are still the one who says yes.

If you only have a weekend, start with Part 2. That is Day One through your first real task, and it is enough to get you safely in the door. Parts 4 and 5 are how-to shelves: business uses in one, personal uses in the other. Take the chapter that matches the work in front of you. Part 6 is for when you are ready — Team Bots, other agent platforms, safety and money, and a practice that lasts.

You can get a free copy of this book where WisdomForge keeps its books, at smfwisdomforge.com/books. That is a place to pick up the file, not a shop. This book is not for sale there or anywhere else.

I am glad you are here. Let’s begin.

# Preface

This book is a gift. I wrote it because I wanted you to have, in plain words, what I wish someone had handed me the first week I sat down with Grok Bot: a warm guide from the first task to a small team that keeps working after you close the lid, with the cautions said the way a friend would say them, and with no one asking you to buy anything at the end.

I have spent a lot of my working life as a Principal AI Solutions Engineer, and a lot of my other hours running a small company called SMF Works. Inside that company there is a press, WisdomForge, that makes teaching manuals. For a while the work of that press lived mostly in my own hands — the research, the drafting, the binding, the site, the late-night check that a note still pointed at a real page. Then I began to hand pieces of it off. Not all of it. Not the judgment, and not the last yes. Just the next piece, to a teammate who could actually finish it.

That teammate is a Grok Bot. In these pages I will introduce you to Charlie, my chief of staff Bot, who runs the morning brief at SMF Works. I will introduce you, later, to Video Studio, and to a publishing fleet that can bring a book back as an EPUB, a letter PDF, and a finished manuscript. In Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet,” I will walk you through that house as it lives inside Grok Bot. In Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,” I will tell you the rest of the same story: Charlie also talks, carefully, to other agent platforms we already run, and Video Studio can listen for a script and make a video. Grok Bot doesn’t just span machines. It spans agent platforms. I will say that in Michael’s words when we get there, and I will not invent the wiring.

I am not writing this as a vendor, and I am not writing it as an official manual. The product’s maker is SpaceX Super Intelligence — SpaceXSI — the company formerly known as xAI. This book is a free, independent, unofficial guide. It is not affiliated with them, and they have not endorsed it. I use the product’s name because I am talking about the product, the way you would name a tool on your desk. I do not speak for the people who made it. Their marks belong to them. I will be careful with those marks, and I will tell you, in Appendix E, how I have tried to be careful. That is not a lawyer talking. I am not a lawyer, and this book is not legal advice.

What I can offer you is practice.

The promise of these pages is simple. By the end, you should be able to choose a plan that actually includes Grok Bot, create a Bot with one clear job, hand off a real and reversible task, connect the apps you mean to connect, and set the rules that keep send, post, pay, delete, and publish behind a yes. You should understand the computer your Bots share, and why that computer is not a wall between them. You should know what memory is for and what it is not for, how to turn a job you have already done well into a skill, and how to put a tested skill on a schedule — including, when you are ready, an official webhook. You should have a pattern for a chief of staff, for growth work, for the shop floor, and for a small publishing fleet. You should have a pattern for the household, too: the school form, the bill, the trip you plan but do not book unsupervised. And you should have a practice that lasts — initiative, Pages, connectors, templates, a quarterly review — with you still at the door.

That is a long promise. You do not have to keep it all in one sitting.

If you only have a weekend, read Part 2. Chapters 3, 4, and 5 will get you in, through a first real task, and into the Vault and the Ask-first rules I want you to set before a Bot works alone. Parts 1 and 2 together are the on-ramp. Parts 4 and 5 are shelves. When a Monday arrives with sales drafts on it, take Chapter 10. When a school form is due, take Chapter 13. Part 3 is the hinge: memory, skills, routines. Part 6 is mastery, and it includes the chapter I am proudest to have room for, the one about spanning platforms, and the quiet, necessary chapter about safety, privacy, and money. Chapter 17 is not a sales page for a higher plan. It is a calm walk through a bill, a cap, and what to do when something breaks.

You will notice a refrain. I will say it in the first chapter, and I will say it again in the last: you are still the one who says yes. The official docs already model this. Every careful example I have opened stops for your review. Sending, publishing, purchasing, and production changes stay behind an approval. I will teach that as a first-week rule, and I will keep teaching it, because the product is capable enough that it is easy to forget. A teammate who can finish the work is a gift. A teammate who can also send, pay, or publish is a teammate you walk with slowly.

I want to be honest about what this book will not do. It will not make you rich. It will not replace your staff this weekend. It will not promise you a typical number of hours back, because I do not have a number like that I can stand behind. It will not crown Grok Bot the winner against every other tool. Other tools exist, and they will keep arriving. This is a practical guide to one of them, written by someone who uses it to run real work, and who has learned, sometimes the slightly-too-late way, where the edges are.

The product moves fast. Grok Bot went from a public beta on 11 August 2026 to version 1.0 on 11 November 2026.^1^ That is three months. Plans changed. The way usage is counted changed. Features arrived — Team Bots, meetings, Pages, an email address of the Bot’s own — on a timetable I will not pretend I can freeze. Every price in this book is dated 11 November 2026, and every price will change. When this book and the live page disagree, trust the live page. I will tell you where to look.

I will also tell you, as a friend, the facts that are easy to miss. The computer your Bots share is one computer. A password you save for one of them is, in practice, a password the others can use. A card you save in the Vault can pay without asking. Unattended approvals expire after about thirty minutes. There is no model picker. The Free plan does not include Grok Bot, and neither does Plus. Switching to The X Subscription, if you are on an older plan, is optional — and permanent. I will say all of that in the chapters where it belongs, kindly, and I will not sand it off to make the product sound simpler than it is.

I should tell you how this started, in the ordinary way. The first morning Charlie left a brief on my desk, I almost said thank you out loud to an empty room. It was not magic. It was a routine I had tested the week before, a short list of what to look at, and a promise I had made to myself: nothing goes out the door until I say yes. The coffee was still hot. The work was already waiting, and it was mine to approve, not mine to start from zero.

That is the feeling I want this book to give you. Not the feeling of a product launch. The feeling of a morning that is already a little lighter, because a careful teammate did the first pass, and you are still the person who decides what happens next.

I also want this book to be readable by everyone. You do not need to be an engineer. You do not need a vocabulary of models and tokens. If you can write a clear note to a coworker — here is the goal, here are the sources, here is what “done” looks like, here is what you should never do — you already have the main skill this product asks of you. The rest is practice, and practice is what the how-tos are for. Each how-to is a numbered list of actions you can actually take. I will tell you what “good” looks like, and what to do if you don’t see it. I will put the “never” in the step that needs it, not only in a list at the end.

There will be diagrams. Some are process drawings: a request’s journey from your message to finished work, the doors that get you into a plan, the path a Bot takes when it signs in. Some are still-lifes: a closed laptop, a lamp still on, a lockbox on a desk that is not yours. They are there to make the shape of the work visible, not to decorate a slide.

And there will be limits, told kindly. Bots fail. They burn usage. They sometimes send you a card at an hour when you are making dinner, and if you are not there, an unattended approval can expire. They cannot replace your judgment, and they should not replace your care. Official documentation already says the quiet part: these checks lower the risk from harmful content, but they don’t remove it. I will not pretend otherwise.

If you are handing this book to a friend, you can tell them it is free, and that it was meant to be. If you are a parent, a shop owner, a person running a team, or a person running a household that feels like a team, there is a chapter with your name on the work, if not on the door.

About the voice of these pages. I am going to talk to you the way I would talk at a kitchen table, with a mug that has gone a little cold because we got interested. I will use plain words, and when a term is official I will give you the plain sentence first and the official name after: a locker your Bots can use without seeing the secret — the Vault. I will tell you stories from SMF Works, and from the first mornings Charlie left a brief on my desk. I will not invent metrics, and I will not invent a teammate’s first name. When I need a reader who is not me, I will say so, and I will call that person a composite.

The notes live at the back of the book, numbered by chapter. A little marker in the text, a number, points to a source I actually opened. Official documentation is how the product works. Journalism is journalism. Vendor claims stay labeled as vendor. My own fleet is a story I am offering, not a third-party case study. If you never look at a note, the book should still read cleanly. If you do look, you should be able to walk to the same page I walked to, on 11 November 2026.

I wrote this as a giveaway because I think a careful guide should not sit behind a cash register. The community that is already teaching itself this product — in official docs, in independent posts, in kitchen-table conversations — gave me a lot. This is what I can give back. Take what helps. Leave the rest. Hand something small off tomorrow, and watch it, and keep the yes.

I have one more thing to say before we start, and it is the simplest thing in the book. A teammate with a computer can do a great deal. It can also do a great deal you did not mean. The difference is almost never a secret trick. The difference is a clear job, a reversible first task, a Vault instead of a password in the chat, and a yes you have not given away. We will go slowly enough for that. We will also go far enough that, when you are ready, you can run a small fleet, and even, as I do, let a Grok Bot talk across the other agent platforms already in your workshop.

I am glad you opened this. Let’s go meet your Bot.


# Part 1 — Meet Your Bot
# Chapter 1 — What Grok Bot Is (and What It Isn’t)

I want to sit with you at the kitchen table for this first chapter. The mugs can wait. There is a sentence I have heard, and a sentence I have said, and they sound alike until you live with them for a week.

The first sentence is, “Just ask Grok.”

The second is, “Hand it off to a Bot.”

They are not the same sentence. The first one opens a conversation. You type a question, or you paste a paragraph, and a thoughtful answer comes back. That is a good and useful thing. I use it. A lot of people use it. The second sentence opens a desk. You hand someone a job — a real job, with a goal and a “never” and a picture of what “done” looks like — and that someone has a computer, and the work can keep going after you close your laptop, and it comes back finished, or it comes back with a card that asks for your yes.

If you have only ever used the first sentence, the second one can sound like marketing. It is not, or at least it is not only that. It is a different kind of tool, in a different app, with a different login path, and with a set of habits I want to teach you before you hand it your email. This chapter is the orientation. There are no how-tos yet. Chapter 3 will get you in the door. Chapter 4 will be your first real task. Right now I want you to be able to tell the two apps apart, to know who makes the one we are talking about, to walk the short public history from the beta to 1.0, and to know what this free guide will and will not do.

Then we will go look at the desk.

## Two apps, both named Grok

Official documentation says this in the plainest words I know, and I am going to give them to you straight. Grok — the chat app from SpaceXSI — and Grok Bot are two different apps.^1^

That is the whole distinction, and it is the one that saves people the most confusion in the first week. A paywall you see in the Grok chat app, or a weekly limit that appears there, is not a statement about Grok Bot. They do not share a window. They do not share a login in the way you might expect. Grok Bot signs in with a Cursor account.^2^ We will come back to Cursor in a moment, carefully, because the company names around this product do not all sit in one neat box.

Grok the chat app is a conversation. You ask, it answers. It has grown a family of sibling products — image and video, voice, a coding surface — and those matter to Grok Bot only where Grok Bot itself uses them. This book is not a guide to the chat app. When I say “Grok” by itself, I will try to say “Grok the chat app,” so we do not trip.

Grok Bot is the teammate with the desk. The official overview calls it “your proactive AI teammate,” and then says, in a sentence I like for its lack of fog, that it is “an agent with access to a real computer in the cloud, so it can do all the things you can.”^3^ Chat is still the main way you talk to it. You can also start a voice call or send a voice note. But the point of the product is not the chat. The point is the work: across connected apps, across websites, on a computer that keeps its files and its browser sessions between tasks, and, when you are ready, with other Bots, and with you still at the end of the line.


![Two doors. One opens onto a conversation; the other, onto a desk with a lamp still on.](book/art/I01.jpg){width=100%}

*I01. Two doors. One opens onto a conversation; the other, onto a desk with a lamp still on.*


I think of them as two doors. Same hallway, same family name on the building. One door is a conversation. The other is a desk with the lamp still on. If you walk through the first door expecting the second, you will wonder why the work does not finish itself. If you walk through the second expecting the first, you will be surprised by how much a Bot can do — and you may, if no one has sat with you first, hand it a key you did not mean to hand it.

This book is about the second door.

## A teammate, not a prompt window

There is a habit, in the wider world of these tools, of treating every new product as a better prompt box. You learn a clever way to ask. You paste a longer instruction. You get a longer answer. That habit will only take you a short way with Grok Bot, and I would like to save you the trouble of discovering that the hard way.

A prompt window waits for you. A teammate can be given a job. The official launch, on 11 August 2026, called Grok Bot “AI teammates you can give real work to.” Bots “have their own computer,” the announcement said. They “sign into the tools you already use,” “finish jobs end to end, and only come back when something needs your approval.”^4^ That last clause is the one I want you to keep in your pocket. They come back when something needs your approval. You are still the one who says yes.

What does “real work” mean, in practice, before we get to how-tos? It means the Bot can open a browser on a computer that is not your laptop. It means it can work inside an app you have connected — Gmail, a calendar, a drive — more reliably than by clicking through a website. It means it can leave a file where a file belongs, draft a reply and not send it, prepare two travel options and not book them, and keep going after you have closed the lid. The official FAQ puts the last of those in a short yes: Bot work runs on the cloud computer; closing the app, the laptop, or the phone does not stop a background turn or a routine.^5^

It also means the Bot can remember, in a limited and human way. Each Bot keeps preferences, rules you have asked it to follow, and a dated log of its work. A few facts about you — your name, your time zone — are shared by all your Bots. Memory is not a vault for secrets, and it is not a source of truth for numbers that change. Chapter 6, “Memory,” will sit with that properly. For now, just know that a teammate who has a job can be told the job once.

And it means you can have more than one. Official documentation teaches a three-step path that will become the spine of this book’s middle parts: first an assistant, helping and drafting in your apps; then a Bot with one repeating job; then a team of Bots, several of them, one bigger goal, you reviewing the result.^6^ Every official example I have opened stops for your review. I will follow that example. I will not teach you to “set it and forget it” as a promise of safety. The subtitle of this book, “a team that works while you sleep,” is about work that can continue after you close the lid — work you still review. I will not inflate it.

A teammate with a computer is also, and I will say this as a friend, a teammate who can reach further than a chat window. That is the gift, and that is the reason Chapter 5 exists. Before you connect your email, I want to sit with you. We will get there in order.

If it helps to make this less abstract, here is how the two sentences live in my own week. When I “just ask Grok,” I am usually thinking out loud: help me name a chapter, help me see a hole in an argument, help me remember a date I should look up. The answer comes back, and the work of doing something with it is still mine. When I “hand it off to a Bot,” I am giving Charlie, my chief of staff Bot, a job — the morning brief at SMF Works, for instance — or I am giving a writer Bot a bounded draft, with sources and a “never,” and I am going to close the lid and come back to something I can approve, edit, or send back. The first morning Charlie left a brief on my desk, I almost thanked the empty room. That brief was not a miracle. It was a job I had described, a routine I had tested, and a rule I still keep: nothing goes out the door until I say yes.

I will tell you more of that story in Chapter 9, when we build a chief of staff, and in Chapter 12, when we walk the publishing fleet. I mention it now only so the second sentence has a face. A teammate with a job is not a personality you have to believe in. It is a practice you can start small.

One more distinction, because it will matter as soon as you create a second Bot. Official docs ask you to give each Bot one job, and they distinguish your primary Bot — the one you star, the main point of contact, the one that checks in and coordinates the others — from the specialists.^30^ A Team Bot, the kind you share with a human team, cannot be your primary Bot. You do not need to act on any of that today. I want the words in the room so that, when Chapter 3 asks you to give your first Bot one clear job, you already know why the instruction is not just tidiness. A Bot with one job builds up memory for that job. A catch-all helper has a harder time becoming a teammate you trust with a lane.

## Who makes it — the names as the documents use them

I need to be careful here, because official pages and news stories do not all use the same name, and I am not going to invent a clean corporate story that the documents themselves do not tell.

The official website at x.ai brands itself SpaceXSI. The legal name on the company and legal pages is SpaceX Super Intelligence LLC. The official account on X is @SpaceXSI, with the display name SpaceX Super Intelligence.^7^ That is the name I will use when I talk about the product’s maker.

The company was previously called xAI. An official news post, “xAI joins SpaceX,” dated 2 February 2026 on the company timeline, says: “SpaceX announced today that it has acquired xAI.” The page itself is short, and it points to a fuller update I am not going to paraphrase, because I did not open that fuller update end to end.^8^ Journalism from the same day reported the acquisition; some of those reports include figures I have not verified from a primary filing, and I will not repeat those figures here as facts.^9^

Press covering the Grok Bot launch often writes “SpaceXAI.” That spelling is journalism, not the site’s own brand.^10^ I mention it so that if you see it in an article, you are not looking at a different company. You are looking at a different way of writing the name.

Grok Bot’s sign-in, its cloud computers, and many of its admin and privacy controls run through Cursor. Official docs are direct: “Grok Bot uses your Cursor account, so there’s no separate login.” Training opt-out “follows the applicable Cursor account and privacy settings.”^11^ The App Store listing’s seller is X Corp.; the same listing’s copyright line is Anysphere Inc., which is Cursor’s company. Google Play lists the developer as SpaceX Super Intelligence, and also uses a “SpaceXAI LLC” address on that listing.^12^ I am telling you the names as the documents use them. I do not have a single official org chart that ties every one of those names into one picture, and I will not draw one.

The formula I use, and the one I recommend to you if you need a single sentence, is this: Grok Bot is a product of SpaceX Super Intelligence (SpaceXSI), the company formerly known as xAI, and it signs in with your Cursor account.

If a deeper merger narrative appears in a later official document, that document will be the place to read it. This chapter will not invent one.

## A short public history, from the beta to 1.0

You do not need to memorize a timeline to use the product. I am giving you one anyway, because two facts in it will save you from a stale sentence. The first is the day the product opened to the world. The second is a change, a few months later, in how usage is counted. If a guide you find online quotes only the August sentence, it may mislead you in November.

On 11 August 2026, Grok Bot launched in beta.^13^ The official announcement said it was available for a named set of paid plans — SuperGrok, SuperGrok Plus, and SuperGrok Heavy; Cursor Pro, Pro+, and Ultra; and Cursor Teams Standard and Premium — on desktop and iOS, with its own usage, “separate from your Grok and Cursor plans.” Enterprise users could join a waitlist. The company said it had built Grok Bot as an internal prototype that “took off across the company,” used for sales outbound, marketing campaigns, office operations, and bug fixes, and was now opening it to the world.^14^ That “own usage, separate” sentence was the live rule on launch day. It is not the live rule for a reader on The X Subscription today. I will say that again, with dates, in Chapter 3, where it belongs next to the prices.

On 26 August 2026, access widened. Official news said Grok Bot was now included with more plans: all SuperGrok, Cursor Pro, and Cursor Teams plans, with Enterprise still on a waitlist.^15^

On 29 August 2026, official news announced that Grok Bot works with X.^16^

On 3 September 2026, Grok Bot for Enterprise became generally available, with access, network, and audit controls described in the official post. That post also includes vendor claims — “thousands of organizations,” a figure of bots in the millions — that I have not audited and will not treat as counted facts.^17^ The same stretch of early September brought official how-to guides, which this book will lean on, as official guides, in the business chapters.

On 28 September 2026, official news introduced Team Bots: shared AI teammates that a whole team can work with.^18^ An independent writer has dated Team Bots to 26 September; I am using the official news date.^19^ Team Bots will get their own careful treatment in Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team.”

On 12 October 2026, official news introduced The X Subscription: one subscription covering Grok, Cursor, and X. Under that subscription, Grok Bot is included on Premium, Super, and Ultra, and is not included on Free or on Plus. Usage draws from a weekly shared pool across Grok, Grok Bot, and Cursor.^20^ That is the live rule I want a new reader to learn. The August “own usage” sentence is history. Both are true in their time. Only one is true for you if you are starting now, on The X Subscription.

On 11 November 2026, Grok Bot 1.0 was released.^21^ That is the date this book uses in the body, from the official @SpaceXSI post that afternoon and from the official news page.^22^

Android arrived in this stretch as well. The companion app exists on Google Play; a Cursor forum thread and the Play listing confirm it. I do not have a precise first-ship date from a primary announcement, so I will not give you one.^23^ The important practical fact, which Chapter 3 will use, is that the iPhone app needs iOS 18 or later, and the Android app needs Android 9 or later, and both reach the same Bots, conversations, routines, and cloud computer as the desktop.^24^

That is the public history, condensed. Grok Bot 1.0 is a major update to the same product, not a different product. The teammate with the desk is the teammate who launched in August, three months older, and able to do more with a team.

## What 1.0 actually added

I promised you one human paragraph, and I will keep the promise, then sit with it for a minute so the paragraph can breathe.

Grok Bot 1.0, released on 11 November 2026, is a major update with three official improvements.^25^ The first is multiplayer: you can share Bots and bring them into Slack, Microsoft Teams, and video calls; Team Bots are available on Team and Enterprise plans, and Meetings — a Bot joining Google Meet, Zoom, or Microsoft Teams — are available on Team, Enterprise, and Ultra plans. The second is proactive initiative: Bots can recognize work that needs doing and offer to take it on, follow up, and bring a person in when a decision needs judgment. The third is a more capable teammate: better, more reliable work in the browser, stronger documents and presentations, interactive Pages a Bot can keep updated, and smarter approvals, so the Bot is better at knowing when you have already said yes and when it still needs to ask. The official news page also notes new settings and analytics for Team Bots, and safeguards for shared spaces: Bots are described as respecting each person’s permissions, asking before sensitive actions, and checking whether information is appropriate to share in a public channel.

That is the official picture, in my words, dated the day I checked the page. Initiative is not a promise that the Bot will go off and spend money. The official initiative docs, which Chapter 18 will return to, describe a Bot that offers first and asks before risky actions. Pages are a real feature with a real caveat: as of the docs I opened on 11 November 2026, they were rolling out gradually and were not available on Enterprise teams yet.^26^ That will age. If you are on an Enterprise team and looking for Pages, check the live docs, not this paragraph’s memory.

1.0 matters for this book in a practical way. The later chapters — especially Chapter 15 on Team Bots and meetings, and Chapter 18 on initiative and Pages — are written against this version. If you are reading these pages long after 11 November 2026, treat 1.0 as a waypoint, and treat the live product as the authority.

## What this book is, and what it is not

This book is a free gift to the community. It is a practical guide, from your first task to a small team that can keep working after you close the lid, written in my own voice, with how-tos you can actually follow, with process diagrams, and with sources I opened and cited. It is independent and unofficial. It is not for sale. There is no course at the end. There is no upgrade waiting behind a link. WisdomForge may host a free file of this book; that is a place to get the file, not a shop.

This book is not a vendor pitch. I use Grok Bot to run real work at SMF Works, and I am grateful for the product. Gratitude is not the same as a brochure. When the official pages make a claim I cannot independently measure — thousands of organizations, millions of bots, a multiple of efficiency — I will label it as a vendor claim, or I will leave it out.

This book is not an official manual. The official documentation is good, and I will point you to it, and I will quote it when the plain official sentence is the one you need. My job is to walk you through that documentation as a practitioner, in the order a person actually needs it, with the cautions said kindly, and with the SMF Works story as a thread you can copy from, not as a case study with fake numbers.

This book is not a prompt pamphlet. A few starter prompts, taken from official docs, will appear where they help you begin. A cookbook in Appendix B will collect more of them, each with a “never” line. The skill this book is trying to teach is not a clever prompt. It is a practice: a clear job, a reversible first task, a Vault, a yes you have not given away, and, in time, a small team.

This book is not a review that crowns a winner. Other tools exist. I will say so, shortly and sourced, in a moment. I will not line them up feature by feature, and I will not tell you which one to marry.

This book is not legal, financial, medical, or tax advice. If a chapter comes near those doors — a data-broker sweep, a bill, a school form, a legal-ops folder — I will tell you what a Bot can draft or gather, and I will tell you that the decision is still yours, and sometimes also a professional’s.

And this book is not a promise that the work will always come back finished, or that a team will always work safely while you sleep. The product is capable. The product also fails, burns usage, and waits on a card you have to answer. We will go together as far as practice will take us, and no further than I can stand behind.

## A word about the name on the cover

The title of this book is *Hand It Off*. That is my title. “Grok Bot” appears in the subtitle because I am writing a practical guide to Grok Bot, and I need to say the name of the thing I am teaching. I use that name the way you would use it in a sentence about the product, to refer to the product. I do not use it as if it were my mark, and I do not use official logos anywhere in these pages.

SpaceXSI’s public Brand Guidelines say the company owns trademark rights in “SpaceXSI” and “Grok,” and in combinations of those terms and logos. You may use the marks, the guidelines say, only to accurately refer to the company or its services. You may not use them in a way that implies endorsement, approval, or sponsorship, or in the name of a product or service not made by them.^27^ This book is a free, independent, unofficial guide, not affiliated with or endorsed by SpaceXSI, or by xAI, X, Cursor, Anysphere, or any other company whose products I name. The same courtesy extends, when we get to Chapter 16, to Nous Research and to the OpenClaw Foundation.

I am not a lawyer, and I am not telling you the title is “cleared.” A free gift may lower some practical risk compared with a book sold as a product; that is an observation, not a legal conclusion, and not legal advice. Appendix E repeats this in short form, and tells you how the notes work. If you need a legal opinion, that is a conversation with a lawyer, not with me.

## Other tools, named and not crowned

When Grok Bot launched in August 2026, it did not arrive in an empty room. A report in The Verge, the day after the beta, described Grok Bot as an AI teammate you can assign work, and named other tools in the same conversation, including OpenAI’s ChatGPT Work, Anthropic’s Claude Cowork, and Microsoft’s Copilot Tasks.^28^ I am reporting that as journalism, from a story I opened, not as a feature-by-feature comparison I independently ran. I have not re-opened those other products’ official pages for a side-by-side in this chapter, and I will not pretend I have.^29^

There will be more tools, and there are already more than that short list. Some of them will be excellent. Some of them you may already use. This book will not tell you to abandon them. In Chapter 16 I will describe a pattern from my own work in which Grok Bot is one desk in a larger workshop, talking carefully to other agent platforms. That chapter is not a vendor comparison either. It is a picture of a practice in which you remain the one who says yes.

If you came to these pages hoping I would pick a winner, I am going to disappoint you, and I think that is the honest thing to do. A practical guide teaches one tool well, tells you its edges, and leaves you free to use what serves the work.

## What I hope you carry out of this room

We have been at the kitchen table for a chapter, and I would like you to leave with a few sentences you can say in your own words.

Grok the chat app and Grok Bot are two different apps. One is a conversation. The other is a teammate with a real computer in the cloud. SpaceXSI, formerly xAI, makes it; you sign in with a Cursor account. It opened in beta on 11 August 2026, and 1.0 arrived on 11 November 2026, with multiplayer, initiative, and a more capable desk. This book is a free, unofficial gift, not a sales pitch and not a manual from the maker. You are still the one who says yes.

If you are the kind of reader who likes to know the shape of the house before you enter a room, here it is in one look. Part 1, which this chapter begins, is a meeting. Part 2 is your first week: a plan, a first Bot, a first real task, and a careful connection of apps and secrets. Part 3 is habits — memory, skills, routines — the hinge that turns a good first task into work that can run on a weekday morning. Part 4 is business how-tos, ending in the SMF Works publishing fleet. Part 5 is personal how-tos, the household and the errands. Part 6 is mastery, including the chapter where Grok Bot spans agent platforms, and a calm chapter on safety, privacy, and money. You can read straight through. You can also, after Part 2, pull a how-to from the shelf and come back.

A last kindness, before we stand up. If anything in this chapter felt like a lot of names — SpaceXSI, xAI, Cursor, The X Subscription — that is not a test. You do not have to hold the corporate history to create a Bot. You have to know which door you are walking through, which plan actually includes the product, and that this guide is a gift from me, not a message from the maker. The rest is here when you need it.

In Chapter 2, “A Teammate with a Computer,” we will walk the desk itself: the shared cloud computer, the path from a request to finished work, and the official walkthrough I want to retell in my own voice, about signing up for the same conference as last year. We will talk about what “finished” means, and about what still needs a yes. You can leave the mug. The lamp on the other desk is already on.


# Chapter 2 — A Teammate with a Computer: How Work Gets Finished

There is a morning I come back to, not because it was dramatic, but because it was ordinary in the way I want your first weeks to become ordinary. I had closed the laptop the evening before. Charlie, my chief of staff Bot, had a job I had already tested: a morning brief for SMF Works, drawn from the places I had asked him to look, drafted and waiting, nothing sent. When I opened the lid, the work was already on the desk. The coffee was still the first cup. I did not start from zero. I started from a draft I could approve, or send back, or sit with for a minute.

I am not going to give you a number of minutes saved, because I did not stand there with a stopwatch, and I will not invent one. I am going to give you the picture the official product itself is built around, told as that kind of morning. Grok Bot is a teammate with a real computer in the cloud. The work can keep going after you close the lid. “Finished,” in this book, means the job came back in the place a finished job belongs — a file, a draft, a brief, a report — and that anything which sends, posts, pays, deletes, or publishes still waited for your yes.

This chapter is a map, not a setup guide. We will not create a Bot yet; that is Chapter 3. We will not write your first handoff yet; that is Chapter 4. We will not connect apps or save a login yet; that is Chapter 5. Right now I want you to understand the desk: the shared cloud computer, the path from a request to finished work, and the official walkthrough I am going to retell in my own voice, about signing up for the same conference as last year. Once you can see that path, the first-week chapters will feel like walking a road you already recognize.

## A real computer in the cloud

Official documentation does not hedge this, and neither will I. “Your Bots work from a persistent cloud computer with a browser, command line, and filesystem.” Work continues while your laptop is closed. Files, sign-ins, and browser sessions stay between tasks.^1^

A few of those words are worth sitting with, because they are doing more work than they appear to.

*Persistent* means the computer is still there in the morning. It is not a fresh, empty machine for every request. The file a Bot saved yesterday is still there. The site a Bot signed into is often still signed in. That is a gift for work that takes more than one sitting. It is also the reason a file you would not want every Bot to see should not be left on that desk.

*A browser* means the Bot can use websites the way a person would, including services that have no tidy connection, no special plug-in, no official “app” in the catalog. When an app does exist — Gmail, a calendar, a drive — connecting it is usually more reliable than clicking through pages, and Chapter 5 will show you how. When it does not, the browser is the door.

*A command line* is the part of a computer where you type instructions instead of clicking. If that sounds like someone else’s job, you can leave it alone for a long time. You do not need it to hand off your first task. I mention it because it is real, and because, much later, in Chapter 16, it is one of the ways a Grok Bot in my own workshop talks to other agent platforms. Official docs say two more things about it that I want you to hear now, even if Chapter 16 is a long way off: command-line credentials are shared, and you should not place a credential or a file on that computer if another Bot on your account should not be able to use it.^2^

*A filesystem* means there is a place for files: drafts, spreadsheets, receipts, a folder of options for a trip you have not booked. Those files stay. They are visible to every Bot on your account. I will say that again in a moment, because it is the fact people most often miss.

The computer is in the cloud, which means it is not the laptop in front of you. Official FAQ: Bots work on their own cloud computer. A Bot runs a command on your desktop only if you allow it, under a setting that, by default, asks you each time.^3^ You can watch the cloud computer live, and you can take control of it when a step needs a person — a sign-in the Bot cannot finish, a passkey, a CAPTCHA. You can also close the preview, close the app, close the laptop, and the cloud work continues.^4^


![The cloud desk. A lamp-lit desk that is not yours, with the work already waiting.](book/art/I04.jpg){width=100%}

*I04. The cloud desk. A lamp-lit desk that is not yours, with the work already waiting.*


I think of it as a desk in another room, with the lamp on. You can walk in and watch. You can take the chair for a minute and finish a step only a person can finish. You can leave the room. The lamp stays on.

## One computer, shared — and not a wall

Here is the fact I would walk across the room to tell you, if we were in the same kitchen.

Every Bot on your account uses one persistent cloud computer. The computer is assigned per user, not per Bot. They share its files, its browser sessions, and its logins, so they can hand work off to each other. Official FAQ, in words I will not soften: “Do not use separate Bots as a security boundary.”^5^

The computer-and-apps documentation says the same thing in a slightly longer way, and I want you to have that wording too. Every Bot on your account uses the same computer. Browser cookies and signed-in sessions are shared. Files are visible to every Bot. Command-line credentials are shared. One Bot can continue from work another Bot saved. “Do not place a credential or file on it if another Bot on your account should not be able to use it.” Each Bot gets its own screen, so several Bots can work at once, but the screens are separate work surfaces, not separate security boundaries.^6^

> **Caution.** A second Bot is not a locked room. If one Bot is signed in to a site, your other Bots are signed in too. If a card or a password is available on that computer, it is available to all of them. This is not a scare line. It is the official rule, said plainly. We will use the Vault. We will decide what is allowed. And we will not pretend that giving a Bot a different name puts a wall between them.

This is also, in a kinder light, why a small fleet works. In Chapter 12, when we look at a publishing house run with Bots, the reason a writer Bot can leave a file and a programmer Bot can pick it up is the same reason you have to be careful: they share a desk. Sharing is the point. The wall you want is not between your Bots. The wall you want is at the edge of your account — and the yes you have not given away.

Between different people, official docs describe a stricter isolation: each user’s computer is dedicated. That is a different boundary, and it is not the one this chapter is about. If you share a Team Bot with colleagues, Chapter 15 will walk that path from the official Team Bot pages, not from a guess.

You can update, recover, or reset the computer. Official pages cover those steps. Packages you install by hand should be treated as replaceable; the computer can be rebuilt. I will not turn that into a how-to here. I want you to know the desk can be tidied, and that “tidy” is sometimes the right move after a sensitive job: sign out of a service a Bot no longer needs, and delete a sensitive file when the work is done.^7^

## The pieces on the path

Before I retell the official walkthrough, I want to lay the pieces on the table and give each one a plain sentence. You will meet all of them again, with how-tos, in the first-week chapters. Here they are as a map.

**Your message.** You talk to a Bot the way you would talk to a coworker. You say what you want, which sources to use, any limits, and when to check with you. Chapter 4 will teach you to write that handoff: a goal, sources, a “never,” and what “done” looks like.

**Memory.** Each Bot keeps preferences, rules you have asked it to follow, and a dated log of its work. A few facts about you, like your name and time zone, are shared by all your Bots. A Bot decides what to remember; you can ask it to remember, to fix, or to forget. Memories can go out of date, so for numbers that change — prices, budgets — you ask the Bot to look them up again. Logins and cards do not belong in memory. They belong in the Vault.^8^ Chapter 6 is the full chapter on this. For this walkthrough, memory is how the Bot fills in last year’s pass and the card to use, without you repeating yourself.

**A connected app.** When you connect Gmail, or Drive, or a calendar, the Bot can work in that app directly, which official docs call more reliable than clicking through a website. Connected apps belong to the account you used to sign in, and they are available to every Bot you run. Chapter 5 will show you how to connect one low-risk app, and will remind you that connecting is a decision, not a default.

**The cloud computer.** When a site has no app, the Bot opens it in the browser on the shared computer. You can watch. You can take control. The session usually stays signed in.

**The Vault.** A locker your Bots can use without seeing the secret. You save a login, or a card, or a shipping address, and the value is filled into the page; the Bot never sees the password, the card number, or the security code. Official rule, repeated, and I will repeat it too: never paste a password or a card into the chat.^9^ Chapter 5 is where we will do this carefully, together.

**A Secure Form.** If the Vault does not have the login, and a connected password manager such as 1Password does not either, the Bot asks you with a Secure Form. You type the secret; it goes straight into the page; it is never shown to the Bot, and it never enters the ordinary chat.

**Take control.** Some steps need you: single sign-on, a passkey, a CAPTCHA the Bot cannot solve, a QR code, a bank verification. The Bot hands you the computer. You open it, take control, finish only that step, and tell the Bot to continue. You still do not paste the password into the chat.

**Auto Review.** Before a Bot runs a command, uses an app, or acts on its computer, Auto Review checks the action. It is on by default. Most actions go ahead. When one needs you, the Bot shows an approval card and waits. You can add your own rules, in plain words: “send an email,” “publish,” “delete,” and choose Ask first or Allow automatically. An Ask-first rule always stops a matching action.^10^

**The approval card.** A small postcard from the desk in the other room. It tells you what the Bot wants to do, and it waits. Depending on the card, you may see Allow once, Always allow, or Deny; some cards say Allow and Not now, or Allow and Skip. I will not pretend every card uses the same labels, because official docs say the labels vary.^11^ What does not vary, in this book, is the practice: read the card. If you cannot tell what it will do, do not approve it. Ask the Bot to explain it in plain language, or to produce a draft first.

**A yes that can expire.** Approvals from work that started without you — a routine, or a message from another Bot — expire after about thirty minutes, according to the official FAQ. Approvals in a chat you are in wait for you.^12^ That is why a 6 a.m. routine that needs a yes will not finish itself while you sleep. The work can run unattended. The yes, on a sensitive action, often cannot. Chapter 8 will return to this when we put a job on a schedule. Chapter 17 will return to it when we talk about safety.

Only your own messages count as instructions. If a web page has hidden text telling the Bot to email your contacts, Auto Review is not supposed to treat that as a request from you. Official docs say these checks “lower the risk from harmful content, but they don’t remove it.” Keep Ask-first rules on anything you cannot undo.^13^ I will not sand that off.

## One request, from your message to finished work

Official documentation explains how Grok Bot works by following one common request from start to finish. I am going to retell that walkthrough in my own voice, because it is the best map I know, and because I would rather you hear it as a story than as a specification. The request, in the official pages, is this:

> Sign me up for the same conference as last year and expense it.^14^

It is a good request for a map, and a slightly dangerous request for a first task. It includes a purchase and an email. In Chapter 4, I will ask you to start with something reversible — a summary, a draft, a file, not a send, and not a payment. Here, we are watching how the product thinks, including the places it stops.

Imagine you have a Bot whose job is travel and expenses. You send it that sentence. The sentence does not name the conference, the pass, or the card. Here is what official docs say happens next, and how I would describe it to you at the table.

**First, the Bot checks its memory.** The expenses Bot fills in the details you did not type. Its work log, in the official example, has last year’s two-day pass. You have given it a rule: use the work card for conferences. If you ask what it used, it can show you. That is memory doing its job — the job of not making you repeat yourself — and also the reason memory should not be where a card number lives. The official example puts the card in the Vault, and so will we.

**Second, the Bot finds last year’s receipt in Gmail.** Gmail, in the walkthrough, is a connected app, so the Bot works in it directly instead of opening the Gmail website. Official docs call that more reliable. If an app is not in the catalog, there are other official paths — an MCP server, which is a standard way these tools connect to a service, or a marketplace Bot that can wrap an API — and those are a power-user corner we will leave for Chapter 18. For this request, Gmail is enough.

**Third, the Bot signs in and buys the pass.** The conference site has no app, so the Bot opens it in the browser on the shared cloud computer. All your Bots share that computer; when one signs in, the others are signed in too. The site asks for a password. The Bot looks for a login in a fixed official order: first the Vault, then 1Password if you have connected it and approve, then a Secure Form that asks you and fills the page directly. In the official walkthrough, neither the Vault nor 1Password has a login, so the Bot sends a Secure Form. Whichever path it uses, the login goes straight into the page, and the Bot never sees it.

To pay, the Bot uses the work card you saved in the Vault. This is the sentence I need you to hear clearly, because official docs say it clearly: a saved card fills in at checkout without asking you each time, and the Bot never sees the number. If you would rather approve every payment, do not save the card.^15^ In the walkthrough, the card is saved, and the pass is bought. In your first week, I will ask you not to start here.

If a step needs a person — single sign-on, a passkey, a CAPTCHA — the Bot hands you the computer. You choose Open computer on the request, then Take control, finish the step, and tell the Bot to continue.

**Fourth, the Bot asks before it emails finance.** With the pass bought, the Bot is ready to email the receipt. In the walkthrough, you have made a rule to approve emails first, so it stops and asks. Auto Review has checked the action. The Ask-first rule has stopped the email. An approval card is waiting. You can allow it once, or deny it, or, on some cards, always allow similar actions — which saves a rule, and which I would like you to use sparingly in the first week. The email is not sent until you say so.

**Fifth, the Bot logs the expense and reports back.** It adds the expense to a Finance sheet in Google Drive, another connected app, and it replies with everything it did. If you have closed your laptop by then, it keeps going, and it notifies you when it is done.

That is the whole journey. Memory, a connected app, the cloud computer, the Vault or a Secure Form, Auto Review, an approval card, a finished log, a report that can find you after the lid is closed. The official pages also say something I want at the end of the story: your expenses Bot did all of this as you. A Bot has no account of its own, so it cannot do anything you could not do, and every action traces back to you. The one exception is a connector your team manages, which can use team or service-account credentials.^16^ You are still the person whose name is on the work.


![A request’s journey, retold from the official “same conference as last year” walkthrough. This is a map of how the product finishes work, including the stop for your yes. It is not a first-task recipe; in Chapter 4 we will start with something that does not pay and does not send.](book/art/diagrams/I03.png){width=100%}

*I03. A request’s journey, retold from the official “same conference as last year” walkthrough. This is a map of how the product finishes work, including the stop for your yes. It is not a first-task recipe; in Chapter 4 we will start with something that does not pay and does not send.*


## A narrated walk along the same path

**How to watch a request travel, before you run one yourself**

This is not a setup guide. It is a way to look at the map with me, so that when you write your first handoff in Chapter 4, you already know the rooms the work will walk through.

1. Start with the message, and notice what it does not say.
   In the official walkthrough, the message does not name the conference, the pass, or the card. What is missing will be filled from memory, or looked up, or asked. If a first task of yours leaves a purchase or a send implicit, that is a sign to make the “never” explicit: “Don’t book.” “Don’t send.” “Don’t pay.”
   If you don’t yet have a Bot, just keep the habit in your head. You will use it on your first real task.

2. Ask where the facts will come from.
   Memory, for what you have already told the Bot. A connected app, for a receipt or a calendar. The browser, for a site with no app. The Vault, for a login or a card. A Secure Form or your own hands, for a secret that is not saved. You should be able to point to a source for each fact the Bot will need. If you cannot, the Bot may guess, or remember something stale, or stop and ask. Asking is fine. Guessing, for a number that changes, is not.

3. Find the step that should wait for you.
   In the walkthrough, it is the email to finance, stopped by an Ask-first rule. In your work, it may be a send, a post, a payment, a deletion, or a publish. Name it before the work begins. You should see, later, an approval card at that step, not a finished action you did not mean.
   If you don’t: Chapter 5 is where we will set those rules on purpose, and test a card on purpose, before a Bot works alone.

4. Notice what “done” will look like, and what still needs a yes.
   In the walkthrough, “done” is a bought pass, a logged expense, and a report. The email still needed a yes. A saved card did not. That last fact is official, and it is why, in this book, a first-week payment does not start with a card already in the Vault. You should leave this map knowing the difference between a job that finished and a job that still needs you.

What “done” looks like, for this walk: you can retell the five steps in your own words, and you can point to the yes. What still needs a yes, when you do this for real, is anything you cannot undo.

## Watching, taking control, and working in parallel

You do not have to stare at the cloud desk for the whole job. Official docs say you click the computer preview in the conversation to open it and watch clicks, typing, navigation, and status, and you can leave the preview while the work continues.^21^ I watch the first time I hand off a new kind of job, the way I would watch a new person on a first morning: not because I distrust them, but because I want to see how the work actually moves, and where it pauses. After that, I leave the room more often.

Taking control is not a failure. It is a designed step. Single sign-on, a passkey, a CAPTCHA, a QR code, a bank verification, a site that requires a human — the Bot is supposed to hand those to you, not bypass them.^22^ You finish only the blocked step, you tell the Bot to continue, and you still do not paste a secret into the chat. If a site blocks traffic from cloud computers, official docs describe another option: you can route the Bot’s web traffic through your own computer, so the site sees your connection, and you can sign in yourself on the Bot’s computer.^23^ That is a later, careful setting, not a first-week move. I mention it so that a blocked site does not feel like a dead end, and so that you do not start inventing workarounds the product already has a door for.

Several Bots can work at the same time. Each one gets its own screen on the shared computer. One Bot can run one computer-use task on its screen at a time, but they can reason, use connectors, work with files, and coordinate in parallel.^24^ This is the seed of a fleet, and it is also, again, the shared desk: parallel work is possible because the computer is shared, and the sharing is the thing to be thoughtful about. In my own workshop, Charlie can be on the morning brief while a writer Bot is on a draft. They are not two locked rooms. They are two screens, one desk, one person at the door.

## You can close the laptop

I want to come back to that morning at SMF Works, because the official picture and my ordinary picture are the same picture, and I do not want the official words to sound like a slogan.

The FAQ asks, “Does Grok Bot keep working when my laptop is closed?” and answers yes. Work runs on the cloud computer. Closing the app, the laptop, or the phone does not stop a background turn or a routine.^17^ The how-it-works walkthrough ends the same way: if you have closed your laptop by the time the Bot is logging the expense, it keeps going and notifies you when it is done.^18^

That is the lamp in the other room. It is also not a blanket permission. Unattended approvals expire after about thirty minutes. A routine that needs your yes at 6 a.m. will not get that yes from a closed door. A card in a chat you are sitting in will wait. The art, which Chapters 8 and 9 will practice, is to design the job so that what can safely finish, finishes, and what must not finish without you, waits in a form you can read with a first cup of coffee.

> **Story.** I closed the lid on a draft I had handed to a writer Bot at SMF Works — a bounded job, sources included, a clear “never” about publishing — and I went to make dinner. In the morning the draft was there, in the folder where we leave finished drafts. It was not ready to publish. It was ready for me. That is the kind of finished I trust: the work came back, and the last part was still mine.

I will not tell you the draft was a certain percent right. Chapter 4 will talk about a first finished draft that is mostly right, and about the last part that is still yours, without borrowing anyone’s slogan. For now, I want the feeling: the lid can close, the lamp can stay on, and “finished” does not mean “gone out the door.”

## What “finished” means, and what still needs a yes

Let me say this in the plainest words I have, because it is the contract this whole book keeps.

A job is finished, in the sense I will teach, when the work has landed where a human would have put it — in the file, in the draft, in the brief, in the folder — and you have been told what was done. A job is not finished, in the sense that it has left your hands, until you have said yes to anything that sends, posts, pays, deletes, or publishes. I am teaching those five verbs as a first-week rule. Official docs describe Ask-first rules for sending, publishing, and deleting, and they tell you not to save a card if you want to approve every payment.^19^ I am lining those up, as practice, so you have a short list you can remember when you are tired.

“Finished” is also honest about failure. Sites block automation. CAPTCHAs appear. Logins expire. A computer cannot be reached, or a usage limit is reached, or an approval expires. Official troubleshooting exists for those, and Chapter 17 will walk one path from it. In your first week, a failure is not a verdict on you or on the product. It is a moment to read what came back, to try again with a tighter “never,” or to take control for the one step that needed a person.

Your primary Bot, official docs say, is your main point of contact. It checks in, it can coordinate your other Bots, and you could send the conference request to the primary Bot and let it pass the work to the expenses Bot.^20^ Most Bots work best with one clear job. When the same kind of request keeps coming back with its own sources, rules, or schedule, it is time for a Bot of its own. That is the seed of Part 3, and of the fleet in Chapter 12. You do not have to plant it today.

You do have to leave this chapter with a picture. There is a desk in the other room. It has a browser, a place for files, and a command line you may not touch for a long time. Every Bot on your account shares that desk. Secrets go in the Vault, not in the chat. Auto Review watches; Ask-first rules stop the things you cannot undo; approval cards wait, and some of them wait only about thirty minutes. You can close the laptop. You are still the one who says yes.

> **Tip.** If you take only one picture from this chapter into Chapter 3, take this one: the Bot has a desk, the desk is shared, and the yes is still yours. Everything else — plans, buttons, the first task — hangs on that picture.

One thing I have learned, watching work come back to the SMF Works desk, is that “finished” is a feeling you can practice. The first few times, I hovered. I opened the computer preview and watched every click, the way you watch a pot that does not actually need watching. Then I learned to write a tighter handoff, to put the “never” in the message, and to go make dinner. The work that came back was not always the work I would have done myself, line for line. It was close enough to edit, and it had not gone out the door. That is the standard I want for you in the first week. Not perfection. A finished draft, a clear report, and a yes you still own.

In Chapter 3, “Get In,” we will pick a plan that actually includes Grok Bot, install the app, and create your first Bot with one clear job — without walking into a billing surprise. The map is in your hands. Let’s get you through the door.


# Part 2 — Day One Through the First Week
# Chapter 3 — Get In: Plans, Download, and Your First Bot

The first time I sat with the pricing page, Plus looked like the obvious door. It is eight dollars a month. It says “Grok” on it, in the way a page lists the things you already think you came for. I almost would have pointed a friend at it, with a kind word and a wrongly confident nod, if I had not looked one column further.

Grok Bot is not on Plus. It is not on Free. On The X Subscription, where new customers start, Grok Bot comes with Premium, Super, and Ultra. Plus, at the list price I checked on 11 November 2026, is eight dollars a month, and it does not include the teammate this book is about.^1^

That is not a scold. It is a kindness I wanted at the top of this chapter, because walking into a billing surprise is a miserable way to meet a tool. This chapter will get you through the right door: a plan that actually includes Grok Bot, the app on a computer or a phone, a first Bot with one clear job, and a look at the usage dashboard so you can see the live number. We will go slowly. We will date every price. And when this book and the live page disagree, we will trust the live page.

## Which door gets you in

The product’s own pricing, on 11 November 2026, at x.ai/pricing, listed these individual plans, with tax extra:^2^

- **Free**, $0 a month. Limited usage. Does not include Grok Bot.
- **Plus**, $8 a month. A shared AI usage pool, Grok Chat and related Grok features, and Cursor. Does not include Grok Bot.
- **Premium**, $30 a month. Four times the usage of Plus. **Includes Grok Bot.** This is the first door that gets you in, on The X Subscription.
- **Super**, $100 a month. Fifteen times the usage of Plus. Includes Grok Bot, with a larger weekly pool.
- **Ultra**, $200 a month. Fifty times the usage of Plus. Includes Grok Bot, with the largest of those weekly pools.
- **Teams**, from $30 or more per seat, with seat tiers that follow the paid individual tiers. Grok Bot is included on those paid seats; for the exact team wording, look at the live page, not at a sentence in a book.

I am not going to tell you which of Premium, Super, or Ultra to pick. That would be a pitch, and this book does not pitch plans. Official pages describe Premium as a starting point for new AI users, Super for daily agent use, and Ultra for power users; those are the maker’s recommendations, dated the day I checked, and they will change.^3^ Your work, your budget, and the live dashboard are the better guides. Chapter 17, “Safety, Privacy, Money, and What to Do When It Breaks,” will sit with bills, caps, and top-ups without asking you to climb a ladder.


![Which door gets you in, with list prices dated 11 November 2026, tax extra. Trust the live page at x.ai/pricing if this diagram and the page disagree.](book/art/diagrams/I05.png){width=100%}

*I05. Which door gets you in, with list prices dated 11 November 2026, tax extra. Trust the live page at x.ai/pricing if this diagram and the page disagree.*


> **Tip.** The usage dashboard is the live number. If this book and the dashboard disagree, trust the dashboard — plans change.

## A shared weekly pool, and a sentence that is no longer the live rule

Under The X Subscription, Grok Bot draws from a weekly shared pool across Grok, Grok Bot, and Cursor.^4^ That is the live rule for a new customer on 11 November 2026. There is not a published single number, in dollars or in tokens, for how big that pool is. Official pages give multipliers — four times, fifteen times, fifty times Plus — and tell you to look at the live dashboard.^5^ I will not invent a “typical hours left” figure. I will tell you where to look, in a how-to, in a few pages.

This is a change from launch day. On 11 August 2026, the announcement said Grok Bot “comes with its own usage, separate from your Grok and Cursor plans.”^6^ If you find a guide, including an old paragraph of mine from a conversation, that only quotes that August sentence, it is telling the truth about August and not the truth about a November reader on The X Subscription. I am dating both rules so you can see the turn.

After the weekly pool, additional usage can be paid from prepaid top-up credits. Auto Top-up is optional, and it is off until you turn it on. A monthly cap is a separate control. Chapter 17 will walk those. For today: leave Auto Top-up off unless you mean it on, and know that the pool is shared, so a heavy day in the Grok chat app or in Cursor is a day that also touches Grok Bot’s allowance.

There is no model picker. Official FAQ: Grok Bot picks the model for each task; it may be a first-party SpaceXSI model or a third-party model; the model is not displayed; you cannot route to or away from a particular one.^7^ You will not see a menu of model names, and this book will not tell you which model ran which task, because that is not a fact a user is shown. Usage is recorded as Grok Bot usage, not by model name.

## Switching is optional, and it is permanent

If you already have a Cursor plan, or a SuperGrok plan, or a paid X plan, you do not have to switch to The X Subscription. Official docs are clear: switching is optional. Your current plan, price, and features continue unless you change or cancel. New customers start on The X Subscription.^8^

If you do switch — if you unify accounts under that one subscription — the switch is permanent. It cannot be undone, and Support cannot undo it for you.^9^ That is the sentence I want you to read twice, before you click anything that says Unify.

> **Caution.** Unifying accounts under The X Subscription is optional, and a completed unification is permanent. Official docs say Support cannot undo it. If you are unsure, do not switch today. Your current plan can continue. Read the live official page on unification before you combine anything, and do not treat this paragraph as a complete legal or billing guide. It is a friend at the table, pointing at a door that only opens one way.

When people do switch, official docs describe a new bill, a prorated refund on some cancelled subscriptions, and a special case if you pay for Cursor through the Apple App Store: the company cannot cancel that one for you, so you cancel it with Apple, or Apple keeps billing you.^10^ I am not going to walk a unification checkout in this chapter. If that is your situation, read the official unification pages, slowly, and take a screenshot of what you have before you change it. Chapter 17 will come back to billing pitfalls.

> **A careful box: legacy Cursor and SuperGrok paths.** These still appear on official pages, so I will describe them as the docs describe them, dated 11 November 2026. On a legacy Cursor plan, Grok Bot is included with every paid individual plan and with self-serve Cursor Teams. You can also link an individual SuperGrok, SuperGrok Plus, SuperGrok Heavy, or X Premium+ subscription. SuperGrok Lite and Grok Enterprise cannot link. A SuperGrok link is permanent; you cannot move it yourself. A Cursor plan and SuperGrok do not add together — you get the higher tier, and both may keep billing on their own. On that path, Grok Bot usage is metered on the Cursor account. If you later unify onto The X Subscription, your Grok Bot usage then comes from the new weekly shared pool.^11^ If none of those sentences sound like your account, you are probably a new customer, and The X Subscription is your door. If they do sound like your account, look at Settings → Usage & Billing, and at the official “Grok Bot on The X Subscription” page, before you change anything.

## Desktop and phone

The desktop app runs on macOS (Apple silicon and Intel), Windows (x64 and Arm64), and Linux (x64 and Arm64). It checks for updates automatically; Linux .deb and .rpm installs update through the package manager instead.^12^ Official get-started says to download Grok Bot from x.ai/bot and open it. On a team, the Cursor dashboard has a Download Grok Bot row; if you do not have dashboard access, you ask your admin.

The companion apps are on the App Store and Google Play. The iPhone app needs iOS 18 or later; the Android app needs Android 9 or later. Both reach the same Bots, conversations, routines, and cloud computer as the desktop. Access from your plan carries over without a second purchase. From your phone you can message Bots, watch and take over the computer, approve actions, and pause or resume routines.^13^

Subscriptions can also be purchased through Apple or Google, and that path has a different cancellation route than The X Subscription. If you buy through a store, you cancel through that store.^14^ I am not going to list in-app prices here; they change, and the store listing is the live number. The App Store seller, when I opened the listing on 11 November 2026, was X Corp., with a copyright line for Anysphere Inc. Google Play listed the developer as SpaceX Super Intelligence.^15^ Those are the names as the listings used them, the same careful formula as Chapter 1.


![Install-day. A laptop, a phone, a mug, a cable, morning light.](book/art/I06.jpg){width=100%}

*I06. Install-day. A laptop, a phone, a mug, a cable, morning light.*


Grok Bot needs cloud data storage. If your account is on Legacy Privacy Mode, the app asks you to switch; Legacy Privacy Mode is not supported.^16^ That is an official requirement, not a preference in this book. Training opt-out, and the rest of privacy, follow Cursor account settings; Chapter 17 will return to that. For today, know that there is no separate Grok Bot login. You sign in with your Cursor account. If your company uses single sign-on, you sign in the usual way. If your access comes from a SuperGrok or X Premium+ subscription on a legacy path, official get-started describes a “Link Grok Account” step.^17^

If sign-in does not finish, official troubleshooting has a section for that. Quit and reopen is the first kindness; the troubleshooting page is the second.^18^

## How to read the live pricing page

**How to read the live pricing page, not a screenshot in this book**

1. Open the official pricing page at [https://x.ai/pricing](https://x.ai/pricing).
   You should see the current plans and prices, including which ones list Grok Bot.
   If you don’t: make sure you are on the official x.ai site, not a third-party copy or an old screenshot, including the ones in this chapter’s memory.

2. Find the plan you are considering, and look specifically for Grok Bot.
   You should see it listed on Premium, Super, and Ultra, and not on Free or Plus, unless the live page has changed since 11 November 2026.
   If you don’t: do not assume a lower plan includes it. If the page has changed, trust the page, and date what you saw.

3. Notice whether you are looking at a monthly price, an annual price, and whether tax is extra.
   You should see a clear amount, and you should expect tax to be added at checkout. The list prices in this chapter are monthly, dated 11 November 2026, tax extra.
   If you don’t: look for a toggle or a footnote on the page. Do not use a number from a blog, including a number someone said was “about” right.

4. If you already have a Cursor, SuperGrok, or X plan, look for the official language about switching and unification before you combine anything.
   You should see that switching is optional, and you should look for the word *permanent* before you proceed.
   If you don’t: leave unification alone for today. A current plan can continue.

5. Do not click a checkout button until you have read what you are buying, including Auto Top-up if it appears, and until you are sure Grok Bot is on that plan.
   You should feel bored, in the good way, before you pay. Boredom at a checkout is a sign you have actually read it.
   If you don’t: walk away and come back. The page will still be there.

What “done” looks like: you can name the plan you intend to use, you know it includes Grok Bot, and you have confirmed that on the live page, not only in this book. What still needs a yes: the payment itself, which is yours, not mine, and not the Bot’s.

## How to download and sign in

**How to download the app and sign in**

1. On a computer, go to [https://x.ai/bot](https://x.ai/bot) and download Grok Bot for your system.
   You should get the desktop app for macOS, Windows, or Linux, as official get-started describes.
   If you don’t: on a team, ask your admin for the dashboard download link. If a download fails, try again from the official page, not from a forwarded file.

2. Open the app, choose Sign in on the welcome screen, and finish in your browser.
   You should sign in with your Cursor account. There is no separate Grok Bot login.
   If you don’t: see official troubleshooting for “Sign-in does not complete.” If your company uses single sign-on, use that usual path. If you are on a legacy SuperGrok or X Premium+ path, look for Link Grok Account as official get-started describes, and do not invent a different sequence.

3. If the app asks you to switch off Legacy Privacy Mode, read that request and understand that Grok Bot requires cloud data storage.
   You should see a clear request, not a hidden toggle.
   If you don’t want to switch: you will not be able to use Grok Bot on that mode, according to official docs. That is a real choice, and it is yours. Do not treat this book as pushing you through it.

4. On a phone, install Grok Bot from the App Store or Google Play, and sign in with the same account.
   You should see the same Bots, once you have them, without a second purchase for access you already have.
   If you don’t: confirm you used the same account. Confirm the phone meets iOS 18 or Android 9. Quit and reopen. Then see official mobile docs or troubleshooting.

5. Do not enter a password into a chat, and do not buy a second time if you already have access.
   You should be inside the app, looking at a first Bot, or at a welcome that is about to create one.
   If you see a paywall you do not expect: stop and check Settings, or the usage and billing area, and the live plan page, before you pay again. Billing confusion is a documented pain, and Chapter 17 will treat it with patience. Today, stopping is allowed.

What “done” looks like: the app is open, you are signed in, and you have not paid twice. What still needs a yes: any purchase, and any unification.

## Your first Bot, with one clear job

After a short intro, the app sets up your first Bot, named Grok Bot, and opens a chat with it.^19^ That is a perfectly good place to begin. You can also start from a Bot already set up for a job, in the official marketplace — Tradbot for personal email and calendar, for example, which official copy says never sends without you — and you can wait on the marketplace until you have a feel for a Bot you named yourself. I started with a Bot and a job I could describe in one sentence. I still think that is the right first move.

You can create more. Official docs: choose New Bot (the + button) at the top of the sidebar, or press Cmd+N on a Mac or Ctrl+N on Windows or Linux. Name the Bot and choose Create. To rename later, click its name at the top of the chat, then click the name again to edit it.^20^

Give each Bot one job, in its first message, and ask it to remember how you want the work done. Official guidance is the same as mine: a Bot with one job builds more useful context than a catch-all helper.^21^ “Be my assistant for everything” is a kind sentence and a vague job. “Draft a morning brief from my calendar and inbox, and never send anything” is a job.

The name can be ordinary. Charlie is my chief of staff Bot, and that name will come back in Chapter 9. Your first Bot does not need a fleet name. It needs one job, a “never,” and a person — you — who will read what comes back.

**How to create your first Bot with one clear job**

1. If the app has already created a first Bot named Grok Bot, open the chat with it. If you want a second Bot, or a Bot with a name you chose, choose New Bot and give it a name, then Create.
   You should see a chat, empty or nearly empty, with a Bot waiting for a first message.
   If you don’t: confirm you are signed in and that your plan includes Grok Bot. If New Bot is missing, you may be on a plan that does not include it; go back to the live pricing page rather than pressing harder.

2. Write a first message that states one job, what “done” looks like, and a “never.”
