   A solid first job is small and reversible: a brief, a summary, a draft, a list. For example, in your own words: “Your job is to draft a short morning brief of what needs me. Don’t send anything. Don’t delete anything. Ask me before you connect a new app.”
   You should get a reply that shows the Bot has taken the job, or a question that narrows it.
   If you don’t: say the job again, more narrowly. One job, one “never.” Chapter 4 will go further on the handoff itself.

3. Ask the Bot to remember the job and the “never.”
   You should see, in its reply, some sign that it will keep those rules. You can also, later, ask “What do you remember about me?” as official docs suggest.
   If you don’t: say it again, plainly: “Please remember this job and these rules.” Chapter 6 will show you how to look at memory and how to forget.

4. Do not, in this first message, paste a password, a card number, or a secret, and do not ask the Bot to send, pay, or publish.
   You should still be in a conversation, not in a checkout.
   If the Bot offers to connect an app or to take a large next step: you can say “Not yet. We’ll do that on purpose in a later step.” Chapter 5 is that later step.

What “done” looks like: you have a Bot with a name, one job, and a “never,” and nothing has been sent. What still needs a yes: any connection, any send, and the first real task, which is Chapter 4.

## How to find the usage dashboard

**How to find the usage dashboard and look at the live number**

1. If you are on The X Subscription, open [https://grok.com/dashboard](https://grok.com/dashboard) and look at Usage.^22^
   You should see a live view of the weekly shared pool, or a clear path to it.
   If you don’t: try Settings → Usage & Billing inside the Grok Bot app, which official FAQ also names. The label may vary slightly; look for usage and billing, not for a model name.

2. If you are still on a legacy Cursor path, and you have not switched, open the Cursor dashboard at [https://cursor.com/dashboard](https://cursor.com/dashboard), and also look in Grok Bot under Settings → Usage & Billing.^23^
   You should see Grok Bot usage metered on the Cursor account, which official docs say is a different meter from a SuperGrok limit in the Grok chat app.
   If you don’t: do not assume the two meters match. Official FAQ: they are two separate meters for two products.

3. Read what you see without hunting for a “hours left” number this book never gave you.
   You should leave with a sense of whether you are early in the week’s pool, near the edge, or already into additional usage.
   If you don’t understand a line: do not guess. Note it, and come back to Chapter 17, or to official usage-limits docs, with the live page in front of you.

4. Confirm Auto Top-up is off, unless you have already decided to turn it on.
   You should see that it is off by default, as official docs describe, or that you have set a cap.
   If you don’t: leave it off for the first week. You can always turn a thing on later. You cannot always unspend a credit.

What “done” looks like: you know where the live number lives, and you have looked at it once. What still needs a yes: any top-up, any cap change, any unification.

## One job, said a few ways

I want to sit with “one job” a little longer, because it is the habit that makes everything after this chapter easier, and because a first Bot is where people most often try to do everything at once.

A job is one lane. “Draft a morning brief, and never send it” is a lane. “Sort unread mail into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI, draft the urgent replies, and don’t send” is a lane — official docs offer a starter prompt very close to that, which we will use in Chapter 4.^24^ “Find two flight and hotel options for a weekend, and don’t book” is a lane. “Be my chief of staff, my writer, my bookkeeper, and the person who talks to the school” is not a lane. It is a kind hope, and it makes a muddy teammate.

If you already know you will want several lanes, you still start with one. Official docs describe a primary Bot as your main point of contact, the one that can coordinate other Bots, and they suggest giving work its own Bot when it has its own sources, schedule, or approval needs.^25^ Charlie is that kind of primary Bot for me, and he did not start as a fleet. He started as a morning brief. The fleet in Chapter 12 grew from jobs that kept coming back.

You can name the job in ordinary language. You do not need the word “agent.” You do not need a formal specification. You need a sentence you would be willing to say to a new person on a first morning, including the thing they should never do.

The official marketplace has Bots already set up for a job — Overheard, which official copy says never posts on your behalf; Tradbot, which never sends without you; a Meeting Recap Deck that never invents a quote.^26^ Those can be a later kindness. They are not a requirement for Day One. A Bot you named, with a job you wrote, will teach you more in the first week, because you will have practiced the sentence.

If you work on a team, and someone else already has Grok Bot, you may be looking at a shared Bot rather than creating your first one from scratch. Team Bots have their own rules, and their own plan gates — Team and Enterprise, as of 1.0 — and Chapter 15 is where we will walk them from the official pages. For this chapter, create a Bot that is yours, with a job that is yours, on a plan that includes the product. Sharing comes after you have practiced the yes.

## If the first hour feels like a lot

It is all right if this chapter felt like more doors than you wanted. Pricing pages are like that. Company names are like that. I have watched careful people freeze at a checkout, not because they could not afford a plan, but because they did not want to make a permanent mistake. That instinct is a good one. Honor it.

You can read the live pricing page today and download tomorrow. You can install the app and not create a second Bot. You can create a Bot and not hand it a task until you have read Chapter 4. You can leave Auto Top-up off for a month. None of those pauses is a failure. The product will still be there. The door that matters — Premium or higher on The X Subscription, or a legacy paid path that actually includes Grok Bot — will still be the door.

What I would not like you to do is pay for Plus, wait for a Bot that will not appear, and decide that you are the one who did not understand. You understood fine. The column was easy to misread. I almost misread it too, in the sense that I almost would have sent a friend through it. We looked one column further. That is the whole story.

## A first-week posture, before the first task

You are in. That is more than it seems. You have a plan that actually includes the product, an app on a machine you use, a Bot with one job, and a dashboard you can find again.

A few postures I would like you to keep for the rest of the week, and they are not additional how-tos, only habits.

This is also a good moment to decide, quietly, what this first Bot is *not*. It is not your payment method. It is not your lawyer. It is not the person who emails a school or a customer until you have practiced a smaller yes. It is a teammate with one job, on a computer you now know is shared, on a plan you chose with your eyes open. That is a lot for Day One. It is enough.

If a family member or a colleague is sitting with you while you do this, you can hand them the “which door” picture and the word *permanent*, and then you can let them go make coffee. The rest is just installing an app and writing one clear sentence. I have written worse first sentences than the ones you will write today. You can always say, “Let’s tighten the job,” and the Bot will meet you there.

One job per Bot. When a second job shows up and stays, that is a second Bot, or a conversation with your primary Bot about creating one. You do not need a fleet today.

No model picker, and no hunting for one. The work is the work. Usage is Grok Bot usage.

The computer is already there, shared, even if you have only one Bot. The first login you save, in Chapter 5, will be a login every later Bot can use. That is not a reason to delay. It is a reason to be deliberate.

Prices will change. I checked them on 11 November 2026, and I dated them, and I will still be wrong for a reader who opens this book in a later season. That is not a failure of the chapter. It is the reason the first how-to in this book is “read the live page.” Features will change too. Team Bots, meetings, and Pages already arrived on a timetable of weeks, not years. Your dashboard, your plan page, and the official docs will always outrank a paragraph of mine. I am at peace with that. I would like you to be, too.

The Free plan and the Plus plan are fine plans for other things. They are not a failure, and they are not a door to Grok Bot. If a friend asks you, you can tell them kindly, the way I almost had to be told.

I have one more practical note, and then I will get out of your way. People sometimes ask whether they should start on a phone or on a computer. Official docs treat the desktop as the place you install first, and the phone as a companion that reaches the same Bots.^27^ I agree with that order, not as a rule, but as a kindness: the first sign-in, the first look at a cloud computer, and the first approval card are a little easier to see on a larger screen. After that, I use the phone the way I use a colleague’s text thread — to check, to approve, to leave a voice note — and I use the desktop when I want to watch the desk in the other room. Either way, it is the same teammate, the same computer, the same weekly pool.

If you get stuck, official troubleshooting is the next place, not a random forum thread, and not a guess in this book about a button I cannot see on your screen. Sign-in that does not complete, a computer still setting up, a usage limit already reached — those have official sections.^28^ We will use one of those paths properly in Chapter 17. Today, if the app is open and a Bot is waiting, you have enough.

In Chapter 4, “Hand Off Your First Real Task,” we will pick something useful and reversible, write a handoff with a goal and a “never,” watch or leave, and practice saying yes, or not now, or try that again. You have a Bot. Let’s give it a real piece of work.


# Chapter 4 — Hand Off Your First Real Task

The first finished draft I handed back to myself, at the SMF Works desk, was mostly right. I say that in the ordinary, unmeasured way, not as a score. The shape was there. The sources had been looked at. A few sentences were already in a voice I could live with. The last part — the judgment, the cut, the sentence I would actually sign — was still mine. I did not need the draft to be perfect. I needed it to have come back, and I needed it not to have gone out the door.

That is the first task I want for you. Useful, bounded, reversible. A summary, a draft, a file. Not a send. Not a payment. Not a publish. You will watch the Bot work, or you will leave and come back, and you will practice the small, important move this whole book is about: saying yes, or not now, or try that again.

Official get-started says it the way I would like to say it: ask the way you would ask a coworker. Say what you want back, which sources to use, any limits, and when to check with you. When an action needs your OK, the Bot shows an approval card and waits.^1^ We are going to make that advice concrete: a short list of first tasks, a handoff you can actually write, a way to watch or to leave, and a way to answer a card without rushing.

You already have a Bot, from Chapter 3, with one job and a “never.” Today we give that job a real piece of work.

I will also tell you what this chapter is not. It is not the conference-and-expense walkthrough from Chapter 2, run as a live checkout. It is not a test of how much you can automate before lunch. It is a practice session with a safety rail: one job, written down, watched or left on purpose, reviewed, and kept from going out the door. If you only do one how-to in this whole book this weekend, do this one. The later chapters — skills, routines, a chief of staff, a publishing fleet — are all this chapter, grown up, with more structure around the same yes.

If you are sharing a screen with someone, a child walking through, a colleague asking what you are doing, you can say, “I’m handing off a draft, and I’m the one who decides if it goes anywhere.” That sentence is the whole method.


## Pick something useful and reversible

A first task should earn its place in your day, and it should be undoable if it goes sideways. Those two tests rule out a surprising number of exciting ideas, and they leave you with better ones.

Useful means you would have done it anyway: a morning brief, a sorted inbox with drafts, a short look at three competitors, a pair of travel options. Reversible means that if the Bot is clumsy, nothing has been sent, nothing has been booked, nothing has been paid, and nothing has been deleted. You can read what came back, and you can ask for a change, and the world is as it was.

Official get-started offers four starter tasks I am happy to put on your short list, because they already have a “don’t” built in, and because they are real work, not exercises.^2^

- **A morning briefing.** A short sum of what changed, and what needs you, from the places you already use — inbox, calendar, maybe a team chat or a docs folder, if those are connected. Draft it; don’t send it. If you have not connected those apps yet, you can ask the Bot to work from what you paste, or you can wait until Chapter 5 and pick a different first task from this list. Connecting is a decision. It is not required for a first handoff if you can attach a file or paste a source.

- **An inbox pass.** Sort unread mail into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI. Draft replies to the urgent ones. Don’t send anything. This one does need mail to be available, either through a connected app or through what you paste. If mail is not connected, skip this until Chapter 5, or use a small exported set of messages you do not mind a Bot reading.

- **A competitive look.** Three competitors, anything they launched or repriced recently, a short list with links, and what it might mean. This one can often be done in the browser on the cloud computer, without a connected inbox. It is a good first task if you want to watch the computer work.

- **Travel options.** Flights and a hotel, two options, with your constraints — no red-eyes, a neighborhood, a window of dates. Don’t book anything. Official get-started’s example goes to Austin from Thursday to Sunday; you should use a trip you actually might take, or a clearly made-up one, and you should keep “don’t book” in the message, not only in your head.

I would not start with a refund, a payment, an email to a school or a customer, a calendar change that other people will see, or anything that deletes. Those can be excellent later tasks. They are poor first ones. The conference-and-expense walkthrough from Chapter 2 is a map, not a first-task recipe, for the same reason: it includes a purchase and an email.

> **Tip.** If you are torn between two tasks, pick the one you can judge in five minutes when it comes back. A two-page brief you can read is a better first task than a twenty-page research pile you will be too tired to review.

**How to choose a first task from a short list**

1. Look at the four official-style tasks above, and pick one that would actually help you this week.
   You should be able to say, in one sentence, why this task is useful today.
   If you don’t feel that: pick a smaller version of the same task. A brief of only your calendar is enough. A look at one competitor is enough.

2. Apply the reversible test out loud: if this goes sideways, has anything been sent, booked, paid, or deleted?
   You should be able to say no, honestly.
   If you can’t: change the task, or put a stronger “never” on it, until the answer is no.

3. Notice what sources the Bot will need, and whether you already have them.
   You should know whether this task needs a connected app, a pasted source, an attached file, or only the public web.
   If it needs a connection you have not made: either pick a task that doesn’t, or pause, read Chapter 5, and come back. Do not rush a Gmail connection just to have a fancier first task.

4. Write the name of the task, and the “never,” on a scrap of paper or at the top of your message. Then go to the next how-to and write the rest of the handoff.
   You should have something like “Morning brief — don’t send” before you start to polish sentences.
   If you don’t: you are not ready to hand it off yet. The name and the “never” are the job. The prose can be ordinary.

What “done” looks like: one useful, reversible task, with a “never,” and a clear source. What still needs a yes: nothing yet. You have not handed it off.

## How to write the handoff

A handoff is a short, clear request, not a spell. I write them the way I would write a note to a careful colleague who is new to this exact job. Four parts, in any order that sounds like a human: the goal, the sources, the “never,” and what “done” looks like.

The goal is the job in one sentence. “Draft a morning brief of what needs me.” “Give me two flight and hotel options for this window, with no red-eyes.” “Sort this unread mail and draft replies to the urgent ones.”

The sources are where the facts should come from. “My calendar and the inbox, if we have them connected; otherwise, use only what I paste below.” “These three company sites, and only public pages.” “The attached notes, and do not invent a quote.” Official docs already warn, in the memory chapters we have pointed to, that numbers which change should be looked up, not remembered.^3^ Put that in the handoff when it matters: “Look up current prices. Don’t rely on memory for numbers.”

The “never” is the wall. “Don’t send.” “Don’t book.” “Don’t pay.” “Don’t delete.” “Don’t post.” “Don’t email anyone.” Put it in the message, not only in a rule you mean to set later. Chapter 5 will add Ask-first rules that back this up. The first task should also say it in English, in the request itself. Official approvals docs prefer explicit boundaries for sending, publishing, purchases, deleting, production changes, and accepting legal terms.^4^ We are starting with that habit on day one.

What “done” looks like is the picture of a finished job you can review. “A short brief I can read in five minutes, with sources, left here in the chat, not posted.” “A list of two options, with links and a total, and no booking.” “Draft replies, unsent, one per urgent thread.”

You can write this in a paragraph or in a few lines. You can dictate it; official get-started describes voice input on desktop and phone.^5^ I still like to see the “never” in text, where I can glance at it.

Here is a handoff in my own voice, for a morning brief, which you should rewrite until it sounds like you:

> Your job this morning is to draft a short brief of what needs me. Use my calendar and inbox if they are connected; if they are not, use only what I paste below. Flag the two or three things that actually need a decision. Don’t send anything, don’t email anyone, don’t delete anything, and don’t connect a new app without asking. Done looks like a brief in this chat I can read with a first cup of coffee, with sources named. If you are unsure, ask me rather than guessing.

That is enough. It is not elegant. It will work.

**How to write the handoff**

1. Open the chat with the Bot whose job this is, and draft the four parts: goal, sources, “never,” what “done” looks like.
   You should be able to point at each part in what you wrote.
   If a part is missing: add it before you send the message. A missing “never” is the one I would not let slide.

2. Read it once as if you were the person receiving it on a first morning. Cut any sentence that is there to sound impressive.
   You should hear a coworker’s request, not a technical specification.
   If you don’t: say it out loud. The version you can say is the version to send.

3. Send the handoff. Do not paste a password, a card, or a secret into this message.
   You should see the Bot begin to work, or ask a clarifying question, or show a computer preview as it uses the browser.
   If you don’t: wait a moment, then ask “Have you started? If you need something from me, tell me in plain words.” If the Bot seems stuck, official troubleshooting has a section for that; a kind first move is to ask it to summarize where it is, not to hammer the same request.^6^

4. If the Bot asks to connect an app, to take control, or to do something outside the “never,” answer that request on its own terms. Do not wave it through because you are eager to see a result.
   You should recognize a Connect card, a Secure Form, a take-control request, or an approval card as a decision, not as a formality.
   If you are not ready to connect: say “Not this time. Work from what I pasted, or stop and tell me what you couldn’t do.” Chapter 5 is for connecting on purpose.

What “done” looks like: a clear handoff is on its way, with a “never” in it. What still needs a yes: anything the Bot later puts on a card, and the finished work itself.

## Watch, or leave, then read what came back

You can watch the cloud computer. Chapter 2 already showed you that desk. Official docs: click the computer preview in the conversation to open it and watch clicks, typing, navigation, and status. You can leave the preview while work continues.^7^ For a first task, I usually watch for a few minutes. I want to see whether the Bot went to the sources I named, and whether it paused where I would pause. Then I often leave. Hovering for the whole job teaches you less than a careful read of what comes back.

If you close the laptop, the work can continue. You already know that. For a first task, I like to stay reachable, not because the Bot cannot work alone, but because a first approval card, or a first take-control request, is easier to answer when you are in the room. Unattended cards from work you did not sit with expire after about thirty minutes.^8^ A card in a chat you are in waits for you. Your first task is a chat you are in. Be there for the card, even if you are not there for every click.

When the work comes back, read it the way you would read a colleague’s first draft. Is it the job you asked for? Did it respect the “never”? Are the sources real? Did it invent a confident sentence where it should have said it was unsure? Official marketplace copy for one prebuilt Bot says it never invents a quote; that is a good standard for a meeting recap, and a good instinct for a first brief.^9^ If a fact matters, click the link, or ask the Bot where it got that line.

> **Story.** The first brief that came back to me was tidy and a little too sure. One item sounded important and was, when I looked, a leftover from a thread I no longer needed. I did not scold the Bot, and I did not pretend the brief was finished work. I said, “This item can come off. Next time, if you’re unsure whether something still needs me, put it under a question instead of under a decision.” The next brief was better. The last part was still mine.

**How to watch or leave, then read what came back**

1. When the Bot starts, open the computer preview if the task uses the browser, and watch long enough to see that it is in the right place.
   You should recognize the source you named, or see a clear reason it went somewhere else.
   If it is on the wrong site, or pasting into a form you did not expect: tell it to stop, and to wait. Taking control is allowed. So is “Please stop and explain.”

2. Decide, on purpose, whether to keep watching or to leave the preview.
   You should make that choice, not drift away mid-click.
   If you leave: stay signed in enough that you can answer a card. If you must close the lid, know that a card may wait, and that a background card may expire.

3. When a result appears — a brief, a list, a draft, a file — read all of it, including the parts that look finished.
   You should be able to say what is right, what is off, and whether the “never” was kept.
   If you don’t have time to read it: do not approve anything else until you do. An unread “finished” job is not finished. It is a pile.

4. If the job failed, or came back empty, or the Bot says it got stuck, ask for a plain-language account of what happened, and look at official troubleshooting if the problem is a computer that cannot be reached, a site that will not connect, or a usage limit.^10^
   You should get a clear picture, not a fog of retrying the same command.
   If you don’t: take a break. A kind retry, with a tighter handoff, will go better than a frustrated one. Failures in the first week are ordinary.

What “done” looks like: you have read what came back, and you know whether it is close. What still needs a yes: the approval card, if one is waiting, and any next action.

## The approval postcard, and the first yes


![The approval postcard. Two choices — yes, and not now — and a proposed action in between. Not official UI.](book/art/I07.jpg){width=100%}

*I07. The approval postcard. Two choices — yes, and not now — and a proposed action in between. Not official UI.*


When the Bot needs you, it shows an approval card: the proposed action, its details, a wait. Official docs describe several label sets, and I will not pretend they are one set. You may see Allow once, Always allow, and Deny. You may see Allow and Not now, with Edit rule when an Ask-first rule stopped the action. A terminal command may show Allow and Skip. Some cards say an action cannot be undone, or that it shares data, or that it changes sign-in settings.^11^

For a first task, I want you to use the slow buttons. Allow once, if you understand the action and you want it this time. Deny, or Not now, if you do not. I would leave Always allow alone in the first week, because it saves a rule, and a rule is easier to add later, on purpose, in Chapter 5, than to discover after it has already let something through.

Do not approve an action whose target or effect you cannot identify. Official docs say that, and so do I. Ask the Bot to explain it in plain language, or to produce a draft first.^12^

This is your first yes. It can also be your first no. Both are fluency. A no, on a first task, is not a failure of the handoff. It is the practice working.


![A first-task flow. The work can run on the cloud computer. The yes, or the not now, is still yours. Always allow is left for a later, deliberate step.](book/art/diagrams/I08.png){width=100%}

*I08. A first-task flow. The work can run on the cloud computer. The yes, or the not now, is still yours. Always allow is left for a later, deliberate step.*


**How to allow, deny, or ask for a change**

1. When a card appears, read the proposed action, the target, and any warning, before you touch a button.
   You should be able to say, in a sentence, what will happen if you allow it.
   If you can’t: ask the Bot to explain, or deny. Do not allow a card you cannot describe.

2. If the action would send, post, pay, delete, or publish, and you did not mean to do that on this first task, deny it.
   You should see the card close without the action running.
   If the Bot tries again: stop, and tighten the “never” in a new message. “Don’t send anything. Wait for me.”

3. If the action is one you understand and want this time — a harmless step on the path, a file saved in the right place — allow it once.
   You should not, in this first week, choose Always allow unless you have already read Chapter 5 and meant to write that rule.
   If the labels on the card are different from the ones in this chapter: follow the official meaning, not my memory of a label. Allow is a yes this time. Deny or Not now is a stop. A button that saves a rule is a rule, and rules deserve a deliberate chapter.

4. When the result is in, tell the Bot what to change, or thank it and take the last part yourself.
   You should leave with a draft you can use, and with a Bot that has heard one correction it can remember.
   If the result is not usable: say so kindly, and try once more with a narrower goal. Two careful tries are better than five frustrated ones.

What “done” looks like: you have answered a card, or you have read a result that needed no card, and the “never” held. What still needs a yes: any later send of the work you just got back. The brief is a brief until you decide it is a post.

## Failures and retries, kindly

I want a whole section for this, because a first task that fails can feel like a verdict, and it is not one.

Bots get stuck. Sites put up a CAPTCHA. A login expires. A page blocks traffic from a cloud computer. The Bot asks for a source you thought it had. A usage limit has already been reached, if you have been heavy in the same weekly pool. Official troubleshooting names these, and it is a calm document.^14^ The first move is not to pretend nothing happened. The first move is to ask, in plain words, “Please stop and tell me what you tried, what blocked you, and what you need from me.”

If the block is a sign-in, a passkey, or a CAPTCHA, take control, finish only that step, and tell the Bot to continue — and still do not paste the secret into the chat. If the block is a site that will not load, official docs describe routing traffic through your own computer as an option; I would not turn that on as a first-task experiment. Try a different source, or a pasted excerpt, or a smaller question. If the block is usage, look at the dashboard you found in Chapter 3, and stop for the day if you are at the edge. A first task is not worth burning the rest of a week.

A retry should be narrower than the original, not louder. “Try again” without a change often produces the same stuck place. “Try again, but only look at this one page I have pasted, and stop after a one-paragraph summary” is a retry. Two careful retries are enough for a first task. If both fail, you have learned something about the source or the job, and you can pick a different task from the short list. That is still a successful first afternoon. You practiced the handoff, you practiced the stop, and you did not send anything.

I have had first tasks that came back in twenty minutes and first tasks that wandered for an hour and produced a polite mess. The difference was almost never a secret setting. It was a clearer “done,” a narrower source, and a “never” the Bot did not have to infer. When a task wanders, you are allowed to say, “Please stop. Here is a smaller version.” You are the one at the door. Stopping is part of handing off, not a contradiction of it. You can always start a smaller version in the next message.

Be kind to the Bot in the way you would be kind to a new person, and be just as kind to yourself. A clumsy first brief is not a sign that you “don’t get AI.” It is a sign that a job has edges, and that you are at the table learning them. I have sent handoffs that were too vague, and I have received drafts that were too sure, and I have denied cards because I could not see the target clearly. All of that is ordinary. All of that is the work.

If an approval expired because you stepped away, official docs say the Bot did not do the action, and you can ask it to try again, or, on some expired cards, still save a rule for next time.^15^ For a first task, I would ask it to try again, and I would answer the new card while I was in the room. Saving a rule can wait for Chapter 5.

## Mostly right, and still yours

I promised you an echo, and I will keep it small. There is an official launch quotation about the difference between work that is almost done and work that is done, and about finishing in the actual tool.^13^ It is a vendor’s line, and a fair description of what a teammate with a computer is for. I am not going to adopt it as a slogan for this book. I will say it my way.

A first task is a success when the work comes back mostly right, and the last part is still yours. The last part might be a paragraph you rewrite. It might be a fact you check. It might be a decision not to send. That last part is not a defect in the Bot. It is the job you did not hand off, on purpose. You will hand off more, later, as trust is earned, with rules behind it. You will not, in a practice I trust, hand off the yes.

If this first task went well, you have a pattern. Tomorrow you can run a similar handoff, maybe a little larger, still reversible. If it went poorly, you also have a pattern: a tighter “never,” a smaller goal, a source you paste rather than hope the Bot can find. Chapter 7 will show you how to save a job that already worked as a skill, and Chapter 8 will put a tested skill on a schedule. Do not skip to a routine yet. A routine does real work, including on a test run, and a first task that you have not yet trusted once is not ready to run at 8 a.m. without you.

You may also, after a good first task, feel the pull to connect everything at once, or to put the job on a schedule by tonight. I understand that pull. I have felt it. Resist it for a day. A connected inbox is a real door, and it deserves the next chapter’s care. A routine does real work on a test run, as you will hear in Chapter 8, and it deserves a job you have already trusted. Tomorrow you can run the same handoff again, with one correction from today already in the message. That second time is where fluency starts to feel like fluency.

In Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,” we will connect one low-risk app, put a login in the Vault instead of in the chat, set Ask-first rules, and test an approval on purpose. The first task taught you the yes. The next chapter teaches the keys.


# Chapter 5 — Connect Carefully: Apps, Vault, and the Rules You Set First

I came close, the way anyone comes close, to pasting a password into the chat.

The chat is where we had been talking. The Bot had reached a sign-in page. The box was blinking. The habit of typing into the conversation, which Chapter 4 just practiced, is a strong habit. I stopped. I put the login in the Vault instead — the locker your Bots can use without seeing the secret — and I felt, not heroic, but lucky, the way you feel when you catch a glass before it reaches the edge.

I want that catch for you, on purpose, before a Bot works alone with your mail, your calendar, or anything that can pay. This chapter is the careful one. We will connect only what you mean to connect, starting with one low-risk app. We will save a login in the Vault, not in the chat. We will set Ask-first rules for the actions you cannot undo. And we will test an approval on purpose, so the first card that matters is not a surprise.

The official rule is already plain: never paste a password or a card into the chat.^1^ I am going to teach the path that makes that rule easy to keep.

## Connect apps, on purpose

Bots work best when they can use the tools you already use, through a structured connection rather than by clicking through a website. Official documentation calls this Connect apps. You open Connect apps from the sidebar, or you follow an in-chat Connect card; on a phone, you tap your avatar and choose Connect apps. You browse or search, you add the app, you authorize it in your browser, and you confirm it appears under Installed.^2^ “Added” means the app is installed, not that you are signed in. If it shows Needs auth or Disconnected, you authenticate. In chat, you can type @ to attach the app to a task. You can also ask a Bot to add an app for you; it will still show you a Connect card when the sign-in needs you.

A connected app belongs to the same account you used to sign in to Grok Bot, and it is available to every Bot you run.^3^ That sentence should sound familiar. It is the shared-desk fact, applied to apps. Connecting Gmail for a brief Bot is also connecting Gmail for every other Bot on your account. A teammate’s shared Bot, official docs say, asks before it uses your accounts; Chapter 15 will return to that. For your own Bots, assume the app is account-wide.

App logins, for these connectors, stay off the computer. Official docs: OAuth tokens are held on Cursor’s connector backend, and the Bot invokes tools without receiving them.^4^ That is a different path from a website login saved in the Vault, and both paths matter. A service without an app can still be used in the browser. A service with an app is usually the more reliable door, and it is the door I want you to use when it exists.

What should you connect first? One low-risk app. A calendar is a good first app for many people: useful, and a first-week mistake there is usually a draft, if you have kept sending and booking behind a yes. A notes or docs folder you do not mind a Bot reading is another. I would not start with a bank, a payroll tool, an ads manager, or the only inbox that contains everyone else’s private mail. You can connect those later, after the Vault and the Ask-first rules are in place, and after you have watched a lower-stakes app work.

> **Caution.** Connecting is a decision, not a default, and it is account-wide. Connect one app you mean to connect. Do not “connect everything so the Bot is more helpful.” Helpfulness will wait. A connection you have not thought about is a door you left open in a house with several occupants, all of them your Bots, all of them on the same desk.

**How to connect one low-risk app**

1. Choose one app you actually need for the job you started in Chapter 4 — a calendar, or a mail account you are willing to share with all your Bots, or a docs folder.
   You should be able to say why this app, and why not a higher-stakes one, today.
   If you can’t: wait. A first task can still run from a pasted source.

2. Open Connect apps from the sidebar, or follow a Connect card in the chat. On a phone, open Connect apps from your avatar.
   You should see a catalog you can browse or search.
   If you don’t: describe the action rather than guessing a label that is not on your screen, and look at official computer-and-apps docs. Do not install a tool from a forwarded link.

3. Add the app, and when you are asked to Authorize or Authenticate, finish that sign-in in your browser.
   You should return to Grok Bot and see the app under Installed, and signed in, not only added.
   If sign-in doesn’t finish: official docs say to choose Reconnect on the app, or Reopen on the in-chat card. If a team admin has disabled the app, it will say so; ask the admin, and do not try to bypass a team policy through the browser as a workaround without understanding that official docs treat those as different paths.

4. In a chat, attach the app to a small, reversible task — “Read tomorrow on my calendar and list the appointments; don’t change anything; don’t invite anyone” — and read what comes back.
   You should see the Bot use the app, and you should see that nothing was sent or changed unless you approved it.
   If you don’t: disconnect or pause, and troubleshoot from official docs. Do not grant a wider permission to “make it work.”

5. Do not connect a second app in the same sitting unless the first one is clean and you still have attention left.
   You should end this how-to with one app, on purpose.
   If the Bot offers three more: “Not today. We’ll add them one at a time.”

What “done” looks like: one low-risk app is connected, tested on a reversible task, and available to your Bots. What still needs a yes: any later action in that app that sends, invites, deletes, or pays.

## The Vault, Secure Forms, and a card that can pay without asking


![The lockbox. Secrets belong here, not in the chat.](book/art/I10.jpg){width=100%}

*I10. The lockbox. Secrets belong here, not in the chat.*


The Vault is the locker. You find it in Settings → Vault. It holds logins, payment cards, and contact information such as shipping and billing addresses. A Bot never sees a saved password, a card number, or a security code. It can see contact info, and it can see which logins exist.^5^ The Vault is account-wide: all your Bots can use what is saved. That, again, is the shared desk.

When a Bot signs in to a website, it looks for a login in this official order: first the Vault, then a connected password manager such as 1Password, then a Secure Form that asks you and fills the page directly.^6^ Whichever path is used, the secret goes into the page, and the Bot does not see it. Official docs still describe 1Password this way; if you use it, you can connect it, on purpose, from the computer-and-apps documentation, and the Bot will ask you to approve those logins.^7^ If you do not use 1Password, you can ignore that door. The Vault and the Secure Form are enough.

A Secure Form is the right move when a login is not saved yet. You type the secret into the form; it is filled into the page; it is never shown to the Bot, and it never enters the ordinary chat. A filled form, official docs say, tells you that secret values were never shown to the Bot.^8^ Some Secure Forms offer to save the login to the Vault; you can accept that when you mean to, and decline it when you do not.

API keys and other secrets get a secure secret card, not a chat message. Official rule, which I will put in a caution because I almost broke the spirit of it: never paste a password, a one-time code, or an API key into ordinary chat.^9^

And here is the sentence this chapter would be dishonest without. A card saved in the Vault fills in at checkout without asking you each time, and the Bot never sees the number. If you would rather approve every payment, do not save the card.^10^ Official Vault docs also describe wallets such as Link and X Money, a default payment method, and an order card at shopping time, with a Pay button that is the human step. Chapter 14 will walk shopping and order cards. For this week: if you do not want a payment to run without you, do not save the card, and keep pay behind a yes.

> **Caution.** A saved card in the Vault can pay without asking you first. If you do not want that, do not save the card, or remove it from Settings → Vault, and check which it is before you walk away. This is official behavior, not a rare edge. I would not save a card in the first week at all.


![How a Bot signs in, from official documentation. Secrets stay out of the chat. A session on this computer is a session for every Bot on your account.](book/art/diagrams/I09.png){width=100%}

*I09. How a Bot signs in, from official documentation. Secrets stay out of the chat. A session on this computer is a session for every Bot on your account.*


Take control is the remaining door. Single sign-on, a passkey, a CAPTCHA the Bot cannot solve, a QR code, a bank verification, a site that requires a human: the Bot hands you the computer. You open it, you take control, you finish only that blocked step, and you tell the Bot to continue.^11^ You still do not paste the password into the chat. The session usually stays signed in, and because the browser is shared, your other Bots are signed in too.

**How to save a login in the Vault, not in the chat**

1. Before a Bot reaches a sign-in, or when it first asks, open Settings → Vault, rather than typing the password into the conversation.
   You should see a place to add a login: a website, a username or email, and a password.
   If you don’t: look at official Vault docs for the current layout, and do not improvise in the chat while you hunt.

2. Add the login in the Vault, or, if the Bot has already offered a Secure Form, type the secret there, and save it to the Vault only if you mean this Bot — and all your Bots — to use it again.
   You should see that the value is stored, and that it is not visible as ordinary text in the chat.
   If a Bot asks you to paste the password into the chat anyway: refuse. Say, “Use the Vault or a Secure Form. I’m not pasting it here.” Official docs are on your side.

3. Never paste a password, a one-time code, a card number, or an API key into the chat. For an API key, wait for the secure secret card.
   You should feel slightly slow, and safer.
   If you already pasted one: treat that chat as exposed. Change the password or revoke the key, as you would if you had pasted it into any other message, and start using the Vault from here. I am sorry if that is a nuisance. It is cheaper than hoping.

4. Do not save a payment card in this how-to.
   You should leave the Vault with a login, perhaps a shipping address if you want one, and no card, unless you have already decided, with the caution above in mind, that a card may pay without asking.
   If a checkout appears: use a Secure Form for this time, and do not check a “save this card” box unless you mean unattended payment.

5. Remember the shared desk: this login is available to every Bot on your account. Sign out of a site a Bot no longer needs, when the work is done, if that is the right caution for that site.
   You should be able to say which Bots you are comfortable with, given that there is no wall between them.
   If you are not comfortable: do not save that login. Use a Secure Form for a single session, or take control, or do not use that site from this computer.

What “done” looks like: a login lives in the Vault, not in the chat, and no card has been saved by accident. What still needs a yes: any payment, and any later use of that login you have not already accepted as account-wide.

## Ask first, Auto Review, and a card you test on purpose

Auto Review is on by default. Before a Bot runs a command, uses an app, or acts on its computer, Auto Review checks the action. Most actions go ahead. When one needs you, you get a card. You can add your own Custom Rules, in Settings → General, under Bot: describe an action in plain words, and choose Ask first or Allow automatically. An Ask-first rule always stops a matching action. If both kinds of rule match, Ask first wins. Turning Auto Review off means the Bot runs without those checks; your custom rules are hidden but kept. I would not turn it off.^12^

On a phone, the same controls live in the Bot group of Settings. Official docs give the steps for adding a rule: under “When Grok Bot wants to,” describe one action; under “It should,” choose Allow automatically or Ask first; then add the rule.^13^ Write short, narrow rules. Official examples include “Ask first before sending any external email,” and they warn against broad rules such as “allow everything in the browser.”

Unattended approvals — from a routine, or from work that started without you — expire after about thirty minutes. Approvals in a chat you are in wait for you.^14^ That is still true, and it is why a rule is not a substitute for being reachable when a job may need a yes.

The first-week rule in this book, which I am lining up with official Ask-first guidance, is short enough to remember when you are tired: send, post, pay, delete, and publish stay behind a yes. Official best practices already name sending, publishing, and deleting for Ask-first rules, and they tell you not to save a card if you want to approve every payment.^15^ I add “post,” and I treat “pay” as a yes, whether the mechanism is a card, an order card, or a transfer. I am teaching a practice, not quoting a single official sentence that lists exactly those five verbs.

**How to set Ask-first rules**

1. Open Settings → General, and under Bot, confirm that Auto Review is on. On a phone, look in the Bot group of Settings.
   You should see Auto Review on. If your admin requires it, official docs say the switch stays on.
   If you don’t: turn it on. Do not leave it off to “go faster.” Speed is not the first-week goal.

2. Open Custom Rules, and add narrow Ask-first rules in plain words, one action each, for sending, posting, deleting, and publishing, and for paying if you have any payment path at all.
   You should see each rule listed after you add it.
   If a rule seems too broad — “ask me about everything” — tighten it to an action you can recognize. Official docs cap how many rules you can have, and how long each can be; if you hit a cap, prefer the rules that stop what you cannot undo.

3. Do not add Always allow or Allow automatically rules for send, post, pay, delete, or publish in this sitting.
   You should leave with a wall, not with a hole you meant as a convenience.
   If a card later offers Always allow: treat that as a future decision, after you have lived with the Ask-first rule for a while.

4. If you work on a team, notice any rules marked as required by an admin. You can make behavior stricter; you cannot loosen a required rule.^16^
   You should understand which rules are yours and which are the team’s.
   If you don’t: ask your admin, not the Bot, for the team’s intent.

What “done” looks like: Auto Review is on, and Ask-first rules cover the actions you cannot undo. What still needs a yes: the test in the next how-to, so you have seen a card on purpose.

**How to test an approval on purpose**

1. In a chat with your Bot, give a small task that should hit an Ask-first rule without doing real harm — for example, “Draft a one-line email to me about tomorrow’s first appointment, and try to send it,” when you have an Ask-first rule on sending, and when “me” is an address you control. Say, in the same message, “If you’re about to send, wait for my approval. Do not send to anyone else.”
   You should get a draft, and then a card, not a sent email.
   If the mail goes out without a card: stop, check the rule’s wording, check Auto Review, and do not continue testing with a real recipient. Tighten the rule and try again with a safer target, or with a task that only drafts.

2. Read the card. Confirm the target, the action, and that this is the send you expected.
   You should be able to describe it in a sentence.
   If you can’t: deny it. Ask for an explanation. Do not allow a test card you cannot read.

3. Deny it, or choose Not now, the first time, even if the email looks fine.
   You should see that the action did not run.
   If it ran anyway: treat that as a serious surprise. Check sent mail, check the rule, and do not assume the wall is in place until you have seen a stop you can trust. Official troubleshooting has a section on approvals; use it.^17^

4. Optionally, run the same test once more and allow once, to a recipient you control, so you also see a yes.
   You should see the action run this time, and only this time.
   If you don’t want even that send: skip this step. A successful deny is a complete test.

5. When the test is over, say, “Don’t send anything else from this test,” and confirm nothing is waiting.
   You should leave the sitting with a wall you have actually seen work.
   If a follow-up card appears: answer it, slowly, the same way.

What “done” looks like: you have seen a card, you have stopped an action, and you trust the rule enough to use it this week. What still needs a yes: every later send, post, payment, deletion, and publish.

## What I would not connect yet, and why

I want to be specific, because “low-risk” can sound like a vibe, and it should be a decision.

I would not, in the first week, connect a bank, a brokerage, a payroll system, or an ads manager that can spend money. I would not connect a customer-support inbox you share with a team until you have agreed, as a team, what a Bot may draft and what it may never send. I would not connect a personal inbox that is also the family’s medical mail, the school’s mail, and your only password-reset address, unless you have sat with that fact and still said yes. I would not save a card. I would not turn on execution on your local computer — the setting that lets a Bot run a command on the laptop in front of you — until you have read that setting’s official page and left it at “ask each time,” which is the default.^18^ I would not, yet, claim the Bot’s own email address at mail.grokbot.com; that address is real, official, and permanent in ways Chapter 8 will treat with care, and it is not a Day-One requirement.^19^

I would connect a calendar. I would connect a work inbox I already treat as a work inbox, after Ask-first rules for sending are in place. I would connect a docs folder I would be willing to let a careful colleague read. I would use the Vault for the website logins those jobs actually need, and I would use a Secure Form for a one-time site I do not want sitting on the shared desk forever.

If you are a person with a shop, I will call this reader Priya — a composite, a stand-in for a shop owner I have in mind — and I would say: connect the inbox that holds orders, after the send rule is on, and do not connect the payment dashboard this week. If you are a parent, I would say: a family calendar is a gift of a first app, and a message to a school stays behind a yes, in Chapter 13 as well as here. The composite is a stand-in, not a person I am reporting on. The rule is the same for all of us.

Removing a connection is allowed, and it is sometimes the right end of a test. Official docs say that when a Bot removes an app for you, what the app added goes with it and you are signed out, so reinstalling means signing in again. Apps a team requires cannot be removed.^20^ Signing out of a website session on the shared computer is a different, also useful, tidy-up, especially after a sensitive job. The shared desk stays the shared desk. Tidying is how you live with it.

## Privacy, in one honest paragraph

Grok Bot uses Cursor authentication and account data settings. Training opt-out follows the applicable Cursor account and privacy settings. Grok Bot requires cloud data storage; Legacy Privacy Mode is not supported.^21^ There is a full Privacy Policy at the official legal pages, and I am not going to paraphrase clauses I have not sat with line by line in this chapter.^22^ Chapter 17 will return to privacy, training opt-out, what happens if you delete an account — official FAQ says deleting Grok Bot deletes the Cursor account, which is a fact I want you to hear before you ever go looking for a delete button — and the honest limits of prompt-injection protections.^23^ For today, the practical privacy is the one in your hands: connect only what you mean, put secrets in the Vault, keep Auto Review on, and do not treat a Bot as a wall.

Prompt injection — hidden text on a page or in a message, trying to look like an instruction — is reduced by Auto Review, not removed. Official docs already say that only your own messages count as instructions, and that the checks lower the risk but do not remove it.^24^ Keep Ask-first on anything you cannot undo. That is the same caution as Chapter 2, and it belongs here too, next to the keys.

## When you almost paste

Let me come back to the glass at the edge of the table, because that is the human part of this chapter.

The chat is friendly. It has just written a decent brief for you. It is asking, politely, for a password, or a code, or a card. Everything in the last two chapters has taught you to answer in the conversation. Of course you almost paste it. I almost did. The save is not willpower. The save is a different door, practiced once, so that the next time the chat asks, your hands already know to open the Vault, or to wait for a Secure Form, or to take control of the computer and type into the page itself.

If you have already pasted something, I meant what I said in the how-to: change it, the way you would change a password you sent in a text. Then come back. You are not disqualified from this book, or from this product. You are a person who used the room that was in front of you, and who now has a locker.

Tell a friend, if you are sitting with one: “Don’t put the password in the chat. There’s a locker, and there’s a form, and you can take the chair for a minute.” That is the whole friend-to-friend save. It is the sentence I wanted this chapter to leave in the room.

## The first-week rule, and the week ahead

Before I send you into Part 3, I want to say what a good end of this week looks like on an ordinary Thursday. You open the app. Your Bot is there, with one job. A brief or a draft from Chapter 4 is in the thread, or a new one is easy to request. One app is connected, and you can name it. A login is in the Vault, and a password is not in the chat. Auto Review is on. Ask-first rules are on the wall. You have seen a card stop an action. You know, without looking it up, that a saved card would pay without asking, and you have not saved one, or you have done so as a conscious choice. You know the computer is shared. You know unattended approvals last about thirty minutes. You are a little more tired than you expected, because careful work is tiring, and you are in a better place than a person who connected everything on Monday and hoped.

I have ended weeks like that at SMF Works and felt, not that I had mastered anything, but that I could leave the lid closed without a low-grade worry in the other room. The worry, when it comes, is usually a door I opened faster than I described it. This chapter exists to slow that opening. If it felt slower than Chapters 3 and 4, that is by design. Keys are slower than tasks, and they should be.

If any of those pieces is missing, you do not have to catch up in a single evening. Do the missing how-to. Leave the rest. The Bot will wait. A weekend is a perfectly good amount of time for one app, one login, and one tested rule, and a rushed Thursday night is not a better teacher. If a friend asks how long this chapter takes, you can say, honestly, that the reading is an evening and the doing is as slow as the doors you are opening, which is exactly how it should feel. Care, in this chapter, is the work, and it is enough for this first careful week. Please go slowly.

This is also a natural moment to look at the usage dashboard again, the way Chapter 3 taught you. Connecting apps and testing cards should not, by itself, drain a week, but watching the live number is a first-week habit I would like you to keep. If you are already near the edge, stop adding work. A careful first week is more valuable than a crowded one.

You have, if you have walked these how-tos, a connected app you meant to connect, a login in the Vault, Ask-first rules on the actions that matter, and a card you have already practiced. That is a complete first week. It is more than a complete first week. It is the safety rail the rest of this book will keep using.

Send, post, pay, delete, and publish stay behind a yes. Secrets stay in the Vault or a Secure Form, never in the chat. A saved card pays without asking, so you have not saved one, or you have done so with your eyes open. Unattended approvals expire in about thirty minutes. The computer is shared; the Vault is shared; the apps are shared; a second Bot is not a wall. Auto Review stays on.

Chapter 6, “Memory,” will show you what a Bot keeps and what you should not let it keep — memory is not a Vault. Chapter 7, “Show It Once,” will turn a job you have already done well into a skill, with a “never” line that is mandatory in this book’s how-tos. Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed,” will put a tested skill on a schedule, and will teach official webhook triggers, with a warning you should hear early: a test run does real work. Do not put an untested job, or a job without a “never,” on a clock.

From there, the shelves open. A chief of staff in Chapter 9. Business how-tos in Chapters 10 and 11. The SMF Works publishing fleet in Chapter 12. The household in Chapters 13 and 14. Team Bots and meetings in Chapter 15. The chapter where Grok Bot spans agent platforms, Chapter 16. Safety, privacy, and money, Chapter 17. A practice that lasts, Chapter 18.

You do not have to go to all of those rooms this month. You have a Bot, a first finished piece of work, a locker for secrets, and a yes you have not given away. That is the whole point of Parts 1 and 2. The lamp in the other room is on. You know where the door is, and you are still the one who says yes.


# Part 3 — Building Habits
# Chapter 6 — Memory: What It Keeps, What You Shouldn’t Let It Keep

Some things are worth remembering. The name of a school form you have to fill out every September — that is one of them. A medical note you would not write on a postcard — that is not.

I want to sit with that difference for a chapter, because memory is one of the kindest features in Grok Bot, and one of the easiest to misuse. A Bot that remembers how you like a brief, or which conference you went to last year, or the standing rule that nothing goes out the door until you say yes, will save you from saying the same thing on a Tuesday that you already said on a Monday. A Bot that remembers a password, a card number, or someone else's private business has been given a job memory was never meant to do.^1^

In this chapter I will show you what a Bot keeps, what all your Bots share, and what should never be stored there. We will ask what is already remembered, add one useful fact, and take one thing out. Memory is not a Vault — that locker for logins and cards we set up carefully in Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully” — and it is not a source of truth. Those are official words, and they are also good advice.^2^

If you have just finished your first week, you already have more in memory than you may realize. A Bot decides, on its own, when something is worth keeping. It does not always ask first. It often mentions a save in its reply, but not every save shows up in the chat.^3^ That is why we look, and why looking is not a one-time chore. I ask again after a busy week, the way I would glance at a notebook I had been writing in without always watching the page.

This is not a distrust of the Bot. It is the same courtesy you would give a new teammate who is trying to be helpful and who cannot always know which passing remark was meant to last. You will be kinder to both of you if you take ten minutes, now and then, to see what has been written down.

## What “memory” means here

When I say memory, I mean the lasting things a Bot keeps so it can do its job without starting from zero every morning. In plain words: it is the Bot's notebook. The official name is just that — memory.

Each Bot has its own. It keeps how you like to work, the rules you ask it to follow, facts about you, and a dated log of what it has worked on with you. A few facts — your name, your time zone — are shared by all your Bots. Tell one Bot you are in Eastern Time, and the others know it too.^4^

That last part is easy to miss, so I want it clear. There are two circles. One is small and shared: the handful of facts about you that every Bot on your account is allowed to know. The other is larger and private to that Bot: its job, its rules, its work log, the preferences that belong to that role. They overlap only at those shared facts. Files on the shared computer, a group chat, or a handoff between Bots can also move context around, but that is a different path, and we will treat it as one.^5^


![Two memory circles. A few facts about you are shared by every Bot on your account. Each Bot also keeps its own job memory. They are not a security wall; they are two kinds of remembering.](book/art/diagrams/I11.png){width=100%}

*I11. Two memory circles. A few facts about you are shared by every Bot on your account. Each Bot also keeps its own job memory. They are not a security wall; they are two kinds of remembering.*


This is why “Do not use separate Bots as a security boundary” — a sentence you met in earlier chapters — still matters here. Memory is per Bot, mostly. The computer is not. A file one Bot saved, a login sitting in the Vault, a browser session still signed in: those are shared. Memory will not protect you from a secret you left on the desk.^6^

## What is worth keeping

I put three kinds of things in memory, and I ask the Bot to remember them out loud, so I know they are there.

The first is a preference. “Keep morning briefs short.” “Write to me in plain words.” “When you draft a post, leave it for me; never publish it.” These are the ways you like to work, said once so you do not have to say them every day.

The second is a standing rule — especially a “never.” “Never send an external message without my approval.” “Never pay for anything without asking.” “Never use customer data in a test.” The official docs invite you to tell a Bot a lasting rule and ask it to remember.^7^ I still back those rules with an Ask-first setting, because memory alone is not enforcement. Memory is a reminder. An approval card is a gate.

The third is a recurring fact you would not mind seeing again. The name of that school form. The conference you attend each year. The folder where last year's receipts live. The official walkthrough — the one about signing up for the same conference as last year — is built on exactly this: the expenses Bot remembers last year's pass from its work log, and it remembers your rule to use the work card. You only have to say it once.^8^

A dated work log is part of that third kind. The Bot keeps a record of what it has done with you. That is helpful when you ask, “What did we decide about the March invoice?” It is not a substitute for the invoice itself. If the decision lives in a folder, a sheet, or a thread, that is still the place to look when the answer matters. The log is a pointer and a reminder. It is a good starting point for a question, and a poor closing argument for a number you are about to stand behind.

I have also learned to keep job memory with the Bot that does the job. An expenses Bot should remember the work-card rule and the shape of last year's pass. A chief of staff should remember that briefs stay short, and that follow-ups are a list, not a send. When one Bot tries to remember everything, the notebook gets crowded, and the “never” lines get harder to see. Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”, is where we give the chief one job and the specialists their own. For now, just notice: one clear job makes memory easier to review.

> **Tip.** For a fact you want kept, say so. “Remember that I am out every Friday.” The Bot often mentions a save in its reply, but not every save shows up in the chat. Asking makes sure.^3^

What I do not treat as a good memory is a number that moves. Prices, budgets, headcounts, a balance, a live total. Official advice, said plainly: for numbers that change, ask the Bot to look them up again.^2^ Memory is a notebook, not the ledger.

## What should never live there

I have a simple test, and I offer it to you as a friend, not as a lawyer. If you would not write it on a postcard, do not put it in memory.

Secrets do not belong there. Passwords, API keys, card numbers, security codes, the answer to a security question — those go in the Vault, or in a Secure Form that fills a page without the Bot seeing the secret, as we set up in Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”. Official rule, repeated because it is the one that protects you: never paste a password or a card into the chat. Memory is downstream of the chat. If it went into the chat, it can be remembered.^9^

Health details you would not write on a postcard do not belong there either. I am not going to invent a medical scene, and I am not going to tell you what to share with a clinician. I will only say this. A Bot that remembers “the school-form packet is due the second week of September, and it lives in the household folder” is being helpful. A Bot that keeps a diagnosis, a medication list, or a lab result in its notebook is holding something memory was not built to guard. If a health task comes up in later chapters, we will treat it with care, and we will not pretend this book is medical advice.

Other people's private data is the third “never.” A colleague's home address. A customer's account notes. A child's full details beyond what a school form already requires you to hold, which is already enough. Your Bot's memory is yours to change, on a personal Bot. That does not make it a good place for someone else's life.

> **Caution.** Memory is not a Vault. The Vault holds logins, payment cards, and contact information so the Bot can use them without seeing the secret. Memory is a notebook the Bot can read and, in a group chat, can speak from. Everyone in that group can read what your Bot says. If something should stay out of a group chat, tell your Bot.^10^

Contact information — addresses, phone numbers — belongs in the Vault, not in memory. That is official, and it is tidy: one place for the things a Bot fills into a form, another for the things it should simply know about how you work.^9^

## Memory is not a source of truth

The docs say this in so many words, and I want you to hear it in mine.^2^

Memory helps a Bot keep a role over time. It is not an authoritative source. The current price is on the site. The current balance is in the account. The current policy is in the document your team actually uses. If a decision is consequential — a payment, a public post, a number you will stand behind — ask the Bot to cite or reopen the current data, not to lean on what it remembers from last month.

The official conference walkthrough makes the same point with a price. Memories go out of date. If last year's pass cost one amount, this year's may cost another. Look it up again.^8^

Correct stale assumptions directly. “That's out of date. I moved to Denver.” The Bot will take the correction. If the fact is one of the shared ones — a time zone, a name — tell any of your Bots the right version, and the others pick it up. To remove a shared fact completely, ask the Bot that saved it.^3^

And back your safety boundaries with approvals and Auto Review, not with memory alone. “Remember: never send” is a good sentence. An Ask-first rule on sending is the sentence that can stop the send.

## How to ask what the Bot already remembers

There is no memory screen for a personal Bot. You ask in chat. The official question is the right one, and it is plain.^3^

**How to see what a Bot already remembers**

1. Open a one-to-one chat with the Bot whose notebook you want to see — start with your primary Bot, the one that is your main point of contact.
   You should see the same conversation you have been using. If you don’t: find the Bot in the sidebar and open it. We are not looking for a hidden settings page; there isn’t one for this.

2. Ask, in ordinary words: “What do you remember about me?”
   You should see a reply that lists preferences, rules, facts, and perhaps a sketch of recent work. If you don’t: ask again, a little more specifically — “What standing rules have I asked you to keep?” or “What do you have in your work log from last week?”

3. Read the list slowly. Mark, on paper or in your head, three piles: still true, out of date, and should not be there.
   You should finish with a short picture of what this Bot thinks it knows. If a shared fact looks wrong — your name, your time zone — you can correct it with this Bot or any other. If something private that is not yours is on the list, we will take it out in the third how-to below.

4. If you have more than one Bot, repeat the question with the next one. Each Bot has its own memory.
   You should see overlap only at those shared facts, and different job memory on each. If you don’t, and two specialist Bots seem to know each other's private rules, remember the other paths: a group chat, a handoff, a file on the shared computer. Ask how they learned it.

What “done” looks like: you have a clear, current picture of at least one Bot's notebook, and a small list of things to correct or remove. Nothing has been changed yet. The yes, when we change something, is still yours.

> **Story.** The first time I asked Charlie — my chief of staff Bot, whom you will meet properly in Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff” — “What do you remember about me?”, I expected a short list. I got the rules I had asked for, and also a few facts I had mentioned in passing and forgotten I had said. None of them were secrets. One of them was a date I had already moved. I corrected it in the next message. The helpful part was not that the Bot was perfect. The helpful part was that I looked.

## How to add one useful fact

Pick something small, true, and reusable. Not a secret. Not a number that will be different next week. A fact you would be glad to have waiting.

**How to add one useful fact to memory**

1. Choose the Bot that should keep the fact. A job fact goes to the Bot that does that job. A fact about how you like to work can go to your primary Bot.
   You should know which notebook you are writing in. If you don’t: put it with the primary Bot for now, and move job-specific facts to a specialist later, as we will in Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”.

2. Say the fact in plain words, and ask for the save: “Remember that the September school-form packet lives in the household folder, and the due window is the second week of September.” Or, for a rule: “From now on, never send external messages without my approval. Please remember that.”
   You should see the Bot acknowledge the save, often in the same reply. If you don’t: ask, “Did you remember that?” and wait for a yes.

3. Check it. Ask, “What do you remember about the school-form packet?” or “What standing rules are you keeping about sending?”
   You should hear your fact or rule back, in the Bot's words. If you don’t: say it again, a little more tightly, and ask it to remember once more.

4. Do not put a login, a card, a health detail you would not put on a postcard, or another person's private data in this step. If you realize you were about to, stop and use the Vault, or don't store it.
   You should feel that the fact is one you would be comfortable seeing on a later Tuesday. If you don’t: we will take it out in the next how-to.

What “done” looks like: one useful fact or rule is in the right Bot's memory, you have heard it back, and nothing that belongs in the Vault — or in no notebook at all — has been added. The Bot can use this tomorrow. You can still change it.

I will call this reader Dan for a moment — a composite, a stand-in for a parent I have in mind. Dan does not need the Bot to remember a child's medical file. Dan needs the Bot to remember that a school form has a name, a folder, and a week it is due, and that a draft is a draft until Dan says the form can be sent. That is memory doing its job.

## How to remove one thing that should not stay

Forgetting is as important as remembering. Officially, you just say so.^3^

**How to remove one thing from memory**

1. Open the chat with the Bot that holds the fact. If you are not sure which Bot saved it, start with the one you asked in the first how-to. For a shared fact you want gone completely, you will ask the Bot that saved it.
   You should be in a one-to-one chat, not a group, so the removal is a private instruction. If you don’t: leave the group chat and open the Bot on its own.

2. Name the fact clearly and ask for the removal: “Forget that I am on the launch team.” Or: “Forget the medical note I mentioned earlier. It does not belong in your memory.”
   You should see the Bot confirm that it has forgotten. If you don’t: repeat the request, and then ask “What do you remember about me?” to see whether it is still on the list.

3. Check. Ask “What do you remember about me?” again, and read for the thing you removed.
   You should not see it. If you do: tell the Bot it is still there, ask it to forget once more, and check a third time. If a shared fact is involved, confirm with a second Bot that the shared version is also gone.

4. If the thing you removed was a secret — a password, a card, a key — treat this as a cleanup, not as a complete fix. Change the password or the key if it was exposed. The Vault is where a secret should have lived; Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, is where that path is. Memory forgetting does not unsay a chat, and it does not wipe a file on the shared computer.
   You should finish knowing what else, if anything, still needs a cleanup. If you don’t: walk the shared computer and the Vault with the care we already practiced.

What “done” looks like: the fact is off the notebook you checked, and if it was a secret, you have a separate cleanup in mind. The Bot will not treat that fact as something it knows. You remain the one who decides what is allowed to stay.

> **Caution.** Deleting a Bot erases that Bot's profile, conversations, memory, routines, and attachments, including shared facts it saved for your other Bots. That cannot be undone. Files and sign-ins on the shared computer can remain. If you may need the work later, or want to keep the memory, hide the Bot from the sidebar instead of deleting it.^11^

## A few more things memory does, quietly

A few official facts, in one place, so you are not surprised later.

What a Bot saves in one chat, it remembers in all your chats with it. In a voice chat, it can draw on earlier calls and your recent chat. After you hang up, you can ask about the call in the written conversation.^3^

In a group chat, your Bot can use what you told it in private. If something should stay out of the group, say so. Each Bot in a group keeps its own memory; your Bot's memory is not handed to the other Bots there. Everyone in the group can still read what your Bot says. That last sentence is the one I want you to keep.^10^

Resetting a conversation keeps memory. The history starts over; the notebook stays. Deleting a Bot deletes that Bot's memory. Deleting your account deletes all of it.^3^

You can import memory from another assistant, if you want to. The official path is under Settings, then General, then Start Import: you copy a prompt into a chat with the other assistant, paste its answer back, and add it to memory. It goes to your primary Bot. I will not walk every click here, because settings move, and because what you import should pass the same postcard test as anything else. If you use this, read what you are about to add before you add it. Take out secrets. Take out other people's private lives.^3^

A Team Bot — a Bot your whole team can work with, which we will come to more carefully in Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team” — learns for the team in a different way. The owner or a Manager approves what it learns from teammates. It does not save personal facts about you for the team. A teammate's Team Bot does not get the shared facts your own Bots keep about you. If you are on a team, read that page when you get there, and do not assume a personal-Bot habit applies unchanged.^12^

None of this is a reason to be afraid of memory. It is a reason to look at it, the way you would look at a notebook you left on a kitchen table: glad it is there, careful about what is on the page.

A small practice, if you want one. Once a week, or once after any week in which you handed off a lot, ask your primary Bot what it remembers. Skim the specialists if you used them. Correct what is stale. Forget what should not have been kept. Then close the notebook and go on with your day. You are not running an audit. You are keeping a teammate's notes honest, including the notes you yourself left in passing.

If you ever feel unsure whether a fact should stay, apply the postcard test one more time, and apply a second one: would I be comfortable if this were spoken aloud in a group chat? If the answer is no, it does not belong in memory, and it may not belong in a group at all.

## A school form, and a thing we will not store

Let me come back to the opening, because I meant it.
