A school form has a name. It has a folder. It has a week it is due. Remembering those things is an act of care. It is the same kind of care as remembering last year's conference so this year's signup is not a scavenger hunt. It is the product working the way the official walkthrough hopes it will, in a household instead of an expenses folder.

A medical note is a different kind of care. The care is in leaving it out. Not because illness is shameful, and not because a Bot cannot be gentle. Because memory is a notebook, not a clinic file, and this book is not medical advice, and a postcard test is a good friend in the room. If you need help with a health task, talk to a professional who is actually yours. If a Bot helps you find a form or draft a letter, it can do that from instructions you give in the moment, without keeping the private heart of the matter in a log.

That is the whole distinction I wanted you to have in your hands.

Tomorrow, or the next time you sit down with a Bot, ask what it remembers. Add one useful fact. Take one thing out if it should not stay. Then, in Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, we will turn a job you already do well into a recipe you can use again — a skill — with a “never” written on the card.

You are still the one who says what is worth keeping. The Bot will help. It will not replace your judgment about what is too private, too changing, or too much someone else's to store. That judgment is a gift you give yourself, and it takes less time than cleaning up after a fact that should never have been written down.

When a memory is right, work gets lighter. The school form has a name. The brief is short because you already said so. The send waits because the rule is remembered and the approval is in place. That is the promise of this chapter, held at a human size: not that the Bot will know everything, but that it will know the right few things, and that you will have looked.


# Chapter 7 — Show It Once: Skills and Teach a Task

I keep a recipe card on the fridge for a soup I make every winter. It is not a speech. It is a short list: what goes in, in what order, how I know it is done, and one line at the bottom that my household has learned to treat as law — never leave the pot on a high flame and walk away. Anyone who cooks from that card gets a soup that tastes like mine. Anyone who skips the last line gets a conversation I would rather not have.

A skill, in Grok Bot, is that card. In plain words: it is a recipe you can reuse. The official name is a skill, and the official idea is simple. You do a job once, carefully. When it comes out the way you want, you save the method. The next time, you ask for the skill by name, instead of teaching the whole thing again.^1^

This chapter is about writing that card, including a “never” line that I will treat as mandatory in every how-to in this book. It is also about a feature called Teach a Task, which lets you demonstrate a browser workflow instead of describing every step — when it is available, and for up to ten minutes. Rollout may be gradual. If you do not see it, you are not behind, and I will show you the written path, which is the one I use most.^2^

A skill is not a routine. A skill is how. A routine, in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, is when. Official advice, which I have learned to treat as a kindness: test the skill on a real one-time task before you put it on a schedule.^3^ We will watch it once. Only then will we talk about a laptop that is closed.

## A recipe, not a speech

The first time I saved a skill, I overwrote it with a paragraph. I told the Bot everything I could think of, including three extra jobs that did not belong on the same card. The soup was no longer soup. It was a week of cooking with no “done.”

A useful skill, according to the official docs, states six things, and I have come to love how ordinary they are:^1^

1. When to use it.
2. Required inputs and access — which folder, which inbox, which app.
3. The sequence of work.
4. How to validate the result — how you know it is right.
5. What to return — a list, a draft, a folder of files, a short note in this chat.
6. What requires approval.

That sixth item is where the “never” lives. “Customer contact always requires approval.” “Do not publish.” “Do not pay.” “Do not use customer data.” If a skill does not say what it will not do, I do not consider it finished. This is my rule for the book, and it is in the spirit of the official examples, which include that approval boundary as a first-class part of the recipe.^1^

Skills are available across your Bots. A Bot may still need the right app or the right login to use one. That is important twice. First, you do not have to rewrite a good inbox-triage skill for every Bot that might run it. Second, a skill is not a security boundary. A skill is a recipe in a shared kitchen. The computer is still shared. The Vault is still shared. A “never” on a card is a strong instruction, and I still back it with an Ask-first rule for anything I cannot undo.^4^

If a Bot cannot find a private skill, the official path is to open Connect apps in the sidebar, select the connected count next to the title, and enable the skill for that Bot under Private skills. Private skills are one library shared by all your Bots. If you are on a team, you can also ask a Bot to save a skill just for itself; if you later share that Bot, the skill goes with it.^1^ I will not pretend those menus never move. If you cannot find the control, ask the Bot to enable the skill by name, and look at what it shows you.

You can also discover packaged skills when you connect apps. To use a skill, ask for it by name. Use `@` when you are calling a Bot, a group, a routine, or an app. We will stay with skills you write yourself in this chapter, because those are the ones that will carry your “never.”


![A recipe card on a fridge. A short sequence, and a last line you would not leave the room without.](book/art/I12.jpg){width=100%}

*I12. A recipe card on a fridge. A short sequence, and a last line you would not leave the room without.*


## Show it once, then save

The official path starts with a one-time task. You make it reliable. You save the method. Only then do you automate it.^1^ That order is the whole chapter, and the next one.

Here is the kind of ask the docs themselves suggest, after a run that already worked:

> Save the process we just used as a skill called “Weekly account health.” Include the source systems, risk definitions, output format, and the rule that customer contact always requires approval.^1^

Notice what is in that sentence. A name. The sources. The definitions that matter. The shape of the output. And a “never,” said as a rule. You can swap in your own job. “Weekly inbox triage.” “Friday project update.” “Morning brief, drafts only.” The structure holds.

I would not make one giant skill called “do all my marketing.” A giant card is how a “never” gets lost, and how a Bot inherits three jobs when it should have one. If a job is big, split it. A skill that gathers last week's sign-ups. A skill that drafts a launch note from those numbers. A skill that checks the drafts against a brand doc. Chapter 10, “Growth Work”, will use exactly that official trio. Here, I only want you to feel the size: a skill should be a recipe you can hold in your hand.

> **Tip.** Save after a successful run, not before. The card should describe what actually worked, not what you hope will work. If the first run was messy, do it once more, cleanly, and save that.

If you belong to a team, you can publish a private skill so teammates can use it. On desktop, the official path is Connect apps, then the skill, then Publish in its header. A skill needs a description first, because other people and their Bots use the description to decide when it applies. After you publish, you can sync later edits, copy a link, or unpublish. If publishing is refused, the message says why: you may need to join a team, or an admin may have turned publishing off. Skills that came with the app, or with a Bot's template, cannot be published.^1^

Strip anything you would not put in a shared document before you publish. A skill that contains an internal URL you would not share, a customer name, or a key, is not ready. Templates and shared skills carry setup, not your logins — and they should not carry secrets you forgot to take out. We will meet that idea again when we talk about sharing Bots in Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet”, and Chapter 18, “A Practice That Lasts”. For now: the card can be shared. The keys cannot.

## A skill is not a memory, and it is not a promise

Chapter 6, “Memory”'s notebook and this chapter's recipe card sit on the same desk, and they do different jobs. Memory holds facts and rules about you: briefs stay short, you are out on Fridays, the school-form packet has a name. A skill holds a method: when to use it, what to open, what to do, how to check, what to bring back, and what must not happen. If you put a whole method only in memory, it gets fuzzy. If you put a passing fact only in a skill, the next Bot will not know it. I let memory keep the standing “never send without me,” and I still write “never send” on the skill, because the card should be complete even if a different Bot picks it up.

A skill is also not a promise that the world is unchanged. Sites move a button. An inbox starts using a new label. A folder gets renamed. Official advice on routines, which applies just as well here: re-test after a website, an app, or a source format changes.^1^ I do a short watch-along when something important moves, the way I would taste the soup again if I changed a pot. The card is a good start. It is not a certificate.

And a skill is not, by itself, permission. I said this already, and I will say it once more because it is the sentence that keeps a recipe from becoming a risk. Skills are shared. The computer is shared. A “never” is an instruction. An Ask-first rule is a gate. For sending, publishing, paying, deleting, or changing a production system, I want both.

## What a good card looks like in practice

Let me write one with you, in words you can almost paste, and then you will make it yours. This is an inbox skill, in the spirit of the official starter prompt that sorts unread mail into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI, drafts the urgent replies, and does not send.^6^

**Name.** Weekly inbox triage.

**When to use it.** On a weekday morning, or when I ask you to run Weekly inbox triage. Use it on unread mail in the connected inbox I named, from the last twenty-four hours unless I give you a different window.

**Inputs and access.** The connected mail app. No other inbox. If you cannot reach it, stop and tell me; do not guess from memory.

**Sequence.** Sort into three piles: urgent, needs a reply, and FYI. For urgent threads, draft a reply in my voice, short, and leave it unsent. For “needs a reply,” give me a one-line recommendation. For FYI, give me a count and the three subject lines that most deserve a glance. If a thread looks like a bill, a school form, or a signature, flag it in its own sentence so I do not miss it.

**How to validate.** Every draft should be a reply to a real thread you can point to. Do not invent a sender. If you are unsure whether something is urgent, put it in “needs a reply” and say why.

**What to return.** A short note in this chat, with the three piles, the drafts under urgent, and links or subject lines I can click. No files unless I ask.

**What requires approval — the never.** Never send. Never delete. Never unsubscribe. Never forward. Never file a message out of the inbox unless I have asked you to in this run. If a draft is ready, leave it ready.

That is a card I would hang up. Notice how much of it is about stopping. Notice also that it does not try to be a chief of staff, a bill payer, and a school-form Bot at the same time. Flags go on the list. Other jobs get other cards.

If your repeated job is a Friday project update, the official Notion example is a good cousin: every Friday, draft a progress update, highlight what changed, and add it once you approve.^6^ The “never” is: do not edit the live page until I say yes. If your job is a competitive sketch, the official prompt asks for a short list with links, and I would add: do not write as if a rumor were a fact, and cite the page you opened. The shape repeats. The “never” changes with the job.

If a job needs a second pair of eyes, give the second pair its own small skill, and its own Bot when we get to teams in later chapters. A writer skill drafts. A fact-check skill reads the draft against the numbers and the brand doc, and does not publish. Official campaign teams are built that way, as you will see in Chapter 10, “Growth Work”.^6^ I mention it now so you do not feel you have to cram the reviewer into the same card. Two short recipes, one “never” each, will beat one long speech every time.

## Teach a Task, when you have it

Sometimes the job is easier to show than to describe. A click path through a school-forms folder. A sequence on a site that has no connector. A report you always pull the same way.

When Teach a Task is available, you can record one browser workflow from the computer view. The Bot turns the demonstration into a draft skill that you review and test. The recording is limited to ten minutes. Official FAQ: the rollout may be gradual.^2^ If the control is not visible, you are fine. Ask the Bot to create a skill from your written instructions and the task you just completed. That is a complete path, not a consolation prize.

I will not tell you that every plan and every account has Teach a Task today, because I cannot honestly map that, and I will not guess.^2^ What I can tell you is how the official steps go, when you do have it, and what to be careful of.

The official sequence is this:^1^

1. Open a one-to-one Bot conversation and its computer view.
2. Choose Teach a Task.
3. Describe the result you are about to demonstrate.
4. Perform the workflow once.
5. Stop the recording and review the skill the Bot creates.
6. Test it on a safe example before scheduling it.

Teaching records visible computer interaction. It does not record microphone audio. Avoid exposing secrets during the demonstration. Use the secure handoff — the Vault, a Secure Form, a takeover for a human-only step — the way we practiced in Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”. Do not type a password into a page while the recording can see it, if you can possibly avoid it. The learned skill is a draft. Add the decision rules, the failure handling, and the approval boundaries that may not be obvious from one example. Add the “never.”

> **Caution.** A demonstration is real work on a real computer. Use a safe example. Do not teach a task with a live customer record, a real payment, or a production toggle. Show the path on a sample, then put the guardrails on the card before you ever run it unattended.

I think of Teach a Task as showing someone the soup once, at the stove, and then writing the card together from what they saw. The card still needs your last line. The Bot is a good observer of clicks. It is not a mind reader of the rules you only say in your head.

If your demonstration is longer than ten minutes, it is too long for this feature, and it is probably too long for one skill. Split it. Show the part that is actually a click path — opening the right folder, filtering the right view, exporting the right file — and write the decision rules by hand. The official docs say to add those rules after the draft appears, because one example will not show every fork in the road.^1^ That is not a flaw in the recording. It is the reason you stay in the room for the review.

I have used the written path more often than the demonstration, even on jobs that live in a browser. I am slower at typing than some people, and I still find that a card I dictated carefully, after a clean run, is easier to audit than a draft I have to correct. Use whichever gets you a card you would show a friend. The feature is a help. The standard is the same.

## How to pick a short repeated job

Before we write or teach anything, we pick the job. The right job is one you already did well, or can do well once, this afternoon. It is short. It repeats. It has a finish line you can see.

**How to pick a short repeated job**

1. Look at the last two weeks. Write down three tasks you asked a Bot — or yourself — to do more than once. Inbox triage. A Friday update. A morning sketch of the calendar. A folder of receipts to match.
   You should have a short list of repeats, not a wish list of dreams. If you don’t: think about the thing you did this Monday that you will do again next Monday. Start there.

2. Choose the smallest one that still has a clear “done.” “Sort unread mail into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI, and draft replies to the urgent ones” is a job. “Handle my whole work life” is not.
   You should be able to say the finish line in one sentence. If you don’t: cut the job in half until you can.

3. Write, on paper, the “never” that belongs to this job. “Don’t send.” “Don’t publish.” “Don’t pay.” “Don’t contact anyone.” “Don’t use customer data.”
   You should have a line you are willing to treat as law. If you don’t: this job is not ready to be a skill. Come back when you know what must not happen.

4. Confirm that a successful one-time run is possible today, with a safe input — last week's mail, a sample folder, a staging site — not a live send.
   You should know what “safe” means for this job. If you don’t: pick a smaller job, or a sample that cannot hurt anyone if it goes wrong.

What “done” looks like: you have one short job, a finish line, a “never,” and a safe way to run it once. You have not saved a skill yet. You are choosing the soup, not yet writing the card.

The school-forms folder is a good example, and I will stay with a generic form, as I did in Chapter 6, “Memory”. Showing a Bot, once, how you open the household folder, find this year's packet, and draft the answers from last year's completed copy — leaving the send for you — is a skill with a human size. It is also a skill whose “never” is obvious: never submit the form, and never store a medical note in memory along the way.

## How to write or teach the skill, with a “never”

**How to write or teach a skill, with a “never”**

1. Do the job once, now, in a one-to-one chat with the Bot that should own it later. Use the safe input you picked. Watch the result.
   You should see a run you would be willing to repeat. If you don’t: fix the process in conversation, run it again, and only continue when the output is one you would sign your name under as a draft.

2. If you are writing the skill, ask the Bot to save what just worked. Name it. Include the six official parts, and say the “never” in the same request. For example: “Save the process we just used as a skill called Weekly inbox triage. Include when to use it, the inbox to read, the three piles, how you draft, what you return to me in this chat, and the rule that you never send.”
   You should see a skill saved under that name, with the approval rule on the card. If you don’t: ask the Bot to show you the skill, and tell it what to add.

3. If Teach a Task is available and the job is a browser workflow worth showing, use it on a safe example instead of — or before — the written save. Open the computer view, choose Teach a Task, describe the result, perform the workflow once, stop, and review the draft. Add the “never,” the failure cases, and any rule that was only in your head.
   You should see a draft skill you can edit. If you don’t see Teach a Task at all: skip this step without worry, and use step 2. If the draft is missing a guardrail: add it before you test.

4. Read the whole card out loud, or nearly so. Confirm the six parts. Confirm the “never.” Confirm that no password, card, or secret has been written into the instructions.
   You should be willing to hand this card to a careful friend. If you don’t: edit until you are.

What “done” looks like: a named skill exists, with a “never,” and it describes a run that already worked once. It has not been scheduled. It has not been left to run while you make dinner. The recipe is written. The tasting is next.

> **Story.** I have a card — a real one, on a real fridge — that my family teases me for, because the last line is longer than the ingredients. I used to be embarrassed by that. I am not anymore. The line is there because I walked away once. A skill's “never” is the same kind of memory: not a lack of trust, a record of care.

## How to run it once while you watch

Official FAQ: test the skill on a real one-time task before turning it into a routine.^3^ A test, in the next chapter, can do real work. This watch-along is the quieter cousin: you are in the chat, the laptop is open, and you can stop.

**How to run a new skill once while you watch**

1. In the same one-to-one chat, ask the Bot to run the skill by name, on a safe input. “Run Weekly inbox triage on yesterday's unread mail. Don’t send.”
   You should see the Bot start the work and stay inside the recipe. If you don’t: remind it of the skill name, and of the “never.” If it cannot find the skill, enable it for this Bot as the docs describe, or ask it to look again.^1^

2. Watch the steps. If the Bot has a computer view for this job, open it. If it is only reading a connected inbox, read the draft it returns.
   You should recognize the sequence you saved. If you don’t: stop, and tell it where it left the recipe. Edit the skill before you run it again.

3. Check the six parts against what actually happened. Did it use the right inputs? Did it validate? Did it return the thing you asked for? Did it stop at the “never”?
   You should be able to answer yes to each, or to name the miss. If a send, a post, a payment, or a delete was even offered in a way you did not want: deny it, tighten the skill, and tighten the Ask-first rule.

4. If the run was good, say so, and leave the skill as is. If it was almost good, edit the card — “Add that if the folder is empty, you report that and stop, instead of using last week's list” — and run one more watch-along.
   You should end with a skill you would trust to run once more, still with you in the room. If you don’t: it is not ready, and that is a fine day's work. A half-true recipe is worse than no card.

What “done” looks like: you have watched a clean run. The “never” held. The output is in the shape you asked for. You are ready, when you choose, to consider a routine in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed” — and you are not required to. A skill you call by name is already a gift. A schedule is a later one.


![From a job you did well to a skill you can reuse, and only then to a routine. The watch-along is not optional in this book. A routine is.](book/art/diagrams/I13.png){width=100%}

*I13. From a job you did well to a skill you can reuse, and only then to a routine. The watch-along is not optional in this book. A routine is.*


## Only then, a routine

I want to land this softly, because Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, is where the laptop closes, and I do not want you to close it on an untested card.

A routine assigns a workflow to one Bot and says when to run: on a schedule, or when something happens. We will do that properly next, including official webhook triggers, and including the fact that a test run does real work.^5^ If you skip the watch-along, a schedule will faithfully repeat a mistake, at 8:00 in the morning, while you are making coffee, and a noisy trigger will repeat it more often than that.

So we stop here, on purpose. You have a recipe. You have a “never.” You have seen it work once with your eyes. That is mastery at the size of a fridge card. The next chapter will hang a clock next to it.

If you do nothing else this week, pick one short job, write the card, and watch it once. The soup will taste like yours. The last line will still be yours. And when we put the card on a schedule, we will do it knowing what we are asking a teammate to repeat, with the laptop in the other room and the lamp still on.

One more kindness, for the days when you are tempted to skip the watch-along. I have skipped it. I have also, on a morning I was in a hurry, found a draft that sounded confident and was quietly wrong — a name from last week, a folder that had been renamed, a “never” the Bot had treated as a preference instead of a stop. Watching once is not ceremony. It is how you find that out while you are still in the room. It takes less time than an apology, and much less than an undo you do not have.

You already know how to ask a Bot to remember. You now know how to teach it a method. Those two habits — a notebook you review, a card you test — are the whole of Part 3, except for the clock. The clock is next. You will still be the one who says yes.


# Chapter 8 — Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed

There is a picture I want you to have before we touch a single setting. A laptop, closed, in the other room. A small clock. A lamp still on. And a morning brief already waiting, written by a Bot that ran a skill you tested, at a time you chose, with a “never” you wrote on the card. That is the promise of a routine. It is also the size of it. The brief is waiting. The send is not. The yes is still yours.


![A closed laptop, a small clock, a lamp still on.](book/art/I15.jpg){width=100%}

*I15. A closed laptop, a small clock, a lamp still on.*


In plain words, a routine is a promise about when a job should run. The official name is a routine. It assigns a workflow to one Bot and says the when: on a schedule, or when something happens — a Slack message, a GitHub, Linear, Sentry, or PagerDuty event, an email to your Bot’s own address, or a webhook call.^1^ Official work runs on the cloud computer. Closing the app, the laptop, or the phone does not stop a routine. That is the closed-laptop fact, and it is why this chapter is careful.^2^

Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, ended on the last box of a diagram we share: a tested skill, and only then a routine. This chapter is that last box, opened.

We will put a tested skill from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, on a weekday schedule. We will add one narrow event trigger. We will ask a Bot to add a webhook trigger, and I will teach you what the official docs actually say about that URL, that key, and a `200` that means accepted, not finished. We will confirm a test run, knowing that a test does real work. We will pause if we need to.

I will also tell you about a morning when an approval card expired because no one said yes. That is not a scare. It is a closed laptop’s other side, and it has a simple remedy: look, and then decide.

## A clock, a time zone, and a wait

You create a routine by asking the Bot that should own the job. The official kind of ask is ordinary English, and I would not dress it up:^1^

> Every weekday at 8:00 AM, run the Daily customer-risk skill against the current account list. Post a linked watch list in this conversation. Do not contact customers. If the source data is unavailable, report the failure instead of using old data.

Swap in your skill. “Every weekday at 8:00 AM, run the morning brief skill, and leave the draft in this chat. Do not send. If a source is unavailable, tell me what you could not reach; do not fill the gap from memory.” Confirm, before you walk away, the owning Bot, the schedule and time zone, the input, the expected result, the approval boundary, and what happens when a source is missing. The Bot creates the routine. It appears in that Bot’s routines.

Schedules use the time zone in Settings, under Bot, labeled Timezone. Auto-detect follows this computer. Pick a zone when the routine should follow a different one — the office where the work belongs, or the household that needs the brief at 8:00 their time, not yours while you are traveling.^1^ Ask for a schedule in plain words, like “every weekday at 8:00 AM” or “every 2 hours.” Then open the routine and check When to run, which shows the saved schedule in words. If it does not match what you meant, choose Edit and tell the Bot what to fix.

Here is the rule that surprises almost everyone, and I want it in a sentence you will remember. A new routine waits for its next scheduled time. It does not run at the moment you create it. If you set up “every day at 8:00 AM” at 8:05, the first run is tomorrow at 8:00 AM. To try it right away, use Test.^1^

On the phone, you can open the Bot’s profile and tap Add routine under Routines. Grok Bot starts a message that begins “Set up a routine to”; you finish the sentence and send it.^1^ The computer is still the cloud computer. The laptop can already be closed. The care is the same.

> **Tip.** After you create a routine, read When to run out loud. Time zones are where good intentions become 5:00 AM surprises. The words on that line are the routine you actually have.

## Events, and the cost of a noisy one

A routine can also start when something happens, instead of when a clock says so. Official event types include a Slack message; a GitHub, Linear, Sentry, or PagerDuty event; an email to your Bot’s own address; and a webhook call. Cursor account integrations start these routines. They are separate from the Slack or GitHub apps, and they may need their own connection. Ask the Bot for the trigger you want. When to run shows it in words, such as “When a Linear issue is created.” Only new activity counts. Events from before the trigger existed do not start a run.^1^

Define a narrow matching rule and a clear response. The official example is the right shape: when a message in a named channel contains a support-ticket link and a phrase such as “needs repro,” open the ticket, reproduce it in staging, and post a repro pack in this conversation. Never post back to Slack without approval.^1^ Your version might be: when mail arrives at a receipts alias, log it on a list, and never pay. Or: when a Linear issue is created in one project with one label, draft a brief, and never change the issue.

Avoid broad listeners such as “every new message.” They create noise, consume usage, and increase the chance of acting on irrelevant input. That is official, and it is also a bill you will feel. An hourly schedule, a short interval, or a Slack trigger on a busy channel can use a week of usage in a day.^1^ If your weekly usage is running down faster than you expect, pause the routines you do not need, then ask the Bot to widen schedules and narrow triggers. Do not treat a higher plan as the first answer. This book will not upsell you one.

A Slack trigger is part of a routine, not the Slack app. The app lets a Bot post and read as the Slack user you connected. A trigger starts the routine when something happens in Slack. Connect Slack on the Cursor account that owns the Bot before you rely on it. A routine can start when the Bot is mentioned, when a word or phrase is mentioned, when you react to a message, or on any message. It can watch one channel or all of Slack. Keep the channel and the phrase narrow.^1^ “Any message, everywhere” is how a helpful teammate becomes an expensive one.

## The Bot’s own mailbox

You can claim one email address at `mail.grokbot.com` — for example, `yourname@mail.grokbot.com` — for your Bots to use. They can receive, read, search, and send from it. New mail does not, by itself, wake a Bot. You set a routine if you want a run when mail arrives, optionally only from specific senders. Sending goes through Auto Review. You get one address, for good. You cannot rename or delete it. Addresses are never reissued, even after an account is deleted. All your Bots can use it in your own chats and routines. It is yours, not one Bot’s.^3^

Plus-aliases — `yourname+receipts@mail.grokbot.com` — land in the same inbox. You can give a service its own alias without claiming another address. A routine that watches an alias runs only for mail to that alias. A routine on the plain address runs for all of it.^3^ That is a tidy way to build a receipts logger that never pays: the alias is the trigger, the skill is “log, don’t pay,” and the approval stays on anything that would move money.

To claim an address, ask a Bot — “Get yourself an email address” is the official kind of ask — or use Connect apps, then Email. The Bot asks what name you want, checks that it is free, and shows an approval card. Nothing is claimed until you approve.^3^ Reserved names are blocked. On a team, agent email is off by default; an admin turns it on before members can claim an address. I will not walk every error message here. If a name is taken, choose another. If the team has it off, that is a team decision, not a Bot failure.

> **Caution.** One address, for good, is a real sentence. Pick the name the way you would pick a public handle: carefully, and only when you mean it. Sending from this address still needs your yes, if you have kept Ask-first on send, which in this book we have.

## Webhooks, as the official docs teach them

This is the section I want you to be able to hand to a careful friend, and I will not add a single field the docs do not give me.

A webhook, in plain words, is a doorbell a program can ring. Another system sends a message to a URL, and a run can start. The official name is a webhook trigger, and it is one of the event types a routine can have.^1^ Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”, will show you, at the level I have actually described my own shop, how Grok Bot Video Studio uses this with other agents. This chapter teaches the doorbell. That chapter tells a story about who rings it. I will not invent a payload, a required JSON field, or a status code beyond what is official. If you need a schema the docs do not publish, you do not have it from me.

Here is what you may assert, because the official skills-and-routines page says it.^1^

Ask the Bot to add a webhook trigger to the routine, then open the routine on desktop. The Webhook section shows three fields:

- **POST to** is the URL.
- **key** is the secret. Senders include it as `Authorization: Bearer <key>`.
- **header** is that full header, ready to copy.

Click a field to copy it. Send an HTTP POST to that URL with the header and, optionally, a JSON body. The Bot receives the body along with the routine instruction. A `200` response means Grok Bot accepted the call and started a run — not that the Bot has finished. Check the Bot’s chat for the result. Any other response means a run did not start, so confirm that the routine is not paused and that the request uses the current key.

That is the whole protocol I will teach. It is enough to use. It is not enough to invent against.

> **Caution.** The key is a secret. Do not paste it into this book, into a group chat, into a public page, or into a screenshot you will share. Do not put it in a skill card. If it leaks, treat it as you would any other secret: it is no longer a secret. Ask the Bot how to rotate or replace it, and stop using the old one. I will not print a sample key, and I will not invent a sample body for you to copy.

The 200 is the sentence people skip. Accepted is not finished. If you are wiring another tool to this doorbell, look at the Bot’s chat — or the run history on the phone — for the actual result. If you get any other response, do not assume the work is in progress. Check the pause switch. Check that the key you used is the current one.

## How a routine fires


![How a routine fires. A schedule waits for the next time. An event — including an official webhook — starts a run from new activity. A 200 on a webhook means the run was accepted, not that the work is finished. Approvals you are not sitting with expire after about thirty minutes.](book/art/diagrams/I14.png){width=100%}

*I14. How a routine fires. A schedule waits for the next time. An event — including an official webhook — starts a run from new activity. A 200 on a webhook means the run was accepted, not that the work is finished. Approvals you are not sitting with expire after about thirty minutes.*


The approval half of this picture is one you already know, and it matters more when you are not in the room. Approvals from work that started without you — a routine, or a message from another Bot — expire after about thirty minutes. Approvals in a chat you are in wait for you.^4^ If a routine tries to send, pay, or publish, and you are at dinner, the card will not wait all night. The action will not run. In the morning, check Approvals first, then usage, then whether the routine is paused.

> **Story.** I closed the laptop on a Sunday and opened a Monday brief that was already written. The coffee was still hot. The work was waiting, and it was mine to approve, not mine to start from zero. A different morning, a card had expired. Nothing had gone out the door, which is the ending I want for that story. I looked, I said yes to the thing that should have gone, and I tightened the routine so the ask came at a hour I actually keep. The closed laptop is a gift when the “never” holds. It is a lesson when a yes is time-limited.

## Test runs do real work

If the routine shows Test, use it after creating or editing: open the routine on desktop and choose Test. The button says Running… until the run finishes, and the result shows up in the Bot’s chat. If the test cannot start, you see “Couldn’t start a test run,” or a message that explains why. Some routines do not show Test; they still run on their schedule or trigger. For those, ask the Bot to do the same work once in chat with a safe input before you rely on the routine, then check the first scheduled run.^1^

I am going to put the official warning in my own voice, and then in theirs.

A test run performs real work. It can navigate websites, change files, and call connected tools. Use safe inputs. Keep write actions behind approval.^1^ If your routine is a receipts logger, test it on a sample, not on a live payment. If it is a brief, test it on this morning, and confirm it did not send. If it is a webhook, a test or a real POST can start the same kind of work. Accepted is not a rehearsal in a sandbox unless you have built the skill that way.

Review, after a test, the official list: whether it selected current inputs; whether the output meets the required format; whether every action has a source; whether it stopped at the intended approval point; whether failure states are explicit.^1^ Then decide whether to leave the routine on, or to pause it until you have edited the card.

## How to schedule a weekday brief

**How to schedule a weekday morning brief**

1. Start from a tested morning-brief skill, with a “never send” (Chapter 7, “Show It Once”). If you do not have one yet, write and watch it once before this how-to.
   You should have a recipe you have already seen work. If you don’t: go back one chapter. A schedule will not fix an untested card.

2. Ask the Bot that should own the brief, in plain words: “Every weekday at 8:00 AM, in my Bot time zone, run the morning brief skill. Leave the draft in this conversation. Do not send. If a source is unavailable, report the failure; do not use old data or memory in its place.”
   You should see a routine created, and When to run should say the schedule in words you recognize. If you don’t: open the routine, read the line, and Edit until it matches. Check the time zone in Settings, under Bot.

3. Remember that it will not run at creation. If it is already past 8:00, the first scheduled run is the next weekday at 8:00. Use Test, in the next how-to’s spirit, when you want a run now — and only with a “never send” that you will watch.
   You should know when the first real scheduled run will be. If you don’t: look at Next run on the phone, or ask the Bot.

4. Confirm the approval boundary is still on the routine’s Instruction, not only in your head. “Do not send” should be on the line the Bot will follow at 8:00, with the laptop closed.
   You should be willing to leave the room. If you don’t: pause the routine (the switch on the row, or Active off on the phone) until you are.

What “done” looks like: a weekday brief exists, in the right time zone, with a “never send,” waiting for its next 8:00. A test, if you ran one, left a draft and did not send. The laptop may close. The yes, for anything that would go out, is still yours.

## How to add a narrow event trigger

**How to add a narrow event trigger**

1. Pick one event you actually need, and make it narrow: one channel and one phrase, one alias, one project and one label. Write the “never” first.
   You should be able to say the trigger in one sentence and the stop in another. If you don’t: do not use “every new message.” Narrow it until you can.

2. Ask the Bot to add that trigger to a routine that already has a tested skill. For example: “When mail arrives at my receipts alias, run the receipts-log skill. Log the item on the list. Never pay, never approve, never file a payment.” Or: “When a message in #customer-escalations contains a ticket link and the phrase ‘needs repro,’ run the repro skill in staging and leave a pack in this chat. Never post back to Slack without approval.”
   You should see When to run describe the event in words. If you don’t: Edit, and ask the Bot to read the trigger back.

3. Confirm any separate connection the trigger needs — Slack or GitHub on the Cursor account that owns the Bot, as the docs describe — before you rely on it.^1^
   You should know whether the connection is in place. If you don’t: ask the Bot, and do not assume the chat app’s login is enough.

4. Do not turn this on for all of Slack, or for every message, as a first experiment. If usage later climbs, pause, then narrow.
   You should feel that a quiet day will not produce a dozen runs. If you don’t: the trigger is too broad.

What “done” looks like: one narrow event will start one tested skill, with a “never,” and only for new activity. Old messages will not replay themselves. You are ready, if you need a doorbell from a program rather than from Slack or mail, to add a webhook.

## How to add a webhook trigger — and not print a secret

**How to add a webhook trigger and read back the official fields**

1. In the routine that should receive the doorbell, ask the Bot to add a webhook trigger. Then open the routine on desktop.
   You should see a Webhook section with three fields: POST to, key, and header. If you don’t: you may be on the phone; the official walkthrough is on desktop. Ask the Bot where to look, and do not invent a URL.

2. Click a field to copy it when you need it. Senders will POST to that URL with `Authorization: Bearer <key>`. The header field is that full header, ready to copy. An optional JSON body may be sent; the Bot receives the body with the routine instruction.^1^
   You should understand those three fields well enough to hand them to a tool you trust, or to a teammate who is allowed to have the key. If you don’t: read the section once more, and do not proceed with a guessed header.

3. Never print the key in a document, a screenshot, a chat log you will share, or this book’s margins. If you write a runbook for yourself, write “the current key, from the Webhook section,” not the value.
   You should have a place for the key that is as careful as the Vault is for a password. If you don’t: stop and make one. A webhook secret is a secret.

4. When you first try a POST — from a tool you control, with a safe routine — treat a `200` as “accepted and started,” not as “finished.” Look in the Bot’s chat for the result. Any other response: check that the routine is not paused, and that you used the current key.^1^
   You should see a run start, or a clear reason it did not. If you don’t: do not retry in a tight loop. Rate limits are real, and a failed run is not always retried for you, as we will cover below.

What “done” looks like: the routine has an official webhook trigger. You know where the URL and the header live. The key has not been written into a public place. A `200` means a run started. The work is finished when the chat says so, and when any approval you required has been given. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”, will use this at the level of my own Video Studio story. The doorbell you just installed is the same kind.

## How to confirm a test, then pause if you need to

**How to confirm a test run’s effects, then pause if needed**

1. Open the routine on desktop and choose Test, if the button is there. If it is not, ask the Bot to do the same work once in chat, with a safe input.
   You should see Running… and then a result in the chat, or a reason the test could not start. If you don’t: wait a moment, then look at run history on the phone — Running, Succeeded, or Failed, with a reason — or ask the Bot what happened.

2. Inspect the real effects. Files changed, drafts created, messages proposed, sites visited: this was real work. Confirm the “never” held. Confirm the inputs were current. Confirm any write you did not want did not happen — or deny the card if it is still waiting.
   You should be able to name what changed. If you don’t: assume something may have, and look more carefully before you run it again.

3. If the test was wrong, or you are not ready for the next scheduled time, pause the routine. On desktop, use the switch on the row, or Pause inside the routine. On the phone, turn Active off. Pausing stops future runs. A routine that never runs does not spend usage; a Test does, because it performs the work.^1^
   You should see the routine marked Paused, or Active off. If you don’t: do not leave the room. Ask the Bot to pause it, and confirm.

4. If the test was right, you may leave it on. Still watch the first scheduled or triggered run the way you watched the first skill. Then, and only then, is the closed laptop earned.
   You should know when that first run will be. If you don’t: check Next run, or When to run, before you close the lid.

What “done” looks like: you know what the test did. The routine is either on, with a first run you can name, or paused until you edit. Nothing is running that you cannot account for.

## If a routine hasn’t run, and other limits

When a routine does not run, the official checklist is better than a guess.^1^

- The routine is not Paused. On the phone, Active is on.
- When to run shows the schedule or trigger you expect, in the right time zone.
- A new schedule waits for its next time. Use Test to run it now.
- For Slack, the message is new, in the channel the trigger watches, and matches its phrase.
- For a webhook, the call returned `200` and used the current key.

Then look at Approvals. A card that expired after about thirty minutes means the action did not run. That can look, from a distance, like a missed routine. It is a missed yes.

Routines have hourly limits: all of your routines together can run up to 300 times an hour, and each routine up to 200 times an hour. A run past either limit fails with “Automation run rate limit exceeded.” If one routine keeps hitting its limit, ask the Bot to narrow its trigger or widen its schedule. Some runs can also fail with this error because of a limit all Grok Bot routines share, even when your own routines run less than that. This is a documented known issue that is being fixed. A run that fails with this error is not retried automatically. Use Test to run it now, or for a webhook, send the call again — once, not in a tight loop.^1^

A Bot can own up to 50 routines. The app keeps the 20 most recent run records for each. Routine schedules must be at least five minutes apart. Deleting a routine stops its future runs and cannot be undone. Deleting a Bot also removes that Bot’s routines.^1^ These are official caps, not advice about how many you should want. I would rather you had five routines you trust than forty you cannot name.

To manage them, click the Bot’s name at the top of the chat, on desktop, and look at the Details tab. Each row shows the name and when it runs, or Paused. Open a routine to read its Instruction and When to run. A webhook routine also shows Webhook. The buttons are Pause or Resume, Test when it is offered, Edit — which starts a message that begins “Edit your routine:” — and Delete, which asks you to confirm.^1^ On a shared Bot, a menu may also ask who can use the routine: only you, or everyone with access. Choose on purpose.

After a long period away, Grok Bot may ask whether to keep routines running, and pause them if there is no response. Review paused routines when you return.^1^ That is a kindness dressed as a setting. Say hello to the clock again before you assume the brief will be there.

I want to add a word about editing, because a routine is not carved in stone. When you choose Edit, the app starts a message to the Bot that begins “Edit your routine:” with the routine’s name. You add what should change and send it.^1^ Change the hour. Narrow the phrase. Tighten the “never.” After you edit, test again, because the first test was of the old instruction. A schedule you no longer want should be paused or deleted on purpose, not left to fire out of politeness. Deleting cannot be undone; pausing can. When I am unsure, I pause.

If you share a Bot with other people — a Team Bot, or a Bot someone else can use — look at Who can use this routine, when that menu appears. Only me, or everyone with access, is a real choice. A doorbell that everyone can ring is a different object from a doorbell only you can ring. Choose it the way you would choose who has a key to the office.

Usage is the quiet partner of every clock. Each run spends it. A Test spends it. A routine that is paused, or that never starts, does not. I look at the usage dashboard when a week feels more expensive than it should, before I blame the plan, and I look at routines first: which ones ran, which triggers are broad, which tests I fired twice. Chapter 17, “Safety, Privacy, Money, and What to Do When It Breaks”, will sit with bills and caps properly. This chapter’s job is to keep you from lighting a noisy trigger and wondering, later, where the week went.

Official design advice, which I have made my own: automate preparation before execution. Have the Bot draft, reconcile, or recommend first. Require approval for sending, purchasing, deleting, publishing, or changing production systems. Include a no-data and stale-data policy. Tell the Bot where to report partial completion. Re-test after a website, an app, or a source format changes.^1^ That is the fridge-card standard, applied to a clock.

You now have a skill, a “never,” a schedule or a doorbell, and a test you treated as real. The laptop may close. In Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”, we will give one Bot the job of chief of staff, and let a morning brief — the one already waiting — be the first thing you and I build together as a practice, not only as a feature.

Let me say one more thing about the webhook, because I know it is the part that can feel like a different book. It is not. The doorbell is official. The key is a secret. The 200 is an acceptance, not a finish line. The work is the same work your skill already described, with the same “never,” and the same approval if something would go out the door. You do not need to become a programmer to use this. You need to be able to hand a URL and a header to a tool you trust, or to a person who is allowed to have them, and then look at the chat. In Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”, when we talk about work that spans other agent platforms, we will come back to this doorbell at the level of a story I can actually tell. We will not turn it into a protocol I cannot source.

If all of this feels like a lot for one afternoon, it is. Do the weekday brief first. Watch one test. Close the laptop on that alone. The narrow event and the webhook will wait for a second sitting. A single routine you trust is worth more than a panel of clocks you do not.

The lamp is still on. You are still the one who says yes.


# Part 4 — Business How-Tos
# Chapter 9 — Your Digital Chief of Staff

The first morning Charlie left a brief on my desk, I almost said thank you out loud to an empty room. It was not magic. It was a routine I had tested the week before, a short list of what to look at, and a promise I had made to myself: nothing goes out the door until I say yes. The coffee was still hot. The work was already waiting, and it was mine to approve, not mine to start from zero.

Charlie is my chief of staff Bot. I will use that name when I talk about SMF Works, because it is the real name of the Bot who runs the morning brief there. You should name yours yourself. A name makes a job feel like a job. “Assistant” is a cloud. “Sam, my chief of staff” — or Priya, or the name of a person who is not a real employee, just a name you like — is someone you can give one job, and a “never,” and a place at the top of a small team.

This chapter is that job. In plain words: a chief of staff coordinates. The official product description of a Chief of Staff is a teammate who scans Slack, email, calendar, and meeting notes, and delivers a succinct readout of what is new and what maps to your priorities, each with a clear source, why it matters, and what to do.^1^ We will write that job down. We will put a morning brief on the weekday routine you learned in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”. We will build a follow-up list that does not send itself. And we will keep the chief from hoarding work that belongs to a specialist. Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet”, is where my own small fleet sits in full. This chapter is the pattern you can take home today.

You still decide. That is not a slogan at the end. It is the first line of the chief’s job.

## One job: coordinate, don’t hoard

Your primary Bot is your main point of contact. It checks in with you on its own. It coordinates work with your other Bots. A shared Team Bot cannot be your primary Bot.^2^ Most Bots work best with one clear job. The primary Bot is different: it is the desk you walk up to in the morning, and the desk that hands work to the right person — the right Bot — when a job has its own sources, rules, or schedule.

That is a chief of staff, in a human size. Coordinate. Don’t hoard.

Hoarding looks like this. Every request goes to Charlie, and Charlie tries to be the inbox, the calendar, the book writer, the marketer, and the person who pays the bills. The notebook from Chapter 6, “Memory”, gets crowded. The “never” lines blur. A brief that should have been short becomes a novel. A follow-up that should have been a list becomes a send, because the same Bot that drafts has also been asked, on a busy day, to “just take care of it.”

I have done this. I have asked one Bot to hold too much because I was in a hurry, and because creating a second Bot felt like a fuss. It is not a fuss. It is a kindness to the work. The official path in the use-case docs is the same path this book follows: first an assistant, then a Bot with one job, then a team of Bots toward a bigger goal. Every official example stops for your review.^3^ A chief who hoards is stuck at a crowded first step. A chief who coordinates is already making a team, even if the team is only two desks and you.

Coordinating looks like this. You ask the chief what’s new. The chief reads across the apps you have connected, writes a short brief with sources, and flags what needs you. When a job repeats with its own rules — invoices, a campaign, a bug on staging — the chief hands it to a Bot who owns that job, or asks you whether it is time to create one. Official advice: start new work with your primary Bot. When the same kind of request keeps coming back with its own sources, rules, or schedule, give it its own Bot, or ask your primary Bot to create one. A Bot with one job builds up memory for that job, so you only explain it once.^2^


![A chief of staff in the middle, specialists around, you at the top. The chief coordinates. The specialists own a job. Every line that would send, book, pay, or publish comes back to you.](book/art/diagrams/I16.png){width=100%}

*I16. A chief of staff in the middle, specialists around, you at the top. The chief coordinates. The specialists own a job. Every line that would send, book, pay, or publish comes back to you.*


I will preview, without stealing, what Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet”, will show. At SMF Works, Charlie is not a bottleneck and not a pile. A writer Bot writes. A marketing Bot drafts site copy and does not publish. A programmer Bot prepares files and a site, and does not merge a pull request alone. Video Studio has its own desk. I still say yes to anything that goes out the door. The pattern is the diagram you just saw, with real work on the lines. You do not need my roster to use the pattern. You need one chief, one job, and a “never.”

## What a morning brief should be

The official starter prompt for a morning briefing is almost the whole lesson:^3^

> Sum up what changed since yesterday in my inbox, calendar, Slack, and docs, and flag what needs me. Draft it as a post for #team; don’t send it.

I like that prompt for three reasons. It has a window — since yesterday. It has sources — inbox, calendar, Slack, docs, or whichever you actually use. It has a “never” — don’t send. You can ask for the brief as a note in your chat instead of a draft post, if you do not want a #team version. You can ask for a Page, which is the official name for an interactive report a Bot can build and can update from a routine, if Pages are available on your plan. As of the docs I opened on 11 November 2026, Pages were rolling out gradually and were not available on Enterprise teams yet. That will age; if you ask and the Bot cannot make one, a clear note in the chat is a complete brief.^4^


A good brief, in my house, is short. Charlie knows that because I asked him to remember it, in the spirit of Chapter 6, “Memory”. It has sources I can click. It tells me why something matters, and what to do. It does not pretend a missing inbox was empty; it says it could not be reached. It does not book a meeting, send a reply, or move a calendar block. Those are follow-ups, and follow-ups, in this chapter, are a list.

The official Sunday-evening calendar example is a cousin: every Sunday evening, read the week ahead, flag clashes and packed days, and send you a short plan for Monday.^3^ “Send you” here means a note to you, and I would write “don’t book, don’t invite, don’t cancel” on the card. The official Gmail example sorts the night’s mail, drafts replies to the threads that need you, and leaves every draft for you to send.^3^ The official meeting-prep prompt is a one-page brief: who is attending, what you last discussed, open threads.^3^ A chief can call those skills, or hand them to a specialist, without becoming all of them.

> **Tip.** If the brief is getting long, the chief is probably doing specialist work. Ask: “What on this brief should belong to a Bot with its own job?” Then give that job away, kindly.

## Standing rules for a chief

I write the chief’s job in the first long message, and I ask the Bot to remember it. Officially, a lasting rule is “From now on, never send external messages without approval,” and a task is “Draft follow-ups for these twelve accounts.” The Bot keeps lasting rules in its memory.^2^ I still put the same “never” on the morning-brief skill and on the routine, because a card should be complete, and because memory is not a gate.

Here is a job I would be willing to hand you, in words you can make your own. It is a teaching draft, not a secret prompt from my shop, and not an official text you must use verbatim.

You are my chief of staff. Your job is to coordinate, not to hoard. Each weekday, prepare a short morning brief from the inbox, calendar, and other apps I have connected. Every item has a source, a why, and a suggested next step. Flag what needs me. When a job repeats and has its own rules, recommend a specialist Bot, or hand work to one we already have. Keep a follow-up list. Never send, never publish, never book, never pay, never delete, never merge. If a source is missing, say so; do not fill from memory. If you are unsure, ask. I am the one who says yes.

That is enough to start. You will edit it after a week, when you know which sentences you actually needed.

Ask-first rules still sit on the same verbs. A standing rule in memory is a reminder. A custom Ask-first rule, in Settings, is a stop. For a chief, I want the stop on anything that sends, books, pays, publishes, or deletes. Calendar and email are where a helpful chief is most tempted to be helpful. We will let it draft. We will not let it invite the whole team to Thursday, unattended.

## The first conversation, spoken slowly

If you like to think out loud, you can arrive at the same job through a conversation instead of a pasted draft. I have done both. The pasted draft is faster. The conversation is how you find the “never” you would have forgotten to write.

I might start like this. “I want you to be my chief of staff. Before we write your job down, ask me what a good morning looks like, what you should never do, and which apps you should read.” Then I answer honestly. A good morning is a short brief, already waiting, with sources. A bad morning is a dozen sends I did not see. The apps are the ones I have already connected, and no others until I say so. I tell the Bot about the follow-up list. I tell it that booking is a yes. I tell it that I would like to be asked, after a week, which parts of the brief I did not use, so we can cut them.

Then I say, “Please write your job back to me, in a short page I can edit, including every never we just agreed. Ask me to confirm before you remember it.” I edit the page. I take out any flourish that sounds like a brochure. I add a sentence about missing sources. I confirm, and I ask it to remember the edited version. That last step is the one people skip. A beautiful draft that was not saved as a lasting rule will fade, and a week later you will wonder why the brief is long again.

If the Bot offers to create three specialists in the same sitting, I usually say not yet. One chief, one brief, one week. Specialists are Chapter 10, “Growth Work”, and Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor”, and they are also any Tuesday you feel the brief get heavy. Officially, you can ask a primary Bot — or dr eggbot — to set up a team.^6^ You are allowed to go slowly. This book is a gift, not a race.

I also tell the chief how to talk to me. Short sentences. Plain words. No fake urgency. If something is actually urgent — a deadline today, a payment that will fail, a school form due this afternoon — say so in one line at the top, and show the source. If nothing is urgent, it is a kindness to say that too. A morning with no fires is a morning, not a failure of the product.

## How to write the chief’s job and “never”

**How to write your chief of staff’s job and “never”**

1. Choose the Bot. If you already have a primary Bot, this is often that Bot. If you don’t, create one, give it a name you will actually say, and, if you wish, make it your primary Bot the way Chapter 3, “Get In”, taught you — or ask it to be your main point of contact, and follow what the app shows you. A Team Bot cannot be your primary Bot.^2^
   You should have one named Bot, with one desk. If you don’t: do not start with three chiefs. Start with one.

2. In a one-to-one chat, paste a job in your own words — you can start from the teaching draft above — and ask the Bot to remember it. Include the “never” in the same message: never send, never publish, never book, never pay, never delete, never merge, unless I have said yes in this run.
   You should see the Bot acknowledge the job and the rules. If you don’t: ask, “What is your job, and what will you never do?” and correct the answer until it is right.

3. Ask, “What do you remember about your job?” and read it back, the way we did in Chapter 6, “Memory”.
   You should hear the coordinate-don’t-hoard line, and the “never,” in the Bot’s words. If a verb is missing: add it, and ask it to remember again.

4. Add or confirm Ask-first rules for send, book, pay, publish, and delete, so the memory has a gate in front of it. Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, is the home of those settings; this step is a check, not a new invention.
   You should know that a send would stop even if a busy morning made the brief too helpful. If you don’t: do not go on to a routine until you do.

What “done” looks like: your chief has a name, a job, a “never,” and a gate. It has not yet been put on a clock. It is ready to write a brief you will read.

I will use Priya once here — a composite, a stand-in for a shop owner I have in mind. Priya names her chief “Lane.” Lane’s job is the morning list: orders that need a person, mail that needs a reply, a calendar that must not double-book the counter. Lane never sends to a customer, never refunds, never changes a listing. Priya drinks her coffee and says yes, or not. The shop is still hers.

## How to put a morning brief on a routine

**How to set a morning-brief routine**

1. Do the brief once, by hand, in the chat, with yesterday’s real sources and a “don’t send.” Save what worked as a skill, with the six parts from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, and a “never send, never book.” Watch it once.
   You should have a tested skill before a clock. If you don’t: stay with the one-time brief for a few mornings. A routine will wait.

2. Ask the chief, in the spirit of Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”: “Every weekday at 8:00 AM, in my Bot time zone, run the morning brief skill. Leave the draft in this conversation” — or “update the morning Page,” if you have one — “and do not send, book, or pay. If a source is unavailable, report the failure; do not use old data.”
   You should see a routine, and When to run should match. If you don’t: Edit, and check the time zone.

3. Use Test, knowing a test does real work. Confirm the brief appeared, the sources are current, and nothing was sent.
   You should be willing to close the laptop on a Sunday. If you don’t: pause, tighten, and test again.

4. Leave the routine on only after that clean test. The first scheduled morning, read the brief as if you were still watching. Then let the habit be a habit.
   You should know when the next 8:00 is. If a card appears, you have about thirty minutes if you are not in the chat.^5^ Answer it, or let it expire and follow up on purpose.

What “done” looks like: a weekday brief arrives, short, sourced, unsent. The coffee can still be hot. The yes is still yours.

If you want a Page that is current every morning, ask for one, then ask the routine to update it. Officially, you ask the Bot to change a page; on-page filters are view-only. Sharing a Page has its own care — a public link needs nothing private on the page.^4^ Chapter 18, “A Practice That Lasts”, returns to Pages as a practice. Here, a Page is optional, and a clear note in the chat is enough.

## A follow-up list that does not send itself

This is the how-to that saves more accidents than any other in the chapter. A chief who is good at noticing will also be good at wanting to close the loop. We will let it close the loop on paper.

**How to keep a follow-up list that does not send itself**

1. Ask your chief, as a standing rule: “Keep a follow-up list for me. Each item has a person or a thread, a suggested next step, and a draft if a draft would help. Never send, never invite, never post the follow-up. The list is a list.”
   You should see the rule remembered, and, when you ask, a list rather than a report that “I reached out.” If you don’t: correct it immediately. “Reached out” is a send unless you can see that it was not.

2. When the morning brief includes something that needs a reply, ask: “Add this to the follow-up list, and draft the reply. Don’t send.”
   You should get a draft under the item, unsent. If a send is offered: Deny, and tighten both the rule and the Ask-first gate.

3. Work the list yourself, at a time you choose. Send the ones you mean. Strike the ones you don’t. Tell the chief what you sent, if you want the list to stay current — or ask it to mark an item “sent by me” only after you say so.
   You should remain the person who touched the outside world. If you don’t: the list has become a sender, and we have left the chapter’s promise.

4. Optionally, put a Friday review on a routine: “Every Friday at 4:00 PM, show me the open follow-ups, oldest first. Do not send any of them. Do not nudge anyone outside this chat.”
   You should get a review, not a burst of mail. If you don’t: pause the routine.

What “done” looks like: follow-ups live on a list, with drafts if you want them, and nothing on that list leaves without you. The chief is coordinating. You are still the one who speaks in your name.

> **Caution.** Calendar and email are where a chief looks most like a person who can “just handle it.” Booking, inviting, sending, declining: those are yeses. Keep Ask-first on. A draft invitation is a gift. An unattended invitation is a meeting you will have to unpick.

## Inbox and calendar, drafts only

The chief’s daily tools are usually mail and the calendar. I want to give each a compact card, so they do not get swallowed by a single giant “run my life” skill — the kind we refused in Chapter 7, “Show It Once”.

The official inbox prompt is: sort unread email into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI; draft replies to the urgent ones; don’t send anything.^3^ You can save that as a skill the chief runs as part of the brief, or as a skill you call when the inbox is the only thing you want. I prefer it as a named skill, Weekly inbox triage or Morning inbox, with the worked “never” from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”: never send, never delete, never unsubscribe, never forward. The chief may summarize the piles in the brief and leave the drafts attached, or hand the whole skill to an inbox specialist if the volume earns a desk. Either way, the outside world does not hear from the Bot until you press send.

The official Sunday-evening calendar prompt is a short plan for Monday: the week ahead, clashes, packed days.^3^ I would run a lighter version on a weekday morning as well, inside the brief: what is already on the calendar, what is double-booked, what needs a decision. The “never” is firm. Never book. Never invite. Never cancel. Never move a block on a shared calendar without a yes. A suggested slot, written on the follow-up list, is a gift. A meeting that appears on someone else’s Tuesday is a surprise, and not a kind one.

If you keep a household or a shop calendar as well as a work one, say so in the skill, and say which calendar may be read and which may never be written to. Dan — our composite parent from Chapter 6, “Memory” — might ask a chief to flag a school-form due date on the household calendar as a suggestion, and still be the person who puts it there. Priya might ask Lane to flag a delivery window that collides with the counter. The pattern is the same. The Bot sees. The person places.

A second brief, for a partner or a co-founder, is a kind idea if you both want it. Ask the chief for a separate note, with a narrower set of sources, and a “never” that includes “don’t send this to anyone; leave it for me to share.” Do not let a Bot mail a partner’s digest on your behalf until you have read it. Other people’s mornings are not a place to be unattended.

## A week with a chief, in ordinary time

Monday, the brief is waiting. You read it with coffee. You send two of the drafted replies, and you leave a third because the tone is almost right but not yours. You tell the chief, “The third draft was too sharp; remember I want warm and short.” That is Chapter 6, “Memory”, happening in a real week.

Tuesday, a source is missing. The brief says the inbox could not be reached, instead of pretending the night was quiet. You reconnect, or you sign in, or you take over a CAPTCHA, the way Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, taught you. The Wednesday brief is whole again. You thank the Bot for saying so, because you want that honesty remembered.

Wednesday, the brief is too long. Half of it is a pile of invoices. You ask the chief to recommend a specialist. You create an invoice Bot — or you will, in Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor” — with a “never pay.” The Thursday brief shrinks. The chief is coordinating again.

Thursday, a follow-up is old. The Friday review is tomorrow, but you ask now: “Show me anything on the list older than a week. Don’t send.” You close two loops by yourself. The list is a list.

Friday, you look at the week and decide one standing rule needs a tighter sentence. You edit. You ask the chief what it remembers. You do not add a second chief. You go into the weekend with a paused sense of enough.

That is not a case study. It is a picture of a practice at human speed. Your week will not match mine, and it should not. The shape — read, correct, give work away, keep the sends — is the thing I want you to borrow.

## When to spin a specialist

You will feel the moment. The brief is long because half of it is invoice matching. Or a campaign is in draft every Monday. Or staging bugs keep arriving in the same shape. Official advice, again: when the same kind of request keeps coming back with its own sources, rules, or schedule, give it its own Bot.^2^ You can ask the chief to create one, or you can create it, or you can start from dr eggbot, a marketplace Bot that asks you a few questions and sets up one-job Bots.^2^,^6^ I will not walk dr eggbot’s whole interview here, because it is a product that can change, and because your answers should be yours. The standard stays: one job, a “never,” a test.

Chapters 10 and 11 are shelves of those specialist jobs: outbound, paid media, content, a talent scout, support, invoices, engineering, a legal-ops pointer. Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet”, is how a small press uses the same idea. You do not need all of them. You need the one that is already crowding your chief.

When you hand work off, say it in the group or in the chief’s chat, and make one Bot responsible for the next step. Official best practice for group chats: one Bot for each step; `@` one Bot, not everyone. Approval and login requests may not appear in the group — check the Bot’s own chat.^7^ Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team”, is the home of Team Bots, Slack, and meetings. For now, the only rule I need you to take is: the chief is allowed to hand work away. That is the job.

A handoff, spoken, might sound like this. “Charlie, this invoice pile is its own job. Please draft a job description for an invoice Bot: match invoices to purchase orders, list mismatches, never approve or pay. Show me the description. Don’t create the Bot until I say yes.” You read the description. You add a “never.” You say yes. The chief creates the Bot, or you do. The next morning, the brief has a line — “invoices are with the invoice Bot; three mismatches need you” — instead of three pages of matching. That is coordinating. It is also how a small fleet begins, without anyone announcing a fleet.

If the specialist is not ready, the chief can still hold a folder. “Leave the invoice drafts in this folder and flag them on the brief. Don’t pay.” A folder is a complete answer for a month. A new Bot is a complete answer for a year. You get to choose when the desk is earned.

## A desk already waiting

I want to end where I began, because the feeling is the point.

Charlie’s brief is already on the desk some mornings when I sit down. It is short, because I asked for short. It has sources, because a brief without a source is a rumor. It has a list of follow-ups that have not been sent. Some days I disagree with a flag, and I say so, and the next brief is a little better. Some days the important thing is a school-form cousin — a deadline, a packet, a thing that would have been missed — and I am simply grateful. I do not have a metric for that gratitude, and I will not invent one. I have a practice.

You can have the same practice without having my company, my roster, or my name for the Bot. Write the job. Write the “never.” Test the brief. Put it on a weekday clock. Keep the follow-ups as a list. Give away the work that has earned its own desk. You are still the person who walks into the room, reads what is waiting, and says yes, or not, or “not like that.”

If you only remember four sentences from this chapter, let them be these. The chief coordinates and does not hoard. The brief is short, sourced, and unsent. The follow-up list does not send itself. You are still the one who says yes. Everything else is a method for making those sentences true on a Thursday when you are tired.

And if a Thursday comes when the brief is wrong — a stale name, a missed deadline, a tone that is not yours — treat it the way we treated a stale memory. Correct it. Ask the chief to remember the correction. Watch the next morning. A chief is a practice, not a product you install once and admire. I have corrected Charlie more times than I can count, and I will not pretend otherwise. The thanks I almost said out loud, that first morning, was real. So is the editing. Both belong in an honest book.

Name your Bot. Sit with the first brief. Send nothing you have not read. That is enough for this week. The growth work, when you want it, has its own specialists, and they will use the same four sentences.

One last practical note. If you travel, check the chief’s time zone, the way we checked it in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”. A brief that arrives at 8:00 in a zone you have left is a brief you will read at the wrong hour, and an approval card that expires in about thirty minutes will not wait for your hotel Wi-Fi. Pause, or shift the clock, or read on your phone — the same Bots, the same routines, the same cloud computer, as the mobile apps allow.^8^ Pause is always allowed. A missed brief is kinder than a missed yes.

I should also tell you what I do not ask Charlie to be. Charlie is not my lawyer, my clinician, or my accountant. Charlie is not a second me, and Charlie is not the publisher of record at WisdomForge. When a morning contains a judgment I would not delegate to a careful human assistant on their first week — a tone that has to be mine, a yes that will spend money, a paragraph that will be public — I do that work myself, often from a draft the Bot left me. The brief made the work smaller. It did not make my responsibility smaller.

If your chief ever sounds sure about a number, a rumor, or a “they said,” ask for the source, the way the official Chief of Staff description already wants: each item with a clear source.^1^ If the source cannot be opened again, the item is a maybe, and a maybe does not go on a follow-up as if it were a fact. Memory is not a source of truth, and a brief is not one either. It is a map. You still walk.

That is a digital chief of staff. It is also just a careful morning. In the next chapter, we will let specialists take the growth work — sales, marketing, recruiting — with the same draft-first rule, and the same yes at the door.


# Chapter 10 — Growth Work: Sales, Marketing, and Recruiting

A campaign waited overnight for a morning yes. That is the picture for this chapter, and it is the whole method. Research ran while the laptop was closed. Drafts were written in a voice you had already taught. A fact-check Bot read the numbers against the words.
