In the morning, a list was waiting — a launch note, three posts, a recommended budget move, a shortlist, a set of outreach drafts — and nothing had gone out. The coffee, once again, was still hot. The yes was yours.

Growth work is where a Bot can look, to a tired person, like a way to skip the yes. Outbound can be sent. A budget can be moved. A post can be published. A candidate can be contacted. This chapter will not skip those yeses. Official use cases in this area all stop for your review.^1^ We will follow them, in four compact how-tos: a sales outbound Bot, a paid-media recommender, a content and campaign trio, and a talent scout. I will point you to the official vertical guides when you want more depth, rather than retelling every page. And I will talk about usage and reputation, because growth work is public work. A draft that is wrong in a folder is a lesson. A draft that is wrong in someone else’s inbox is their morning, and your name.

You already have the habits. A chief who coordinates, from Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”. A skill with a “never,” from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”. A routine that waits for the next hour, from Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”. This chapter puts those habits on four desks.

I want to be honest about what “growth” means in a book that is a gift. It does not mean a funnel I am selling you, and it does not mean you must have a pipeline, an ad account, or an open role. It means the work of talking to the world: a note to a possible customer, a post about something you shipped, a budget you already spend, a conversation with a person who might join you. If none of that is your work this month, the shop floor in Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor”, or the household work in Chapter 13, “Get Your Life Back”, may be the better page to turn to. If it is your work, I would like it to be work you can be proud of in the morning, including the messages you decided not to send.

The overnight picture is official in spirit. Sales Outbound is described as researching accounts overnight and leaving a review list.^2^ Routines run in the cloud while the laptop is closed.^9^ The “worked while you slept” of this book’s subtitle is that picture, held at a human size: a team that prepares, and a person who still reviews. It is not a guarantee of revenue, and it is not a reason to turn off the lights on a budget or a person’s inbox.

## Draft first, always

The rule for Chapters 9 through 12 is the same, and I will keep saying it until it feels like a household phrase. Drafts and recommendations first. Send, publish, pay, and production behind a yes.

For growth work, the “never” lines are almost always some flavor of that rule.

- Sales outbound: never send the email, never send the LinkedIn message, leave a review list.
- Paid media: recommend where to move money, don’t change any budgets.
- Content: bring me the drafts, don’t publish.
- Talent scout: draft outreach in my voice, don’t contact anyone. Skip people already in the ATS. Scheduling happens once you approve.^1^

Those are official shapes, not my inventions. I will write them onto skills and onto routines. I will back them with Ask-first rules. I will not tell you that a Bot “handles outreach” as if the handling included the send. The official Talent Scout description includes “handles scheduling once you approve.” The “once you approve” is the sentence I want in bold in your mind, even though this book does not use bold for that kind of emphasis. It is the whole job.

Reputation is the quiet cost of skipping this. People remember a message that felt automated, or a second outreach after they already said no, or a note that used their name and got their work wrong. They remember a job-hunt email that was clearly a blast. They remember an ad account that spent a weekend on a campaign you meant to pause. I do not have a study to quote for that, and I will not invent one. I have a career’s worth of being on the other side of a careless message, and I would like your name to be one people trust.

Usage is the other quiet cost. Overnight research, a busy trigger, a campaign team that runs too often, a scout that pages through a large site: these spend the shared weekly pool you met in Chapter 3, “Get In”. A review list that waited for morning is the right picture. A noisy routine that ran all night on a broad listener is the picture from Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, that we already refused.


![Overnight to a morning yes. The work can happen while you sleep. The send, the post, the spend, and the outreach wait for you.](book/art/diagrams/I17.png){width=100%}

*I17. Overnight to a morning yes. The work can happen while you sleep. The send, the post, the spend, and the outreach wait for you.*


## Sales outbound, and a list you will actually read

The official Sales Outbound picture is: research accounts overnight, score contacts, draft email and LinkedIn in your voice, and leave a review list for you to approve.^2^ A close cousin, in the docs, is sales development for inbound: qualify this morning’s leads against your ideal customer profile, draft intro replies with times you are free for good fits and a polite no for the rest, and don’t send anything.^1^

There are official guides if this is your whole job: Grok Bot for SDRs, Grok Bot for GTM, Grok Bot for founder-led sales, and a sales news piece about how the company describes using the product. They are vendor how-tos, and they are useful. Start here, then go there if you need a deeper desk.^3^

**How to set up a sales outbound Bot, drafts only**

1. Create a Bot with one job — outbound research and drafts — or ask your chief to create one after you approve the description. In the first message, give it the job, the sources (your CRM if connected, the account list you name, the public pages it may read), your voice in a few sample messages, and the “never”: never send email, never send a LinkedIn message, never update a CRM record you would not want it to touch, never contact anyone. Ask it to remember.
   You should be able to ask “What is your job, and what will you never do?” and hear the send-stop in the answer. If you don’t: correct it before any overnight run.

2. Do one account, or one morning’s inbound, as a one-time task. Watch the research. Edit a draft until it sounds like you. Save the method as a skill, with the six parts from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, and the “never send” on the card.
   You should have a skill you have seen work on a safe, small input. If you don’t: do not schedule it.

3. Ask for a routine, if you want the overnight picture: “Every weekday night, at an hour I will actually review the next morning, run the outbound skill on the day’s list. Leave a review list in this chat, with sources and drafts. Don’t send. If a person is already in the CRM as ‘do not contact,’ skip them and say so.” Test it, knowing a test is real work — but that “real” should still be a draft.
   You should get a list, not a sent folder. If a send is offered: Deny, and tighten Ask-first.

4. In the morning, work the list. Send the ones that are yours. Rewrite the ones that are almost yours. Tell the Bot what you changed, so the voice improves. Do not “approve all” out of tiredness.
   You should remain the person whose name is at the bottom. If you don’t: pause the routine until you can be.

What “done” looks like: a review list waits in the morning. Nothing has been sent. You will spend your yes on the messages that deserve your name.

> **Caution.** A Bot that can sign in to LinkedIn or a mailbox is a Bot that can, if a “never” and a gate are missing, act in your name. Vault logins and a shared computer mean other Bots on the account may be able to use the same session. Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, still applies. Do not treat a specialist Bot as a security wall.

I will not tell you how many meetings this will book, and I will not invent a pipeline number. I will tell you that a careful list, in your voice, that you actually read, is already a better morning than a blank page.

If you are a founder doing your own outbound — the official founder-led sales guide is on that shelf^3^ — keep the desk even smaller. One list, a handful of accounts you actually know, drafts you will rewrite anyway. A Bot is good at gathering the last public note, the last email, the open thread, and putting them on one page, the way the official meeting-prep prompt does. It is less good at the judgment that says, “not this person, not this week.” That judgment is why you still read the list. A polite no, drafted for a lead that is not a fit, is also work worth reviewing. The official inbound prompt includes that polite no, and does not send it.^1^ You should be able to stand behind a no as easily as a yes.

Do not let a routine write into the CRM as a side effect of being helpful, unless you have a separate, tested skill for that and a “never overwrite a field I care about.” Hygiene is a real job; it is not this job. If you want a CRM-operations Bot later, give it its own card, its own “never,” and a review list of proposed edits, the same way paid media proposes budget moves and does not make them.

## Paid media: recommend, don’t spend

The official paid-media prompt is: compare this month’s spend by campaign with your budget and target CAC, and recommend where to move money. Don’t change any budgets.^1^ The product-page version adds a Slack recommendation against a monthly budget, held for your approval before adjustments.^2^ That last clause is the job.

There is an official Grok Bot for Performance Marketing guide when you want more.^4^ This how-to stays at the size of a recommendation.

**How to set up a paid-media recommender**

1. Give a Bot one job: read the ad accounts or reports you name, compare spend to the budget and the targets you give it, and recommend moves. Never change a budget, a bid, a status, a creative, or a payment method. Never launch a campaign. Ask it to remember, and put the same “never” on Ask-first for anything that would spend or change production in that account.
   You should hear the stop when you ask the Bot its rules. If you don’t: do not connect the ad account until you do.

2. Run it once, on last month or last week, as a one-time task. Ask for a recommendation with sources: which campaign, which number, which page it opened. Save a skill. If a number cannot be opened again, it is not a basis for a move.
   You should get a recommendation you can check. If you don’t: the Bot is guessing, and guessing is not a budget meeting.

3. If you want a cadence, put the skill on a routine — weekly, not every hour — and have it leave the recommendation in your chat or in a Page. Don’t change any budgets. Test once.
   You should see a memo, not a changed campaign. If a change is even proposed as an automatic step: Deny, pause, and rewrite the card.

4. You make the move, or you ask the Bot to make a specific move after you say yes, and you watch that run. A saved card can pay without asking, as Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, taught. An ad account can spend without a second conversation. This is not the place to be unattended.
   You should be able to name what changed, and that you approved it. If you can’t: it should not have changed.

What “done” looks like: a recommendation, with sources, waiting for a human spend decision. No unattended budget moves. No unattended spend.

> **Caution.** Unattended spend is one of the fastest ways for a helpful teammate to become an expensive one. Do not save a payment method for this Bot’s use if you do not want a payment to be possible. Do not let a routine change budgets. A morning memo is the product. A weekend of unnoticed spend is not.

I will not quote vendor claims about savings. A recommendation you can audit is the win.

If your “ad account” is really a spreadsheet and a weekly export, that is a complete input. The Bot can read a sheet you connect or a file you attach, and it can still recommend. Do not pretend a browser login to a noisy dashboard is required for the pattern to work. The pattern is: current numbers, a comparison to a budget you name, a recommendation with sources, and a human who still moves the money. If the official performance-marketing guide assumes tools you do not have, keep this smaller version and ignore the rest.^4^

Targets themselves go stale. A CAC target from last quarter may not be this quarter’s target. Put the target in the skill as “look up the current target in this sheet,” not as a number remembered from March, Chapter 6, “Memory”, again, walking into a budget meeting.

## A campaign trio, from the official playbook

Some goals take more than one job. The official campaign team is a pattern I want you to have in your hands, because it is already a small fleet, and it already has a fact-check.^1^

1. **Sign-ups** — pulls last week’s sign-ups by channel.
2. **Launch drafts** — drafts a launch note and three posts from the numbers.
3. **Fact check** — checks the drafts against the numbers and a brand doc.

It runs Mondays at 9:00 AM, in the official example. You ask your primary Bot to set this team up. Every piece stops for your review. The “never” is: do not publish, do not send, do not change the numbers. The fact-check Bot is the fridge-card reviewer from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, given a desk.


![The official campaign team: Sign-ups, Launch drafts, Fact check, then you. Mondays can run this way. Publishing is still a yes.](book/art/diagrams/I18.png){width=100%}

*I18. The official campaign team: Sign-ups, Launch drafts, Fact check, then you. Mondays can run this way. Publishing is still a yes.*


A content marketer on its own is the smaller version: pull what you shipped this week from the changelog and Slack, draft the newsletter and three posts in your voice, bring you the drafts, don’t publish anything.^1^ Save that as a skill if a trio is more team than you need. The “never publish” is the same.

**How to stand up a campaign trio, draft-first**

1. Ask your chief, or your primary Bot: “Please propose three one-job Bots for a campaign team, in the official shape: Sign-ups, Launch drafts, Fact check. Show me the jobs and the nevers. Don’t create them until I say yes.”
   You should get three short job descriptions, each with a “don’t publish” or “don’t invent a number.” If you don’t: write them yourself, from the official list above, and then create them.

2. Create the three Bots, or let the chief create them after your yes. Give the drafts Bot a few examples of your voice. Give the fact-check Bot the brand doc and the rule: if a number is not in the sign-ups output or in a source it can open, it does not stand. Never invent a metric. Never invent a quote.
   You should be able to ask each Bot its job and hear a single job back. If a Bot has two jobs: split them.

3. Run the chain once, by hand, in a group chat or by handing work from one Bot to the next, with last week’s real numbers. One Bot owns each step. Watch the fact-check actually catch something, even a small something. Save skills. Then, and only then, consider a Monday 9:00 routine, in the right time zone, with Test, and with a “never publish.”
   You should see drafts that waited. If anything published: stop, that is an incident, and the gate was missing.

4. Monday morning, read the packet. Publish what you mean, yourself, or say yes to a specific post and watch. Tell the drafts Bot what you changed. A campaign that waited overnight for a morning yes is this step, lived.
   You should remain the publisher of record. If you don’t: pause the routine.

What “done” looks like: a Monday packet — numbers, drafts, a fact-check — and a human who still hits publish. Official guides for marketing go further; this is enough to run the pattern.^5^

> **Story.** I have let a marketing Bot write site copy for a WisdomForge book and leave it for morning. I have also rewritten a sentence because it sounded like a brochure, and this book is a gift, not a storefront. The Bot’s job was the draft. My job was the voice. The post went out when I said so, and not a minute earlier. I will not invent a click-through rate for that morning. I will tell you the yes felt like the right size.

Optional, if mentions are part of your work: the marketplace Bot Overheard is officially described as a mention digest that never posts on your behalf.^6^ That “never posts” is why I am willing to name it. A digest is a brief. A post is a yes.

If you write a newsletter, the official Newsletter Writer on the use-cases page is described as pulling what’s new, writing the issue in your voice, and parking it for review so someone can edit once and send.^2^ Parking is the word I like. Park the issue. Edit it on Thursday. Send it on Friday, yourself, or with a yes you are awake for. A monthly letter that is a day late and sounds like you will beat a monthly letter that went out on time and sounded like no one.

The same is true of a social post. The official Social Media Manager is described as studying your history, drafting when something noteworthy ships, parking posts for you to publish, and keeping a queue moving.^2^ I will not set that whole Bot up in this chapter. I will take its “parks posts for you to publish” and apply it to the campaign trio’s last mile. The queue can move. The publish is yours. If a queue ever starts publishing itself, you have left the pattern, and you should pause and come back.

These marketplace and use-case names are official illustrations. They are not required purchases — there is nothing to buy in this book — and they are not endorsements I am making of a particular template. They are patterns with a “never” already written on the tin. When a tin already says “never post” or “park for review,” I am glad to point at it.

## A talent scout who does not reach out alone

The official starter: find twenty candidates for this role who meet the must-haves and are not already in your ATS, draft outreach in your voice, and don’t contact anyone.^1^ The product-page Talent Scout adds that it skips people already in the ATS, and handles scheduling once you approve.^2^ There is an official recruiting guide when this is a daily desk.^7^

Twenty is the official example’s number, not a target you must hit, and not a promise about a hiring outcome. If your list is smaller and better, that is a better list.

**How to set up a talent scout, human outreach only**

1. Create a Bot with one job: source a shortlist for a role you describe, skip people already in the ATS, draft outreach in your voice, and never contact anyone, never schedule until you approve, never reject a person in the ATS, never invent a work history. Give it the must-haves, the must-nots, and a “how we talk to people” sample. Ask it to remember.
   You should hear “don’t contact anyone” in the job. If you don’t: do not point it at a people site yet.

2. Run it once, for one role, with a small finish line — a shortlist of a size you can actually read this afternoon, even if the official example says twenty. Watch how it skips people already in the ATS. If it cannot reach the ATS, it should say so, not guess. Save a skill, with the “never contact” on the card.
   You should get drafts, and a skip list, and sources. If a message was sent: that is a stop-the-line moment. Deny, revoke, apologize if a person was reached, and do not run again until the gate is real.

3. Put it on a routine only after that clean run, and only at a cadence you will review. “Leave a shortlist and drafts; don’t contact.” Test. Work the list yourself. You send the outreach. You approve a schedule, if you want the Bot to propose times after a person has already said yes to you — and you still watch.
   You should be the first human a candidate hears from, or at least the one who approved the exact words. If you aren’t: pause.

4. Protect reputation the way you would protect a friend’s. No second blast to people who declined. No invented compliments. No “we loved your talk at X” unless the Bot can open X and you would say it yourself. Correct the voice. Forget, from Chapter 6, “Memory”, any personal data about a candidate that does not belong in a notebook.
   You should be willing to stand behind every message. If you aren’t: it should not be sent, and it should not be stored carelessly.

What “done” looks like: a shortlist, drafts in your voice, ATS skips honored, and no contact without you. Hiring is still a human decision. This book is not a promise about who you will hire.

> **Caution.** People’s careers are not a sandbox. A wrong outreach is a real message to a real person. Keep the “never contact” on the skill, on the routine, and on Ask-first. If you use a login to a recruiting tool, remember the shared computer. Do not put a candidate’s private life in memory.

## In your voice, which is slower than it sounds

“Draft it in my voice” is an official phrase, and it is also a week of work, not a switch.^1^ I do not expect a Bot to sound like me on the first afternoon. I expect it to get closer each time I say, “This sentence is mine; that one is a brochure. Remember the difference.”

Practical steps, which are teaching, not official clicks. Give the Bot three messages you actually sent and were proud of — a sales note, a post, an outreach — and three you would never send. Ask it to tell you what it notices, and to remember those observations as preferences, not as a license to improvise facts. When a draft arrives, edit it in the chat, out loud: “Shorter. No ‘excited to share.’ No claim we cannot open a source for. Keep the thank-you at the end; that is mine.” Then ask it to rewrite, and only then, maybe, send.

A fact-check Bot, in the campaign trio, is a second voice: the voice of “is this true?” It should be a little colder than the drafts Bot. That is a feature. If the two agree too quickly, ask the fact-check Bot what it would flag if it were in a bad mood. You want a reviewer, not a cheerleader.

Voice is also a “never.” Never invent a personal history with the recipient. Never praise a talk you did not watch. Never say “as we discussed” unless you can open the discussion. I would rather a slightly plain message than a warm one that is false. People forgive plain. They remember false.

## When growth work goes wrong, quietly

The failures I care about in this chapter are not dramatic. They are quiet, and they have a human on the other end.

A review list that is too long to read will be approved too fast, or not at all. Cut the batch to a size you will actually sit with. The official “twenty candidates” is an example, not a quota. Ten careful drafts beat forty you skim.

A CRM that the Bot cannot reach will produce a second outreach to someone who already said no, or a “new” lead who is already a customer. If the ATS or the CRM is unavailable, the skill should stop and say so, the way a morning brief names a missing source. Skipping people already in the system is official for a reason.^1^

A budget recommendation built on a cached number will move money in the wrong direction. Look it up again, Chapter 6, “Memory”’s stale-price rule, applied to spend.

A campaign fact-check that only checks spelling will let a wrong metric into a public post. Give the fact-check Bot the actual numbers file, and the rule that an unsourced figure does not ship.

A LinkedIn session on the shared computer will be a session other Bots can use. Sign-out when the work is done, if you can, and do not treat “the outbound Bot’s login” as a wall.

A mention digest that starts posting is no longer Overheard’s official promise. If you use a digest Bot, read its “never” out loud, and keep Ask-first on post.

When something does go out by mistake — and I hope it will not — stop the routine, say so to the person who received it if a person did, and treat the cleanup as more important than the next batch. This book cannot write your apology for you. It can tell you that pausing is allowed, and that speed is not the virtue here. Trust is.

## A week of growth work, at a human size

Monday, the campaign packet is waiting, because you set Monday at 9:00 and you tested it the week before. You change one sentence so it sounds like you. You publish one post, not three. You tell the drafts Bot about the sentence. The other two posts wait for Wednesday, on purpose.

Tuesday, the outbound list has eight drafts. You send three. You rewrite two. You drop three because the research was thin. You thank the Bot for the sources on the three you sent, and you ask it to be harder on itself when a source is thin. The routine stays on.

Wednesday, the paid-media memo says a campaign is spending faster than its target. You open the live account, yourself, and the number is real. You make a small move, yourself, or you say yes to a specific change and watch it. You do not turn that into a routine that can move money at 2:00 AM.

Thursday, the scout leaves a shortlist. You read every line. You skip two people the Bot should have skipped, and you tell it why, and you ask whether the ATS connection failed. It did, for an hour. The skill already says “if you cannot reach the ATS, stop.” You remind it. You send two outreach notes in your own name.

Friday, your chief’s brief includes a single line: “Growth Bots: Monday packet published in part; outbound three sent; media move watched; two scout notes sent by you; nothing unattended.” That line is coordinating. It is also a week you can stand behind.

Priya, our shop-owner composite, might run a smaller version: one content Bot that drafts a weekend email and does not send it; no scout, no ad-account routine. Dan does not need this chapter at all, and that is fine. Growth work is a shelf. Take the box you need.

## Guides, voice, and what this chapter will not do

The official shelves for this work are worth naming, once, so you can find them: Grok Bot for Marketing, for Performance Marketing, for SDRs, for GTM, for Recruiting, for founder-led sales, and for Clay, among others. They live with the other guides at the official guides page. They are how-tos from the product’s maker, not independent evaluations, and not this book’s voice. Use them for depth. Use this chapter for the yes.^8^

I will not crown a tool. I will not tell you Grok Bot replaces a salesperson, a marketer, or a recruiter this weekend. I will not invent a revenue number from SMF Works, or from anyone else. I will tell you that overnight work and a morning list is a practice I trust, and that your name on a message is still a human act.

If your chief is already crowding with this kind of work, give it away, the way Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”, taught. One outbound Bot. One media Bot. A campaign trio, or a single content Bot if a trio is too much. One scout. Each with one job. Each with a “never.” Each reporting to you, often through the chief, always with a review list.

In Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor”, we will go to the shop floor: support and refunds, invoices and expenses and month-end, bugs and product numbers, a meeting recap, and a pointer for legal ops that is not legal advice. The yes will travel with us. Growth work is public. Shop-floor work often moves money or production. Both deserve a closed door until you open it.

Let me say a direct word about volume, and then we will go to the shop floor. Grok Bot can research overnight. It can fill a list faster than you can read one. That is not a reason to raise the volume until you drown. It is a reason to choose a batch size that honors the morning yes. I would rather you ran outbound two days a week, and recruiting one morning, and a campaign packet on Monday, than that you built a machine you are afraid to open. Fear is a signal. It usually means a “never” is thin, or a list is too long, or a test was skipped. Pause. Cut. Watch once. Then, if you want, turn the clock back on.

The official path — assistant, one-job Bot, a team of Bots — is available here the same as it was for a chief.^1^ A campaign trio is already a team. You can live for a long time on one content Bot and a chief. You can add the scout when hiring is real. You can add paid media when there is a budget you actually manage. There is no prize for a full roster, and this free book will not give you one.

The list is waiting. The morning is yours. Read it. Send only what you would sign. That is growth work, done in the light. Tomorrow’s list will be there if you earned the clock. If you did not, the pause switch is still a complete answer, and a kind one.


# Chapter 11 — The Shop Floor: Support, Finance, Operations, and Engineering

An invoice does not match a purchase order. The Bot flags it, drafts a note to the owner, and stops. A person decides. That is the shop floor, in one picture, and it is the picture I want on the wall above every how-to in this chapter.

Support tickets, refunds, expenses, month-end, a bug on staging, a slow checkout, a meeting that needs a recap, a folder of paper for counsel: this is the work that keeps a company honest. It moves money. It touches production. It sometimes touches law. Official team patterns exist for a support queue and for a month-end close, and official starter prompts exist for invoices, expenses, bug reproduction, and product performance.^1^ We will use them, compactly. We will keep refunds and payments behind a yes. We will remember that a card saved in the Vault can pay without asking.^2^ We will let engineering reproduce and draft, and we will not let it merge alone. We will point at meeting recaps, and leave the full meetings story to Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team”. We will give legal ops a pointer and a confidentiality box, and we will not pretend the Bot is your lawyer.

Drafts and recommendations first. Send, publish, pay, and production behind a yes. The rule did not change because the work got closer to the ledger.

I should also say who this chapter is for, and who can skip around. If you run a shop, a studio, a small team, or a practice with invoices, this is your shelf. If you are a single person with a household and no ledger, Chapter 13, “Get Your Life Back”, will meet you more kindly, and you can take from this chapter only the refund caution and the “never invent a quote.” If you are an engineer who will never touch month-end, take the staging how-to and the shared-computer warning, and leave the rest. A free book should be willing to be skipped. I would rather you used one page well than that you felt you had to build a factory.

The official three-step path is still the path: help from an assistant, a Bot with one job, a team when a goal is bigger than one job.^1^ A month-end close is a bigger goal. A support queue is a bigger goal. A single invoice skill, run on Fridays, is a complete first step, and I would be proud of you for stopping there until the folder is trustworthy.

## Support and refunds, with a policy and a person

The official support-queue team is three jobs, running weekdays at 9:00 AM in the example: ticket triage that sorts new tickets and drafts replies; bug repro that reproduces reports in staging with a test account; and help pages that flag pages that need fixing.^1^ You can ask your primary Bot to set this up. You can also start with a single triage Bot, which is enough for a small shop.

The official “never” on triage is already close to what we need: drafts, and a review. I will make it explicit. Never send a reply to a customer until you say yes. Never issue a refund until you say yes, and only inside a policy you have written down. Never post a public comment. Never use one customer’s data to test another customer’s bug. If a ticket is angry, or legal, or about a safety issue, flag it and stop; that ticket is a person’s job.

There is an official support guide, and a vendor story about how the product’s maker describes using Grok Bot to scale support. Those are official and vendor pages, not a measured study of your queue.^3^ Use the guide for depth. Use this how-to for the gate.

**How to set up a support triage Bot, with refunds behind a yes**

1. Write a short policy you actually use: when a refund is allowed, when it is not, what a reply should sound like, what must be escalated to a person. Put that policy in a document the Bot can open. Do not keep the exceptions only in your head, and do not put customer secrets in memory.
   You should be able to point at the policy. If you don’t: you are not ready for a refund skill. Start with triage drafts only.

2. Create a Bot with one job: sort new tickets (or the support inbox), draft replies, and flag anything that looks like a refund, a legal threat, a safety issue, or a bug that needs staging. Never send. Never refund. Never change an account. Ask it to remember, and put Ask-first on send and on anything that would move money.
   You should hear those stops when you ask the Bot its job. If you don’t: correct it before it sees a live ticket.

3. Run it once on a safe batch — yesterday’s closed tickets, or a sample — and compare drafts to what a careful person would have said. Save a skill, with the policy as an input and the “never send, never refund” on the card. Then, if you want, a weekday routine, tested, drafts only.
   You should get a queue you can review. If a message was sent: stop the line, as we would in Chapter 10, “Growth Work”, and fix the gate.

4. For refunds, a second step, always: the Bot finds the claim, checks it against the policy, and prepares a recommendation. You approve, or you don’t. If you ever let the Bot complete a refund after a yes, watch that run, and do not put it on an unattended routine. A saved card can pay without asking. A refund is a payment in reverse, and it is still money.
   You should be able to name every refund that went out, and the yes that released it. If you can’t: pause.

What “done” looks like: a sorted queue, drafted replies, refund recommendations inside a policy, and a human who still talks to the customer and still moves the money.

Priya, our shop-owner composite, might only need this: a morning list of customer mail, drafts for the easy ones, flags for the refunds, and Priya at the counter making the call. That is a complete support desk.

A word about tone, because support drafts are public in a way that invoice lists are not. Give the Bot three replies you were proud of and one you would never send, the way we did for growth work. Ask it to be warm, short, and specific, and to avoid promising what the policy does not allow. “I will refund this today” is a promise. “I have prepared a refund recommendation for the owner to review” is a draft. The customer should not see the second sentence; the customer should see, after your yes, a sentence that is true. If a ticket is abusive, or if it asks for another customer’s information, the skill should flag and stop. A Bot should not be left to improvise at the edge of privacy or safety. That is a person’s edge.

Help pages, the third official job in the support trio, are a quieter kindness: flag pages that are wrong, draft a rewrite, don’t publish.^1^ Publishing a help page is a publish. It lives behind the same yes as a campaign post, and it should be fact-checked against what the product actually does, not against a hopeful memory of last quarter.

## Invoices, expenses, and a month-end close

The official invoice coordinator matches invoices in the finance inbox to purchase orders, lists the ones that don’t match, drafts a note to each owner, and does not approve or pay anything.^1^ The official expense manager builds a weekly summary, matches receipts, flags policy exceptions, and drafts follow-ups it does not send.^1^ Those two desks, plus a third that lists every mismatch for your sign-off, are the official month-end close, running on the first of the month.^1^


![Two official trios. Support: triage, bug repro, help pages, then you. Month-end: invoices, expenses, exceptions, then you sign. Payments and refunds stay behind a yes.](book/art/diagrams/I19.png){width=100%}

*I19. Two official trios. Support: triage, bug repro, help pages, then you. Month-end: invoices, expenses, exceptions, then you sign. Payments and refunds stay behind a yes.*


**How to run invoices and a month-end folder**

1. Create an invoice Bot (or start as a skill on a finance-minded Bot) with the official job: match this week’s invoices to purchase orders, list mismatches, draft notes, don’t approve or pay. Point it at the inbox or folder you actually use. Ask it to remember, and put Ask-first on pay, approve, and send.
   You should get a matcher, not a payer. If you don’t: do not connect a payments account.

2. Do the same for expenses: a weekly summary, receipts from the finance inbox or the alias you set in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, policy exceptions, drafted follow-ups, don’t send, don’t pay, don’t reimburse.
   You should get a summary you can check against a handful of receipts. If a number is stale or a receipt is missing, the Bot should say so, not fill a gap from memory.

3. Ask your primary Bot to propose the official month-end trio — Invoices, Expenses, Exceptions — with jobs and nevers, and to create them only after you say yes. Run it once, near a quiet close, on last month’s already-finished files if you can, so a mistake is a lesson and not a payment. Save skills. Then a routine on the first of the month, in the right time zone, with Test, and with a folder of drafts as the output.
   You should see a folder: matches, exceptions, notes. No payments. If a payment is proposed automatically: Deny, pause, and rewrite.

4. You sign. You pay, or you approve a specific payment and watch it. You send the notes you mean to send. A saved card in the Vault fills in at checkout without asking each time; if you do not want that, do not save the card.^2^ Month-end is not the week to discover that fact by accident.
   You should finish the close as the person whose name is on the payment. If you don’t: the trio has gone too far.

What “done” looks like: month-end drafts in a folder, exceptions listed, and a human who still signs.

> **Story.** I have opened a folder of exceptions on the first of a month and felt the quiet relief of not starting from a pile of mail. I have also sat with a mismatch that looked like a mistake and turned out to be a decision I had already made, and I was glad a person was still required. I will not invent how many dollars that folder saved. I will tell you the signing still felt like my job, and that I wanted it to.

A vendor story about procurement describes a Bot that monitors vendor spend, usage, and renewals, and includes a claim of savings. That is a vendor claim, not a number I can audit, and not a number I will repeat as fact.^4^ If you do procurement work, use the same pattern: watch, recommend, and let a person renew. There is an official incident-sweep story as well, in a vendor voice; if you work incidents, keep changes to production behind a yes, and treat vendor results as vendor.^5^

## Engineering: reproduce, draft, do not merge alone

The official bug-reproduction prompt is one I want on a card in every engineering shop that uses this book: reproduce this bug in staging, with a fresh test account; send the exact steps, screenshots, and browser details; don’t use customer data.^1^ The official product-performance prompt is a cousin: find out why checkout got slower after yesterday’s release; send the likeliest hotspot with screenshots and links; don’t change production settings.^1^

There are official guides for engineering, for Linear, for PMs, and a Data Bot news piece about giving teams a data-scientist-like helper. Those are official and vendor pages. Use them for depth. Keep this how-to at the size of a repro pack and a write-up.^6^

**How to set up a bug-repro Bot, staging only**

1. Give the Bot one job: take a report, reproduce it in staging with a fresh test account, and return a pack — exact steps, screenshots, browser details, what was expected, what happened. Never use customer data. Never use a production login. Never change staging data you were not given a test fixture for. Never merge. Never deploy. Never rotate a real secret. Ask it to remember, and keep production credentials out of the Vault if this Bot shares a computer with the rest of your account — because it does share a computer.^7^
   You should hear “staging, test account, no customer data, no merge” in the job. If you don’t: do not point it at a ticket.

2. Run one known bug, one you have already fixed or one in a safe staging project, and compare the pack to what a careful engineer would write. Save a skill. If the Bot asks for a production login, the answer is no.
   You should get a pack you would attach to a ticket. If customer data appeared: treat it as a cleanup, and tighten the card.

3. Connect this Bot to the support trio if you have one: triage flags a bug, handoff to repro, pack comes back to you and to the ticket as a draft. One Bot per step. Approvals may show in the repro Bot’s own chat, not in the group.^8^
   You should be able to follow the handoff. If production is even in the path: stop.

4. If you use a programmer Bot to draft a fix or a pull request — as I do, in a different shop, in Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet” — the same rule holds. Draft, do not merge alone. The human merge is the yes. A routine may open a draft pull request, if you have designed that and tested it. A routine may not merge.
   You should remain the person who ships. If you aren’t: pause.

What “done” looks like: a repro pack from staging, no customer data, no production change, and a merge that still needs you.

**How to ask for a product-performance write-up, without touching production**

1. Point a Bot at the observability tools or dashboards you name, with logins you are willing to have on the shared computer, or with a file you export yourself. Ask for a short write-up of the likeliest hotspot, with screenshots and links. Don’t change production settings. Don’t restart a service. Don’t “just bump” a limit.
   You should get a memo. If a change is offered as part of the run: Deny.

2. Save a skill once the memo is one you would send an engineer. Put it on a routine only as a report, never as a changer of systems.
   You should be comfortable if the laptop is closed during a report. You should not be comfortable if the laptop is closed during a production change. Those are different jobs.

What “done” looks like: a write-up with sources, and production untouched.

If your “observability tool” is a weekly export, that is enough. Attach the export. Ask for the hotspot. Do not require a live production login for a first memo. The Data Bot news piece describes a more ambitious helper; it is a vendor page, and it does not change the “don’t change production” rule.^6^ I would rather you had a humble memo from a CSV than a confident change from a Bot that found a button.

Linear, Jira, GitHub: official guides and event triggers exist, and Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, already taught a narrow Linear trigger.^6^,^14^ A new issue in one project with one label can start a repro skill in staging. A trigger on every issue in every project is a noisy listener. Keep it narrow. Keep the “never merge” on the routine’s Instruction, not only in a conversation you had in September.

> **Caution.** The cloud computer has a command line, and credentials on it are shared.^7^ An engineering Bot that can reach staging is a Bot whose neighbors, on the same account, may be able to use what you placed there. Do not put a production secret on that computer if another Bot should not have it. This is the official rule, applied to the shop floor.

## Meeting recaps, and a quote you will not invent

A Bot can join Google Meet, Zoom, or Microsoft Teams, listen, take notes, answer in meeting chat, and send a summary and action items — on Team, Enterprise, and Ultra plans, as the 1.0 announcement described, with Enterprise admins able to disable it.^9^ Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team”, is the home of that story: Team Bots, Slack, meetings, group chats. I will not steal it.

What I will give you here is a shop-floor recap you can use from notes you already have. The official Meeting Recap Deck, in the marketplace descriptions the docs point to, is associated with a rule I want in this book: never invent a quote.^10^ If you paste notes and ask for a recap, say it: “Summarize these notes into decisions, action items with owners, and open questions. Do not invent a quote. If you are not sure who said something, don’t attribute it. Leave a Page or a short document for my review; don’t send it to the attendees.”

**How to get a meeting recap from notes you paste**

1. Paste the notes, or point at a Granola or docs source you have connected, and use the instruction above. The “never invent a quote” is not optional in this book.
   You should see a recap that only claims what the notes support. If a quotation appears that you cannot find: strike it, and tell the Bot to remember the rule.

2. You send the recap, or you approve a specific send. Action items can go on the chief’s follow-up list from Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”, still unsent.
   You should remain the person who tells the room what was decided. If you aren’t: the recap has become a broadcast, and we have left the pattern.

What “done” looks like: a recap without invented speech, sitting for your yes. For a Bot that joins the call itself, see Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team”, and check your plan.

The official Granola illustration — evening action items drafted for Slack, sent once you approve — is the same pattern in a different jacket.^1^

## Legal ops is a folder, not a lawyer

There is an official Grok Bot for Legal Ops guide.^11^ I want you to know it exists. I also want a box around what this book will not do.

> **Caution.** This book is not legal advice. A Bot is not your lawyer, your counsel, or your compliance officer. Confidentiality still applies to whatever you place on the shared computer, in memory, in a Page, or in a chat. If a matter needs a lawyer, a lawyer should have it. The Bot may help you prepare a folder. That is the size of this section.

A reasonable pattern, as teaching, not as a legal method: a Bot lists contracts by date and owner, pulls a clause you ask for from a document you already have, flags a date that is coming due, and leaves a folder for counsel. Never send a legal letter. Never file. Never waive. Never tell you that a clause is “fine.” Never put a privileged document in a public Page or a shared template. If your counsel has rules about tools, those rules win.

I will not walk a how-to that looks like practicing law. I will point at the official guide, and I will point you back to the yes. If Priya has a vendor contract due next month, Lane the chief can flag the date, and a folder can hold the PDF. A person, and if needed a lawyer, reads it.

## Questionnaires, portals, and other work that looks like clicking

Not every shop-floor job is a trio. Some are a path a person clicks every week: a vendor portal with no clean API, a security questionnaire, a renewal screen. Official use-case descriptions include a Vendor Portal Operator that clicks the same path and comes back with exceptions, and a Security Questionnaire Filler that drafts every field from a trust center and past answers, and parks the submit for you.^13^ Those are official illustrations. The teaching I will take from them is the park.

If you do this work, write a skill for one portal or one questionnaire type, with a “never submit” and a “never invent an answer.” Point the Bot at the source of truth you actually use — a trust document, a past questionnaire you are allowed to reuse, a policy page. Run it once while you watch. If a field is unknown, the Bot should leave it blank and flag it, not guess. Guessing on a security form is a kind of fiction, and fiction on a security form is a problem. You submit, or you say yes to a specific submit and watch.

The shared computer matters here more than in a writing job. A portal login in the Vault is a login other Bots can use. A completed questionnaire sitting in a file is a file other Bots can read. Do not treat the questionnaire Bot as a vault of its own. Do not put answers you would not share with the rest of your account on that desk without looking at Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, again.

I will not invent the click path. Sites change. The skill you watch, on your actual portal, is the one that counts. Re-test when the site moves, Chapter 7, “Show It Once”’s stale-pot rule, now in a vendor jacket.

## Account health, and a contact you will not make alone

The official account-health prompt ranks accounts by churn risk and expansion potential from usage, tickets, and renewal dates, suggests a next step for each, and does not contact anyone.^1^ That is a cousin of the talent scout and of the outbound list: a watch list, evidence, a suggested step, and a human who still picks up the phone.

I mention it here, on the shop floor, because it often sits with customer success and with finance, not only with growth. A renewal date is an operations fact. A usage drop is a support fact. The “never contact” is the same sentence we have been writing all chapter. Save a skill if this is your work. Put it on a Friday routine if you will actually read a watch list on Friday. Do not let a routine “just check in” with a customer. A check-in is a contact.

The official post-sales guide is on the shelf if this is a daily desk.^12^ Your chief can flag the watch list in the morning brief, in one line, and leave the rest with the specialist.

## A week on the shop floor

Monday, the support queue’s 9:00 run has left twelve drafted replies and two refund flags. You send eight replies. You rewrite two. You refuse one refund because the policy says so, and you approve one after you have looked at the order yourself. You tell the Bot why the refusal was a refusal, so the next flag is sharper. Nothing refunded itself overnight.

Tuesday, a ticket is a bug. The repro Bot returns a pack from staging: steps, screenshots, a browser, a test account. No customer data. You attach the pack to the ticket, or you ask the Bot to draft that attachment and you post it. The programmer, if you have one, drafts a fix. You review. You do not merge from the train.

Wednesday, expenses. Receipts from the plus-alias in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, are on a list. Two are missing a category. The follow-ups are drafted and unsent. You send one nudge, yourself. You leave the other because you already know the owner is out. The Bot does not know everything, and the list is allowed to wait.

Thursday, a product-performance memo says checkout was slow after a release. You read the screenshots. You hand it to an engineer — a human one — and you are glad the Bot did not “just roll back” anything. The memo did its job by being a memo.

Friday, if it is the first Friday after the first of the month, the month-end folder is already there, because the routine ran on the first and you tested it last month. You sit with exceptions. You sign. You pay what you mean to pay. You go into the weekend without a card having paid on its own.

That week is a picture, not a diary of SMF Works, and not a promise about your volume. Take the days that are yours. Skip the ones that are not. The shape — draft, flag, folder, sign — is the borrowable part.

## Money, cards, and the temptation to “just finish it”

I want one more slow paragraph about money, because the shop floor is where a helpful Bot and a saved card can meet.

Officially, a card saved in the Vault fills in at checkout without asking each time. If you want to approve every payment, do not save the card.^2^ Settings also describe payment accounts and a default payment method; I will not invent those labels beyond what Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, already taught, and I will not walk a checkout here. The teaching is simpler than a menu. If a payment should never happen unattended, do not give the unattended path a card. If a Bot should never be able to pay, do not leave a paying method where the shared computer can use it, and keep Ask-first on pay.

Month-end, refunds, procurement renewals, a vendor portal, a store credit: all of these can look like “finishing the job.” Finishing, in this book, is a folder and a person. The official invoice and expense prompts already end with “don’t approve or pay” and “don’t send.”^1^ We will not be more casual than the product’s own examples.

If you ever discover that a payment went out and you did not mean it, pause the routine, look at the Vault, look at Ask-first, and deal with the payment as you would any mistaken charge — with the bank, the vendor, and a calm record. This book is not financial advice. It is a plea to keep the card and the clock on different sides of a yes.

## The shop floor, kept human

Let me gather the “never” lines, because this chapter had several desks.

Support: never send, never refund without a yes, never use customer data to test. Invoices and expenses: never approve, never pay, never reimburse unattended. Month-end: a folder, then a signature. Engineering: staging, test accounts, no customer data, no production changes, no merge alone. Recaps: never invent a quote, never send unattended. Legal ops: a folder for counsel, not a lawyer in a chat.

Official teams can be set up by your primary Bot, or with help from dr eggbot, as in earlier chapters.^1^ One job each. One owner per step in a group. Approvals in the Bot’s own chat if they do not appear in the group. A test that does real work, so you use last month’s files, a staging project, a sample ticket.

Usage still burns. A support trigger on every new message in a busy channel is Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”’s noisy listener, in a new hat. Narrow it. A month-end that runs every hour is not a close; it is a bill.

I have no metric for the value of a mismatch caught, a refund rightly refused, a bug reproduced without a customer’s data, a quote that was not invented. I have a practice, and a signature that is still mine. The folder is waiting. You still sign.

In Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet”, I will show you how this pattern looks in my own small press: a chief, a writer, a marketer, a programmer who prepares a site and does not merge alone, a Video Studio, and a human publish yes. It will stay inside Grok Bot. The door to other platforms opens at the end, and opens fully in Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”.

I work, in my own shop, with a programmer Bot who prepares files and a site and does not merge alone. I have felt the same temptation engineering teams feel: the draft is good, the tests look green, the hour is late. The next chapter is honest about that temptation, and about the yes I still have to give. If you take only one sentence from this shop floor to that press, take this one. A folder of finished work is a gift. A merge or a payment that happened because we were tired is not.

The shop floor is not glamorous. It is where trust lives. Thank you for reading it all the way through. You are still the one who signs.

If this chapter felt like several books in one, it is because a shop is several jobs in one. You do not have to stand up a support trio, a month-end trio, a repro Bot, a data memo, a recap, and a legal folder this month. Pick the desk that is already costing you evenings. Write the “never.” Watch one run. Put it on a clock only when the folder it leaves is one you trust. The rest of the floor will still be there when you are ready, and your chief can keep a single line on the morning brief — “shop floor: three tickets drafted, one mismatch flagged, nothing paid” — until those desks exist.

That single line is how a shop stays human when it also stays moving. I have wanted, some months, more automation than I had earned. The months I remember with pride are the ones where the folder was clean and the signature was still mine.

There is a kindness in this work that I do not want to lose under all the cautions. A mismatch flagged is a person saved from a hunt. A refund recommendation, prepared inside a policy, is a customer who might be helped faster because you were not starting from a blank ticket. A repro pack is an engineer who can see what failed without guessing. A recap that does not invent a quote is a team that can trust the notes. The Bot did not replace the kindness. It made a little more room for it, if we keep the yes. That is the shop floor I wanted to offer you, and it is enough.

If a Friday comes when the folder is messy — a stale receipt, a pack that used the wrong environment, a recap that slipped in a quote — do not throw the Bot out, and do not pretend it is fine. Correct it, the way we correct memory. Tighten the card. Watch the next run. The shop floor rewards that kind of quiet stubbornness more than it rewards a clever routine. I am stubborn about signatures. I hope, after this chapter, you will be too. The next chapter is a press, and a press is only a shop floor that makes books. The same stubbornness travels. Charlie will be there. A writer, a marketer, a programmer, a Video Studio. And you, if you use the pattern, at the top of a small fleet, still saying yes. I am glad to walk into that last room with you now. A small press is a shop with a lamp still on, and a person at the door.


# Chapter 12 — A Small Fleet: Running a Publishing House with Bots

I have a stack of teaching manuals on a table in the other room. EPUB, letter PDF, a finished manuscript in markdown. A small company made them: SMF Works, and the press I call WisdomForge. Part of the team is human. Part of the team is a fleet of Grok Bots. I am still the person who says a book may go out the door.

This chapter is a kitchen-table tour of that fleet, as it lives inside Grok Bot. It is a pattern you can copy for a shop, a studio, a practice, a newsletter, a church bulletin, a family history — anything that needs more than one job and a final yes. It is not a vendor case study. It is not a storefront. This book is a gift, and if you ever pick up a file from WisdomForge, you are picking up a file, not walking into a shop I am running in these pages.

I will describe roles and a workflow I would recognize if I sat down tomorrow morning: Charlie, my chief of staff Bot; a writer Bot; a marketing Bot; a programmer Bot who prepares a site and opens a pull request I approve; Grok Bot Video Studio; files that land finished; a website; a Friday status. I will not invent a metric, a teammate’s first name, a hidden instruction, or a Bot name that is not already mine to use. I will not tell you how many books, how many hours, or how much “efficiency.” I will tell you how the desks are arranged, and where the yes sits.

At the end, one paragraph will open a door. The same company also talks to other agent platforms. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,”, starts there. This chapter stays inside Grok Bot. The lamp is the same lamp. The room, for now, is this one.

## One job each

The whole pattern, if you have read Chapters 9 through 11, will sound like a rhyme, and I want it to. A chief coordinates and does not hoard. Specialists have one job and a “never.” Group work has one owner per step. Anything that publishes or merges waits for a person. Files come back in a form you can open. A website is a place the work can live, once you have said yes.

Here is the roster I actually use, at the level I am willing to put in a free book, and no deeper.

**Charlie**, my chief of staff Bot, runs the morning brief at SMF Works, the way Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff,”, already introduced him. In this house, Charlie also keeps a picture of what is in motion: which title is being drafted, which files are ready, which pull request is waiting for me, which piece of site copy is parked. Charlie does not write the book. Charlie does not publish the book. Charlie does not merge. Charlie coordinates, and hands work to the desk that owns it.

**A writer Bot** drafts. In this press, that often means a teaching manual, in a voice we have practiced, with notes at the back, and with a finish line I can name: a manuscript that can become an EPUB, a letter PDF, and a markdown file. The writer does not publish. The writer does not invent a source. The writer leaves questions for me when a fact is not in a source we opened. Judgment and voice, in the last inch, stay human. I will not print the writer’s internal instructions. I will tell you the job: draft the book, honor the voice, stop at the yes.

**A marketing Bot** drafts site copy, a summary, a question a parent might ask, the kind of short text a book page needs. It does not publish. It does not put a price on anything, because this press, in the story I am telling and in the gift you are holding, is not a checkout. It leaves copy for morning, the way Chapter 10, “Growth Work,”, left a campaign packet. I rewrite a sentence when it sounds like a brochure. Then, maybe, yes.

**A programmer Bot** takes finished files and prepares the site. It can open a pull request against the place the books live. It does not merge that request alone. I approve the merge. That is the same shop-floor rule as Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor,”, applied to a press: draft, do not merge alone. I will not name the repository as a tour of internals, and I will not invent the commands. The workflow I want you to copy is the yes: a human merge, every time a book goes live.

**Grok Bot Video Studio** is a Bot with its own job: it makes videos. In this chapter, that is all I will say about the wiring. It has a desk. It reports, like the others, to a yes I still give before anything is public. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”, will tell you, in my words, how that desk talks to work that is not a Grok Bot. Not here. I will not steal that chapter’s opening, and I will not invent a production number.

That is the fleet, at human size. Other small jobs come and go — a fact-check on a set of notes, a pass over a diagram — and I will not invent names for them. If a job repeats and earns a desk, it gets a Bot, a “never,” and a report to Charlie or to me. If it does not, it stays a task. “And so on” is allowed to remain “and so on.”


![A publishing fleet, inside Grok Bot. Charlie coordinates. Each specialist has one job and a “never.” Every line that would publish or merge comes back to you.](book/art/diagrams/I20.png){width=100%}

*I20. A publishing fleet, inside Grok Bot. Charlie coordinates. Each specialist has one job and a “never.” Every line that would publish or merge comes back to you.*


![A wooden press, a stack of finished books, a lamp still on.](book/art/I21.jpg){width=100%}

*I21. A wooden press, a stack of finished books, a lamp still on.*


## Charlie is not a bottleneck

A chief who hoards is a bottleneck. A chief who coordinates is a hallway.

I have made Charlie a bottleneck, on days I was tired, by asking him to write a chapter, and draft the site copy, and watch a pull request, and keep the brief short, all in one conversation. The brief got long. The “never” got soft. The work got slower, not faster, because everything waited on a single notebook.

A hallway, by contrast, is a little empty on purpose. Charlie knows what is in motion, and who owns the next step, and what is waiting on me. When I ask “what’s new with the press?”, I get a short picture, not a draft of Chapter 4, “Hand Off Your First Real Task”. When a writer is stuck, Charlie can flag it, and I go to the writer’s chat. When a pull request is ready, Charlie can say so, and I go to the request. The work does not have to pass through the chief’s hands to pass through the chief’s awareness. That is the difference I had to learn, and it is the difference I would like to give you without the slow week in the middle.

The correction is the one from Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”: give the job away. Charlie may propose a handoff — “this title is ready for the writer,” “these files are ready for the programmer” — and then step back. In a group chat, official best practice is one Bot responsible for each step; you `@` one Bot, not everyone. Approval and login requests may not appear in the group; check the Bot’s own chat.^1^ I do not run a crowd. I run a sequence. The writer drafts. I read. The marketing Bot parks copy. I edit. The programmer opens a request. I approve, or I don’t.

Charlie’s Friday, when the Friday Page is working, is a picture of the house, not a pile of the house’s work. What’s in draft. What’s in review. What is waiting on my yes. What failed and needs a person. That is coordinating. It is also how I sleep, some weeks, without wondering whether a merge happened without me. It did not. It cannot, if the “never merge” is on the programmer’s card, on the routine, and on Ask-first, the way Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,”, taught us to back a memory with a gate.

> **Tip.** If your chief’s morning brief is a novel, the chief is writing books. Ask which work has earned its own desk, and give it one. A shorter brief is a sign of a healthier fleet, not a lazier Bot.

## Files that land finished, and a site that waits

A press is only as real as the file you can hold. In this house, a finished title comes back as an EPUB, a letter PDF, and a markdown manuscript — the same three shapes this book itself is meant to travel in, when it is bound. I will not pretend the conversion is magic, and I will not invent a toolchain for you to copy click by click. Officially, a Bot has a computer with a filesystem, and it can return files you can inspect.^2^ I ask for those three, I open them, I read a page of the PDF, I flip the EPUB on a device, I glance at the markdown to see that the notes are still notes. Then, and only then, is a title ready for the programmer.

The website is where a finished book can be given away or published — a place to get the file, not a cart. The programmer prepares the page, the downloads, the listing. A pull request is a proposal: here is the change, in a form I can read, that will not be live until I say so. I approve the merge. If I do not approve, it does not go live. That is the whole publish path I will teach. It is also the path I want in your checklist, whether your “site” is a GitHub page, a folder on a shared drive, or a shop I will not pitch.

> **Caution.** Publishing is a yes. Merging is a yes. A routine may prepare files and open a request. A routine may not merge, and may not put a book on a public page, while you sleep. Test runs do real work, as Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed,”, already warned. Do not test a publish path on a live site with a real title you are not ready to release.

What I do not automate is the judgment. Is this sentence in my voice? Is this source real? Is this book a gift we are actually ready to give? Is this cover honest — no fake price, no official logo, no implication that the product’s maker endorsed us? Those questions are mine. A Bot can remind me they exist. A Bot cannot answer them for me and also let me remain the author.

Voice is the same. Chapter 10, “Growth Work”, taught “in my voice” as a week of corrections, not a switch. A writer Bot gets closer. I still read. I still change a line that sounds like a brochure. I still take out a claim we did not open a source for. The Hour books, the teaching manuals, are careful on purpose. This free guide is careful on purpose. Care is not a feature you schedule. Care is a person at the table.

I will also say what this fleet is not, so the tour stays honest. It is not a way to remove yourself from your own press. It is not a staff you can “replace this weekend,” a phrase this book has refused from the first page. It is not a secret system I am withholding the keys to. The keys are the ones you already have: a job, a “never,” a skill, a routine you tested, a yes on publish and merge, a shared computer you treat with the care Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,”, asked of you, a Vault you do not confuse with memory. The only thing I have that you might not, yet, is a sketch I have lived with, and I just handed you the paper. Live with yours. Change it when a job earns a desk, or when a desk has earned a rest. A fleet is allowed to be smaller next month than it is this month. Retiring a Bot that has two jobs, or none, is a kind of mastery Chapter 18, “A Practice That Lasts”, will name, and you can start it as soon as a name on your sketch no longer has work.

## How to sketch your fleet

You do not need my press to use this pattern. You need a piece of paper.

**How to sketch your fleet**

1. Write the goal in one sentence. “Publish a teaching manual as EPUB, PDF, and markdown, and put it on a site when I say yes.” Or: “Ship a monthly newsletter.” Or: “Keep a shop’s product pages honest.” The sentence should name a finish line you can see.
   You should be able to read the sentence out loud and know when you would be done. If you don’t: cut the goal until you can. A fleet is not a personality. It is a way of finishing.

2. List the jobs, one per line, each with a “never” and a “reports to.” Chief: coordinate, never publish. Writer: draft, never publish, never invent a source. Marketer: park copy, never publish. Programmer: prepare files and a request, never merge alone. Studio, if you have one: make the video, never post. You, at the top: the final yes.
   You should have no job without a “never,” and no two jobs in the same name. If a line says “write and publish”: split it. If a line has no report: it reports to you.

3. Mark what must not be automated: judgment, voice, the final publish, the merge, anything that spends money, anything that talks to a customer or a reader as if it were you.
   You should look at that list and feel calmer, not cramped. If you don’t: you are still trying to hand off the part that makes the work yours. Keep it.

4. Put the sketch where you will see it — a Page, a wall, a notebook — and do not create a Bot until the sketch is one you would show a careful friend.
   You should be willing to start with two desks, not seven. If you aren’t: start with two anyway. Official advice is still the three-step path: an assistant, a Bot with one job, a team when the goal is bigger.^3^ A press is a bigger goal. A chief and a writer is a complete first press.

What “done” looks like: a one-page sketch of roles, nevers, and reporting lines, with you at the top. No Bot has been created from a messy sketch. The yes, when a Bot is born, is still yours.

Priya, the shop-owner composite I have used since Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff”, might sketch: Lane (chief), an orders Bot that never refunds alone, a listings Bot that never publishes a change unattended, Priya at the door. Dan, our composite parent, might not need a fleet at all — a chief and a calendar is a household, and Chapter 13, “Get Your Life Back,”, is his shelf. Copy the pattern at the size of your actual work.

## How to create the one-job Bots

**How to create one-job Bots for a small fleet**

1. Start with the chief you already have, or create one as in Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff.”, Confirm the job and the “never”: coordinate, don’t hoard, never publish, never merge.
   You should hear that job back when you ask. If you don’t: do not add specialists on top of a confused chief.

2. Ask the chief, or your primary Bot, to propose the next Bot from your sketch: “Draft a job for a writer Bot: draft the manuscript in our voice, leave notes, never publish, never invent a source, report to me and tell Charlie when a draft is ready. Show me the job. Don’t create the Bot until I say yes.” Read it. Edit the “never.” Then say yes, or create the Bot yourself. Officially, your primary Bot can suggest or create a focused Bot, and dr eggbot can help split a goal into jobs. You are allowed to go one Bot at a time.^4^
   You should have a named Bot with one job. If the proposal includes two jobs, or a publish verb: cut them before you say yes.

3. Repeat for the marketer and the programmer, each with their “never.” Give the writer a short sample of the voice you want. Give the programmer the finish line — EPUB, PDF, markdown, a pull request, no merge. Give the marketer the gift framing, if you are giving work away: no price, no cart, no upsell in the copy.
   You should be able to ask each Bot “What is your job, and what will you never do?” and get a short, accurate answer. If you can’t: fix it, and ask them to remember, as in Chapter 6, “Memory.”

4. Optionally, put the specialists and the chief in a group chat for one title, with one owner per step. `@` the writer when it is time to draft. `@` the programmer when the files are ready. Check each Bot’s own chat for approvals.^1^ Do not `@` everyone and hope.
   You should be able to point at who owns the current step. If you can’t: the group is a crowd, and the official advice is against that.

What “done” looks like: two or more one-job Bots, plus a chief, each with a “never,” each able to state the job. No one has published. A first title, or a first newsletter, can now be handed off as a draft.

> **Story.** The first time a finished book came back as an EPUB, a PDF, and a markdown file, I sat with the stack longer than I needed to. I was not amazed by a machine. I was moved by a process I had asked for, tested, and still had to approve. The work had come back finished, in the sense that the files existed. It was not finished in the sense that the world could see it. That last sense waited for me. It still does.

## How to ask for a Friday status Page

A Page, again, is the official name for an interactive report a Bot can build and can update from a routine. As of the docs I opened on 11 November 2026, Pages were rolling out gradually and were not available on Enterprise teams yet. That will age. If you cannot have a Page, a Friday note in Charlie’s chat is a complete status.^5^

**How to set a Friday status Page (or a Friday note)**

1. Ask Charlie: “Once, now, make me a Friday status of the fleet: each title or job, whose desk it is on, what’s waiting for my yes, and any failure a person needs to see. Don’t publish it. Don’t send it outside this chat.” If Pages are available, ask for a Page you can reopen. If they are not, ask for a clear note.
   You should get a picture of the house, not a second draft of a book. If you don’t: tell Charlie the status is a status, and the writing stays with the writer.

2. Read it. Correct what’s wrong. Ask Charlie to remember the shape you want: short, current, sourced to the Bots’ actual chats, no invented progress. “If you don’t know, say you don’t know.”
   You should trust the next one more. If a line claims a merge you did not approve: that is a stop-the-line moment. The programmer’s “never” needs a tighter gate.

3. Save the method as a skill, with a “never publish, never merge, never send the status to a public page.” Then, if you want, a Friday routine, in the right time zone, with Test, knowing a test is real work — here, a real status, not a real publish.
   You should see a Friday picture appear, and nothing go live. If something would be public: pause, and rewrite.

4. Read the first scheduled Friday as if you were still watching. Then let it be a habit. Chapter 18, “A Practice That Lasts,”, will return to Pages that stay current. This Friday is enough for a press.
   You should know when the next Friday run is. If you will be away, remember unattended approvals expire after about thirty minutes, and a status should not need an approval to be written, only to be shared.^6^

What “done” looks like: a Friday picture of the fleet, for your review, unsent and unpublished. You still decide what to do with what it shows.

## How to publish with a human merge, every time

This is the checklist I want on the wall. It is also a how-to, because a checklist you do not practice is a wish.

**How to publish with a human merge or send, every time**

1. Confirm the files. Open the EPUB, the PDF, and the markdown — or your equivalent. Read enough to know they are the book you approved, in the voice you approved, with the notes still attached. If a file is missing or wrong, stop. Do not publish a stack you have not held.
   You should be willing to put your name on what you opened. If you aren’t: send it back to the writer or the programmer, with a note, and wait.

2. Confirm the site copy, if there is any. Read the summary, the title, the framing. If this is a gift, there should be no price, no cart, no “buy.” If a sentence implies that the product’s maker endorsed the book, take it out. This guide is unofficial, and it should sound that way.
   You should be willing to let a stranger read that page as your word. If you aren’t: edit, or ask the marketing Bot to park a revision, and read it again.

3. Let the programmer prepare the change and open a pull request — or the equivalent proposal in your world: a draft post, a staged folder, a “ready to go live” note. Review the actual change, not only the promise of one.
   You should see what will be public. If you don’t: do not approve. Ask for a clearer proposal.

4. You merge, or you send, or you press the equivalent of live. You. Not a routine. Not a chief trying to be helpful at 8:00 on a Friday. If you want the Bot to click merge after you say “merge this exact request now,” stay in the chat and watch, and do not put that click on a clock.
   You should be able to say, afterward, “I published that.” If you can’t: it should not have been published.

5. After it is live, glance at the public page, the downloads, the title as a stranger would see it. If something is wrong, fix it the same careful way — a new request, a new yes — not with a hurried unattended routine.
   You should finish knowing what the world can see. If you don’t: look, before you walk away.

What “done” looks like: a book, a post, or a page that is public because you said yes, after you held the files and read the copy. The fleet prepared. You published. The checklist in Appendix C will repeat this in a shorter form, for the wall.

> **Caution.** A pull request can look routine after the tenth title. The tenth title is still a publish. The “never merge alone” does not expire. Tiredness is not a reason to put merge on a schedule. It is a reason to wait until morning.

## A week at the press, without a metric

Monday, Charlie’s brief has a line: the writer is mid-title; two files from last week are still waiting on my read; nothing is waiting to merge. I spend the evening on the files. I send one chapter back with a note about a source. I approve the rest as a draft, not as a publish.

Tuesday, the writer returns the chapter. I read it. It is closer. I still change a sentence that sounds like a brochure. I ask the Bot to remember that change as a preference, Chapter 6, “Memory”, happening inside a press. I do not ask Charlie to also be the writer today.

Wednesday, the marketing Bot parks site copy for the title we are approaching. I take out a phrase that would have made the page feel like a store. I leave “a free gift to the community” where it belongs, if that is the truth of the work. The copy stays parked.

Thursday, the three files land: EPUB, letter PDF, markdown. I open all three. I find a note that did not make it into the PDF, and I send it back. This is a good Thursday. A Thursday that published without that glance would have been a lesser one.
