Friday, the programmer opens a pull request. The Friday Page — or the Friday note — shows “waiting on your yes.” I am at dinner. I do not merge from the table. Saturday morning, I read the request, I look at the public-facing words, and I approve. Then I look at the live page, as a stranger, and I am glad I waited.

That week is a picture, not a log of a real week I am documenting with dates, and not a promise that your week will be tidy. Some of my weeks are messier. The shape is the gift: a brief, a draft, a park, a stack, a request, a yes. You can take any two of those and already have a press.

## What a small company feels like

I want to tell you what this feels like, without turning it into a testimonial and without inventing a number.

Some weeks, Charlie’s brief is already on the desk, and a writer Bot has left a chapter, and a PDF is waiting, and I spend an evening being an editor and a publisher, which is to say, being a person. Some weeks, I send a draft back because a source was thin, or a sentence was not ours. Some weeks, a pull request sits for a day because I have not had the hour to read it properly, and I would rather a book be late than a book be wrong. The fleet does not resent the wait. The fleet does not get to resent the wait. The wait is the work being honest.

The team is partly human and partly Bot. I will not invent my human teammates’ names, and I will not pretend I do this without other people, when other people are in the room. I will also not pretend a Bot is a person. It is a teammate in the sense this book has used all along: a durable job, a computer, a “never,” a report. The warmth I feel on a morning when the stack is ready is gratitude for a practice, and for a tool, and for the chance to give the work away. It is not a reason to skip the yes.

If you copy this, copy the practice, not the letterhead. Your fleet will have different desks. Your “publish” may be an email, a folder, a church bulletin, a product page. The sketch, the one-job Bots, the Friday picture, the human merge: those four how-tos are the whole copyable press. Official guides about running several teams of Bots exist, including a staff writer’s experimental pattern of channels and boards; they are official or vendor pages, and they are not a requirement that you work the way someone else works.^7^ Work the way your sketch says, and keep the yes.

## A door, not a second house

This chapter was a Grok Bot fleet: a press, a chief, specialist jobs, files that come back finished, a site that waits for a merge I approve. That is the house I wanted to walk you through, and we are at the door.

The same company, SMF Works, also talks to other agent platforms. The workshop has more than one kind of desk in it. I will not start that story here, and I will not borrow the sentence that opens it. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,”, is where that door opens fully, in my words, at the level of wiring I am willing to stand behind, and no further. You can run a complete press with everything in this chapter and never walk through that door. You can also, when you are ready, see how a chief and a studio sit in a larger workshop. Either way, you remain the person at the door.

If you are holding this book as a file you did not pay for, you are already on the far side of a yes I gave, on a path that looks like this chapter. I will not turn that fact into a sales story. I will turn it into a thank you, and into an invitation: if the pattern helps, use it. If a smaller pattern helps more, use that. If you only take the checklist — hold the files, read the copy, merge it yourself — I will have been glad I wrote the chapter.

The stack is on the table. The lamp is still on. You are still the one who says yes. That is a publishing house, at the size of a kitchen table, and it is enough to begin.


# Part 5 — Personal How-Tos
# Chapter 13 — Get Your Life Back: Admin, Family, Bills, and the Mental Load


![A kitchen table in morning light. A folder waiting, a mug, a closed laptop.](book/art/I22.jpg){width=100%}

*I22. A kitchen table in morning light. A folder waiting, a mug, a closed laptop.*


The last few chapters were about work: a chief of staff, a shop floor, a small press that can bring a book back finished. This chapter is about the other pile. The one that lives on the kitchen table. The school form. The bill under a grocery receipt. The email that says a package is out for delivery, and the one that says a subscription has renewed, and the one you meant to answer three days ago.

I want to say this first, kindly, before we hand anything off. A Bot can take a real share of the household admin. It can watch a calendar, sort a night's mail, put the bills in a folder, and make a short list of what needs you. What it cannot do is care in your place. It cannot sit at the table and be the parent, the partner, or the person who decides what this family actually needs. It is a teammate for the mental load, not a replacement for the people who carry it.

If you only take one chapter from this part of the book, take this one slowly. The how-tos are household-scale. They are smaller than a publishing fleet, and they matter more on a Tuesday morning than a lot of what we called work in Part 4. Calendar changes, and anything that emails a school or a person, stay behind a yes. We will go through that in every step that needs it.

## A school form that would have been missed

I will call this reader Dan — a composite, a stand-in for a parent I have in mind. Dan has a household calendar that looks, by Friday, like a map drawn by several people at once. Pickup times. A dentist. A form that was due, or is due, or was due yesterday depending on who you ask. The form itself is ordinary. A permission slip, a health update, a request to confirm a half day. It arrived in email, or it came home in a backpack and was photographed onto a counter, and then the week moved.

Dan is not careless. Dan is busy. The form is the kind of thing a careful person misses because a careful person is already holding three other things.

Here is what I want a Bot to do with a form like that. I want it to find the form in the mail or in a connected calendar. I want it to put the due date on a list I can actually see — a family Page, which is a living report a Bot can build and come back to, as we will set up in a moment. I want it to draft the reply, if a reply is what is needed, and then stop. I do not want it to email the school. I do not want it to change the calendar until I have looked. I do not want a child's full name, or a school's, traveling farther than the people who already need it.

The official product already has a picture of this job. There is a prebuilt Bot in the Marketplace, Tradbot, that “watches your personal email and calendar so school forms, bills, and RSVPs don't slip.” It “drafts the reply, catches the pickup clash, and never sends without you.”^1^ That last clause is the whole method. Draft. Catch. Never send without you.

I am not telling you to install that particular Bot. I am telling you that the job is a real one, and that the official example already stops where I want you to stop. Every careful official example I have opened does the same. Sending, publishing, purchasing, and the rest stay behind an approval.^2^ A school form is not a purchase, but it is a message to a real person about a real child. It stays behind a yes.

The first time you try this, try it on one form, in front of you, not on a routine that runs at dawn. Hand the Bot the email, or a photo of the paper. Ask it to tell you what the form wants, when it is due, and what a complete reply would include. Ask it to draft that reply, and to stop. Read the draft as if a helpful neighbor had written it: grateful, and not finished until you say it is. If a date is wrong, or a name is spelled in a way your family does not use, correct it and ask the Bot to remember the correction for next time. Then send it yourself, or sign the paper yourself. After you have watched that happen once, you can decide whether a Sunday routine should look for the next form. A routine you have not watched is a routine I would not run on a school.

Dan's Tuesday gets a little lighter, not because a Bot became the parent, but because the form is on a list instead of at the bottom of a pile. Dan still reads it. Dan still signs it, or asks a question, or decides it can wait until Thursday. The care is still Dan's. The first pass is not.

> **Story.** I have watched a form like that sit under a receipt. I will not name a child, and I will not name a school. I will only say that the form was ordinary, and that ordinary is how these things hide. A teammate that can put “this is due” on a single page, before the coffee is gone, is a teammate I will make room for at the table.

## The family calendar

If you already connected Google Calendar in Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,” your Bots can read the week the way the official docs describe. One official picture is simple and good: every Sunday evening, a Bot reads the week ahead, flags clashes and packed days, and sends you a short plan for Monday.^3^ That is a work picture, and it is also a household picture. A clash is a clash whether it is two meetings or a pickup and a dentist.

Ask for the household version in plain words. Tell the Bot whose calendars to look at, if more than one is connected, and what “a packed day” means in your house — two events after school, or a late evening, or a morning that starts too early. Tell it to draft a short plan, and not to move anything. Tell it to remember that rule, the way Chapter 6, “Memory,” taught you to ask.

A useful Sunday plan, in my house, is one page and a little kind. It names the days. It names the clashes. It does not narrate. It leaves a space for what I still have to decide. If a school form is sitting in the inbox with a date on it, I want that date on the same page, not in a separate pile.

Pickup clashes are where this gets its value. Two places at 3:15 is not a scheduling puzzle for a Bot to solve by moving an event. It is a family conversation. The Bot can see it. You decide who drives.

> **Caution.** Do not let a Bot change a shared family calendar until you have looked. A moved pickup is not a draft. It is a person standing in a parking lot. Keep calendar changes behind a yes, and keep anything that emails a school, a teacher, a coach, or another parent behind a yes as well.

If you use a different calendar — Outlook, Apple, something a Bot can reach in the browser on its cloud computer — the same rule holds. Chapter 2, “A Teammate with a Computer,” already told you that the computer has a browser for the sites that have no connected app.^4^ The method does not change. The Bot reads. It drafts a plan. You approve the moves.

## How to make a weekly family Page

A page, in Grok Bot, is something a Bot builds for you to read: a report, a tracker, a short living document. It can hold text, tables, and simple controls. It lives in the Bot's Library, so you can come back to it, and a routine can update it.^5^ A routine, as you saw in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed,” is a scheduled run: the Bot does a job at a time you chose, in the Bot's time zone, even if the laptop is closed.^6^

This is a household Page, not a dashboard for a company. Keep it small.

**How to ask for a weekly family Page**

1. Open a chat with the Bot that already watches your household mail and calendar — the one with that single job, if you gave it one in Chapter 3 or Chapter 7.
   You should see the same conversation you have been using for this work.
   If you don’t: create a Bot with one clear job, “household calendar and forms,” and connect only the mail and calendar you mean it to see, the way Chapter 5 walked you through. Do not connect a work inbox to a family Bot unless you have thought about what that mixes.

2. Ask, in plain words, for a page. You can say something like: “Make a weekly family page for the week ahead. Put the days in a table. Flag pickup clashes, forms that are due, and bills that need a look. Leave a short list of decisions for me. Do not change the calendar. Do not email anyone.”
   You should see a card in the chat with the page's title and a way to open it.
   If you don’t: say what the page is for again, and what should be on it. If pages are not in your app yet, they were still rolling out on 11 November 2026, and they were not available on Enterprise teams; update the app and check the live docs, or keep the same list in a file the Bot writes for you.^7^

3. Open the page. Read it the way you would read a note on the table. If something is wrong, tell the Bot in the chat beside the page — the official docs call that panel “Message about,” and the page's title.^8^
   You should see the same page change, not a second copy.
   If you don’t: ask the Bot to edit “this page,” and name the title. Do not start a new page for every correction.

4. When the page looks right, ask the Bot to update it on a schedule you choose — Sunday evening is the official picture; Saturday morning is fine if that is when your house is quiet. Ask it to create a routine that rebuilds or updates this same page, and not to email anyone or change the calendar when it runs.
   You should see a routine with a schedule and a time zone you recognize, waiting for its next run, not running at the moment you create it.^9^
   If you don’t: open the routine and check that it is enabled, that the time zone is the Bot's, and that it points at this page. Chapter 8 has the longer troubleshooting path.

5. Put a “never” in the Bot's memory, out loud, once: “Never email a school, a teacher, or a parent. Never change the shared calendar until I say yes. Never put a child's full name on a page I might share.”
   You should be able to ask, later, “What do you remember about the family page?” and hear that rule back.
   If you don’t: tell it again, and ask it to remember. Memory is not a vault; Chapter 6 is the careful version of that warning.

What “done” looks like: every week, a page is waiting. It is short. The clashes are visible. The forms that would have sat under a receipt are on the same page as the week. Nothing has been sent. Nothing on the shared calendar has moved. What still needs a yes: any calendar change, and anything that would email a school or a person.


![Mail to decisions. Household mail becomes a short list; you still decide what goes out.](book/art/diagrams/I23.png){width=100%}

*I23. Mail to decisions. Household mail becomes a short list; you still decide what goes out.*


*Mail to decisions. The Bot's job is the first pass. Yours is the yes. (Chapter 13.)*

## The household inbox

The official picture for mail is as plain as the calendar one. A Bot can sort the night's mail before you are up, draft replies to the threads that need you, and leave every draft for you to send.^10^ The official starter prompt is almost a household prompt already: sort unread mail into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI; draft the urgent replies; don't send anything.^11^

For a house, I would change the buckets slightly. Urgent: a school, a doctor, a bill with a date, a package that failed. Needs a reply: a person who is waiting. FYI: everything that is only telling you something. Newsletters can go in their own pile; we will use that pile later, when we look at subscriptions.

Ask for a morning digest, not a novel. “What needs me” is a better prompt than “summarize my inbox.” A digest that is two screens long is a second inbox. A digest that is one short list, with the drafts waiting underneath, is a first pass.

If two people in the house want a look, you can ask for a second digest — a version that is safe to share, with nothing private from a work inbox, and with children's details kept to what that other person already knows. Do not point a family Bot at a work account and then forward the digest. The computer your Bots share is one computer, as Chapter 2 and Chapter 5 already said; a login you leave there is, in practice, a login the other Bots can use.^12^ Keep the family Bot's connections on the family side.

> **Tip.** Leave sending off. The official examples do. If you have already set an Ask-first rule for sending, as Chapter 5 asked you to, the Bot will stop even if a prompt is sloppy one morning. That rule is a kindness to your future self.

A draft is only useful if it sounds like you, or close enough that you can edit it in a minute. The first week, you will edit a lot. That is the point of a first week. When a draft is right, you can save the way it was done as a skill, the way Chapter 7, “Show It Once,” taught you. The skill is “how we answer a school thread.” The send is still yours.

## Packages on the way

Package mail is almost a gift to this kind of teammate. It is structured. It has a carrier, a number, a status, and a window. It arrives in the same inbox as everything else, and it is easy to lose between a bill and a newsletter.

Ask the Bot, once, to watch for shipping mail and to put anything on the way into a short list: what it is, if the mail says; who it is from; when it is supposed to arrive; whether it needs you at the door. Put that list on the same family Page, or in a section of the morning digest, so you are not hunting. A routine can refresh it. You do not need a new Bot for packages unless the household inbox Bot is already doing two other jobs well and is starting to blur.

Dean Christensen, who published an independent account of running his inbox and calendar this way, describes a morning digest that includes the agenda, the packages, and the emails that need him.^13^ I mention him because the pattern is a good one, and because it is his published practice, not a vendor claim and not a result I measured. Your digest will look like your house.

> **Caution.** A tracking number is not secret in the way a password is, but a delivery address is a home address. Do not put a family Page on a public link, the kind that needs no sign-in, if it holds where you live. Chapter 18, “A Practice That Lasts,” will come back to how Pages are shared. For now, keep the household Page private.

If a package is late, the Bot can draft a note to the sender or find the carrier's page in the browser. You still send the note, if you send one. You still make the call, or you wait for Chapter 14, “Errands That Finish,” where I will show you how a Bot can place a US phone call — with you on the card before it dials.

## Bills and the sticky admin

Some household work is sticky. A bill that needs a login. An insurance notice. A form that is not a school form, but might as well be, because it has a due date and a portal and a password you set three years ago. This is the work that sits in a browser, and it is exactly the work a Bot with a computer can help with, if you let it go slowly.

The method is a folder and a list, not a Bot with your bank password in the chat.

**How to keep a bills folder**

1. Pick one Bot for household money-admin — bills, insurance notices, the sticky forms. Give it that one job, and do not also make it your cheerful grocery Bot.
   You should see a Bot with a clear name and a clear job in the sidebar.
   If you don’t: create one, the way Chapter 3 taught you, and write the job in a sentence: “Find bills and insurance notices, put them in a folder, and make a list of what needs me. Do not pay. Do not send.”

2. Tell it where the mail lives, and where you want the folder. A folder on the Bot's computer is fine; a folder in a connected Drive is fine if that is where you already keep this. Ask it to file the notices it finds, and to make a short list: who it is from, how much if the amount is in the mail, when it is due, and what it wants you to do.
   You should see a list you can read in one sitting, with links or files you can open.
   If you don’t: ask it to show you the folder, and to try one bill you name, so you can watch.

3. Put the secrets in the right place. A login for a utility or an insurer belongs in the Vault — the locker your Bots can use without seeing the secret — or in a Secure Form at the moment of sign-in, never in the chat.^14^
   You should see the login in Settings, under Vault, not in a message.
   If you don’t: do not paste the password into the chat to “just get it done.” Stop, and use the Vault or a Secure Form, as Chapter 5 described.

4. Decide, before a due date is close, how payment works. A card saved in the Vault fills in at checkout without asking you each time. If you want to approve every payment yourself, do not save the card.^15^
   You should know, before you walk away, which of those two you have chosen.
   If you don’t: open Settings, then Vault, and look. If a card is there and you do not want unattended payments, remove it. Chapter 17 will say this again, because it is easy to forget.

5. For any bill the Bot can only reach in a browser, stay nearby the first time. Some sites will ask for a code, a CAPTCHA, or a new login. The Bot should hand those steps to you, not try to get around them.^16^
   You should see a request for you to take over the computer, or a card that asks for a code, not a password in the chat.
   If you don’t: take over the computer and finish the human step yourself. Then tell the Bot to continue.

What “done” looks like: a folder that is current, and a list that is honest. “Due Thursday, I have not paid it” is a good line. “I paid it” is a line you write after you have paid it, or after you have approved a payment you meant to approve. The Bot does not get to decide that a bill is handled because the portal was confusing. What still needs a yes: every payment, and every message to a utility or an insurer.

> **Caution.** This book is not financial advice, and it is not a promise about money. People have published reviews of Bots helping with bills and insurance. Those are reviews, not results I can stand behind, and I will not retell them as facts.^17^ A Bot can find a notice, gather a file, and draft a letter. You still read the letter. You still decide what is true.

If a bill needs a phone call, wait for Chapter 14. Calls are available in the US, as the official docs describe, and they start with your agreement and a card that shows who will be called.^18^ They are a real tool. They are also easy to rush. We will not rush them here.

## Subscriptions you may have forgotten

The subscriptions are quieter than the bills, and that is why they last. A trial that became a monthly charge. A newsletter you do not open. A tool you used for a season. The official product already has a name for the job. In the 26 August 2026 plans announcement, a “digital declutterer” “audits email, Drive, and paid subscriptions around the clock” and “only discards or unsubscribes if you say so.”^19^ On the official use-cases site, a Subscription Cleaner “collates receipt and newsletter mail, suggests what to kill, and unsubscribes what you approve.”^20^

Notice the last words. Suggests. What you approve. If you say so. That is the method I want you to keep, even if you never touch those prebuilt Bots and only ask your household Bot to do the same work.

Ask for a list, not an action. “Show me the paid subscriptions you can find in my mail for the last year. For each one, tell me the name, the approximate amount if a receipt shows it, and when it last charged. Recommend what I might cancel. Do not cancel. Do not unsubscribe. Do not discard.” Then you sit with the list. Some of those charges are ones you want. Some you had forgotten and are glad of. Some you will cancel yourself, or you will tell the Bot, one at a time, to draft the cancellation or to prepare the unsubscribe — and you will confirm.

> **Caution.** Canceling is not always a single click, and it is not always fair. Some companies make it hard on purpose. A Bot can draft the request and find the page. You still read the page. You still confirm. Do not give a Bot a standing instruction to “unsubscribe from everything I don't open.” That is how you lose something you did mean to keep.

A quarterly pass is enough for most houses. Put it on a routine after you have watched it once by hand. The first run is a conversation. The later runs are a list you already know how to read.

The same Bot can, if you want, make a complementary list of the newsletters and the clutter — the mail that is not charging you, but is wearing you out. The same rule holds. It suggests. You approve. It does not discard a folder of old mail because a prompt was broad.

## A careful sweep of your name

There is a quieter job I want to handle carefully, because it is easy to over-promise. Your name and address appear on sites that collect and resell personal information — data brokers, as they are often called. Dean Christensen, in the same independent account I mentioned earlier, describes using Grok Bot to look for those listings, and he describes using the official Do Not Call Registry as well. He has written about what he found and what he got taken down, and about running the pass again on a quarterly rhythm.^21^ Those results are his, from his published practice. They are not a guarantee, and they are not mine to present as a typical outcome. Some of those companies are hard to deal with. Some will put a listing back. A sweep is not a miracle, and I will not sell it as one — not that this book is selling anything.

If you want to try the work, try it this way.

Ask the Bot to search for your name and the city you are willing to name, and to make a list of listings it finds, with links. Tell it, clearly, what not to include: a child's name, a previous address you do not want repeated, a number you do not want in a page. Ask it to draft, for each listing you choose, the removal request the site itself asks for. Then you read the request. You send it, or you submit it, or you decide that this particular broker is one you will handle another way. You do not give the Bot a standing “remove me everywhere” that can talk to a site you have not seen.

For the Do Not Call Registry, use the official government site, and read the page yourself. A Bot can open a browser and help you find the right place. The submission is yours. I will not invent a click-path for a government form that can change.

> **Caution.** This is not a privacy panacea, and it is not legal advice. A removal request is a letter, not a wand. Keep the list of what was sent, with dates, on a private page or in a private folder. Do not share that page with a public link. And do not ask a Bot to pretend to be you on a site that is already in the business of collecting you.

A quarterly routine is reasonable once you trust the process — Christensen's rhythm is a sensible one, as a published practice, not as a rule. The routine makes a new list. You still approve every request.

## The mental load, and an honest list

I have been talking about forms and bills, which are objects. The mental load is not an object. It is the running list that lives in a person's head: the form, the birthday, the prescription to refill, the email that would only take a minute, the thing you said you would do and have not done. People come to a tool like this because that list is heavy. Some of them come because following through is especially hard, for reasons that are their own.

I want to be careful here. This book is not medical advice. I am not a clinician, and a Bot is not a treatment. I will not tell you that a routine will fix a diagnosis, and I will not borrow other people's reviews as if they were results.^22^ What I can tell you is more modest, and I think more honest. A teammate that starts the first pass — that puts “this is due” on a page, that drafts the reply, that reminds you at a time you chose — can make follow-through more possible. The yes is still yours. The starting is not only yours.

That is why I want the last how-to in this chapter to be a list that is honest about limits.

**How to keep a “what I would have forgotten” list**

1. Ask the household Bot for a short, standing list: the things that have a date, a person waiting, or a consequence if they slip. Tell it to take items from the family Page, the morning digest, and anything you add by hand. Tell it to cap the list — ten items, say — so it never becomes a second life.
   You should see a short list, dated, with the next due item at the top.
   If you don’t: ask it to show only what is due in the next two weeks, and to drop what is done when you mark it done.

2. Tell it the limits, and ask it to remember them. It will miss things. Mail goes to other addresses. A form comes home on paper and never becomes a photo. A date in memory goes stale; official guidance is to look changing numbers up again, and not to treat memory as a source of truth.^23^
   You should hear those limits back when you ask what it remembers.
   If you don’t: write them again, plainly. “If you are not sure it is still true, look it up or ask me. Do not invent a due date.”

3. Decide what the Bot may do when it notices something. It may add the item to the list. It may mention it in the morning digest. It may not email a person, and it may not nag you every hour. A routine once a day, or twice, is plenty. Noisy triggers burn usage, as Chapter 8 warned, and they also wear a person out.
   You should see a routine you recognize, not a Bot that messages you all afternoon.
   If you don’t: open the routine and slow it down. Turn down notifications for that Bot if the alerts are the problem, the way the official settings allow.^24^

4. Once a week, you look at the list. You cross off what is done. You add what the Bot could not have known. You forgive the rest, or you move it.
   You should feel, after a few weeks, that the list is a partner and not a judge.
   If you don’t: shorten it. A list that makes you feel worse is too long, and that is not a failing in you.

What “done” looks like: a list you trust enough to look at, and a Bot that has not been asked to become your conscience — not an empty list. The mental load gets a teammate. It does not get a new boss. What still needs a yes: anything on that list that would email a person, and any routine that would nudge you more often than you agreed.

> **Tip.** If you live with someone who also carries this list, share the family Page with that person in a way that stays private — not a public link — and let the list be a shared table, not a report one of you is graded on. The Bot is not the third person in the marriage, or in the house. It is a page on the table.

## A habit Bot, kept humble

There is room, after the admin, for a smaller kind of teammate: a Bot that helps a household habit along. Groceries for the week. A simple chart on a Page. A short family note that is not a newspaper and not a performance. I have seen people ask for all of that, and some of it can be sweet.

Keep it humble. If the habit touches health — sleep, food, medicine, mood — this book is not a clinician, and the Bot is not one either. Use a Page and a routine if they help you remember. Do not ask a Bot for a diagnosis, a dose, or a plan that belongs with a professional you trust. Chapter 14 will pick up the errands that go with a household — the shopping run, the call, the return — and the same humility will travel with us.

I want to add one practical kindness about the shape of this small team. In Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff,” and in Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet,” I asked you to give each Bot one job. The household is not a second company, but the same habit helps. A Bot that watches the family calendar and the school mail can also, if the house is small, keep the package list and the morning digest. When you add bills and logins and a saved card, I would rather you made a second Bot for that money-admin, not because separate Bots are a security wall — they are not; they share one computer — but because a clear job is easier to watch, and easier to pause, and easier to talk to when something feels off.^25^

If your work and your home share a person — they do; that person is you — try not to let them share a Bot. A morning brief for SMF Works is a different voice, and a different set of apps, from a Sunday plan for a family. Mixing them is how a work thread ends up on a family Page, or how a school form ends up in a digest you later share with a colleague. Two Bots, two jobs, one computer you still treat carefully: that is the household version of the fleet.

You do not need a Bot for every corner of a life. One household Bot, one bills Bot if the first job is already full, and a Page that is current on Sunday, is already a lot of “get your life back.” The rest is care, and care is still yours.

When you are ready, we will leave the table and go run a few errands. The trip you plan and do not book unsupervised. The gift with a Pay button that waits. The phone call, if you are in the US, with you on the card before anyone is dialed. That is Chapter 14. The yes goes with you.


# Chapter 14 — Errands That Finish: Travel, Shopping, Calls, and Returns
Chapter 13 was the kitchen table: the form, the bill, the list that lives in a person's head. This chapter is the errands that grow out of that table. A trip you want to plan. A gift you want to send. A call to a business, if you are in the United States, about a table or an order. A return that needs a label and a box.

The method does not change. The Bot does the first pass. It brings you options, a card, a transcript, a packet. You still say yes — and on the errands in this chapter, the yes is often a real button, with a real total, or a real phone on the other end. We will go slowly enough for that.

I want to start with a small scene, because this chapter has a button in it that I would like you to remember.

> **Story.** A gift, ready to go. The Bot has found the thing, and it has put the facts on a card: the store, the item, where it ships, what you will pay with, the total. Because it is a gift, there is a row for that too — who it is for, your message, whether a gift receipt is included, and when it arrives.^1^ The pay button sits there and shows the total. It waits. Nothing is charged while it waits. You can choose Cancel, and nothing is charged then either. The errand is almost finished. The last inch is yours.

That waiting button is the spirit of this whole chapter. Plan the trip; do not book it unsupervised. Shop up to the card; you pay, or you don't. Let a Bot dial only after you have seen who it will call. Let a return packet be a packet, and you submit it if a submission is required.

## A trip folder of options, not tickets

The official starter prompt for travel is one I would almost hand you as a gift by itself: find the flights, with the constraints that matter — no red-eyes, a hotel near downtown — and “send me the two best options; don't book anything.”^2^ The official use-cases site has a Travel Coordinator that “compares flights and hotels to your rules, confirms before booking, and drops itinerary plus calendar.”^3^ I like the first of those sentences better than I like any software that books while you are making dinner. Confirms before booking is the right idea. “Don't book anything” is the rule I want you to keep until you are looking at the options with your own eyes.

Dean Christensen, in the independent account I already mentioned in Chapter 13, describes travel planning the same way: a trip, planned, without the booking.^4^ That is his published practice, and it matches the official starter. It is also mine.

A trip is a bundle of decisions that look like facts. Dates. Airports. A hotel that is near the thing you came for, or near a person. A budget you have not always said out loud. If a Bot books the first reasonable flight, it has not finished an errand. It has made a decision you cannot easily undo, with someone else's money and someone else's Tuesday morning. So we will make a folder, and we will fill it with options, and we will not call the folder a ticket.

**How to make a trip folder of options, not tickets**

1. Open a chat with a Bot that can use a browser, and give it the trip in plain words: where, when, who is going, what “good” looks like, and what is out of bounds. Include the official kind of constraint — no red-eyes, a hotel near a neighborhood you name — and add your own. Then say, clearly, “Send me the two best options. Don't book anything.”
   You should see a short comparison, with links, prices as they stood when the Bot looked, and a reason each option fits.
   If you don’t: ask it to show its sources, and to try again with a tighter constraint. If a site blocks the Bot or asks for a login, the Bot should hand that step to you, not go around it.^5^

2. Ask the Bot to put the options in a folder — on its computer, or in a Drive you already use — and, if you want, on a small page: two flights, two hotels, a short list of open questions. Ask it to date the prices, because prices move.
   You should be able to open the folder and see the same options, with links you can click yourself.
   If you don’t: ask it to show you the folder path, and to add a screenshot of each option as it appeared.

3. Tell it the “never,” and ask it to remember: “Never book a flight, a hotel, a car, or a ticket until I say yes, in this chat, to a specific option. Never enter a payment card for travel unless I am looking at the card with you.”
   You should hear that rule back when you ask what it remembers about travel.
   If you don’t: say it again. Put an Ask-first rule on purchasing, if you have not already, the way Chapter 5 taught you.

4. When you have chosen, you book — yourself, on the airline's site or the hotel's, or you sit with the Bot and approve one specific option, one specific total, with you at the screen. If a calendar entry is needed after the booking is real, the Bot can draft it. You still decide when it goes on a shared calendar, for the same reason a pickup move stayed behind a yes in Chapter 13.
   You should have a confirmation you can open without asking the Bot to remember it.
   If you don’t: get the confirmation into the folder yourself, then ask the Bot to file it, not to recreate it from memory.

What “done” looks like: a folder you can hand to a person you are traveling with, and not a ticket you did not mean to buy. What still needs a yes: the booking itself, and any event that would go on a shared calendar. After the trip is booked, the same Bot can keep the itinerary and the confirmations together, and can watch the mail for a gate change or a hotel notice. The watch is useful. The unsupervised book is not.

If you are traveling with someone, the folder is a better gift than a forward of twenty emails. You can ask the Bot to make a version that is safe to share — times, places, confirmation numbers, and not the card that paid. You still look at that version before it goes to anyone. A shared family calendar, as we already said in Chapter 13, does not get a new week of events until you say yes. A hold is not a booking, and a booking is not a hold. If a Bot uses those words, ask it to show you the confirmation, or the lack of one, before you relax.

Prices move. That is why I asked you to date them. A “two best options” from Sunday night is a starting point on Wednesday, not a promise. When you are ready to look again, ask the Bot to check the same two options and one alternative, and to tell you what changed. Then you look. This is one of the places official guidance about memory is more than a philosophy: numbers that change should be looked up again, not trusted because they were right two days ago.

Some trips are not flights. A train, a long drive, a hotel for a family event, a reservation that is really a table — the folder method still holds. Options, constraints, a date on the price, and a “don't book” that you have not watered down. If the errand turns into a phone call for a table, that is the next section but one, and the same “never” about unsupervised commitments goes with you.

> **Caution.** Do not save a card in the Vault for a Bot that also plans travel, if you do not want a card to fill in at checkout without asking.^6^ A trip is an easy place to move too fast. The folder is the product. The booking is a later, human step.

## A shopping run that stops at the card

Shopping with a Bot is one of the more finished official flows I have opened, and it is worth taking in the official order, because the cards are the method.

You ask a Bot to find something to buy. It can reply with product cards. You can open a product and see its photos, its options, a “Why it fits,” and a “Where to buy,” and you can buy it from there. On desktop, each card also has “Buy now,” or it says “Out of stock.” If a product you open is sold out, you can choose “Notify me,” and the Bot sets up a routine that tells you when it is back.^7^ That routine is a Chapter 8 kind of routine: it does real work when it runs, so you only want it for a product you still want.

When you are ready to buy, the Bot sends an order card. The official card holds the store, the items, “Ship to,” “Pay with,” and the “Total.” If you are sending a gift, a “Gift” row shows who it is for, your message, whether a gift receipt is included, and when it arrives. You check those facts. Then you choose the pay button, which shows the total, or you choose Cancel. If you cancel, nothing is charged. Once the order is placed, the card shows what you paid with. If the order card expires, you ask the Bot to check the price again. Only a Bot's owner can pay its orders.^8^

I want you to hear the gift row twice. It is a kindness, and it is also a checklist. The name. The message. The receipt. The date. If any of those is wrong, you do not press the button that shows the total. You say so, and the Bot corrects the card, and the button waits again.

**How to run a shopping errand that stops at the order card**

1. Ask the Bot, in plain words, for what you want: the thing, the size or color if you know it, a budget if you have one, and whether it is a gift. If it is a gift, say who it is for and what should not be in the message.
   You should see one or a few product cards, not a long essay.
   If you don’t: open a product and look at “Why it fits” and “Where to buy.” If nothing fits, tell the Bot what was wrong and ask it to try again. Do not ask it to buy from a card you have not opened.

2. If a product is out of stock and you still want it, choose “Notify me” only if you are willing to get a real routine for that item. If you are only browsing, do not set the routine.
   You should see, if you opted in, a routine you can find later and pause.
   If you don’t: ask the Bot to show you the routine, or to not set one.

3. When you are ready, let the Bot send the order card. Read every row: the store, the items, “Ship to,” “Pay with,” the “Total,” and the gift row if there is one.
   You should see a total that matches what you meant to spend, a ship-to you recognize, and a payment method you meant to use.
   If you don’t: choose Cancel. Nothing is charged.^9^ Then tell the Bot what to correct, and wait for a new card. If the card has expired, ask the Bot to check the price again, and read the new card as if you had never seen the old one.

4. Check how the payment will be made before you press anything. If a card is saved in the Vault, remember that a saved card can fill in at checkout without asking you each time.^10^ If you have not saved a card, the official docs say the Bot first asks for one with a Secure Form; a card you do not save there gets no order card, and the Bot asks you to approve the exact total in the chat instead.^11^ Either path is fine, if you chose it on purpose.
   You should know, before you pay, which path you are on.
   If you don’t: Cancel, and go look at Settings, then Vault, as Chapter 5 and Chapter 17 describe. Do not press Pay in order to find out.

5. If the card is right, and you are the Bot's owner, choose the pay button, which shows the total. If it is not right, or you are not ready, choose Cancel.
   You should see, after a payment, the card show what you paid with.
   If you don’t: do not ask the Bot to “try again” until you have checked your email and the store for a confirmation. A second Pay is how people buy a thing twice.

What “done” looks like: a confirmation in the folder, and a Pay button you either pressed on purpose or did not press — not a package on the porch that surprises you, unless the surprise was the point, and even then, you were the one who said yes. What still needs a yes: the Pay button itself. Cancel if the card is wrong.

I want to sit with the “Ship to” row for a moment, because it is the quiet one. A gift that arrives at your house when it was meant to arrive at someone else's is a small comedy. A gift that arrives at an old address, or at a workplace you did not mean to use, is less funny. The official card shows “Ship to” for a reason. Read it. If the Bot has contact info in the Vault — the Vault can hold shipping and billing addresses, as the official docs describe^20^ — check that the address on the card is the one you meant for this errand, not the default that was convenient last time. For a gift, I would rather type the address myself, once, than trust a memory of a house I have been to.

The same care belongs on “Pay with.” If a default payment method is set, the official docs say that is the method Grok Bot pays with first. Defaults are useful, and they are also how the wrong card pays for a household gift. If you are not sure which method is default, Cancel and go look, then come back to a fresh card.

Household shopping — groceries, the refill, the thing that ran out — can live on the family Page from Chapter 13 as a short list. The Bot can draft the list from what you tell it, or from a habit you have already agreed on. When the list becomes an order, you are back on this chapter's card. A grocery order is still an order. The total is still on the button. There is no special grocery exemption from reading the card, and I say that with a smile, because I have wanted there to be one at the end of a long day.


![Order-card flow. A product becomes a card; you still press Pay or Cancel.](book/art/diagrams/I24.png){width=100%}

*I24. Order-card flow. A product becomes a card; you still press Pay or Cancel.*


*Order-card flow. The Bot can get you to the card. Only the Bot's owner can pay, and only after reading it. (Chapter 14.)*

> **Tip.** A Bot can also send one order card for a batch of payments — the official docs describe a batch of up to 500 card payments, paid with a saved card, with one approval covering payments for 24 hours.^12^ That is a power-user tool, and a household almost never needs it. If a Bot offers you a batch, read it as slowly as you would read a single gift. One approval is still an approval.

Wallets such as Link and X Money are named in the official Vault docs as payment accounts you can connect, and there is a default payment method that Grok Bot pays with first.^13^ I will not walk you through a wallet's own screens, because those are the wallet's, and they change. If you use one, read the card anyway. The card is the moment.

## A call with a written “never”

A Bot can call a business for you — the official examples are booking a table, or asking about an order — and then tell you how it went. Phone calls are available in the United States only, from the Grok Bot desktop and phone apps.^14^ If you are not in the US, this section is one you can skip, or read for later, and no part of the rest of the book depends on it.

I am going to be plain about the terms, because they are the official terms, and because a phone is a person on the other end.

The first time, the Bot asks you to agree to the calling terms, including that you will only call US businesses and people who have consented to hear from you. You choose Accept or Decline. Before each call, the Bot shows who it will call; you choose Call or Cancel.^15^ That second card is not a formality. Read the name. Read the number. If it is wrong, Cancel.

While the call runs, its card shows the status and a live transcript. You can choose “End call” to hang up. When “Take over” shows, you can choose it to join the call yourself; the card says “Connecting you…” and then “Transferred to you.” If the other side asks something the Bot can't answer, a card shows the question and how long you have to respond; you type in “Your answer” and choose Send, or you choose Skip. If the Bot wants to give the call more details while it is live, it asks you first.^16^

When the call is over, the card says “Ended,” and the length. You can open the card to read the transcript and play the recording. On desktop, a “Call actions” menu also has “Copy transcript,” “Export transcript,” and “Download recording.”^17^

The official docs also say, in a quieter place, that the reader is responsible for who is called.^18^ I will say it here, as a friend. A Bot with a phone is a powerful errand. It is also a way to reach a person who did not ask to talk to software. Stay with the official terms. Stay with businesses and people who have consented. Stay with the card that shows who will be called, every time.

**How to place a call with a written “never”**

1. Write the “never” first, in the chat, and ask the Bot to remember it. Mine would read something like this: “Never call a person who has not consented. Never call a number I have not seen on the card. Never call a school, a medical office, or a government office unless I have named that exact office in this chat and approved the card. Never give out a password, a card number, or a Social Security number. Never agree to a payment on the call. If you are unsure, end the call and ask me.”
   You should be able to ask “What are your calling rules?” and hear that back.
   If you don’t: write it again. Do not place the first call until it is remembered.

2. The first time you use calling, read the terms the Bot shows. Choose Accept only if you agree, including the official limit: US businesses and people who have consented to hear from you. If you do not agree, choose Decline, and do not proceed.
   You should see the terms, and a clear Accept or Decline.
   If you don’t: do not try to skip the terms. If calling is not available, you may not be in the US, or the feature may not be on this plan or this app; do not invent a workaround.

3. Tell the Bot the errand in one sentence: “Call this restaurant and ask for a table for four at 7 on Friday,” or “Call this store and ask where my order is.” Then wait for the card that shows who it will call.
   You should see a name and a number you recognize, and a Call or Cancel choice.
   If you don’t: Cancel, or do not choose Call. Ask the Bot to show you how it found the number. Do not let it dial a number you have not seen.

4. If the card is right, choose Call, and stay nearby. Watch the live transcript. If the other side asks something the Bot cannot answer, you will get a card with the question; answer it, or Skip, or Take over and join the call yourself. If anything feels off, End call.
   You should see the status and the transcript move in real time.
   If you don’t: End call, and look at the card. A call you cannot watch is a call I would not let run.

5. When the card says “Ended,” open it. Read the transcript. If you need the recording, download or export it from the desktop menu. File it with the errand, if the errand needs a record.
   You should have a transcript you can read without calling the Bot's memory a source of truth.
   If you don’t: ask the Bot to show the call card again. Do not rely on a summary if the exact words matter.

What “done” looks like: a transcript of a call you approved, to a number you saw, about an errand you meant — not a reservation you cannot find, and not a person who was surprised to be called. What still needs a yes: the Call button on the card that shows who will be called, and any commitment made on the line.

Let me make the restaurant example a little more concrete, without pretending I can promise you a table. You tell the Bot the name of the place, the day, the time, and the number of people. You have already written the “never.” The Bot shows you a card with a number. You recognize the place, or you look the number up yourself, and then you choose Call. You stay in the room. The transcript starts. If the restaurant asks a question the Bot cannot answer — a high chair, an allergy, a name for the reservation — a card appears, and you have a little time to type. If you would rather just talk, you take over, and the card tells you when you are on the line. When it ends, you have a transcript. If a reservation was made, you look for the name, the time, and the party size in those words, and you put them on the family Page, yourself or with the Bot filing what you approved. If a reservation was not made, you have a record of why, and you can decide whether to try another time or another place. The point is not that a Bot is charming on the phone. The point is that you were present for a commitment, or you were present for the lack of one.

The same shape works for “where is my order,” which is often a better first call than a reservation, because the commitment is smaller. You still read the number. You still stay nearby. You still end the call if the other side asks for a payment or a password. And you file the transcript with the package list from Chapter 13, so the morning digest and the call agree.

I will not walk you through calling a medical office, a school, or a government agency as a worked example. You can see why in the “never” I wrote above. If you ever decide that a particular office should be called, you name that exact office, you approve that exact card, and you take over more readily than you would for a restaurant. This book is not medical advice, and it is not a guide to dealing with an agency. It is a method for an errand, with you on the line when the errand matters.


![Phone-call flow. Terms, then a card, then a live call you can take over, then a transcript.](book/art/diagrams/I25.png){width=100%}

*I25. Phone-call flow. Terms, then a card, then a live call you can take over, then a transcript.*


*Phone-call flow, as the official docs describe it, for US calls from the desktop or phone app. You see who will be called before anyone is dialed. (Chapter 14.)*

> **Caution.** A call is not a place to store a secret. If a business asks for a password, a card, or an account number, take the call over, or end it and do the step yourself. Do not type a password into the ordinary chat so the Bot can “just tell them.” Chapter 5 already asked you to keep secrets out of the chat. The same rule holds while someone is listening.

## A return packet

Returns are less of a single official button than shopping is, and I will not invent a feature that I have not opened. What I can tell you is the method that follows from tools you already have. The Bot can find the order in your mail. It can open the store's return page in the browser. It can fill a draft of the form, and put the policy, the deadline, and the next step in a small packet — a folder, or a short page. If a label has to be printed, or a form has to be submitted, you do that, unless you have approved a specific submission the way you would approve a send.

**How to make a return packet**

1. Tell the Bot what is going back: the store, the item, the approximate date, and why, if why matters for the policy. Ask it to find the order in your mail, and not to start a chat with the store until you have seen the packet.
   You should see the order: what it was, what you paid, when it arrived, and a link to the store's return page if the mail has one.
   If you don’t: give it a confirmation number, or a photo of the receipt, and ask it to try again. Do not let it email the store in order to “just find out.”

2. Ask it to read the return policy on the store's site, in the browser, and to write down, in a folder: the deadline, whether a label is provided, whether the return is free, and what condition the item has to be in. Ask it to screenshot the policy as it appeared.
   You should have a packet you can open, with a date and a source, not a memory of a policy.
   If you don’t: open the policy yourself, and drop the page or a photo into the same folder. Then ask the Bot to file what you gave it.

3. If a form is required, ask the Bot to draft it, and to stop. Read the draft. Correct it. If the form has to be submitted on the site, you submit it, or you sit with the Bot and approve that exact submission. If a label has to be printed, you print it.
   You should hold a label, or a confirmation, that you can put on a box.
   If you don’t: do not assume the return is in motion. A draft is a draft. A printed label is a step you can see.

4. Put the “never” on this Bot, too: “Never submit a return, never start a pickup, and never charge a restocking fee to a card, until I have approved that exact step.”
   You should hear the rule back.
   If you don’t: say it again before a deadline makes you hurry.

What “done” looks like: a box you can drop off, or a pickup you approved, with a packet that would still make sense if the Bot were not there to explain it — not a hope that “it handled it.” What still needs a yes: the submission of the form, a pickup on the calendar, and any message to the store.

A good packet, if I can be fussy for a minute, has four things I can put my hand on: the original order, the policy as it appeared on the day you read it, the draft or submitted form, and the label or the confirmation. If a deadline is close, the deadline is on the first page of the packet, in a size you can see without coffee. If the store wants the item in a certain condition, that sentence is in the packet too, in the store's words, not only in a paraphrase. Paraphrases are how people argue with a policy that was clear on a page they can no longer find.

Some returns are a drop-off; some are a pickup; some are a box you mail. The Bot can tell you which kind the policy describes. You still have to do the kind. A pickup in particular is a commitment at a time of day, and it belongs on the family calendar the same way a school form did: drafted, then approved, then on the calendar, not the other way around. If a carrier needs a number at the door, that number is in the packet, on paper or on your phone, not only in a chat you might not have open.

If the store declines the return, the packet is what you will be glad you kept. The Bot can draft a follow-up. You still send it, and you still decide whether the follow-up is worth your Tuesday.

If a return needs a phone call, you already have the calling how-to. The “never” about payments and passwords travels with you. If it needs a follow-up in mail, you already have the household inbox from Chapter 13: the Bot drafts, you send.

## The errand and the evening

I want to close this chapter the way I would close a good errand day: with the bags in the hall, and nothing surprising on a card you did not see.

You can ask a household Bot to keep a short errands list on the same family Page from Chapter 13 — the trip folder that is still options, the gift whose Pay button is waiting, the return whose label is printed and not yet dropped off. That is plenty. You do not need a Bot for every store. You do not need a call for every question a website would answer.

The limits are the same ones I will spend a whole chapter on in Chapter 17, and they already belong here. A saved card can pay without asking. Approvals from work that started without you expire after about thirty minutes, so a routine that wants a yes at an hour you are asleep will not finish that step while you sleep.^19^ The computer is shared. A shopping Bot and a bills Bot are not two vaults. They are two jobs on one desk.

And the gift, one more time, because I meant the story. The card waits. The total is on the button. You can press it, or you can press Cancel, or you can close the laptop and decide tomorrow. The errand is ready. The last inch is still yours.

In the next chapter we leave the household and go back to other people: Team Bots, Slack, Microsoft Teams, meetings, and group chats. The yes will have company. It will still be a yes.


# Part 6 — Mastery
# Chapter 15 — Bots on Your Human Team: Team Bots, Slack, Meetings, and Group Chats
The last two chapters were a household: a form, a bill, a gift whose Pay button waited. This chapter is other people. A Bot that is not only yours. A thread in Slack, or in Microsoft Teams, where a teammate can mention a Bot the way they would mention a colleague. A meeting the Bot can join, take notes in, and come back from with a summary. A group chat where several of your own Bots share one outcome, with one owner for each step.

Grok Bot 1.0, which was released on 11 November 2026, made this the center of the product's own story. The official news called it “multiplayer teammates”: you can share Bots and bring them into Slack, Microsoft Teams, and video calls. Bots, the post said, can follow a discussion, bring information from earlier conversations into a meeting chat, and keep track of decisions.^1^ That is a warm picture. It is also a place where it is easy to mix up three quiet questions, and I want us to keep those questions on the table: whose apps is this Bot using? Whose computer is it on? Whose usage is this reply drawing from?

I will call this reader Alex — a composite, a stand-in for a mid-career professional I have in mind, trying Team Bots at work. Alex does not work at a company I will name. Alex has a team, a Slack, a weekly meeting, and a reasonable hope that a Bot might remember last week's decision so the meeting does not have to. We will get Alex there by the official path, and we will not skip the questions.

> **Story.** A meeting is underway. The Bot is in the call, admitted by the host, taking notes. Someone asks, in the meeting chat, what the team decided last week. The Bot can bring that earlier conversation into the room, the way the official 1.0 picture describes, and it can keep track of what is decided today.^2^ I like that picture. I also like the quieter official sentence that sits beside it: the Bot will not share your credentials in a meeting.^3^ The memory is a gift. The credentials stay home.

## A Bot the team can share

A Bot is either personal or a Team Bot. Officially, you share a Team Bot the way you would share a doc: with your Cursor team, your whole organization, or only certain people and groups. Sharing does not make a copy. Each person's chat with a Team Bot is private.^4^ That last sentence is one I want you to hear twice. The owner cannot read your chat. You cannot read the owner's. Managers cannot read them either. What the Bot learns for the team is a different stream, and it waits for an approval, which we will come to.

Team Bots are available on Team and Enterprise plans, for members who have Grok Bot.^5^ The official 1.0 news says the same: Team Bots on Team and Enterprise.^6^ If you are on an individual Premium, Super, or Ultra plan, this chapter's Team Bot sections are ones you can read ahead, or skip until your team has a plan that includes them. Meetings, which come later in the chapter, have a slightly wider gate: Team, Enterprise, and Ultra.^7^

A Team Bot always runs in the cloud. A Bot that runs on your own computer cannot be shared.^8^ If you have been experimenting with local-computer work from the official settings, that Bot stays yours. The one you share is a cloud teammate.

**How to create or share a Team Bot**

1. If you are creating one for the team, choose “+” in the sidebar, then “Create a Bot,” and give it a name. Tell it, in a sentence, what it should help the team with. Follow its setup steps for apps, secrets, skills, and files. Only add keys and files you are happy for everyone you share it with to use. Until you publish it, it is a draft only you can see.
   You should see a Bot that is still a draft, with a job you recognize.
   If you don’t: you may not be on a Team or Enterprise plan, or the app may need an update. The official docs say to choose “+” and look for “Create a Bot” and “Browse shared Bots”; if “Browse Team Bots” is not there, you are not on a Cursor team, or the app needs an update.^9^

2. When the Bot says it is ready to share, choose “Publish.” Teammates can then find it. If you are sharing a Bot you already have, you can do that instead; sharing does not duplicate it. Before you share, check its apps, secrets, skills, and files, because they work in every teammate's chat.^10^
   You should see a published Bot, with a “Share” control.
   If you don’t: look at the top right of the Bot's chat for “Share.” If it is disabled and the official message is that only team admins can share Bots, an admin has set “Bot sharing” to “Admins only.” Ask an admin to share it, or to change the setting.^11^

3. In “Share this Bot,” under “Who has access,” choose your team to share with everyone on the team; or, if your team is part of an organization, the organization; or “People you invite” for only the people and groups you add. Limiting access asks you to confirm with “Limit access.” The owner and Managers can always use the Bot. Search under “Add people or groups” to add someone as a Collaborator, or open a person's role and choose “Manager” if they should also be able to change how the Bot works and share it. “Copy link” gives you a link you can send. To stop sharing, the owner can choose “Only you,” and confirm with “Remove.”^12^
   You should see a list of who has access, and roles you recognize.
   If you don’t: do not send a link until the “Who has access” line matches what you meant. A Team Bot's link opens the same Bot for everyone; it is not a template, which would give each person their own copy.^13^

4. Tell teammates how to find it: “+,” then “Browse shared Bots,” then “Add” to keep it in the sidebar. On desktop they can also press Cmd/Ctrl+K and choose “Browse shared Bots,” or open a link and choose “Start a chat.” On a phone, “+,” then “Browse shared Bots.”^14^
   You should be able, as a teammate, to add the Bot and start a private chat with it.
   If you don’t: confirm you are on the team it was shared with, and that you have Grok Bot. If a teammate still cannot see it, the owner's share list is the first place to look.

What “done” looks like: a Bot the team can talk to, each in a private chat, with a job and a share list you chose on purpose — not a copy of your personal Bot, and not a window into anyone's conversation. What still needs a yes: the Publish, the share list, and any key or file you are about to add.

> **Caution.** Only add keys and files you are happy for everyone you share with to use. The official docs say this at the moment of setup, and I will say it at the kitchen table. A Team Bot is not a place to leave a personal login, a customer's file, or a card. Sharing a Bot is also not a security boundary in the other direction: a public template link, which Chapter 18 will treat carefully, copies configuration, not your computer — and you still strip secrets before you share.^15^

## What it learns, and what it does not show

Each person's chat is private. What the Bot learns for the team is not a back door into those chats. Officially, a Team Bot keeps team memory and skills that everyone who uses it can benefit from. When a teammate teaches it something for the team — a better way to do a task — the Bot suggests the change instead of saving it straight away. Suggested memory and skill changes wait until the owner or a Manager approves them, under “Review learnings” in the Bot's details. Each suggestion says what it does, with “Approve” and “Reject.” Changes the owner or a Manager makes in the Bot's main chat apply right away. What the Bot learns about you personally stays between you and the Bot. It is not saved for the team, and the Bot does not save anything about you personally for the team.^16^

That is a good design, and it is one you should still treat with a little care. If you would not put a sentence on a shared team wiki, do not teach it to a Team Bot as a team learning. The owner will see the suggestion. Personal instructions — how you like a draft, what to call you — can stay in your own chat, as personal memory.

If you are the owner, make “Review learnings” a small weekly habit, the way you might review a pull request. Approving a better way to file a status update is a gift to the team. Approving a memory you have not read is how a Team Bot quietly changes its mind. Rejecting is allowed. It is not unkind. It is the same yes we have been practicing, pointed at what the Bot will remember for everyone. The official 1.0 news described this as a Bot that becomes more useful as it works with different people, and as a place where a better way to complete a task can benefit everyone who uses it.^50^ That is the point of a Team Bot. It is also the reason the approval sits there.

A Team Bot also has settings and analytics in 1.0 — officially, a way to “manage what a Team Bot knows and see how your team uses it.”^51^ I will not invent a tour of those screens. If you are an owner, open them, look at what the Bot knows, and take off what should not be there. Chapter 6 is still the chapter about memory. The team version of that chapter is: the same humility, with more people in the room.

## Whose apps, whose computer, whose usage

This is the section I asked you to keep on the table. The official Team Bots page is the authority, and I am going to follow it closely, because a paraphrase is how these three questions get tangled.^17^

A Team Bot uses the apps and secrets its owner added. Those work the same for everyone who chats with it. In your own chat with a Team Bot, it can also use your apps. It asks first. The card says “Allow,” then the app name: “Allow once” for this time; “Always allow,” which has a menu for this Bot or for every Team Bot; or “Skip.” In Slack channels, threads, and group chats, a Team Bot uses only its owner's apps, and still asks before it uses your accounts. A connected account acts as the person the Bot is answering. One teammate's connection is never used for someone else, unless a Manager shares it as a shared connector.

A shared connector is a deliberate exception, and it should feel like one. The owner or a Manager sets it up under the Bot's details, under “Setup,” then “Service accounts.” The choices are “Use individual accounts,” “Share your account,” or a separate account that is the Bot's own, when the app supports that. “Share your account” means everyone who uses the Bot acts through your account, including to make changes. The official docs are blunt: connect the app for yourself first, and only share an account that holds nothing sensitive or private. A team admin has to turn on “Allow Shared Logins” first.^18^ I would leave shared logins off until you have a reason, and a clean account, and an admin who has thought about it.

Which computer? In your own chat with a teammate's Team Bot, the Bot usually works on your Grok Bot computer — your files, the sites you are signed in to there. Sometimes it uses a separate computer just for that conversation, for example when your privacy mode is stricter than the owner's. A Slack DM with a Team Bot also works on your own computer. In Slack channels, threads, group DMs, and group chats, a Team Bot uses one shared computer of its own.^19^

Whose usage? A Team Bot reply uses the Grok Bot usage of the person it is answering. That is true in your own chat, in Slack, and in group chats. Your own limits apply. The owner's usage pays for the owner's own messages, and for Slack posts from apps or workflows that are not linked to a Cursor account. A reply to someone in Slack who has not linked an account, or who is not on the team, uses the team's usage.^20^ Chapter 17 will talk about the shared weekly pool. For this chapter, the practical sentence is: if you mention the Bot, the reply is on your usage. Mention it on purpose.

Approvals follow the same “person it is answering” rule. In your own chat, you answer the cards. In a Slack thread, the Bot posts a “Review an action” card with “Allow once,” “Deny,” and “View details,” and only the person who asked can answer it. In a group chat, only the person whose message the Bot is answering can answer.^21^

> **Tip.** If you want every Team Bot to ask you again about your apps, the official path is Settings, then General, then Team Bots, and “Clear” next to “Clear connector preferences.”^22^ That is a kindness after a season of “Always allow” that has gotten ahead of you.

## Slack, and Microsoft Teams

A Team Bot can come into Slack, and it can come into Microsoft Teams. In both places, the official idea is the same: it acts for the person who asked, uses their usage, and asks before risky actions.^23^ I will give you the official setup, not a guessed click-path, and I will tell you where an admin has to help.

For Slack, the owner or a Manager adds the Bot after it has been shared. You open the Bot's details, or choose “Manage” and look under “Channels.” You find “Bring to your team's Slack” or “Slack,” and choose “Connect.” If it says “Awaiting admin approval,” a Slack workspace admin needs to approve the app. Once it says “Connected,” teammates can message the Bot directly or mention it in channels. In a channel thread or a DM, the Team Bot acts for the teammate who mentioned it. It can hand work to that person's own Bots, and the replies come back into the same thread. A personal Bot, by contrast, does not hand work to other Bots from a channel thread.^24^ Link your Slack account so the Bot knows who you are: Settings, then General, then Team Bots, then “Link.” If you message the Bot before you link, it replies with steps.

Who can talk to it in Slack depends on who the Bot is shared with. If it is shared with your organization, any full member of the Slack workspace can mention it in channels and threads, even without a linked account or a seat on your team — they are told, once per thread, that some features will be limited, and a DM still needs a link first. If it is shared only with your team or with certain people, only those people. Slack guests, Slack Connect channels, and other Slack workspaces are never included.^25^ That last sentence is one for the person who is about to share a Bot with “the organization” and then forget that the workspace is larger than the team.

For Microsoft Teams, a team admin turns Microsoft Teams on first, on the Grok Bot page of the team dashboard, under “Shared Bots,” with “Enable” next to “Enable Microsoft Teams,” signing in with a Microsoft Teams or Global administrator account and accepting the permissions Microsoft shows. Then the owner or a Manager connects the Bot from its details, under “Bring to your team's Microsoft Teams,” and a Microsoft Teams admin approves it in the Teams admin center, under “Teams apps,” then “Manage apps,” then “Pending approval.” Microsoft does not notify them, so you send them the link the dialog shows. Once it is approved, setup can take up to an hour and finishes on its own. The first time you message the Bot, it replies with a “Link Account” card that only you can see.^26^ If any of those doors is closed — Teams not enabled, the Bot still a draft, a declined approval, a policy that blocks custom apps — the official Microsoft Teams page has a table of what the message means, and I will not invent a fourth door.^27^

> **Caution.** In a channel, a Bot can be mentioned by more people than you are picturing. Share narrowly until you have watched it work. And remember the 1.0 safeguard, in the official wording: in shared spaces, Bots “respect each person's permissions, ask before taking sensitive actions, and check whether information is appropriate to share in a public channel.”^28^ That is a vendor description of a safeguard, and it is a good one. It does not replace your judgment about what belongs in a public channel in the first place.

## A Bot in the meeting

A Bot can join your Google Meet, Zoom, or Microsoft Teams calls. It listens, takes notes, answers questions in the meeting chat, and sends you a summary and action items when the call ends. Meetings are available on Team, Enterprise, and Ultra plans. Enterprise admins can turn Meetings on or off.^29^

**How to invite a Bot to a meeting and admit it**

1. Paste the Google Meet, Zoom, or Microsoft Teams link into your chat with the Bot, and ask it to join and take notes.
   You should see the Bot prepare to join, and then an approval card.
   If you don’t: open the Bot's details. If it has not joined a meeting before, “Add to meeting” under “Suggested” drafts a question asking the Bot how to add it.^30^ Confirm that your plan is Team, Enterprise, or Ultra, and that an Enterprise admin has not turned Meetings off.

2. Approve the card when the Bot asks.
   You should see the meeting appear under “Upcoming” in the Bot's details, with a countdown, or “Taking notes” once it is in the call.^31^
   If you don’t: look at the card again. If you decline, the Bot will not join. If the card expires, ask the Bot to try again — and stay with the next step, because a host still has to admit it.

3. When the meeting starts, the host admits the Bot. It joins as “Bot name (your first name's Bot) - transcribing,” or, for a Team Bot, “Bot name (Team Bot) - transcribing.”^32^ If you are the host, this is your moment. If you are not, tell the host, before the meeting, that a Bot will be waiting in the lobby, and that you asked it to be there.
   You should see the Bot in the call, with that transcribing label.
   If you don’t: check that the host has admitted it, and that the link you pasted is the link the meeting is using. A Bot that follows your calendar can also join scheduled meetings on its own; you can still Cancel a scheduled one from “Meeting actions,” or Quit one it is already joining.^33^

4. Decide, before or during, whether the Bot should talk. “Disable Interactivity” keeps it listening and taking notes, without answering in the meeting; the row shows “Muted.” You can turn interactivity back on while the meeting is still scheduled; once the Bot is joining or in the call, you quit and add it again to turn it back on.^34^
   You should know, before a sensitive discussion, whether the Bot is muted.
   If you don’t: mute it from “Meeting actions” while you still can, or Quit and add it again, muted, if the meeting has already started.

5. To talk to the Bot during the meeting, address it by name in the meeting chat. In Zoom, a private chat message to the Bot gets a private answer. A request from a conference-room device counts as coming from the room, not from one person. If someone asks for something the Bot held back, the Bot tells you who asked and offers to send it. It will not share your credentials in a meeting.^35^
   You should see answers in the meeting chat only when you meant the Bot to be interactive, and never a credential.
   If you don’t: Quit the Bot from the meeting, and deal with the request yourself.

6. When the call ends, look in the Bot's own chat. The Bot sends a summary and action items there. If you have Pages, a longer report goes on a page, with the link in the chat. You can ask anytime for the summary or transcript of a past meeting. Meetings use the settings of the person who started them.^36^
   You should have a summary you can read, and a page if Pages are available to you.
