   If you don’t: ask the Bot for the summary or the transcript by the meeting's name or time. Do not rely on memory if the exact decision matters; ask for the transcript.

What “done” looks like: a summary of a meeting you invited the Bot to, admitted (or had admitted), and can still read — not a Bot in a meeting no one agreed to admit, and not a credential that leaked into a chat. What still needs a yes: the approval card before the Bot joins, the host's admit, and any request the Bot held back and offers to send.


![Meeting journey. A link, a yes, a host’s admit, notes, a summary.](book/art/diagrams/I27.png){width=100%}

*I27. Meeting journey. A link, a yes, a host’s admit, notes, a summary.*


*Meeting journey, from the official meetings documentation. The host admits the Bot. You still say yes to the card. (Chapter 15.)*

A Team Bot can join meetings when its team allows meetings. On Enterprise plans, admins turn meetings on or off for everyone's Bots with “Meetings” under “Bot Capabilities” on the Grok Bot page of the team dashboard. When it is off, Bots cannot join or read meetings, and a Bot that was scheduled to join tells you meetings are no longer available.^37^ If you are Alex, and the Bot does not join, this setting is a kinder first guess than “the product is broken.”

## A group chat with one owner per step

A group chat is for when several of your Bots need one shared outcome and visible handoffs. You can also add a Bot a teammate shared with you, but not alongside Bots that run on your own computer.^38^ The official docs describe creating one: “New chat,” or Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+N, or the command palette, or, on a phone, “+,” then “New Group Chat.” In the “To:” field, you add two to six Bots.^39^ That range is the official one, from the chat documentation I opened. I will not add a number on top of it.

The official best practice is the one I want you to keep: make one Bot responsible for each step. Type “@” and select a Bot when one teammate owns the request. Mention several only when the request genuinely needs each of them. Use “@everyone” sparingly. The docs even give a kickoff you can learn from, which I will quote as official, not as a set of names I am inventing for you: “@Researcher gather the source material and link every claim. @Writer turn the findings into a launch draft. @Reviewer check the draft against the sources and list only blocking issues. Do not publish anything.”^40^ One owner per stage. A “do not publish” at the end. That is the method.

**How to run a group chat with one owner per step**

1. Start a new group chat, and add only the Bots this outcome needs — two at the small end, and not more than the official six, and fewer if fewer will do. Every Bot reply counts toward your usage, including replies in a group and messages between Bots, so fewer Bots means fewer replies.^41^
   You should see a group with a generated name you can edit, and the Bots you chose.
   If you don’t: on a phone, use “+,” then “New Group Chat.” If you cannot add a teammate's Team Bot beside a Bot that runs on your own computer, that is the official limit; use Team Bots together, or personal Bots together, as the docs allow.

2. In the first message, name the shared outcome, name the owner of each step with “@,” and write the “never” — “Do not publish,” “Do not send,” “Do not pay” — in the same message.
   You should see each named Bot pick up its step, and the others wait.
   If you don’t: “@” the Bot that should be working, and tell the others to wait. If they start talking to each other more than you want, tell them, in the group, “Don't message other Bots. Ask me first,” and ask each to remember it. There is no switch for this; the official docs say so, and then give you that instruction.^42^

3. Watch where the approvals go. Approval requests, secret and login requests, and email or Slack drafts do not appear in a group. They live in the Bot's own chat. Bot-to-group handoff messages are text-only.^43^
   You should open the working Bot's own chat when a step needs a yes, a secret, or a draft.
   If you don’t: you may think the work is stuck. It may be waiting for you in a different conversation. Look there before you repeat the request in the group.

4. If you want replies to stay in the group, say so there: “Reply here in the group. Don't message me privately about this.” To make it a lasting rule, ask each Bot to remember: “Remember: in group chats, reply in the group.”^44^
   You should see the work stay where the team can see it.
   If you don’t: repeat the rule, and check the Bots' own chats for a side conversation.

What “done” looks like: a shared outcome you can point to, with one owner per step, and with every send, post, pay, delete, or publish still behind a yes you gave in the right chat — not six Bots talking to each other until the weekly pool is thin, which Chapter 17, “Safety, Privacy, Money, and What to Do When It Breaks,” will also warn you about. What still needs a yes: those actions, which live in the Bot's own chat, not in the group.


![People and Bots in a thread. One owner per step; approvals in the Bot’s own chat.](book/art/diagrams/I26.png){width=100%}

*I26. People and Bots in a thread. One owner per step; approvals in the Bot’s own chat.*


*People and Bots in a thread. You “@” one Bot at a time. Approvals, secrets, and drafts wait in that Bot's own chat, not in the group. (Chapter 15.)*

## A tag on X, and a chat on X

Two more doors, both official, both easy to over-hope.

You can tag @bot in a post or a reply on X to hand that post to your Grok Bot as a task. @bot answers on X with a short reply confirming that your Grok Bot has the request. The work, and its results, stay in that Bot's conversation in the Grok Bot app; @bot posts only the confirmation on X. The first time, X sends you a notification that tagged requests start work automatically and use your Grok Bot usage.^45^ You need a Grok account with your X account connected, Grok Bot access on that same account, and at least one Bot of your own. Bots that someone shares with you cannot take tagged requests.^46^

You also need an X account based in a region where tagging is available. Officially, as of the documentation I opened on 11 November 2026, tagging @bot works for X accounts based in most countries, and it is not yet available for accounts based in Australia, the United Kingdom, the European Union, Iceland, Liechtenstein, or Norway. It goes by the country X has on record for your account; your current location and any VPN have no effect. If X lists only a broader region, or no country at all, tagging is not available yet either, and @bot does not reply.^47^ If you are in one of those places, this is not a broken Bot. This is a regional gate, and it may change; check the live docs.

You can also chat with Bots in X Chat. The official initiative documentation notes that in X Chat, a Bot can follow up on a conversation in its own words.^48^ I will not invent a tour of X Chat's screens. If you use it, the same rules travel with you: a send is a send, a post is a post, and a tagged request that starts automatically is a request you should be willing to have started.

## The meeting after the meeting

Alex wanted a Bot that already knew last week's decision. Officially, that is part of what 1.0 was for: a Bot in a video call, in the chat, bringing earlier conversation into the room, keeping track of decisions, learning how the team works.^49^ You can have that, on the plans that include it, if you invite the Bot, if the host admits it, and if what it learned for the team is something an owner approved.

You also have, in the same official breath, the safeguards: permissions, a question before a sensitive action, a check before something is shared in a public channel. Use them. Add your own “never” for the rooms that need one. And when the summary arrives in the Bot's chat, read it. A decision is not a decision because a summary said so. A decision is a decision because the people in the room meant it, and you can still find it in a transcript.

I want to add a practical kindness about when not to bring a Bot. Not every meeting needs one. A one-to-one that is really a conversation, a gathering that is confidential by agreement, a room where someone has asked that a Bot not be present — those are rooms I would leave alone. Officially, the host admits the Bot; unofficially, as a person, I would tell the room first. “I have a Bot that will take notes, if that’s all right” is a short sentence, and it is a respectful one. If the answer is no, the answer is no. The product will still be there on Thursday.

The same is true of Slack. A Team Bot in every channel is a noisy way to burn a weekly pool, and it is a noisy way to live. Start with one Bot, one job, one channel or one thread, and watch whose usage the replies land on. If a teammate is surprised by a Bot's presence, the share list is too wide, or the introduction was too thin. Alex, my composite, does not have to get this perfect on the first Monday. Alex has to get it official, and kind, and reversible: a Bot you can remove from a sidebar, a connection you can revoke, a meeting you can Quit.

At SMF Works, I use Team Bots the way I use the rest of this product: for a job I can name, with a yes I have not given away. I will not invent a roster of teammates, and I will not invent a metric for how many meetings a Bot has joined. I will only say that the meeting I trust is the one whose summary I still read, and whose transcript I can still open. The Bot in the chat that already knows last week's decision is a good colleague. The colleague who publishes a decision I have not agreed to is not a colleague I want, however early it arrived in the morning.

The next chapter leaves the human team and looks at a different kind of span. Grok Bot doesn't just span machines. It spans agent platforms. I will say that in the words I said it in, and I will not invent the wiring.


# Chapter 16 — Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms
I want to tell you, in the words I would use at the table, how the work actually runs when it leaves a single Grok Bot and talks to the rest of the workshop.

Charlie, my chief of staff Bot, also manages SMF Works’ Hermes Fleet and OpenClaw Fleet, communicating with them via CLI, and Grok Bot Video Studio works with several Hermes Agents via webhook to produce videos that the Hermes agents submit scripts for. So Grok Bot doesn’t just span machines, Grok Bot spans agent platforms.

That is the whole technical claim I am going to make about our wiring. I am going to spend this chapter making sure you can hear what it means, and what it does not mean. I will tell you what Hermes Agent is, and what OpenClaw is, at the level their official sites support. I will tell you what Grok Bot officially gives us — a command line on a shared cloud computer, and a webhook that can start a routine. I will tell you the pattern you can take home, if your work already lives on more than one of these platforms. I will not invent the commands Charlie types. I will not invent the shape of a JSON body. I will not invent a metric for how many videos came back last month. The yes, at the end of every one of those videos, is still mine.

## What that line means

Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet,” was a fleet of Grok Bots: a press, a chief of staff, specialist jobs, a path that can bring a book back as an EPUB, a letter PDF, and a finished manuscript. That fleet lives inside Grok Bot. This chapter is the same company talking, carefully, to other agent platforms. The workshop has more than one kind of desk in it.

I need to be careful with the word “platform,” because it is a word vendors like, and I am not selling you a platform. I mean something plainer. A Grok Bot is one kind of teammate, with one kind of computer, one kind of chat, one kind of routine. Hermes Agent is another kind of agent, built by another organization, with its own official site and its own license. OpenClaw is another kind again: a self-hosted assistant, stewarded by a foundation, that runs on your own hardware. At SMF Works, Charlie can talk to the fleets we already run on those other platforms, and Video Studio can receive a script from a Hermes agent and make a video. The work crosses a boundary. You remain the one who says yes at the far side of it.

If you only use Grok Bot, you do not need this chapter in order to finish the book. You can read it as a picture of how one small company has its workshop arranged, and as a pattern you might use later, if you already have other tools. You should not read it as a promise that Grok Bot “includes” Hermes, or “includes” OpenClaw, or that those projects have anything to do with this book. They do not. This book is not affiliated with or endorsed by Nous Research, or by the OpenClaw Foundation, or by the product’s maker. Accurate names, no borrowed authority. That is the same courtesy I asked for in the preface, extended across the room.

I should also tell you what I mean by the first half of the line, “doesn’t just span machines.” You already know this from Chapter 2, “A Teammate with a Computer.” A Grok Bot works on a persistent cloud computer. You can close your laptop. The work can continue. That is a span across machines: your desk, and a desk in the cloud that is still yours, in the official sense that it is assigned to your account. A Team Bot, in Chapter 15, can sit in a meeting and in a Slack thread. That is a span across rooms, with people in them. This chapter is a third kind of span. It is not a second laptop, and it is not a second meeting. It is a conversation between Grok Bot and agents that are not Grok Bots — agents that have their own makers, their own licenses, their own way of being talked to. When I say “spans agent platforms,” I mean that conversation. I do not mean that the products have merged, or that a setting has appeared in Grok Bot called “Hermes,” or that you should go looking for one.

The one-job habit from earlier chapters still holds, and it holds harder here. Charlie is my chief of staff Bot. Charlie runs the morning brief, and Charlie also talks to the other fleets. Video Studio is a different Bot, with a different job: it makes videos, and it listens for scripts. I did not make Charlie into a studio, and I did not make Video Studio into a chief. A workshop with several platforms is a workshop where it is tempting to build one Bot that “does everything, including the other tools.” I would not. A clear job is easier to watch, easier to pause, and easier to forgive when a day goes sideways. The shared computer still sits under both of them. The wall between them is a job description, not a security boundary. I will say that as many times as the workshop has lamps.

> **Story.** A script arrives for Video Studio from a Hermes agent. The video comes back. I still watch the cut before anyone else sees it. The handoff is real. The yes is still mine. I will not tell you how many times this has happened, or how long a cut takes, because I did not come here to invent a production number. I came here to tell you that a script can arrive, a video can be made, and a person still sits down and watches it.

What I am watching for is ordinary, and that is the point. Does the script I approved still sound like the script I approved, or did a helpful hand smooth a sentence I meant to keep rough? Is there a name in the picture that should not be public? Is there a claim that would need a note if it were in a book? A video can be “done” in the sense that a file exists, and not done in the sense that a person would stand behind it. The webhook can tell Video Studio to begin. Officially, a 200 tells you only that a run was accepted. The file can appear. I still sit down. Sometimes I say yes. Sometimes I ask for another cut. Sometimes I say no, and the video stays in the workshop. All three of those endings are a working system. Only the first one is public, and only when I have said so.

I want you to notice who is not in that story. There is no automatic post. There is no automatic premiere. There is no number on a dashboard that I am going to recite as proof. The story is a script, a studio, a cut, and a person. If you remember only one picture from this chapter, I would like it to be that one — not because it is dramatic, but because it is the practice. The same practice, in Chapter 12, is a book that comes back bound and still waits for a merge I approve. The same practice, in Chapter 14, is a Pay button that waits. The platforms change. The last inch does not.

## What Hermes Agent is

Hermes Agent is a separate product. It is built by Nous Research. Its official product site is where I am drawing this brief picture from, along with the company’s site and the project’s official repository.^1^ I am not writing a Hermes manual. I am telling you enough that Charlie’s job, and Video Studio’s job, have a real other party on the far side of the line.

The official product site describes Hermes Agent as “the self-improving AI agent built by Nous Research.” It is free and open source under the MIT license. An official FAQ states that model providers and optional hosted services have their own pricing.^2^ That last sentence matters for this book. Hermes the software is not a Grok Bot plan. Its costs, if you use a model provider or a hosted service, are not Grok Bot’s costs, and I will not collapse them into Chapter 17’s weekly pool. If you run Hermes, you read Hermes’s own pricing, on Hermes’s own pages, on the day you need the number.

The official site describes a command-line interface — the `hermes` command — and a Desktop app. It lists channels including Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Signal, Email, and CLI.^3^ That is the official list I opened. I will not recite a longer one from memory, and I will not tell you which of those channels we use at SMF Works, because that is more wiring than I came to give.

You will see, on Nous Research’s own company site, wording about Hermes Agent being widely used. That is a vendor claim. I am not going to repeat it as a fact, and I am not going to borrow other superlatives from a marketing page and dress them as research.^4^ You do not need a superlative to understand the sentence I opened with. You need to know that Hermes is a real, separate, MIT-licensed agent, with a CLI and a Desktop app, able to live in several chat channels, and that it is not Grok Bot.

If you want to learn Hermes, you go to its official site, and to its official repository, and you follow that project’s own docs. This book will not teach you to install it, and it will not give you a command to paste. It will not tell you how, or whether, Hermes is installed on Grok Bot’s computer in our workshop; I did not describe that, and I will not fill the gap with a guess.^12^ Chapter 17’s cautions about a shared computer will still apply if a tool like that ever lives on that computer. We will come to that.

I also will not walk you through Hermes’s own features beyond this brief. The official site describes more than a CLI and a set of channels — memory, scheduling, and other capacities appear there as product features.^13^ If you need those, you read that site. If I started to teach them, I would be writing a second book I am not qualified, in these pages, to finish, and I would be at risk of mixing two products’ vocabularies until you could not tell which teammate you were talking to. The point of naming Hermes carefully is so that you can keep the names straight.

## What OpenClaw is

OpenClaw is also a separate product. Its official site, its official docs, and its official repository are the sources I opened.^5^ I am not writing an OpenClaw manual either.

Officially, OpenClaw is a self-hosted AI assistant that runs on your own hardware — a gateway that connects chat apps to AI agents. The official homepage includes marketing phrases I will not turn into a guarantee; if I quote one, I will quote it as the site’s, not as mine.^6^ Officially, the homepage says “No subscription. No hosted tier. No token.” It is stewarded by the OpenClaw Foundation, which describes itself as an independent US 501(c)(3).^7^ That tax status is the foundation’s official-site statement. I have not opened IRS records, and I will report it as a statement, not as a finding I independently verified. OpenClaw is MIT licensed. Its official command-line name is `openclaw`. Official materials list channels including WhatsApp, Telegram, Discord, Slack, Signal, iMessage, and Microsoft Teams, among others as documented on the page you are looking at.^8^ I am citing the official site I opened, not a memorized complete list, and I am not going to pretend the list cannot change.

If you run OpenClaw, you run it. There is no hosted tier in the official homepage’s own wording, and there is no subscription in that same wording. Its costs are not Grok Bot’s costs. Its security is not Grok Bot’s security. Its foundation has not endorsed this book, and this book is not an official guide to OpenClaw.

What I need you to take from this brief is simple. OpenClaw is a real, separate, MIT-licensed, self-hosted assistant gateway, with a CLI, stewarded by a foundation that describes itself as it describes itself. At SMF Works, Charlie talks to our OpenClaw Fleet via CLI. That is the claim. Then I stop.

Self-hosted is a word I want to sit with for a second, because it is the real difference in this brief, and because it is easy to hear as a slogan. Officially, OpenClaw runs on your own hardware. That means, in plain words, that if you use it, you are closer to the machine than you are with a cloud teammate. You own more of the setup, and you own more of the upkeep, and you own more of the way it fails. That can be exactly what a person wants. It is also why I will not treat OpenClaw as a feature of Grok Bot’s cloud computer, and why I will not write a setup guide that pretends those two machines are one. Charlie talking “via CLI” is a conversation across that difference, not a dissolving of it.

I will also not write you a tour of OpenClaw’s channels beyond the official list I already gave. If you use WhatsApp, or Signal, or Microsoft Teams, on that side of the workshop, you already know those rooms. This book’s Team Bots chapter is about Grok Bot in Slack and in Microsoft Teams. Those are different doors, with different official docs. Keeping the doors labeled is part of keeping the yes in the right place.

I am not going to compare Hermes and OpenClaw as if this were a review that crowns a winner. They are two desks in a workshop. They do different work, on different terms, for different reasons a small company might already have them. If you already have one of them, you already know why. If you do not, this chapter is not a reason to go get one. It is a reason to understand a line about spanning platforms without inventing a product that does not exist: a “Grok Bot Hermes,” or a “Grok Bot OpenClaw.” Those are not products. They are two conversations, carefully kept official, across a boundary you can still see.

At SMF Works, the reason those desks are in the room is not a puzzle I need to dramatize. We had work that already lived there. Charlie’s job, as chief of staff, includes talking to that work so I do not have to be the only messenger between rooms. Video Studio’s job is to take a script that a Hermes agent submits and make a video I can watch. Those are ordinary reasons. They are also complete ones. I am not going to add a business case with a number, and I am not going to suggest that your company, or your household, is behind if your sketch has only one desk. A single desk that finishes the work, and waits for you, is already the book I set out to write.

## The Grok Bot side we can actually state

For this conversation to be possible, Grok Bot has to have two ordinary capacities, and both are official, and both you have already met in earlier chapters. I will not reprint a protocol. I will tell you why those two capacities are enough to make my opening sentence intelligible.

The first is a command line. Officially: “Your Bots work from a persistent cloud computer with a browser, command line, and filesystem.” Officially, it “has a command line and files that stay between tasks.” Officially, “Command-line credentials are shared.” Officially, “Do not place a credential or file on it if another Bot on your account should not be able to use it.” And the sentence I have already asked you to keep: “Do not use separate Bots as a security boundary.”^9^ Charlie communicating “via CLI” means, at the level I am willing to state, that Charlie’s computer has a command line, and that Charlie uses it to talk to the Hermes Fleet and the OpenClaw Fleet we already run. It does not mean I am going to type you a command. It does not mean I will tell you where a binary lives, or what a flag is called, or which path holds a key. If I wrote those things, I would be inventing them, or I would be publishing a secret. I will do neither.

The second is a webhook. In Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed,” you already learned — or you can go back and learn — that you can ask a Bot to add a webhook trigger to a routine. Officially, the desktop shows a POST URL, a secret key, and the full header. The key is sent as `Authorization: Bearer <key>`. The caller sends an HTTP POST, with an optional JSON body. The Bot receives that body along with the routine instruction. A 200 response means the run was accepted and started, not that the work has finished. Other responses: check whether the routine is paused, and whether the current key is the one you used. Test runs do real work.^10^ That is the official webhook. That is all of the official webhook I will put in this book, and I am putting it here only to use it, not to teach it a second time.

Video Studio working with several Hermes Agents “via webhook” means, at the level I am willing to state, that a Hermes agent can submit a script by calling a webhook that starts a Video Studio routine, and that Video Studio can then produce a video. I will not tell you the shape of that body. I will not invent required fields. I will not invent a status code beyond the official 200, which means accepted, not finished. If a script arrives and a video comes back, I still watch the cut, and I still wait for finished, because accepted is not finished.

> **Caution.** A webhook secret is a secret. It belongs in the same careful place you would put any other key, not in a chat, and not in a page you might share. Officially, test runs do real work. A test that points at Video Studio is not a rehearsal in a vacuum. And a 200 is a start, not a premiere.

## The wiring, only as I described it

Let me say the wiring once more, so there is no extra furniture in the room.

Charlie, my chief of staff Bot, manages SMF Works’ Hermes Fleet and OpenClaw Fleet and communicates with them via CLI.

Grok Bot Video Studio works with several Hermes Agents via webhook to produce videos from scripts those Hermes agents submit.

I am the person who says yes before a video is public, and before anything else that should not go out the door goes out the door.

That is it. There is no third fleet I have not named. There is no diagram behind the curtain with ports and paths. There is a chief who can use a command line, and a studio that can listen for a webhook, and a person at the door.

If you look at that drawing and want more arrows, I understand the impulse. I have it too, in a part of my life that likes a neat architecture. I am choosing not to satisfy it here. Extra arrows would be extra claims. Extra claims would be, in this case, either invented or unwise. The workshop is real. The lamps are different. The person at the door is you, or, in our story, me.

## A pattern you can take home

If your work already lives on more than one agent platform, Grok Bot may be one desk in a larger workshop: a chief who can use a command line; a studio that can listen for a webhook. The how-to is not a step-by-step integration guide. I cannot source one, and I will not guess one. The how-to is: stay official, keep secrets off the chat and off a shared computer you do not mean to share, test, and keep the final yes.

**How to review a webhook trigger, without putting a secret in a book**

1. If you already created a webhook trigger in Chapter 8, open that routine on the desktop, and look at what the official docs say you will see: a POST URL, a secret key, and the full header.
   You should see those three, and you should treat the key as a secret.
   If you don’t: ask the Bot to add a webhook trigger, the way Chapter 8 teaches, and follow that chapter rather than this one. I will not reprint the protocol.

2. Remember what a 200 means: accepted, started, not finished. Remember that a test run does real work. If this webhook would start a studio job, or any other job that touches a real file, a real channel, or a real person, do not test it with a throwaway curiosity.
   You should be able to say, out loud, what “accepted” will cause to begin.
   If you don’t: pause the routine until you can. A paused routine is cheaper than a published surprise.

3. Put the secret where secrets go — a locker your Bots should not be chatting about, and a place another Bot on the shared computer should not have if that Bot should not be able to start this job. Rotate a key if it has ever been in a screenshot, a page, or a message.
   You should know who can start this routine.
   If you don’t: do not hand the URL to another platform until you do.

What “done” looks like, for this review: a webhook you understand at the official level, with a secret that is still a secret, and a clear idea that 200 is not “the video is ready.” It is not a sample payload. I have not given you one, on purpose. What still needs a yes: any test that would start a real studio job, and any later decision that a finished video is ready to be public.

**How to sketch your platforms on paper, with a “never”**

1. Take a piece of paper, or a page that will not be public. Draw yourself at the top, the way the diagram in this chapter does. Draw each agent platform you already run — Grok Bot, and any other you truly already have. Do not add a platform you are only curious about. This is a drawing of a workshop you are in, not a workshop you might one day build.
   You should see a small number of desks, with names you can point to in real life.
   If you don’t: you may only have one desk. That is a complete workshop. You can close this how-to and go on to Chapter 17.

2. For each line you want to draw, write only the kind of line it is: “via CLI,” or “via webhook,” or “I do this by hand.” If you cannot name the kind, do not draw the line yet.
   You should have fewer lines than you first imagined.
   If you don’t: erase until each line is one you could explain to a friend without a password.

3. For each line, write a “never.” Mine, for this workshop, would include: never put a webhook secret in a chat; never place a credential on Grok Bot’s computer if another Bot on the account should not use it; never treat a 200 as a finished video; never publish a cut I have not watched; never invent a command I cannot source; never let a Hermes agent, or any other agent, be the final yes.
   You should be able to read the “never” out loud and mean it.
   If you don’t: write a shorter one, and mean that.

4. Decide what still needs you. A script can arrive. A video can be made. A chief can talk to a fleet. A book can come back bound. The public thing — the post, the publish, the merge, the premiere — waits. Put that decision on the paper, at the top, where “You — the final yes” already is.
   You should see yourself on the page, not only the tools.
   If you don’t: add yourself, then look again.

What “done” looks like: a sketch you could tape inside a notebook, with a “never” you intend to keep — not an integration guide, and not a request that I (or anyone) send you a command to paste. What still needs a yes: every public thing at the far side of a line, and any secret you have not yet decided belongs on a shared computer.

I will not give you a third how-to with a sample invocation. The brief for this chapter said not to, and my own judgment agrees. A guessed flag is not a kindness. It is a guess that looks like a fact.

## Cautions that travel across a boundary

The shared-computer rule does not stop at Grok Bot’s own apps. If a command-line tool lives on that computer, its credentials are on that computer, and command-line credentials are shared.^11^ Do not place a credential there if another Bot on your account should not be able to use it. Do not use a second Bot as a wall. I have said this in earlier chapters. I am saying it here because a workshop with several platforms is a workshop where a key is easy to justify and hard to enclose.

Webhook secrets are secrets. I have said that too. I will add: a secret that can start a Video Studio job can start a Video Studio job. Treat it with the same care you would treat a key that can send a message or pay a bill. If you ever have to put a secret on a machine, ask who else sits at that machine. On Grok Bot’s computer, the answer is: your other Bots.

Other platforms have their own security and their own pricing. Hermes: model providers and optional hosted services have their own pricing, officially. OpenClaw: you run it; the official homepage says there is no subscription and no hosted tier; that does not mean there is no cost to hardware, or to electricity, or to your time, and it does not mean Grok Bot is paying that bill. Those costs are not Grok Bot’s, and Grok Bot’s weekly pool is not theirs. Chapter 17 will talk about Grok Bot’s pool honestly. It will not invoice Nous Research, and it will not invoice a foundation.

Do not treat Hermes or OpenClaw as Grok Bot features. They will not appear in Grok Bot’s settings as built-in rooms. They will not be covered by Grok Bot’s official troubleshooting page. If Hermes is down, that is Hermes. If OpenClaw needs an update, that is OpenClaw. If Charlie cannot talk to a fleet today, the first question is a human one — what changed? — and the first answer is not a guessed command from a book.

Do not imply, and I will not imply, that Nous Research or the OpenClaw Foundation endorsed this book, or that this book is an official guide to their work. It is a free, unofficial gift about Grok Bot, with a chapter that names two other projects accurately, at the level their official sites support, because that is the workshop I actually have.

And do not skip the watch. A script can be good. A video can be good. A cut can still be wrong in a way only a person who knows the audience will see. I watch the cut. I am asking you, if you ever build a line like this, to watch yours.

> **Caution.** This chapter is the easiest place in the book to want a recipe I cannot give you. If a sentence in your notes starts looking like a command, or like a JSON template, close the notes and come back to the sketch. The official Grok Bot docs, the official Hermes site, and the official OpenClaw site are the places to learn those tools. A practical guide should know when it has reached the edge of what it can honestly teach.

## What this is, at the door

I opened with a line I meant. Grok Bot doesn’t just span machines, Grok Bot spans agent platforms. At SMF Works, that span is Charlie talking via CLI, and Video Studio listening via webhook, and a person — me — watching a cut before the work is public.

You can take the pattern and leave the fleets. You can take the caution and leave the pattern. You can close this chapter and still have a complete practice: one Bot, one job, a Vault, a yes. The workshop I am describing is a later room, for people who already have more than one desk, and who want those desks to talk without pretending they have become one product.

If you are already in that later room, I want to give you a little more company for the first week you draw the sketch. The first week is not “connect everything.” The first week is one line, on paper, with a “never,” and a test you are willing to watch. If the line is a webhook into a studio, you watch a single script, on a single routine, with a secret you have not put in a chat, and you watch the cut. If the line is a chief talking via CLI to a fleet you already run, you watch one request, of a kind you already understand on that other platform, and you confirm that what came back is what you meant. Then you stop for the day. A workshop is not more serious because it has more lines on the first afternoon. It is more serious because the lines it has are ones a person can still stand in front of.

If a line fails, you will be tempted to debug it by inventing a command or a payload and pasting it from memory, or from a forum, or from a book that should have known better. Come back to official docs for the platform that failed. Grok Bot’s official pages for the computer and for routines. Hermes’s official site, if Hermes is the other desk. OpenClaw’s official docs, if OpenClaw is. And a human look at the secret, the pause switch, and the question of whether a 200 has been mistaken for a finish. Chapter 17 will give you a calm troubleshooting path for Grok Bot itself. It will not troubleshoot Hermes or OpenClaw, and it should not pretend to.

I have one more thing to say to the reader who does not have a second desk, and who felt, halfway through this chapter, that the book had left them. It has not. You have a complete gift without this room. The first task, the first routine, the family Page, the Pay button, the meeting summary — those are the practice. This chapter is a door I wanted to open because it is a door I actually use, and because “spans agent platforms” is a true sentence about Grok Bot as I have lived with it. A true sentence does not have to become your sentence. Take what helps. Leave the rest. That will also be the last page of the book, and it is already true here.

In the next chapter I will talk about safety, privacy, money, and what to do when something breaks. Some of that chapter’s facts are already in this one: a shared computer, a secret that is a secret, a 200 that is not finished. Chapter 17 will put them next to a bill, a cap, and a calm path through a problem. It is not a sales page for a higher plan. It is the chapter I want you to have when a quiet Tuesday brings a usage notice, or a routine that did not run, or a question about who can see what.

The door of this workshop is still a door. You stand in it. That is the part I like most. A teammate can talk across a boundary I used to walk myself, with a script in one hand and a question in the other. I am glad of the help. I have not stopped walking to the door. I do not plan to. If you take nothing else from the sketch, take that walk. The platforms can talk. The person still enters, and the person still leaves, and the public work is the work a person has seen. That is a span I am happy to live with, and a span I am not willing to give away.


# Chapter 17 — Safety, Privacy, Money, and What to Do When It Breaks


![A safety card on a kitchen table: Ask first, the Vault, a shared computer, a card that can pay, approvals that expire.](book/art/I29.jpg){width=100%}

*I29. A safety card on a kitchen table: Ask first, the Vault, a shared computer, a card that can pay, approvals that expire.*


This chapter is the one I want you to have on a quiet Tuesday.

Not a dramatic Tuesday. An ordinary one. The kind where the work has been going well enough that you have stopped watching the meter, and then a notice arrives, or a Bot stops replying and says you have hit a usage limit, and your first thought is that something is broken. Officially, it may not be broken at all. Your weekly usage may be used up. It resets each week.^1^ The kindness, first, is that sentence. The fix comes after, and the fix is not “buy a higher plan,” and this chapter is not a sales page for one.

I am going to walk you through a bill, a cap, the facts about privacy and training that I can source, the shared computer I have been asking you to take seriously, and a calm path when something actually breaks. I will tell you four facts in particular, plainly, because they are the ones people miss, and because I promised I would not sand them off:

- All of your Bots share one computer. Separate Bots are not a security boundary.^2^
- A card saved in the Vault pays without asking you each time.^3^
- Approvals from work that started without you expire after about thirty minutes.^4^
- Under The X Subscription, costs come out of a shared weekly pool, across Grok, Grok Bot, and Cursor.^5^

If you only remember those four, and you keep the yes, you will already be safer than a person who has memorized a dozen tips and skipped the pool.

## The pool, and how it changed

On 11 August 2026, when Grok Bot was introduced in public beta, the official announcement said Grok Bot had “its own usage, separate from your Grok and Cursor plans.”^6^ If you remember that sentence, and you are on The X Subscription now, that sentence is no longer the live rule. I am dating both, on purpose.

The X Subscription was introduced on 12 October 2026.^7^ Under that subscription, as of the official pages I opened on 11 November 2026, Grok Bot is included on Premium, Super, and Ultra, for individuals and for team seats. Plus and Free do not include it. Grok Bot draws from the same weekly usage pool as Cursor and the Grok apps. Prepaid credit covers usage past the pool. Super and Ultra include larger weekly pools than Premium.^8^ List prices on the official pricing page that day, tax extra, were: Free at $0; Plus at $8 a month, without Grok Bot; Premium at $30 a month, with four times Plus’s included usage, and with Grok Bot; Super at $100 a month, fifteen times; Ultra at $200 a month, fifty times; Teams at $30 or more per seat, with the live page as the authority for the exact team wording.^9^ Those numbers will change. When this book and the live page disagree, trust the live page, at [https://x.ai/pricing](https://x.ai/pricing).

The official usage documentation says each paid seat receives a weekly pool for eligible first-party usage, shared across eligible Grok and Cursor surfaces rather than split into separate product quotas. Usage rates vary by product, so different activities can draw different amounts. Additional Usage is any third-party model usage in Cursor, plus any first-party usage beyond the included weekly pool. Top-ups buy additional usage credits. Third-party models in Cursor are not in the included pool; they are Additional Usage from the first use.^10^ I cannot tell you a single public number, in dollars or in tokens, for how large the weekly pool is. Official pages give multipliers and point you at the live dashboard.^11^ I will not invent a “typical hours left.”

Switching to The X Subscription, if you are on an older plan, is optional, and once it is done it is permanent. Officially, it cannot be undone, and Support cannot undo it for you.^12^ If you do not switch, your current plan continues. New customers start on The X Subscription. I am not going to talk you into switching, and I am not going to talk you out of it. I am going to ask you to read the live page, and to hear the word “permanent,” before you choose.

On a legacy Cursor path, which is still documented, Grok Bot is included with every paid individual Cursor plan and with Cursor Teams, and you can link certain SuperGrok or X subscriptions as the official FAQ describes. A SuperGrok link is permanent and cannot be moved by the user. A Cursor plan and SuperGrok do not add together; you get the higher tier.^13^ That path has its own meter. If you are on it, your dashboard is the Cursor one until you switch. Chapter 3, “Get In,” already asked you to look at the live plan screen and not at a screenshot in a book. This chapter asks the same, with more at stake, because a Tuesday notice is a poor moment to discover which path you are on.


![The shared usage tank, as of 11 November 2026. One weekly pool, three taps.](book/art/diagrams/I28.png){width=100%}

*I28. The shared usage tank, as of 11 November 2026. One weekly pool, three taps.*


*The shared usage tank, dated 11 November 2026, from official pricing and usage pages. One weekly pool for eligible first-party usage, shared across Grok, Grok Bot, and Cursor. Rates vary by product. Trust the live dashboard if this drawing and the product disagree. (Chapter 17.)*

## A notice on a quiet Tuesday

> **Story.** A usage notice, on a quiet Tuesday. I will not invent a particular Tuesday and ask you to treat it as a memory. I will tell you the shape, because the official docs already have it, and because I have wanted, as a friend, to be in the room when it arrives. The Bot stops. It says you hit your usage limit. It is not broken. The weekly pool is used up. It will reset. You can wait. You can, if you choose, add prepaid credit, with a cap, and with Auto Top-up left off unless you mean it on. You can also, if you choose, look at a larger plan — and I will not recommend one, and I will not name Ultra as a prize. You open the dashboard. You read the number. You decide, with the week you actually have, not with a scare.

That is the kindness, and then the fix.

**How to open the usage dashboard and read it**

1. On The X Subscription, go to [https://grok.com/dashboard](https://grok.com/dashboard) and open Usage. Officially, that is where you check live usage, and, after you have switched, where you manage it.^14^
   You should see how much has been used, and a next reset date.
   If you don’t: confirm which path you are on. If you have not switched to The X Subscription, the official docs say to open the Cursor dashboard at [https://cursor.com/dashboard](https://cursor.com/dashboard) before switching.^15^ If the two disagree with a memory you have of an August sentence, trust the dashboard. The rule changed.

2. Read three things, slowly: what is used, when the pool resets, and whether any Additional Usage or prepaid credit is in play. Do not hunt for a “hours left” that official pages do not publish.
   You should be able to say, out loud, when the week starts again, and whether you have asked the product to keep going on paid credit.
   If you don’t: stay on the page until you can. A notice is easier when the reset is a date, not a feeling.

3. If the Bot has stopped and the page says the weekly usage is used up, treat that as the explanation. Officially, to keep going now you can add prepaid credit (The X Subscription), turn on on-demand usage (legacy Cursor plans), or move to a bigger plan — and a Bot already working can finish past an on-demand monthly limit.^16^ I am going to ask you to prefer waiting, or a capped credit you chose, over a hurried upgrade. This chapter will not tell you to move up.
   You should know whether you are waiting, or paying a little more on purpose, or done for the week.
   If you don’t: leave Auto Top-up off, and come to the next how-to before you add credit.

What “done” looks like: a page you have read, on the day you read it, with a reset you can name — not a guess, and not a plan you bought in a hurry. What still needs a yes: any prepaid credit, any Auto Top-up, and any move to a different plan. This chapter will not recommend one.

In early November 2026, journalism reported that some users hit weekly limits unexpectedly, around the Halloween weekend. TechCrunch, on 2 November 2026, and The Verge, on the same day, covered it. TechCrunch quoted Elon Musk, from a 1 November 2026 reply on X, as saying “The cost of serving Grok Bot was unsustainably high relative to revenue.” I am attributing that quote to TechCrunch’s report, and to The Verge’s, because I am working from those reports; I am not treating the post itself as a page I opened here.^17^ TechCrunch also reported that he had not yet said how limits would change going forward. I will not fill that gap. I will tell you that usage is a live thing, that it has already been a public conversation, and that your dashboard on the day you look is the number that matters.

## A cap, and Auto Top-up left off

Auto Top-up is optional, and it is off until you turn it on. Switching to The X Subscription does not turn it on for you. When it is on, it charges a saved payment method when your prepaid credit drops below a threshold you choose.^18^ A monthly additional-usage cap is a separate control: the most paid Additional Usage allowed in a calendar month. Adding prepaid credit does not raise that cap. Additional Usage can stop even when credit remains, if the cap is reached.^19^ Officially, a cap does not stop a Bot in the middle of a run, so a running task can go a little past it.^20^

**How to set or confirm a cap, and leave Auto Top-up off unless you mean it on**

1. On The X Subscription, open Additional usage credits at [https://grok.com/dashboard/billing](https://grok.com/dashboard/billing), which is the official FAQ’s address for leaving Auto Top-up off or setting a monthly additional usage limit.^21^
   You should see Auto Top-up, and a place for a monthly limit, and the prepaid credit if any.
   If you don’t: use the live dashboard’s billing views, which official usage docs also point to.^22^ If you are on a legacy Cursor plan, the official FAQ says to turn off on-demand or set a cap in Grok Bot under Settings, then Usage & Billing, then “On-demand monthly limit,” or on the web at the Cursor dashboard, under Spending, “Monthly Limit.”^23^

2. Confirm that Auto Top-up is off, unless you have already decided, on a calm day, that you want it on. If you want it on, set a threshold you understand, and set a monthly cap you can live with.
   You should be able to say whether a saved payment method can be charged this week without you.
   If you don’t: leave it off. Off is the official default, and it is a good one.

3. If you want a cap, set one. Leave it unset only if you mean there to be no cap. Read the official warning again: a running task can go a little past it.
   You should see a number you chose, or a clear “no cap” you chose.
   If you don’t: do not add a large prepaid credit in order to “just not think about it.” Credit without a cap is a decision, not a convenience.

4. Do not treat a cap as a reason to turn Auto Review off, or to save a card you would not otherwise save. Money and safety are not a trade.
   You should leave the safety settings where Chapter 5 left them, or tighter.
   If you don’t: walk the safety card in the next how-to before you come back to billing.

What “done” looks like: a cap you can name, and an Auto Top-up state you chose — not a surprise charge on a Wednesday, and not a lecture from me about which plan is “for” you. What still needs a yes: turning Auto Top-up on, and any additional credit you have not decided to add.

What burns the pool, as a practical matter, is the kind of work that talks a lot or clicks a lot. Officially, every Bot reply counts toward your usage, including replies in a group and messages between Bots, so fewer Bots in a group means fewer replies.^24^ Officially, usage rates vary by product.^25^ Officially, broad listeners — the Chapter 8 warning about a routine that fires on every new message — burn usage and act on noise. Independent writers have also warned that group chats, browser clicking, and long chats can run a pool down faster than a person expects; I will leave that as their published caution, and I will tell you to watch your own dashboard after a heavy afternoon, rather than to trust a number I do not have.^26^ The official habit that helps is already in this book: prefer one owner per step, “@” one Bot, do not run a routine more often than you need, and prefer a connected app over a long click-tour when a connection exists — and if I cannot source that last preference as a single official sentence, I can still tell you, as practice, that a connector is usually a shorter path than a browser that has to hunt.^27^

The shared pool also means a quiet kindness and a quiet caution between products. If you spend a long afternoon in Cursor, or a long evening in Grok, that is the same week, under The X Subscription, as the Bot that runs a routine at dawn. I am not telling you to stop using those products. I am telling you that the tank is one tank, as of 11 November 2026, and that a heavy day in one place can be a thin day in another. If you live with that fact, the Tuesday notice is less of a shock. If you do not, the notice can feel like a punishment, and it is not one. It is a pool. You can plan a heavy job for the start of a week, after a reset, rather than for a Sunday night when the pool is already tired. You can also, simply, do less for a day. A Bot that is paused is a Bot that is not drawing. A routine that runs once a morning, instead of every hour, is a routine that leaves something in the tank for the work you actually needed to finish.

I will also say, once, to the person who is ashamed of the notice: please don’t be. People who write about this product, and people who make it, have already talked in public about limits and about cost. You are not the first person to meet the edge of a week, and you will not be the last. Open the dashboard. Read the reset. Make a small decision. Then go make dinner. The pool will be there in the morning, or on the date the page says, and so will the work. You will still be the person who says yes, and that, even on a thin week, is enough of a practice to keep.

> **Caution.** Do not try to “beat” a weekly pool by creating more Bots. They share the pool, and they share the computer. More Bots that talk to each other are more replies, not a new tank.

Billing has a few pits I want to point at, calmly. Unification is permanent. A SuperGrok link, on the legacy path, is permanent and cannot be moved by the user. Spent prepaid credit is not refundable, as the official FAQ describes.^28^ Grok Bot does not have a separate refund path, because it does not have a separate subscription.^29^ An App Store or Google Play purchase is a different cancellation path, through Apple or Google, and an App Store free trial “turns into a paid Apple subscription unless you cancel first.”^30^ That trial is not the same as the “Start Trial” some accounts see in Grok Bot itself, which the official FAQ says is a usage credit and a seven-day window, and which “never turns into a paid plan” — though one big task can use all of it, and used credit is not restored.^31^ If you bought something through a store, cancel it where you bought it. If you are leaving, we will come to account deletion, which is more than signing out.

## Privacy, training, and the shape of the data

Grok Bot uses Cursor authentication and account data settings. Training opt-out follows the applicable Cursor account and privacy settings.^32^ Grok Bot requires cloud data storage, so Legacy Privacy Mode is not supported; official getting-started and FAQ wording is that the app asks you to switch.^33^ I will not pretend there is an on-prem version I can point you to; the official security FAQ has been asked about that, and you should read the current security pages if that is a requirement you have, rather than take a paraphrase from me.^34^

The product-page FAQ, which is vendor wording, says that Grok Bot uses the same Cursor SSO, authentication, and privacy mode “you already trust,” and that “your cloud computer is encrypted in transit and at rest, with training opt-out.”^35^ I am labeling that as vendor, on the product page, and I am pointing you, for contractual detail, at the official approvals documentation, which tells you to review the current Cursor Privacy Policy and security information.^36^ The SpaceXSI Privacy Policy is at [https://x.ai/legal/privacy-policy](https://x.ai/legal/privacy-policy); I opened it in research, and I will not paraphrase a long consumer policy from memory in a way that sounds like legal advice. If you need the clauses, read the clauses, on the day you need them. Official FAQ also points at a sub-processors page, because third-party models may process data.^37^

If you delete your Grok Bot account, you should know what that means before you reach for it. Grok Bot uses your Cursor account, so deleting it deletes the Cursor account. Deletion is self-serve and permanent. Signing out, or uninstalling the app, leaves the account in place. There is no way to delete Grok Bot without deleting your Cursor account. Officially, deletion removes the Cursor account and Grok Bot access; Grok Bot agents, chats, computers, and connected apps; and Cursor chat history and settings. All data is removed within thirty days. Files on your own computer stay there. An App Store or Google Play subscription, a linked SuperGrok subscription, and a linked X Premium+ subscription are separate: you cancel each with Apple, Google, Grok, or X if you no longer want it.^38^ The official docs give desktop and phone steps for this; if you are on a Teams or Enterprise seat, you ask a team admin or contact support. I will not make this sound casual. It is a real door, and it is a door that closes more than the Bot.

## The shared computer, the card, and a risk that is reduced, not gone

Chapter 2, “A Teammate with a Computer,” and Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,” already walked the shared computer and the Vault at first-week length. I will not reprint those how-tos here. A brief recap is enough, because this is the safety chapter, and these are the facts people miss.

All of your Bots share one cloud computer. Files, browser sessions, and command-line credentials on that computer are available across your roster. Do not use separate Bots as a security boundary.^39^ Vault is account-wide as well: everything you save is available to every Bot on your account.^40^ A household Bot and a bills Bot are two jobs, not two vaults. Sign out, remove a sensitive file, and revoke a connector, the way those earlier chapters taught, when the work is done.

A card saved in Vault fills in at checkout without asking you each time. If you want to approve every payment yourself, do not save the card; if you already saved one, remove it from Settings, then Vault, as Chapter 5 showed you.^41^ I am saying the sentence again because Chapter 14’s shopping flow is a pleasant place, and this fact is easy to forget after a gift that went well. Permanent unification, if you are considering The X Subscription from an older plan, is Chapter 3, “Get In,” and the earlier section of this chapter: optional, and permanent, and Support cannot undo it.

Approvals from work that started without you — a routine, a trigger, a message from another Bot — expire after about thirty minutes. The card then shows “Expired,” and the action does not run. Approvals in a chat you are in wait for you.^42^ If you want a 6 a.m. routine to finish a step that needs a yes, you will need to be there, or you will need an “Always allow” you actually trust, or the step will wait. Official troubleshooting says, to catch approvals in time, to turn on notifications for Bots that run routines, to choose “Always allow” for actions you trust, and to ask the Bot to try again after an approval expires.^43^ “Always allow” is a tool. It is not a personality trait. Use it narrowly.

Auto Review is on by default. Turning it off means the Bot runs without those checks; official docs also say that custom rules are hidden but kept.^44^ I would not turn it off. I would add Ask-first rules for sending, publishing, deleting, purchasing, and changing production systems, as the official FAQ already invites you to.^45^

Prompt injection — a page, or a file, trying to tell the Bot to do something you did not ask — is reduced, not gone. Officially, only your own messages count as instructions. Hidden text on a conference page telling the Bot to email your contacts would not be treated as a request from you. “These checks lower the risk from harmful content, but they don’t remove it.” Keep Ask-first on anything you cannot undo.^46^ That last official sentence is one I want in your ear. A reduced risk is not a removed one. A careful teammate is still a teammate you walk with slowly.

Sharing a Bot as a template is not a share of your computer or your logins, and it is also not a reason to be careless: the link exposes configuration, and you strip secrets and anything confidential before you share.^47^ A Team Bot is different, as Chapter 15 said: the owner’s apps and secrets work in every teammate’s chat.

**How to walk the safety card**

1. Write the card down, or open the checklist in Appendix C, and walk it once, on a calm day, not on a Tuesday notice. The items I want on it are official, and they are these:
   - Ask-first rules on send, publish, delete, purchase, and anything you cannot undo.
   - Auto Review on.
   - Secrets in the Vault or a Secure Form, never in the chat.
   - No card in the Vault if you want to approve every payment.
   - A clear look at Settings, then Vault, so you know what is there.
   - The shared computer, named as shared: files, sessions, command-line credentials; separate Bots are not a wall.
   - Notifications on for Bots that run routines, so a thirty-minute approval does not expire in silence.
   - Sending left off, as the official examples already model.
   You should be able to point at each item in your own settings, not only on this page.
   If you don’t: pick the first one you cannot point at, and fix that one today. Chapter 5 is the first-week version of this walk. Appendix C is the short version. This is the one I want you to repeat.

2. For each item, say whether it is true of your account right now. If a card is saved and you did not mean it to pay without asking, remove it. If Auto Review is off, turn it back on. If a login is on the computer that should not be, sign out, and revoke it at the source.
   You should have a shorter list of “I will do this Thursday” than you had when you started.
   If you don’t: the card is doing its job. Thursday is allowed. A rushed revoke is how people lock themselves out of a site they still need.

3. Put a date on the walk. A quarterly pass belongs in Chapter 18. A first pass belongs today, or this week.
   You should have a date, even a rough one, for the next look.
   If you don’t: write “after the next usage reset” and mean it.

What “done” looks like: a card you have walked, with a computer, a Vault, and a set of rules that match what you believe they are — not a feeling that you are “probably fine.” What still needs a yes: removing a card, turning Auto Review back on, or revoking a login, if those are the items that were off.

> **Caution.** This book is a free, unofficial gift. The trademark caution from the preface still applies, in this chapter as in the first. I use the product’s name to talk about the product. I do not speak for its maker. Non-commercial use may lower practical trademark risk relative to a book sold as a product; that is an observation, not a legal conclusion, and this book is not legal advice.^48^ I will not tell you that a title is “cleared.” I will tell you that the caution still deserves a calm reading of the public brand guidelines, if you are making something of your own that names this product.

## When it breaks, and when to ask for help

The official troubleshooting page is long, and it is good, and it is the authority.^49^ I am going to walk one path with you, in the spirit of a Tuesday, and then tell you how to contact support without handing them a password.

**How to walk one official troubleshooting path**

1. If the Bot stopped and mentioned a usage limit, take the path we already walked: the dashboard, the reset, a cap, Auto Top-up left off. Officially, that case “isn’t broken.”^50^
   You should know, in a minute, whether this is a pool or a problem.
   If you don’t: look at the exact words in the chat. “Usage limit” and “Bot failed to respond” are different sentences, and they have different official paths.

2. If you think the service itself is down, check [https://status.cursor.com](https://status.cursor.com) and look at the Grok Bot row. The app does not show service status, so the status page is the place. You can subscribe to updates there. If the status page shows a Grok Bot incident, wait for it to be resolved, then send your message again. Do not Reset the computer, and do not reinstall the app; neither fixes a service problem, and Reset can lose very recent changes if the computer cannot be reached to save them.^51^
   You should have looked at the status page before you did anything dramatic.
   If you don’t: go there now. If there is no incident, and the Bot says “Bot failed to respond” or “Model provider is overloaded,” the official page says to wait a moment and send the message again; if it still does not reply, fully quit and reopen Grok Bot.^52^

3. If an approval expired, read the card. Officially it shows “Expired,” and the action does not run. Ask the Bot to try the task again. If a card is no longer actionable, reject or cancel it if you can, send a replacement instruction, and ask the Bot to regenerate the action with the corrected scope. If an action keeps requiring approval, check Custom Rules under Settings, then General, then Bot, for a matching Ask-first rule, including team rules an admin requires. Ask-first takes precedence over Allow automatically.^53^
   You should have a new card, or a rule you now understand, or a task you have decided to do yourself.
   If you don’t: do not “Always allow” in irritation. Always allow is for actions you trust, on a calm day.

4. If a routine did not run, open it. Officially, you verify that it is enabled, not Paused; that “When to run” shows the schedule and time zone you expect; that the owning Bot still exists; that required plugins remain authenticated; that the computer can reach the source; and that usage or account access has not been paused. Inspect recent run history. If the routine shows Test, use it only with safe input; a test run can perform real external actions.^54^
   You should see a reason, or a test you are willing to watch, or a bug you are ready to report with the Bot name and the routine details, as the official page describes for a routine that is not paused and has not run for twenty-four hours or more with no error.^55^
   If you don’t: do not delete and recreate the routine as a first move. Read it first. Chapter 8 has more of this path.

5. If a Bot appears stuck, check the status in the sidebar and the conversation. Open the computer to see whether the Bot is waiting on a page. Look for a question, an approval, a login, a CAPTCHA, or a secret request. Send a short redirect if the current approach is wrong, or a direct “Stop now” if the work should end.^56^
   You should see what it is waiting on, or a stop you meant.
   If you don’t: wait one beat before you send “Stop now” to a computer-use task that may need to finish the current screen; the official page notes that an already active computer-use task may need to finish or be redirected before another can start.

What “done” looks like: a path you can name, from an official page, with a next step that is either “wait,” “try again,” “change a setting,” or “ask support” — not a Reset in a panic, and not a second account you created to get around a pool. What still needs a yes: a Reset, a deletion, and any message to support that would include more than the official list. Do not include a password.

When you do need the support team, the official page is specific, and I will follow it. Email [support@x.ai](mailto:support@x.ai) from the email on your Grok Bot account. Billing and cancellation questions go to the same address, with a quick summary. From the desktop app, you can also open the account menu and choose Support, then Send Feedback, and include the current conversation ID when the problem is in the open conversation. On a phone, Settings, then Send Feedback, and include the Bot ID if the problem is with one Bot. Collect: the email on the account; the Grok Bot version and platform, from Support, then About, then “Copy version info”; what you were trying to do, and the Bot or routine name; the exact error, or a screenshot of the error or plan screen; the approximate time and time zone; the request ID or conversation ID if one is shown (right-click a message and “Copy request ID,” or the official “More message actions”); and the steps you already tried. Do not include passwords, one-time codes, private keys, or secret values.^57^

> **Tip.** The version, the request ID, and the exact error are more useful than a long theory. Support can do more with a fact you copied than with a feeling you polished.

## What I want this chapter to have done

I want you to be able to read a bill. I want you to have a cap, and Auto Top-up off unless you turned it on. I want you to know that training opt-out lives in Cursor’s settings, that cloud storage is required, and that deleting Grok Bot deletes Cursor. I want the four facts I opened with to be boring, in the best sense: shared computer, a card that can pay without asking, approvals that expire, a weekly pool that is shared. I want you to have a status page, a troubleshooting path, and an address for support. I want you to have, still, a book that is not trying to sell you Ultra.

I also want a little warmth at the end of a chapter that has had to be stern. A usage notice is not a moral failing. An expired approval is not a Bot that “let you down”; it is a clock of about thirty minutes, doing what the official docs said it would do. A shared computer is not a trick. It is a fact you can live with, if you live with it on purpose: fewer secrets on the machine, a Vault you have actually looked at, a card you saved only if you meant it to pay. Privacy settings are a page you can open, not a rumor. Support is an address, and a request ID, and a promise not to paste a password. All of that is the same kindness I wanted on the first page of the book, pointed at money and at risk, because money and risk are where kindness is easy to forget.

The product will move. Plans will change. A November journalism story will be followed by other stories, and by other official pages. Date the sentence you are standing on. Trust the live dashboard. Keep the yes.

Chapter 18 is a practice that lasts: initiative, Pages, connectors, templates, a quarterly review. It is the last full chapter, and it is a calm one. You will still be the person at the door.


# Chapter 18 — A Practice That Lasts: Initiative, Pages, Connectors, Templates, and Mastery


![A Monday page on a kitchen table, morning light, a mug, a closed laptop.](book/art/I30.jpg){width=100%}

*I30. A Monday page on a kitchen table, morning light, a mug, a closed laptop.*


I want to end the last full chapter the way I wanted to begin the book: with a morning that is already a little lighter, and with you still the person who says yes.

A Page is current every Monday. I asked for it once. A routine updates it. I open it with the coffee, the way I open Charlie’s brief, and I am no longer the person who has to middle-man every handoff just to know where the work stands. I still read it. I still decide what goes out the door. The first pass is not mine. The last inch is.

That is the practice I want this chapter to leave you with. It is not a new product, and it is not a graduation. Grok Bot 1.0, released on 11 November 2026, gave us a name for part of it — initiative, a Bot that offers first — and it highlighted interactive Pages a Bot can keep updated.^1^ The rest is habits you already have, if you have been walking with me: one job, a skill, a routine, a yes, a quarterly look at whether the workshop is still the workshop you meant.

We will take initiative without hype. We will make a Monday Page. We will treat connectors and MCP as a power-user corner, marked as such, and we will share a template only after we have stripped the secrets. We will retire or split a Bot that has grown two jobs. And we will keep a short review, four times a year, so a practice that lasts is a practice you still recognize.

## Initiative, without the hype

Officially, your Bot “doesn’t only wait for instructions.” It notices work it can take off your plate and offers to handle it, and it tells you when something needs your attention. You stay in charge: it offers first, and it asks before risky actions.^2^

That is a good sentence. I want to keep it at that size. I do not want to turn it into a promise that the work will run itself, or that you can “set it and forget it,” which this book will not say as a guarantee of money or of safety. An offer is an offer. A yes is still a yes.

What does a Bot do, officially, without being asked? Three things, on the initiative page I opened. It offers to take on work: your primary Bot — the one marked with a star in the sidebar — spots tasks it can handle and offers to do them. It flags problems with your apps: when an app such as Gmail signs you out, the primary Bot tells you in its chat and offers to reconnect. And it can follow up: in X Chat, a Bot can follow up on a conversation in its own words.^3^ A Team Bot cannot be your primary Bot. A Team Bot can still ask you for something it needs; for example, when one of its routines needs one of your connected apps, it asks in its Slack thread and tags you.^4^

When you say yes, it does the work the same way it does any task you gave it, and it still asks before risky actions.^5^ The 1.0 news described the same idea in the product’s own voice: Bots can recognize work that needs to be done and offer to take it on; they can follow up on unfinished tasks, anticipate what a team needs next, and bring people in when a decision requires their judgment.^6^ A vendor example in that post is a product-launch Bot that notices an upcoming deadline and prepares materials. I will leave the example as the company’s, and I will tell you what I actually do with an offer: I read it. I decide. Sometimes I say yes. Sometimes I say not this week. An offer I do not answer is not a failure in the Bot, and it is not a failing in me.

If the alerts are too many, you can turn off Notifications for that Bot. On the phone, the switch is in the Bot’s profile. On desktop, you can ask the Bot to turn its notifications off. Officially, notifications control alerts, not the check-ins themselves. If you would like the check-ins turned off, the official path is to contact support.^7^ That distinction is worth hearing. Quiet is allowed. Quiet is not the same as “stop noticing.”

I treat initiative the way I treated the first real task in Chapter 4. An offer to draft a brief I already have a skill for is an offer I will often take. An offer to reach out to a person, or to change a live system, or to “go ahead and handle it,” is an offer I will read slowly, and often decline, and sometimes turn into a smaller yes: “Draft it, and stop.” The product’s 1.0 story is a Bot that can anticipate what a team needs next. My practice is a person who is glad of the anticipation and still holds the last inch. Those two sentences can live in the same workshop. They do, in mine.

If you are just through your first week, you do not need initiative to be “on” in a big way for this book to have helped you. A Bot that waits for you is a complete teammate. Offers will arrive, or they will not, as the product’s own habits go. You already know what to do with them. Read. Decide. Keep the yes.

> **Tip.** Initiative is a gift when the offer is something you already trust the Bot to draft. It is a nuisance when it is a new kind of work you have not watched yet. You can say “not this, and please don’t offer this kind of thing unless I ask.” Chapter 6, “Memory,” is where that kind of request lives. The yes, when you give it, is the same yes as Chapter 4, “Hand Off Your First Real Task.”

## A Monday Page that stays current

A page, as Chapter 13 already said in a household voice, is something a Bot builds for you to read: a report, a memo, a dashboard, a tracker, an explainer. It can hold text, tables, charts, and simple controls. It lives in the Bot’s Library, so you can come back to it, and a routine can update it.^8^ The 1.0 news called out “interactive Pages that you can edit together or ask them to keep updated.”^9^

Pages, as of the documentation I opened on 11 November 2026, were rolling out gradually, and they were not available on Enterprise teams yet. If you do not see pages, the official docs say to update Grok Bot to version 0.60.0 or later on your computer and 1.14.0 or later on your phone.^10^ That will age. Check the live docs. I am dating the sentence, and I am leaving the Enterprise caveat as a gap that a later pass can close, not as a forever fact.^11^

I asked, at SMF Works, for a Monday Page: the week ahead, the work that is waiting for a yes, the things Charlie’s brief already pointed at, in a form I can open without asking for a new report every time. A routine updates the same page. When the Bot updates it, an earlier card in the chat says “Updated below,” and I click it to open the latest version.^12^ I still read it. I no longer have to be the person who collects every handoff into a document by hand. That is the story I promised this chapter, and it has no metric in it, on purpose.

**How to ask for a Monday Page that a routine updates**

1. Open a chat with the Bot that already owns this kind of work — a chief of staff Bot if you have one from Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff,” or the household Bot from Chapter 13 if this is a family page. Ask for a page, in plain words: what it is for, and what should be on it. You can say, “Make a Monday page for the week ahead. Put what’s waiting for my yes, what’s due, and any clashes. Do not send, post, pay, or publish anything. Update this same page, not a new one, when we put it on a routine.”
   You should see a card in the chat with the page’s title and a way to open it, labeled, officially, “Open.”^13^
   If you don’t: say again what the page is for. If pages are not in your app, update as the official docs describe, or keep the same list in a file the Bot writes, and check the live docs. On an Enterprise team, pages may not be available yet; I am not going to invent a workaround.

2. Open the page. On desktop, it fills the window, and the chat stays in a smaller panel beside it, so you can keep talking. If something is wrong, type in that panel — officially labeled “Message about” and the page’s title — and ask the Bot to edit this same page.^14^
   You should see the same page change, not a second copy.
   If you don’t: name the title, and say “this page.” Do not start a new page for every correction. Filters and switches on a page change what you see, not what the page holds; nothing you do on the page is saved. To change the page itself, ask the Bot.^15^

3. When the page looks right, ask the Bot to create a routine that updates this same page on a schedule you choose — Monday morning is the picture in this chapter’s title; Sunday evening is the official calendar picture from Chapter 13. Tell it not to email anyone, and not to publish the page, when it runs.
   You should see a routine with a schedule and a time zone you recognize, waiting for its next run, the way Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed,” taught you.
   If you don’t: open the routine and check that it is enabled, that it points at this page, and that it is not running a test you did not mean. Test runs do real work.
