4. Decide who can see it. A new page is private to you until you share it. You can share with people you invite, with your team, or — with care — with anyone who has the link, which needs no sign-in and opens at an address on `pages.grok.bot`. Officially, share a page that way only when it holds nothing private. Public sharing is off on Enterprise teams.^16^
   You should know, before the first Monday, whether this page is only yours.
   If you don’t: leave it private. A household Page with a delivery address, as Chapter 13 already warned, is not a public page. A Monday Page with a customer’s name is not one either.

What “done” looks like: a page you can open every Monday, updated by a routine you chose, with a share setting you meant. What still needs a yes: anything on that page that would send, post, pay, publish, or move a calendar, and any public link you have not decided to create.

> **Story.** The first Monday I did not have to ask for the report, I almost looked for the question I usually type. The page was already there. It was not magic. It was a routine I had tested, a title I recognized, and a list I still had to read. I said yes to two things, and not yet to a third, and I went to the rest of the day with the feeling I described in the preface: the work was already waiting, and it was mine to approve, not mine to start from zero.

## Connectors, and a power-user corner

Bots work best when you connect the everyday tools you already use. Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,” is the first-week version of that sentence. Official overview examples include Slack, Notion, Drive, Teams, Gmail, Google Calendar, and Granola. If a service has no connector, the Bot uses the browser on its cloud computer.^17^ That is the ordinary path, and for most of this book it is enough.

This next part is a power-user corner. I am marking it as such. You can skip it and still have a complete practice.

Officially, Grok Bot “supports custom tooling, so it can interact with any MCP or API.”^18^ MCP is a standard way AI tools connect to a service — the official docs call it that, when they tell you what to do if an app is not in the catalog: ask a Bot to connect the app’s MCP server. If the service has an API but no MCP server, a Marketplace Bot named tinkabot can wrap the API in a plugin with an MCP server and skills, test it, and publish it only when you say so.^19^ That last clause is the whole method. Test. Publish only when you say so.

I want to distinguish, carefully, two catalogs that can be confused. Grok, the chat app, has its own connectors documentation, with built-in connectors and a catalog and custom MCP connectors, at the Grok connectors page.^20^ Grok Bot’s “Connect apps” is the path Chapter 5 taught you. They are related families, and they are not automatically the same list. If you are connecting a tool for a Grok Bot, follow the Grok Bot docs and the Connect-apps path, not a Grok-chat page you opened by accident.

**How to use a custom MCP connection only as official docs allow, and publish only on a yes**

1. Confirm that you actually need this. If the app is already in the catalog, connect it the ordinary way, from Chapter 5. If the Bot can do the job in the browser, for a task you run rarely, consider whether a custom connection is worth a new door on a shared computer.
   You should be able to say why this service, and why a custom connection, in a sentence.
   If you can’t: stop here. A power-user corner is optional. A yes you cannot explain is a yes I would not give.

2. If the app has an MCP server, ask a Bot to connect it, following the official computer-and-apps path, not a guessed recipe from a forum.
   You should see a connection you can name, and revoke, later.
   If you don’t: read the live official docs for “Connect an app.” Do not paste a server address you do not trust. A server you run must be one you mean to expose; official Grok-chat connector docs, for that product, note that an MCP server must be reachable, and I will not invent a tunneling guide here.^21^

3. If the service has an API but no MCP server, and you still want a plugin, you can ask tinkabot — the official Marketplace Bot — to wrap the API. Officially, it tests the plugin and publishes it only when you say so.
   You should see a test, then a wait for your yes, then a publish you chose.
   If you don’t: do not treat a test as a publish. Say “do not publish until I say yes,” in so many words, the way the official description already models.

4. Keep secrets out of the chat. An API key goes through a secure secret card, as Chapter 5 taught, not through a message. A plugin that can act on a service is a door. Put an Ask-first rule on anything it could send, change, or delete.
   You should be able to point at the key’s home, and at the rule.
   If you don’t: do not publish. Revoke what you are not sure of.

What “done” looks like: a connection or a plugin you can name, tested, published only after a yes, with secrets out of the chat. What still needs a yes: every publish, and any later action that changes the other service.

> **Caution.** A custom connection lives, in practice, in a workshop whose computer is shared. Do not place a credential on that computer if another Bot on your account should not use it. Chapter 2, “A Teammate with a Computer,” and Chapter 17, “Safety, Privacy, Money, and What to Do When It Breaks,” already said this. The power-user corner does not get a new wall.

## A template you share safely

A template is a recipe, not a meal. Officially, you share a template when someone else should start from the same Bot. The recipient gets a copy on their account. They never get your computer, your logins, or your conversation history.^22^ That is the official kindness, and it is also the official limit: the link exposes the Bot’s shared configuration — its identity, description, skills, and routines. You remove API keys, internal URLs, customer data, and anything else you would not put in a public document, before you share.^23^ Logins do not travel. Secrets should not travel either, and the way to be sure is to take them out, yourself, and then look at what the template still holds.

A new template link is public, unless your team has limited sharing. Enterprise teams start with template sharing limited to the team. If your team does not allow public templates, or has turned sharing off, the app will say so, and it may not offer “Create a copy.”^24^ Adding a shared template accepts the third-party bot terms shown at that point. Shared templates are created by other users, not by Grok Bot.^25^ The Marketplace, at [https://x.ai/bot/marketplace](https://x.ai/bot/marketplace), is the official gallery of prebuilt Bots and templates; I named a few of them earlier, including Tradbot in Chapter 13, and tinkabot just above.^26^ Evaluate a template the way you would evaluate any tool you did not write: read what is in it before you add it.

**How to share a template safely**

1. Open the Bot you mean to share a recipe of. Before you touch Share, walk its skills, its memory, its routines, and its description, and remove anything you would not put in a public document: API keys, internal URLs, customer data, a person’s private facts, a webhook secret, a household address.
   You should be able to say, out loud, what will be in the recipe.
   If you can’t: do not share yet. Ask the Bot to show you what it would include, and to strip what should not travel. Then look yourself.

2. On desktop, click the Bot’s name at the top of the chat to open its details, choose Share, then choose “Create a copy.” The Bot builds a template and posts it in the chat. Choose “Publish” on the template card, then “Copy link.” On a phone, the official path is the Bot’s menu, then “Share as Template.”^27^
   You should see a preview you can read, and a link you have not sent yet.
   If you don’t: if the app does not offer “Create a copy,” your team may have limited or turned off template sharing. That is an official stop, not a broken button. Do not invent a second path.

3. Open the preview, or “View template details,” and read it once more, as if you were the recipient, or a stranger who found a public link. If anything is still there that should not be, go back, strip it, and, if you have already published, use “Update template” only after the strip, or “Delete Template” to turn the link off.^28^
   You should be willing to have anyone who can open that link see exactly this.
   If you don’t: Delete Template. A recipe you are unsure of is a recipe you should not hand out.

4. Send the link only to the person you meant, or, if it is public by design, accept that anyone with the link can open it. Tell the recipient that they are getting a copy, not your Bot, and that they will need the Grok Bot app to finish adding it. Remind yourself that this is not a Team Bot: a Team Bot is one Bot the team talks to, from Chapter 15; a template is a copy each person customizes.^29^
   You should know which of those two you meant.
   If you don’t: stop, and go back to Chapter 15 if what you wanted was a shared teammate, not a recipe.

What “done” looks like: a template whose details you have read, with secrets stripped, a share scope you chose, and a recipient who gets a copy without your computer or your logins. What still needs a yes: the publish, and any later “Update template” that would push new changes.

> **Caution.** A public page and a public template are different doors, and both need a look. A page on `pages.grok.bot` can be opened with no sign-in. A public template link exposes configuration. Neither is a place for a customer’s file, a child’s name, or a key.

## A quarterly look, and a Bot that has grown two jobs

A practice that lasts is a practice you review. I like a quarterly pass because it is slow enough to be kind, and often enough to catch a Bot that has quietly grown a second job, a routine you no longer want, a login that should have been signed out, or a page that is more public than you remember.

You can pick a date that already means something — the first of a quarter, or a quiet Friday, or the week after a usage reset, so Chapter 17’s dashboard is in your hand at the same sitting. You do not need a formal meeting with yourself. You need a list, a look at the safety card, and an hour in which you will not also try to invent a new fleet. If the hour only gets through the starred Bots and the Vault, that is a complete hour. The rest waits until the next pass, or until a Tuesday makes it urgent, which is a worse time, and a fine reason to do a little now.

**How to do a quarterly practice review**

1. List your Bots, by job, on a short page or on paper. One sentence each. If a Bot’s sentence has an “and” in the middle — “calendar and bills and the shop and the videos” — put a star next to it. That is a candidate to split.
   You should see a list you can read in a few minutes.
   If you don’t: ask your primary Bot to list your Bots and what it thinks each one does, then correct the list yourself. Memory is not a source of truth; look, and edit.

2. Walk the safety card from Chapter 17, “Safety, Privacy, Money, and What to Do When It Breaks”: Ask-first still on, Auto Review still on, Vault looked at, no card saved if you do not want unattended payment, shared computer still treated as shared, notifications on for Bots that run routines. Open the usage dashboard and note the reset, the way Chapter 17 taught, without treating a thin week as a moral fact.
   You should be able to point at each item in your settings.
   If you don’t: fix the first one you cannot point at, this week, not “sometime.” Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,” is the first-week version; this is the repeat.

3. For each routine, ask whether you still want it, at this frequency, in this time zone. Pause the ones you do not. For each connected app, ask whether you still want it connected. For each page you have shared, check who can view it. For each template link you have published, look at “View template details” again.
   You should have fewer doors than you had when you were enthusiastic in week two, or the same doors, chosen again.
   If you don’t: pause first, delete later. Officially, if you may need a Bot’s work later, hide it from the sidebar rather than delete it. Deleting erases its profile, conversations, memory, routines, and attachments, and cannot be undone; files and sign-ins on the shared computer can remain, and you clean those up separately.^30^

4. Split or retire. If a Bot has two jobs, make a second Bot for the second job, and give the first Bot its original sentence back. If a Bot has no job you still want, hide it, or, if you are sure, delete it and then clean the computer: sign out, remove sensitive files, revoke a connector at the source, the way Chapter 17 and Chapter 2 described.
   You should have one job per Bot you still talk to.
   If you don’t: split the starred ones first. You do not have to finish the whole workshop in an afternoon.

What “done” looks like: a list of Bots with one job each, a safety card you have walked again, routines and shares you still mean, and a computer you have not left holding a login it no longer needs. What still needs a yes: any deletion, any revoke you are unsure of, and any new share.


![The mastery loop. Try, then a skill, then a routine, then a review, and back. You still say yes.](book/art/diagrams/I31.png){width=100%}

*I31. The mastery loop. Try, then a skill, then a routine, then a review, and back. You still say yes.*


*Mastery loop. Try, skill, routine, a living Page, a review. The loop is a practice, not a graduation, and the yes does not leave the picture. (Chapter 18.)*

I want to say one more plain thing about “mastery,” because the word is in this chapter’s title and I do not want it to puff up. Mastery, here, is not a certificate, and it is not a claim that you will never get a Tuesday notice, or an expired approval, or a draft that does not sound like you. Mastery is a loop you are willing to walk again. You try a job. You save a skill when it is good. You put a tested skill on a routine. You let a Page stay current. You look, once a quarter, at whether the workshop is still yours. You split a Bot that has grown two jobs. You keep the yes.

At SMF Works, that loop is how I stopped being the middle of every handoff without giving the ends away. Charlie still runs a brief. A Monday Page is still current. Video Studio still waits for me to watch a cut, as Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,” already told you. The fleet is a fleet. I am still at the door.

I want to describe that “no longer middle-man” feeling honestly, because it can be misunderstood as a disappearance. I have not disappeared. I still approve a merge. I still watch a cut. I still read a brief and decide what matters today. What I no longer do, most Mondays, is assemble the picture from scratch: asking each Bot what it has, pasting the pieces into a document, being the hallway through which every status has to walk. The Page is the hallway now, or the table. The people — and the Bots — still do the work. I still walk up to the table. I just do not have to build the table every Monday before I can sit down. That is a quieter kind of mastery than the word usually gets, and it is the kind I trust.

A Bot that has grown two jobs is the other half of this honesty. It happens the way a kitchen drawer happens. You open it to put in a spoon, and a year later it holds string, a manual, and three things you meant to sort. A Bot that began as “household calendar” and is now also bills, also shopping, also a travel folder, is a drawer. It will still try. It will also blur. Splitting it is not a reprimand. It is the same kindness as Chapter 3, “Get In,” when I asked you to give a first Bot one clear job. The quarterly review is just a date on which you look, and say, “this is two jobs,” and make a second Bot, and feel a little lighter, the way a sorted drawer is lighter.

If initiative offers something new, I treat it like a first task: small, reversible, watched. If a template looks useful, I read the details before I add it. If a connector would be clever, I ask whether I need it. If a Bot has become a drawer of leftover jobs, I give it one sentence, or I give it a rest.

You do not have to adopt every piece of this chapter. A Monday Page without MCP is a complete practice. A quarterly review without a Marketplace template is a complete practice. A single Bot with a single job, a Vault you have looked at, and a yes you have not given away — that was a complete practice in Chapter 4, and it still is. The rest is a workshop you can grow into, and a workshop you can also keep small.

The coda is next, and it is short. I will not offer you a course, and I will not offer you a next book to buy. I will offer you a small handoff, tomorrow, and the same sentence I have been saying since the preface: you are still the one who says yes.


# Coda
You are still the one who says yes.

I have said that sentence since the preface, and I want it to be the last full sentence you hear from me as well, not because I could not think of a grander ending, but because it is the true one. A teammate with a computer can finish a great deal. It can sort a night’s mail, fill a Monday Page, join a meeting, take a script and make a video, bring a book back bound. It can also send, pay, publish, and speak in a room you have not agreed to. The difference is almost never a secret trick. The difference is a clear job, a reversible first task, a Vault instead of a password in the chat, and a yes you have not given away.

The work can come back finished. I have seen it come back finished, at a kitchen table and at SMF Works, as an EPUB and a letter PDF and a brief that was waiting while the coffee was still hot. I have also seen a card expire, and a pool run thin, and a draft that did not sound like me. Both kinds of morning are part of a practice that lasts. The finished work is a gift. The unfinished work, the expired card, the notice on a Tuesday, is a reason to keep the practice small enough to watch.

This book was a gift. I wrote it for anyone who has stared at a pile and wished a careful teammate would take the next piece, finish it, and bring it back. You do not have to buy anything. There is no course at the end of this page, and no upgrade waiting behind a link, and no “if you want more” that leads to a shop. If a free copy of these pages helped you, you can hand the file to a friend the way it was handed to you. WisdomForge may be a place to pick the file up; it is not a storefront, and this book is not for sale there or anywhere else.

I wrote it as a giveaway because the community that is already teaching itself this product — in official docs, in independent posts, in kitchen-table conversations — gave me a lot, and this is what I could give back. If you are the friend who is being handed the file, you are welcome. Start wherever the pile is. You do not have to have read the chapters in order. You do not have to finish the book in a weekend. Part 2 is enough to get safely in the door. The shelves are there when a particular kind of work arrives. The last chapters are there when you are ready, and not before. If a chapter does not fit the life you have, leave it. The gift was never a syllabus you had to complete. It was a careful friend, handing you a tool, and then getting out of the way so you could use it. I am getting out of the way now, except for this last request, which is not a request to buy, or to join, or to come back: hand something small off tomorrow, and watch it, and keep the yes.

Take what helps. Leave the rest.

If all you take is Part 2 — a plan that actually includes Grok Bot, a first real task, a Vault, an Ask-first rule — that is a complete taking. If you take a family Page from Chapter 13, “Get Your Life Back,” and a Pay button that waits from Chapter 14, “Errands That Finish,” that is a complete taking. If you take a Team Bot who does not read anyone’s private chat, from Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team,” that is a complete taking. If you take only the line from Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,” and a sketch on paper with a “never,” and you never draw a second desk, that is a complete taking. If you take Chapter 17’s four facts — a shared computer, a card that can pay without asking, approvals that expire, a weekly pool that is shared — and you walk the safety card once a quarter, from Chapter 18, “A Practice That Lasts,” that is a complete taking. You do not owe this book a fleet.

Hand something small off tomorrow.

Something you can watch. A digest you will read. A folder of options, not tickets. A draft that does not go out until you say so. Watch it. Keep the yes. If it comes back finished, you will know what that feels like, and you will be able to hand off the next small thing. If it comes back needing a correction, you will also know, and the correction is part of the work, not a reason to take the pile back onto your own shoulders alone.

I almost said thank you out loud to an empty room, the first morning Charlie left a brief on my desk. I told you that at the start. I will tell you, at the end, that I still almost do, and that I still look at the brief before anything goes out the door. The feeling did not wear off because I learned the product. The feeling stayed because the practice stayed: a job I can name, a routine I tested, a yes I have not given away. If tomorrow’s small handoff gives you even a little of that feeling — a table that is a little clearer, a Tuesday that starts with a list instead of a pile — then this gift did what I hoped it would do.

You will not need every chapter. You will not need every platform. You may never invite a Bot to a meeting, or draw a sketch with a second desk, or walk a Marketplace template. That is all right. A single Bot, with a single job, and a person who still says yes, is already a team that can work while you sleep, in the only sense I am willing to mean that phrase: a teammate you still review, on a computer you still treat as shared, with a pool you still look at, and a door you still stand in.

I am glad you came this far. I am glad you opened a free book and stayed for the cautions as well as for the mornings that are already a little lighter. A careful guide should not sit behind a cash register, and a careful teammate should not sit behind a yes you did not mean. You have the guide. You have the teammate, if you choose one. You have the door, and you are standing in it.

You are still the one who says yes. That is the whole point. That is enough.


# Appendix A — Glossary

These are the words this book uses on purpose. For each one I give a plain sentence first, then the official name, so you can recognize it in the product and in the notes. Where a word belongs to another product — Hermes Agent, OpenClaw — I say so. Those are not Grok Bot features. They appear in Chapter 16 because they are platforms in my own workshop, described at the level official pages and my own wiring allow.

**Approval card.** A notice that asks for your yes before a Bot takes a sensitive action, and then waits. Official docs call this an approval card; the buttons may say Allow once, Always allow, Deny, Allow, Not now, or Skip, depending on the card.

**Ask first.** A rule you write in plain words that always stops a matching action and shows you a card. Official name: Ask first, under Custom Rules.

**Allow automatically.** A rule that lets a matching action proceed when Auto Review does not have another reason to stop it. Official name: Allow automatically. If an Ask-first rule and an Allow-automatically rule both match, Ask first wins.

**Auto Review.** A built-in check that looks at an action before a Bot runs a command, uses an app, or acts on its computer. It is on by default. Official name: Auto Review (sometimes Auto-review in settings). Turning it off means the Bot runs without those checks.

**Bot.** A named teammate, in Grok Bot, that you can give a job. Official name: Bot. Most Bots work best with one clear job.

**Command line.** The part of a computer where you type instructions instead of clicking. Official docs say the cloud computer has a command line, and that command-line credentials are shared. This book does not invent commands. In Chapter 16, “via CLI” means through that command line, at the level I described, not as a recipe.

**Connect apps.** A structured way to let a Bot work in a service you already use, such as Gmail or a calendar, which official docs call more reliable than clicking through a website. Official name: Connect apps. A connected app is account-wide: every Bot on your account can use it.

**Connector / MCP.** A connector is a connection to a service. MCP is a standard way AI tools connect to a service; official docs say a Bot can add an MCP server, and that a marketplace Bot can wrap an API. This book treats custom MCP as a power-user corner, in Chapter 18, not as a first-week skill.

**Cursor.** The account you use to sign in to Grok Bot, and the company through which the cloud computer and many admin and privacy settings run. Official wording: Grok Bot uses your Cursor account, so there is no separate login.

**Custom Rules.** Your own list of Ask-first and Allow-automatically rules, written in plain words. Official name: Custom Rules, in Settings → General, under Bot.

**Filesystem (files that stay).** A place on the cloud computer for files, which persist between tasks and are visible to every Bot on your account. Official docs name a browser, a command line, and a filesystem.

**Grok (the chat app).** A separate conversation app from SpaceXSI. Official FAQ: Grok (the chat app from SpaceXSI) and Grok Bot are two different apps. A limit in the Grok app is not a statement about Grok Bot.

**Grok Bot.** A separate app: a proactive AI teammate with a real computer in the cloud. Official overview: “Grok Bot is your proactive AI teammate,” “an agent with access to a real computer in the cloud.” Made by SpaceXSI, formerly xAI; signs in with a Cursor account.

**Grok Bot 1.0.** A major update to Grok Bot, released on 11 November 2026 in the body of this book, from the official @SpaceXSI post and the official news page. It added multiplayer teammates, proactive initiative, and more capable browser, documents, Pages, and approvals. Team Bots: Team and Enterprise plans. Meetings: Team, Enterprise, and Ultra.

**Group chat.** A conversation that can include more than one Bot. Official best practice: make one Bot responsible for each step, and @ one Bot, not everyone. Approval cards may not appear in the group chat; check the Bot’s own chat. This book does not state a size cap; official docs should be checked for that number.

**Hermes Agent.** An AI agent built by Nous Research, free and open source under the MIT license, with a command-line tool and a desktop app. It is not a Grok Bot feature. Official site: hermes-agent.nousresearch.com. This book is not a Hermes manual and is not affiliated with or endorsed by Nous Research. In Chapter 16, Charlie talks to SMF Works’ Hermes Fleet via CLI, and Video Studio works with several Hermes Agents via webhook, only as I described that wiring.

**Initiative.** A Bot offering to take on work, rather than only waiting for a request, and asking before risky actions. Official 1.0 name: proactive teammates, or initiative. The primary Bot is the one that reaches out.

**Meetings.** A Bot joining a video call — official docs name Google Meet, Zoom, and Microsoft Teams — to listen, take notes, and send a summary. Available on Team, Enterprise, and Ultra plans, as of the 1.0 news page.

**Memory.** The lasting things a Bot keeps — preferences, rules you asked it to follow, a dated work log — so it does not start from zero every morning. Official name: memory. A few facts, such as your name and time zone, are shared by all your Bots. Memory is not a Vault, and official docs say it is not a source of truth for numbers that change. Chapter 6 is the full chapter.

**OpenClaw.** A self-hosted AI assistant that runs on your own hardware, stewarded by the OpenClaw Foundation. It is not a Grok Bot feature. Official site: openclaw.ai. Official homepage language includes “No subscription. No hosted tier. No token.” This book is not an OpenClaw manual and is not affiliated with or endorsed by the OpenClaw Foundation. In Chapter 16, Charlie talks to SMF Works’ OpenClaw Fleet via CLI, only as I described that wiring.

**Order card.** A shopping summary a Bot shows you — store, items, ship-to, pay-with, total — so you can Pay or Cancel. Official name: order card. Only the Bot’s owner can pay its orders. Chapter 14 walks this.

**Page (Pages).** An interactive report or dashboard a Bot can build and update, including from a routine. Official name: Pages. As of docs opened on 11 November 2026, Pages were rolling out gradually and were not available on Enterprise teams yet; re-check the live docs.

**Primary Bot.** Your main point of contact, usually starred: the Bot that checks in, can coordinate other Bots, and is the one that takes initiative. Official name: primary Bot. A Team Bot cannot be your primary Bot.

**Prompt injection.** Hidden or untrusted text, on a page or in a message, that tries to look like an instruction from you. Official docs: only your own messages count as instructions; Auto Review lowers this risk but does not remove it.

**Routine.** A workflow assigned to one Bot, told when to run — on a schedule, or after an event such as a message, a mail to the Bot’s address, or a webhook. Official name: routine. A new scheduled routine waits for its next time; a test run does real work. Chapter 8 is the full chapter.

**Secure Form.** A form the Bot shows you so you can type a secret that goes straight into a page, never into the chat, and never shown to the Bot. Official name: Secure Form.

**Shared cloud computer.** The persistent computer in the cloud that all of your Bots use, with a browser, a command line, and files that stay between tasks. Official wording: “Your Bots work from a persistent cloud computer…” It is assigned per user, not per Bot. Official FAQ: “Do not use separate Bots as a security boundary.” Command-line credentials are shared. Do not place a credential or file on it if another Bot on your account should not use it.

**Shared weekly pool.** The weekly allowance, under The X Subscription, that Grok, Grok Bot, and Cursor draw from together. Official wording: Grok Bot usage draws from the weekly shared pool across Grok, Grok Bot, and Cursor. There is not a single published dollar or token number; official pages give multipliers and point to the live dashboard.

**Skill.** A reusable recipe for how to do a task — steps, rules, output, and what needs approval. Official name: skill. Chapter 7 is the full chapter. In this book, a skill has a “never” line.

**SpaceXSI (SpaceX Super Intelligence LLC).** The company that makes Grok and Grok Bot, formerly known as xAI. Official site brand: SpaceXSI. Legal name: SpaceX Super Intelligence LLC.

**Take control.** You temporarily drive the Bot’s cloud computer yourself, for a step only a person can finish — single sign-on, a passkey, a CAPTCHA, a QR code. Official path: Open computer, then Take control, then tell the Bot to continue. Never paste the secret into the chat.

**Teach a Task.** An official way, when it is available, to record a browser workflow of up to ten minutes and turn it into a draft skill. Official name: Teach a Task. Rollout may be gradual; this book does not assert who has it on which plan. Chapter 7 returns to it.

**Team Bot.** A Bot a whole human team can work with, available on Team and Enterprise plans. Official name: Team Bots. Chapter 15 is the full chapter.

**Template.** A shareable copy of a Bot’s setup, not its logins or history. Official docs: strip secrets before you share. Marketplace Bots are related starting points, already set up for a job.

**The X Subscription.** One subscription, introduced 12 October 2026, that can cover Grok, Cursor, and X. New customers start here. Grok Bot is included on Premium, Super, and Ultra, not on Free or Plus. Switching from a legacy plan is optional and, once done, permanent; official docs say Support cannot undo it.

**Unattended approval.** An approval for work that started without you — a routine, or a message from another Bot — which official FAQ says expires after about thirty minutes. Approvals in a chat you are in wait for you.

**Vault.** A locker for logins, payment cards, and contact info, which fills secrets into a page without the Bot seeing them. Official name: Vault, in Settings → Vault. It is account-wide. A saved card fills in at checkout without asking you each time; if you want to approve every payment, do not save the card. Never paste a password or card into the chat.

**Webhook.** An official way to start a routine by sending an HTTP POST to a URL the desktop shows you, with a secret key as an Authorization Bearer token, and an optional JSON body. A 200 response means the run was accepted, not that the work has finished. Test runs do real work. Chapter 8 teaches this; Chapter 16 uses it, at the level I described, without inventing a payload. Do not print secrets in a book or a chat you do not mean to share.

**1Password.** A password manager official Grok Bot docs still describe as a connected option, used after the Vault and before a Secure Form, with your approval. If you do not use it, you can ignore this door.

For company and product names, and for how this book uses trademarks, see Appendix E. For starter prompts, see Appendix B. For checklists, see Appendix C. For dated official URLs, see Appendix D.


# Appendix B — Cookbook

This appendix is a shelf of starter prompts: most of them official, a few teaching drafts from the chapters you just read, each with a “never” line. It is not a bag of tricks, and it is not a set of secret instructions from my own fleet. If a prompt is official, I say so. If it is a teaching draft I wrote for this book, I say that too. I have not printed any hidden system prompt, and I have not invented a command or a webhook body for another platform.

Use them as starting sentences. Make them yours. Test on a safe input, with you in the room, before you put anything on a clock. Chapter 7, “Show It Once,”, and Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed,”, are the home of that rule. A prompt is a recipe’s first line, not a reason to skip the watch-along.

Every item below has a “never.” If you take only one habit from this shelf, take that one. Back the “never” with an Ask-first rule for anything you cannot undo, as Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,”, taught. Memory is not a gate.

## How to read a card on this shelf

Each card has a name, a prompt you can almost paste, a “never,” and a pointer to the chapter that sits with it properly. When official wording already includes “don’t send” or “don’t publish,” I keep that wording and still label the “never,” so you can see it at a glance.

Do not put a password, a card number, a key, or another person’s private life into any of these prompts. Secrets belong in the Vault, not in a cookbook and not in a chat. Chapter 6, “Memory,”, is the postcard test; it applies here.

A webhook card will tell you to add a trigger and to treat a `200` as accepted, not finished. It will not print a key, and it will not invent a JSON schema.^1^

## Memory

**What do you remember?**

> What do you remember about me?

**Never:** Do not treat the answer as a source of truth for a number that changes, and do not leave a secret or a medical note on the list — ask the Bot to forget those. Official path: there is no memory screen for a personal Bot; you ask in chat.^2^

From Chapter 6, “Memory.”

**Remember this.**

> Remember that I am out every Friday. Keep morning briefs short. From now on, never send external messages without my approval. Please remember that, and tell me what you will keep.

**Never:** Do not ask it to remember a password, a card, a health detail you would not put on a postcard, or someone else’s private data. Logins and cards belong in the Vault.^2^

**Forget this.**

> Forget that I am on the launch team. Then tell me what you remember about me, so I can see that it is gone.

**Never:** If what you are removing was a secret that was exposed, forgetting is a cleanup, not a complete fix. Change the key or the password. Check the shared computer, as Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,”, already asked.

**Look it up again.**

> For this number, look it up in the current source. Do not rely on memory. Cite what you opened. If you cannot open it, say so.

**Never:** Do not make a consequential decision — a payment, a public post, a figure you will stand behind — from a remembered price or a remembered balance.^2^

## Skills and teaching

**Save a skill after a good run.** (Official shape.^1^)

> Save the process we just used as a skill called “Weekly account health.” Include the source systems, risk definitions, output format, and the rule that customer contact always requires approval.

Swap the name and the rule for your job. A useful skill, officially, states when to use it, inputs and access, the sequence, how to validate, what to return, and what requires approval.

**Never:** Do not save a skill without a “never.” Do not write a secret into the card. Do not make one giant “do all my marketing” skill. Test once while you watch before any routine.

**Weekly inbox triage.** (Teaching card, in the spirit of the official inbox prompt.^3^)

> Run Weekly inbox triage on unread mail from the last twenty-four hours. Sort into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI. Draft replies to the urgent ones in my voice. For “needs a reply,” give me a one-line recommendation. For FYI, a count and three subject lines. Flag bills, school forms, and anything that needs a signature.

**Never:** Never send. Never delete. Never unsubscribe. Never forward. Leave drafts unsent.

**Teach a Task, when you have it.** (Official steps, not a paste-prompt.^1^)

When Teach a Task is available, open a one-to-one chat and the computer view, choose Teach a Task, describe the result, perform the workflow once (up to ten minutes; no microphone audio), stop, review the draft skill, and add the “never” and the failure rules one example will not show. Rollout may be gradual; if you do not see the control, ask the Bot to create a skill from your written instructions and the completed task.^4^

**Never:** Do not demonstrate with a live payment, a customer record, or a production toggle. Do not expose secrets during the recording. Use the Vault or a Secure Form, as Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, taught.

## Routines, mail, and webhooks

**A weekday morning brief.** (Official spirit; teaching wording.^1^,^3^)

> Every weekday at 8:00 AM, in my Bot time zone, run the morning brief skill. Leave the draft in this conversation. Do not send, book, or pay. If a source is unavailable, report the failure; do not use old data or memory in its place.

**Never:** Do not send. Remember: a new routine waits for its next scheduled time; it will not run at creation. Use Test when you want a run now, and know that a test does real work.^1^

**A narrow Slack or ticket trigger.** (Official shape.^1^)

> When a message in #customer-escalations contains a support ticket link and the phrase “needs repro,” open the ticket, reproduce the issue in staging, and post a repro pack in this conversation. Never post back to Slack without approval.

**Never:** Never post back without approval. Never use a broad listener such as “every new message.” Narrow the channel and the phrase, or you will burn usage and act on noise.^1^

**A receipts alias, log only.**

> When mail arrives at my receipts alias, run the receipts-log skill. Log the item on the list. If you cannot read an attachment, say so.

**Never:** Never pay, never approve, never file a payment. Claiming an address at `mail.grokbot.com` is permanent in the official sense: one address, for good, never reissued, cannot be renamed or deleted. Claim it only on purpose, after an approval card, as Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”, taught.^5^

**Add a webhook trigger.** (Official fields only.^1^)

> Please add a webhook trigger to this routine. Then show me, on desktop, the Webhook section: the POST URL, the key field, and the full header. I will copy what I need. Do not paste the key into this chat again, and do not write it into a document I might share.

**Never:** Never print the key in a page, a screenshot, a group chat, or a skill card. Senders use `Authorization: Bearer <key>`. An optional JSON body may be sent; the Bot receives the body with the routine instruction. A `200` means accepted and started, not finished — check the chat. Any other response: confirm the routine is not paused and that the key is current. This book will not invent a payload schema. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,”, uses this doorbell at the level of a story, not a protocol.

## A chief of staff

**Official morning briefing.**^3^

> Sum up what changed since yesterday in my inbox, calendar, Slack, and docs, and flag what needs me. Draft it as a post for #team; don’t send it.

**Never:** Don’t send it. Don’t book. Don’t pay. A note in the chat is a complete brief if you do not want a #team draft.

**Official inbox manager.**^3^

> Sort my unread email into urgent, needs a reply, and FYI. Draft replies to the urgent ones and don’t send anything.

**Never:** Don’t send anything. Don’t delete. Don’t unsubscribe.

**Official meeting prep.**^3^

> Prep me for my 2 PM: who is attending, what we last discussed, and any open threads. Keep it to one page.

**Never:** Do not invent a quote. Do not mail the prep to the attendees. If you are not sure who said something, don’t attribute it. See also Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor,”, and Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team.”

**Official Sunday calendar plan.**^3^

> Every Sunday evening, read the week ahead, flag clashes and packed days, and leave me a short plan for Monday.

**Never:** Never book, never invite, never cancel, never move a shared block without a yes. “Send you a plan” means a note to you.

**A chief’s job, teaching draft.** (Mine for this book, not a secret from SMF Works; edit until it is yours. From Chapter 9, “Your Digital Chief of Staff.”)

> You are my chief of staff. Your job is to coordinate, not to hoard. Each weekday, prepare a short morning brief from the inbox, calendar, and other apps I have connected. Every item has a source, a why, and a suggested next step. Flag what needs me. When a job repeats and has its own rules, recommend a specialist Bot, or hand work to one we already have. Keep a follow-up list. Never send, never publish, never book, never pay, never delete, never merge. If a source is missing, say so; do not fill from memory. If you are unsure, ask. I am the one who says yes. Please remember this, and read it back.

**Never:** The nevers are in the prompt. Back them with Ask-first. Do not create three specialists in the same sitting unless you mean to; one chief, one brief, one week is enough.

**A follow-up list that does not send.**

> Keep a follow-up list for me. Each item has a person or a thread, a suggested next step, and a draft if a draft would help. Add this thread to the list, and draft the reply. Don’t send. On Friday at 4:00 PM, show me the open follow-ups, oldest first, and do not send any of them, and do not nudge anyone outside this chat.

**Never:** Never send, never invite, never post the follow-up. “Reached out” is a send unless you can see that it was not.

## Growth work

**Official sales development (inbound).**^3^

> Qualify this morning's inbound leads against our ideal customer profile. Draft intro replies with times I'm free for good fits and a polite no for the rest; don't send anything.

**Never:** Don’t send anything. Don’t update a CRM field you would not want touched, unless that is a separate, tested skill. Read the list; do not “approve all” out of tiredness. Chapter 10, “Growth Work.”

**Official content marketer.**^3^

> Pull what we shipped this week from the changelog and Slack, and draft the newsletter and three posts in our voice. Bring me the drafts; don’t publish anything.

**Never:** Don’t publish. Don’t invent a metric or a quote. Park the drafts. You publish, or you say yes to a specific post and watch.

**Official paid media.**^3^

> Compare this month's spend by campaign with our budget and target CAC, and recommend where to move money. Don't change any budgets.

**Never:** Don’t change any budgets, bids, statuses, creatives, or payment methods. Don’t launch a campaign. Look up current targets; do not use a remembered number. Unattended spend is not a routine. Do not save a card for this job if you do not want a payment to be possible. Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,”, and Chapter 10, “Growth Work”.

**Official talent scout.**^3^

> Find 20 candidates for this role who meet the must-haves and aren't already in our ATS. Draft outreach in my voice; don't contact anyone.

**Never:** Don’t contact anyone. Don’t schedule until you approve. Don’t reject a person in the ATS. Don’t invent a work history or a compliment you cannot source. Twenty is the official example’s number, not a quota; a smaller list you will actually read is better. If the ATS cannot be reached, stop and say so. People’s careers are not a sandbox.

**A campaign trio, proposed first.** (Official team; teaching ask.^3^)

> Please propose three one-job Bots for a campaign team, in the official shape: Sign-ups (last week’s sign-ups by channel), Launch drafts (a launch note and three posts from the numbers), Fact check (against the numbers and our brand doc). Show me the jobs and the nevers. Don’t create them until I say yes. Don’t publish. Don’t invent a number or a quote.

**Never:** Don’t publish. Don’t create the Bots until you say yes. The fact-check Bot is a reviewer, not a cheerleader.

**Overnight outbound, review list.** (Official Sales Outbound spirit.^6^)

> Tonight, in my Bot time zone, run the outbound skill on today’s list. Leave a review list in this chat, with sources and drafts in my voice. Skip anyone already marked do-not-contact. Don’t send email. Don’t send a LinkedIn message.

**Never:** Don’t send. A login to LinkedIn or mail sits on a shared computer; Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully”, still applies. Reputation is the quiet cost of skipping the yes.

## The shop floor

**Official invoice coordinator.**^3^

> Match this week's invoices in the finance inbox to their purchase orders. List the ones that don't match and draft a note to each owner; don't approve or pay anything.

**Never:** Don’t approve. Don’t pay. Don’t send the notes until you say yes. A saved card can pay without asking; if you do not want that, do not save the card.^7^ Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor.”

**Official expense manager.**^3^

> Build this week's expense summary, match receipts from the finance inbox, and flag policy exceptions. Draft one follow-up per owner; don't send them.

**Never:** Don’t send. Don’t pay. Don’t reimburse unattended. If a receipt is missing, say so; do not fill a gap from memory.

**Official month-end close, proposed first.**^3^

> Please propose the official month-end trio — Invoices, Expenses, Exceptions — with jobs and nevers, and create them only after I say yes. On the first of the month, leave a folder of matches, exceptions, and notes for my sign-off. Don’t approve. Don’t pay.

**Never:** Don’t pay. You sign. You pay what you mean to pay. Test first on last month’s already-finished files if you can.

**Official support-queue shape, and a refund gate.**^3^

> Sort new tickets and draft replies. Flag refunds, legal or safety issues, and bugs that need staging. If you propose a refund, check it against the policy document I named, and leave a recommendation. Don’t send a reply. Don’t issue a refund. Don’t use one customer’s data to test another’s bug.

**Never:** Don’t send. Don’t refund without a yes, and only inside the policy. Don’t post publicly. Angry, legal, or safety tickets are a person’s job: flag and stop.

**Official bug reproduction.**^3^

> Reproduce this bug in staging with a fresh test account. Send me the exact steps, screenshots, and browser details; don't use customer data.

**Never:** Don’t use customer data. Don’t use a production login. Don’t merge. Don’t deploy. Don’t change production. Credentials on the cloud computer are shared; do not place a production secret there if another Bot should not use it.^8^

**Official product performance.**^3^

> Find out why checkout got slower after yesterday's release. Send me the likeliest hotspot with screenshots and links, and don't change production settings.

**Never:** Don’t change production settings. Don’t restart a service. Don’t “just bump” a limit. A memo is the product. A weekly export you attach is a complete input.

**Official account health.**^3^

> Rank the accounts in my book by churn risk and expansion potential from usage, tickets, and renewal dates. Suggest a next step for each; don't contact anyone.

**Never:** Don’t contact anyone. A “check-in” is a contact. If a source is missing, say so.

**A recap that does not invent a quote.** (Teaching, in the spirit of official Meeting Recap Deck’s “never invent a quote.”^9^)

> Summarize these notes into decisions, action items with owners, and open questions. Do not invent a quote. If you are not sure who said something, don’t attribute it. Leave a short document for my review; don’t send it to the attendees.

**Never:** Don’t invent a quote. Don’t send unattended. For a Bot that joins a live meeting, see Chapter 15, “Bots on Your Human Team”, and check your plan (meetings: Team, Enterprise, and Ultra, as of the 1.0 announcement).^10^

**A folder for counsel, not a lawyer.** (Teaching; this book is not legal advice.)

> List the contracts in this folder by date and owner. Flag any date in the next sixty days. Pull the clause I named from the document I pointed at, and quote only what is there. Leave a folder for counsel.

**Never:** Never send a legal letter. Never file. Never waive. Never tell me a clause is “fine.” Never put a privileged document in a public Page. If a matter needs a lawyer, a lawyer should have it. Chapter 11, “The Shop Floor”.

**A questionnaire or portal, parked.** (Official illustrations: Security Questionnaire Filler parks the submit; Vendor Portal Operator returns exceptions.^6^)

> Draft the fields from the trust document and the past answers I pointed at. If a field is unknown, leave it blank and flag it. Don’t invent an answer. Don’t submit. Don’t renew.

**Never:** Don’t submit. Don’t invent. Don’t renew unattended. A portal login is shared on the computer.

## A small fleet

**Sketch, then propose, then create.** (Teaching, from Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet.”)

> Here is my fleet sketch: a chief who coordinates and never publishes; a writer who drafts and never publishes and never invents a source; a marketer who parks copy and never publishes; a programmer who prepares EPUB, PDF, and markdown and a pull request, and never merges alone. Please propose jobs and nevers for the specialists I don’t have yet. Show me the text. Don’t create any Bot until I say yes.

**Never:** Don’t create until you say yes. Don’t publish. Don’t merge. Don’t invent Bot names you will not want to live with. Start with two desks if seven is a crowd.

**Friday status.** (Teaching.)

> Once, now, make me a Friday status of the fleet: each title or job, whose desk it is on, what’s waiting for my yes, and any failure a person needs to see. Don’t publish it. Don’t send it outside this chat. If you don’t know, say you don’t know. Don’t invent progress.

**Never:** Don’t publish the status. Don’t merge. Don’t claim a merge you did not approve. If Pages are not available, a note in chat is complete.^11^

**A human publish.** (Teaching; also Appendix C.)

> Prepare the change for the site from these three files — EPUB, letter PDF, markdown — and open a pull request (or the equivalent proposal). Do not merge. Do not put the book on a public page. Wait for me.

**Never:** Don’t merge. Don’t publish. You hold the files, you read the copy, you merge or send. Tiredness is a reason to wait until morning, not a reason to put merge on a schedule.

## A few official starters that live on other shelves

These are official prompts I want on the same shelf, even though their full how-tos belong to later chapters. The “never” is already in the official wording, or I have added one in this book’s voice.^3^

**Official competitive intelligence.**

> Check these three competitors for anything they launched or repriced this week. Send me a short list with links and what it means for our messaging.

**Never:** Do not write as if a rumor were a fact. Cite the page you opened. Don’t publish the list. Don’t contact a competitor. If you cannot open a source, leave the item off, or mark it as unverified.

**Official travel coordinator.** (Chapter 14, “Errands That Finish,”, is the home of this work.)

> Find flights to Austin for Thursday, back Sunday, with no red-eyes, and a hotel near downtown. Send me the two best options; don't book anything.

**Never:** Don’t book anything. Don’t pay. Don’t save a card for this job if you want every payment to be a yes. A recommendation is a gift. A ticket is a yes.

**Official meeting-notes to Slack, if you use Granola.**^3^

> Each evening, read the day’s notes, pull out every action item with its owner, and draft a post for my team’s Slack channel that goes out once I approve.

**Never:** Don’t send until you approve. Don’t invent an action item or an owner. Don’t invent a quote.

Use these the way you use the rest of the shelf: paste, make them yours, watch once, then maybe a clock.

## A last word on this shelf

If a prompt on this shelf and a live official page disagree, trust the live page, and date what you see. Products move. This appendix was checked against pages opened on 11 November 2026.

If you write a prompt of your own, give it a “never,” test it once, and only then consider a routine. If you share a skill or a template, strip secrets first. If you are tempted to add a Hermes or OpenClaw command “example,” don’t. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”, will not invent those either, and this cookbook follows that courtesy.

Take what helps. Leave the rest. Hand something small off tomorrow, and watch it once.


# Appendix C — Checklists

These are lists for the wall, the fridge, or the Friday you are tired. They are not a substitute for the chapters, and they are not a way to skip a “never.” If a list and a live official page disagree, trust the page, and date what you see.

Chapter 5, “Connect Carefully,”, is where Ask-first, the Vault, the shared computer, and saved cards were set. These lists point back; they do not reprint that chapter. Sending, posting, paying, deleting, and publishing stay behind a yes.

## A first week

Use this after Part 2, or as a quiet review of the week you already had.

- You chose a plan that actually includes Grok Bot, from the live pricing page, not from a memory of this book. Chapter 3, “Get In.”
- You created a first Bot with one clear job, and a name you will actually say.
- You handed off one real, bounded task, and you read what came back. Chapter 4, “Hand Off Your First Real Task.”
- You connected one low-risk app, not everything at once.
- A login, if you needed one, went in the Vault, not in the chat.
- Auto Review is on. Ask-first is on for send, post, pay, delete, and publish.
- You have not saved a card, or you saved one as a conscious choice, knowing a saved card can pay without asking.^1^
- You know the computer is shared, and that separate Bots are not a security boundary.^2^
- You asked your primary Bot, “What do you remember about me?” and you corrected or forgot what should not stay. Chapter 6, “Memory.”
- You saved one short skill, with a “never,” and you watched it once. Chapter 7, “Show It Once.”
- You have not put an untested skill on a clock. If you scheduled a brief, you tested it, knowing a test does real work, and you confirmed it did not send. Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed.”
- You looked at the usage dashboard at least once. The live number wins.

Done, for a first week, is this: one job, one careful connection, one “never,” one watched run, and a yes that is still yours. You do not need a fleet yet.

## Safety, for any week after

A short card. Walk it when you connect something new, or when a routine starts to feel too helpful.

- **Ask-first** is on for sending, posting, paying, deleting, publishing, merging, and changing production.
- **The Vault** holds logins and cards. The chat does not. Memory does not. A skill card does not.
- **The computer is shared.** A credential you place on it is, in practice, a credential other Bots on the account can use. Do not use separate Bots as a wall.^2^
- **A saved card can pay without asking.** If you do not want that, do not save the card.^1^
- **Send, post, pay, delete, publish, merge** stay behind a yes. Drafts and recommendations come first.
- **Unattended approvals** from a routine or another Bot expire after about thirty minutes. Approvals in a chat you are in wait for you.^3^ If you will be away, do not leave a payment or a publish waiting on a card.
- **Secrets in a webhook** stay secrets. Do not print a key. A `200` means accepted, not finished. Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”.
- **Auto Review** stays on unless you have a reason you can say out loud, and even then, this book would rather you left it on.

If any line on this card is a no, pause the routine, and fix the line, before you close the laptop.

## Publish or send

From Chapter 12, “A Small Fleet,”, and from every growth and shop-floor yes in Chapters 10 and 11. On the wall next to the merge button.

- I have **held the files** (EPUB, PDF, markdown, or your equivalent) and I am willing to put my name on them.
- I have **read the copy** that will be public. If this is a gift, there is no price, no cart, no upsell. Nothing implies an endorsement the maker did not give.
- I have **reviewed the actual proposal** — the pull request, the draft post, the staged folder — not only the promise of one.
- **I** will merge, or send, or press live. Not a routine. Not a chief being helpful at 8:00 on a Friday.
- If I ask a Bot to click after I say “this exact request, now,” I will **stay and watch**. I will not put that click on a clock.
- After it is live, I will **glance as a stranger** would, and if something is wrong, I will fix it with a new proposal and a new yes.

Tiredness is a reason to wait until morning. It is not a reason to skip this list.

## If a routine did not run

Official checks, in one place.^4^

- The routine is not **Paused**. On the phone, **Active** is on.
- **When to run** shows the schedule or trigger I expect, in the **right time zone**.
- A new schedule **waits for its next time**. It does not run at creation. Use **Test** to run it now, and remember a test does real work.
- For Slack: the message is **new**, in the **channel** the trigger watches, and **matches the phrase**.
- For a webhook: the call returned **`200`** and used the **current key**. Any other response: the run did not start.
- Then look at **Approvals**. A card that expired after about thirty minutes means the action did not run. That can look like a missed routine. It is a missed yes.
- Then look at **usage**, and at rate limits. A failure that says a rate limit was exceeded may need a narrower trigger, a wider schedule, or a manual rerun; some such failures are a documented known issue and are not retried automatically.^4^

If you are unsure, pause, and ask the Bot what happened, in its own chat. Do not fire a webhook in a tight loop.

## A cross-platform line

If your work already lives on more than one agent platform, do not invent a protocol from this book. Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,”, offers a paper sketch: which Grok Bot talks to which, with a “never,” and with you at the final yes. The official webhook how-to lives in Chapter 8, “Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed”; Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms”, uses it and does not reprint it. There is no checklist here that replaces that sketch, and no sample command to another platform. Stay official. Keep secrets off the chat and off a shared computer you do not mean to share. Test. Keep the yes.

## After a long time away

Officially, Grok Bot may ask whether to keep routines running after a long period away, and may pause them if there is no response.^4^ When you come back:

- Review which routines are **Paused**, on purpose or because the product asked.
- Read **When to run** and the **Instruction** again, including the “never.”
- Look at **run history** if you have it: Running, Succeeded, Failed, and why.
- Check **Approvals**, then **usage**, before you turn everything back on.
- **Test** one routine, on a safe input, before you resume a set.
- Say hello to the chief’s brief. If it is wrong, correct it, as Chapter 6, “Memory”, taught, before you trust a Monday.

A pause is a complete answer. Resuming is a yes.

## A last look

You can run a complete practice with the first four lists and never need the fifth. You can also, on a quiet Sunday, walk the safety card and the publish list out loud, the way you would walk a recipe’s last line. The fridge card from Chapter 7, “Show It Once”, was not a joke. These lists are that card, cut into weeks.

You are still the one who says yes. Print the safety card if a print helps. Leave the publish list next to the place you merge. The lists are a gift inside a gift. Use the ones you need, and let the others wait until the week you need them too. A list you actually walk is worth more than five lists you mean to walk. Start with safety and the first week. Add publish when you have something to publish. That is enough of a wall for now.


# Appendix D — Resources
This appendix is a pocketful of official addresses, dated 11 November 2026. It is not a second manual, and it is not a shop. When a page in this book and a live page disagree, trust the live page. Prices, plans, and features change. I have not placed any affiliate links here, and I will not. A free gift should end with doors you can walk through, not with a funnel.

You do not have to visit every address. If you are in your first week, the product page, Get started, the FAQ, and the pricing page will do. If you are connecting an app or a card, add the Vault page and the approvals page. If a Tuesday notice arrives, add the usage dashboard, the troubleshooting page, and the status page. If you are sharing a Bot with a team, add Team Bots and Meetings. If Chapter 16 sent you here, the Hermes and OpenClaw lists are at the bottom, labeled as those projects’ sites.

I have grouped the Grok Bot addresses first, because this book is a guide to Grok Bot. After those, there is a short, clearly labeled list of official Hermes Agent and OpenClaw pages. Those are those projects’ sites, not Grok Bot documentation, and they appear because Chapter 16, “Grok Bot Spans Agent Platforms,” names those platforms. This book is not affiliated with or endorsed by SpaceXSI, by Nous Research, or by the OpenClaw Foundation. Appendix E says more about names, trademarks, and how the notes in each chapter work. That appendix is not legal advice, and this list is not either.

# Appendix E — Names, Trademarks, and Sources

This appendix is a short, plain account of the names in this book, of how I have tried to use other people’s marks, of how the notes work, and of a few facts I could not close. It repeats, in short form, that this book is a free, independent, unofficial gift, not for sale, and not endorsed by the product’s maker. It is not legal advice. If you need a legal opinion, that is a conversation with a lawyer, not with these pages.

## This book is a gift

*Hand It Off* is a free giveaway, a gift to the community, and it is not for sale. It is a free, independent, unofficial guide. It is not affiliated with or endorsed by SpaceX Super Intelligence LLC (SpaceXSI), or by xAI, X, Cursor, Anysphere, Nous Research, the OpenClaw Foundation, or any other company whose products are named in these pages. “Grok,” “Grok Bot,” and related marks belong to their owners and are used only to refer to those products. Prices, plans, and features were checked on 11 November 2026 and will change. This book is not legal, financial, medical, or tax advice.

You may share the file freely, with this notice kept with it. Please do not sell it. A WisdomForge page may be a place to get a free copy; it is not a shop in these pages, and this book is not a funnel to anything paid.

## Company names, as the documents use them

I have not drawn a single official org chart, because I did not open one that reconciles every name. I report the names as the documents use them.^1^

The official website at x.ai brands itself **SpaceXSI**. The legal copyright on company and legal pages is **SpaceX Super Intelligence LLC**. The official account on X is **@SpaceXSI**, display name SpaceX Super Intelligence.

The company was previously **xAI**. An official news post dated 2 February 2026, “xAI joins SpaceX,” says: “SpaceX announced today that it has acquired xAI.” The page is short and points to a fuller update I did not open end to end; I do not paraphrase that fuller update here.

Journalism often writes **SpaceXAI**. That is journalism’s spelling, not the site’s own brand.

**Grok Bot** signs in with a **Cursor** account. Many admin, computer, and privacy controls run through Cursor. The App Store listing’s seller, when I opened it on 11 November 2026, was **X Corp.**; the same listing’s copyright line was **Anysphere Inc.**, which is Cursor’s company. Google Play listed the developer as SpaceX Super Intelligence, and also used a **SpaceXAI LLC** address on that listing.

**Grok** the chat app and **Grok Bot** are two different apps, both from SpaceXSI.

**Hermes Agent** is a product of **Nous Research**. **OpenClaw** is stewarded, on its official site, by the **OpenClaw Foundation**. This book is not affiliated with or endorsed by those organizations either, and it is not a manual for those products. They appear in Chapter 16 as other agent platforms in my own work.

My own company, in this story, is **SMF Works**, and the press inside it is **WisdomForge**. **Charlie** is my chief of staff Bot. **Grok Bot Video Studio**, after first introduction, is Video Studio. **Hermes Fleet** and **OpenClaw Fleet** are SMF Works’ fleets on those other platforms, not Grok Bots. When I need a reader who is not me, I use a named **composite** — Priya, Dan, or Alex — and I say so.

I do not name my employer. If a job title is needed, it is Principal AI Solutions Engineer.

## Trademarks, in one careful summary

SpaceXSI’s public Brand Guidelines, dated 14 February 2025 and opened for this book on 11 November 2026, say that SpaceXSI owns trademark rights in “SpaceXSI” and “Grok,” in combinations of those terms, and in logos.^2^ You may use the marks, the guidelines say, only to accurately refer to the company or its services. You may not use them in a way that could misrepresent your relationship with SpaceXSI, mislead, or imply endorsement, approval, or sponsorship. You may not use the marks in an app title, a domain name, or the name of a product or service not made by them, or in or as part of your own marks. If you publish Grok-generated text or images, the guidelines ask for a visible “Written with Grok” or “Created with Grok.” Official logos, if they are used at all, must be used exactly as provided, without alteration; this book does not use official logos.

The title of this book is *Hand It Off*. That is a distinctive main title. “Grok Bot” appears in the subtitle referentially, because this is a practical guide to Grok Bot. I do not use “Grok” as if it were this book’s mark, and I do not speak in the company’s “we.”

The book is a free, non-commercial community gift. The trademark caution in those guidelines **still applies**. Non-commercial use may lower practical trademark risk relative to a book sold as a product. That is an observation, **not a legal conclusion, and not legal advice**. I do not tell you the title is “cleared,” “safe,” or “fair use, definitely.” Whether a particular title is lawful is a question for counsel, not a question this appendix can close.^3^

The same courtesy — accurate reference, no endorsement, no official logos — extends to Cursor, Anysphere, X, Nous Research, Hermes Agent, the OpenClaw Foundation, OpenClaw, 1Password, and every other named product.

## How the notes work

In the body of a chapter, a small marker — ^1^, ^2^, and so on — points to a note at the back of that chapter. The notes start at 1 in each chapter. They use this form:

> N. Author or Organization, “Title,” URL (accessed 11 November 2026).

Official documentation is how the product works, and is attributed to SpaceXSI or to the name the page itself uses. Journalism is named as journalism, with the outlet, and is not treated as a primary for product behavior when an official page exists. Vendor claims stay labeled as vendor. My own SMF Works fleet is a story I am offering; it is not a third-party case study, and I do not invent a footnote that pretends it is.

The body of this book uses **11 November 2026** as the date Grok Bot 1.0 was released, citing the official @SpaceXSI post and the official news page.^4^ The official company timeline also lists “Introducing Grok Bot 1.0” on **2 November 2026**. That date appears in notes, not in the body. I found no primary explanation of the gap, and I did not invent one.

When this book and a live official page disagree, trust the live page, and date what you saw. Prices, plans, and features age.

## What I could not close

A research pass leaves holes. I want those holes in prose, not as a hidden list.

I could not reconcile the 2 November and 11 November dates for 1.0 with a primary explanation. I could not open the full “xAI joins SpaceX” update beyond the short official stub. I could not produce an official org chart of SpaceXSI, SpaceXAI, X Corp., and Anysphere. I did not treat journalistic figures about filings or income as verified fact. I preferred the official “about thirty minutes” for unattended approval expiry over a shorter unofficial figure. I did not state a group-chat size cap, or which model runs which task, or a typical number of hours left in a weekly pool. I did not freeze Pages-on-Enterprise as an eternal no; the 11 November 2026 docs said not yet. I did not treat Teach a Task as available to every plan. I did not run a feature-by-feature competitor comparison; other tools were named from journalism, not crowned. I did not open every secondary essay a search turned up, and I did not quote them. I did not independently verify store-review claims of dollar recoveries or medical results, and I did not treat vendor counts of organizations or bots as audited. I did not invent CLI commands or webhook payloads for the fleets in Chapter 16. I did not cite GitHub star counts. I did not treat Nous Research’s “most widely used” wording, or the OpenClaw Foundation’s 501(c)(3) statement, as independently verified facts; the latter may be reported as the foundation’s own official-site statement.

Those are limits, not mysteries I am keeping. If a later official page closes one of them, that page wins.

## A last word on endorsement

Nothing in this book — not the title, not the diagrams, not the fact that I use the product to run real work — is an endorsement by SpaceXSI, xAI, X, Cursor, Nous Research, the OpenClaw Foundation, or anyone else. It is a free guide from me to you. Take what helps. Leave the rest. Keep the yes.

## Chapter 8 — Routines That Run While Your Laptop Is Closed

1. SpaceXSI, "Skills and routines," <https://docs.x.ai/grok-bot/skills-routines-and-automations> (accessed 11 November 2026). This chapter’s facts on schedules, time zones, the wait-until-next-time rule, event triggers, Slack triggers, webhooks (POST URL, `Authorization: Bearer <key>`, optional JSON body, 200 meaning accepted not finished), test runs performing real work, pause and management, missed-run checks, rate limits and the documented known issue, usage, and design advice all come from this page.

12. This is a gap I am leaving open on purpose. Michael’s described wiring is “via CLI.” How Hermes or OpenClaw are installed on Grok Bot’s computer, if they are, was not described and is not invented here.

13. Nous Research, “Hermes Agent,” <https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/> (accessed 11 November 2026). The official site describes additional product features such as persistent memory, scheduling, subagents, web access, and sandboxing. This book names them only to decline to teach them.
